Asymptotic initial value representation of the solutions of semi-classical systems presenting smooth codimension one crossings Thanks: Acknowledgements . Clotilde Fermanian Kammerer acknowledges the support of the Region Pays de la Loire, Connect Talent Project High Frequency Analysis of Schrödinger equations (HiFrAn 2022 07750), and from the France 2030 program, Centre Henri Lebesgue ANR-11-LABX-0020-01. Caroline Lasser was supported by the Centre for Advanced Study in Oslo, Norway, research project Attosecond Quantum Dynamics Beyond the Born–Oppenheimer Approximation, and by the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG, German Research Foundation) – TRR 352 – Project-ID 470903074.
Clotilde Fermanian-Kammerer, Caroline Lasser, Didier Robert

Abstract
. This paper is devoted to the construction of approximations of the propagator associated with a semi-classical matrix-valued Schrödinger operator with symbol presenting smooth eigenvalues crossings. Inspired by the approach of the theoretical chemists Herman and Kluk who propagated continuous superpositions of Gaussian wave-packets for scalar equations, we consider frozen and thawed Gaussian initial value representations that incorporate classical transport and branching processes along a hopping hypersurface. Based on the Gaussian wave-packet frame work, our result relies on an accurate analysis of the solutions of the associated Schrödinger equation for data that are vector-valued wave-packets. We prove that these solutions are asymptotic to wavepackets at any order in terms of the semi-classical parameter.



Chapter 1
Introduction

Since the early days of semi-classical analysis, operators that approximate the dynamics of a semi-classical propagator have been the object of major attention. The theory of Fourier integral operators answers to this question by proposing methods for constructing approximative propagators of a scalar semi-classical Schrödinger equation (see
[
71
, Chapter 12]
or
[
16
]
).
Pioneering work about a semi-classical theory of FIOs is
[
7
]
, extended in
[
36
]
and
[
59
]
.

Few results exist for systems except for those that are called
adiabatic
, because the eigenvalues of the underlying Hamiltonian matrix are of constant multiplicity. The analysis of such systems can be reduced to those of scalar equations through a diagonalization process using the so-called
super-adiabatic projectors
. The super-adiabatic approach has been carried out by Martinez and Sordoni
[
51
]
as well as Spohn and Teufel
[
62
]
, see also
[
19
,
53
,
54
,
5
]
for earlier results or
[
4
,
58
]
for more recent results in a similar direction.

The present study gives the first complete construction of an integral representation of the propagator associated to a Hamiltonian generating non-adiabatic dynamics in a very general situation. It focuses on those Hamiltonian matrices that have smooth eigenprojectors, with smooth eigenvalues, though of non constant multiplicity. The framework applies to generic situations where two eigenvalues cross along a hypersurface on points where the Hamiltonian vector fields associated with these eigenvalues are transverse to the crossing hypersurface. This set-up has already been the one of the work of Hagedorn
[
31
, Section 5]
and Jecko
[
40
]
. The Fourier integral operators approximating the propagator associated with these non-adiabatic Hamiltonians are based on Gaussian wave-packets and the Bargmann transform, in the spirit of the
Herman–Kluk propagator
.

The Herman–Kluk propagator has been introduced in theoretical chemistry (see
[
37
,
43
,
38
,
44
]
) for the analysis of molecular dynamics for scalar equations. The mathematical analysis has been performed later by Rousse and Swart
[
63
]
and Robert
[
60
]
, independently.
The action of the Herman–Kluk propagator consists in the continuous decomposition of the initial data into semi-classical Gaussian wave-packets and the implementation of the propagation of the wave-packets as studied in the 70s and 80s by Heller
[
37
]
, Combescure and Robert
[
15
]
, and Hagedorn
[
29
]
.
It involves time-dependent quantities that are called
classical quantities
because they can be interpreted in terms of Newtonian mechanics. Such an approximative description of the propagator in terms of several Gaussian wave packets motivates numerical methods that naturally combine with probabilistic sampling techniques, see
[
45
]
or more recently
[
47
,
46
]
.

We prove the convergence of two types of approximations, respectively called
thawed
and
frozen Gaussian approximations
, both built of continuous superpositions of Gaussian
wave-packets, the frozen one in the spirit of the original Herman–Kluk propagator. Their difference mainly consists in the way the width matrices resulting from the propagation of the individual semi-classical Gaussian wave packets are treated. The presence of crossings requires to add to the semi-classical Gaussian wave packet propagation some transitions between the crossing hypersurfaces. Therefore, these Fourier integral operators incorporate classical transport along the Hamiltonian trajectories associated with the eigenvalues of the Hamiltonian and a branching process along the crossing hypersurface. Some of these ideas have been introduced in
[
23
,
24
]
, in particular in
[
23
]
where the propagation of wave-packets through smooth crossings has been studied. Here, we revisit and extend these results, by proving that a wave-packet propagated through a smooth generic crossing remains asymptotically a wave-packet to any order in the semi-classical parameter. We then prove uniform estimates for the associated semi-classical approximations of propagators when acting on families of initial data that are
frequency localized
in the sense that their
L^{2}
-mass does not escape in phase space to
\infty
when the semi-classical parameter goes to
0
, neither in position, nor in momentum. This class of initial data is typically met for the numerical simulation of molecular quantum systems.

Codimension one crossing
is the simplest case of crossings. They are classified by the codimension of the set of dicontinuities of the multiplicity of the eigenvalues of the matrix-valued Hamiltonian. In his monograph
[
31
]
, George Hagedorn has settled a first classification of the crossings involved in molecular dynamiccs that have been improved and extended to general matrix-valued Hamiltonian in 2000 by the normal form results of Colin de Verdière
[
13
,
14
]
. Codimension 1 crossings have the advantage that, despite the discontinuities of the multiplicity of the eigenvalues, the latter are smooth after adequate renumbering. In higher codimensions, the eigenvalues display conical singularities and the transitions generated by the crossing are at leading order
[
31
,
32
,
33
]
(the last two references are treating avoided crossings, i.e. gaps that shrink with the semi-classical parameter, which generates conical singularities in position variables and this parameter). For codimension 1 crossing transitions are of the size of the square root of the semiclassical parameter. Various other questions have been addressed for crossings of codimension 2 and 3, such as resonances asymptotics
[
28
,
27
]
or propagation with nonlinear potentials
[
34
]
. We do not discuss these aspects in this monograph and focus on the analysis of the propagator through a (smooth) codimension one crossing.

The analysis of the propagation through smooth eigenvalue crossings has been
pioneered by Hagedorn in
[
31
, Chapter 5]
. He considered Schrödinger operators with
matrix-valued potentials and propagated initial data that are known as
semi-classical wave packets or generalized coherent
states
[
15
, Chapter 4]
. The core of the wave-packet had to be chosen such that
it classically propagates to the crossing.
In the same framework adjusted to the context of solid states physics, Watson and Weinstein
[
69
]
analyze the propagation of wave-packets through a smooth crossing of Bloch bands.
The results developed here extend
[
31
, Chapter 5]
and
[
69
]
in two ways.
The single wave-packet is turned into an initial value representation with uniform control for frequency localized initial data. The Schrödinger and Bloch operators are generalized to Weyl
quantized operators with smooth time-dependent symbol.

1.1.
First overview and notations

The remainder of the introduction specifies the mathematical setting (assumptions on the Hamiltonian
operator and the initial data), discusses the classical quantities involved in the approximation,
reviews the known results on the thawed and frozen initial value representations in the adiabatic setting,
and then presents the main results of this paper: Theorem
1.23
on the thawed approximation
with hopping trajectories, Theorem
1.24
and Theorem
1.25
on the frozen approximation with hopping trajectories, that are pointwise and averaged in time, respectively,
and Theorem
1.26
on wave-packet propagation through smooth crossings to arbitrary order.

We prove Theorems
1.23
,
1.24
and
1.25
in Chapters
2
and
3
. These proofs rely on Theorem
1.26
, that is proved in
Chapters
4
and
5
.

Chapter
2
recalls elementary facts about the Bargmann transform.
Then, it introduces the new notion of frequency localization, which will be crucial
for controlling the remainder estimates for both the frozen and the thawed initial value representations
in Chapter
3
.

The refined wave-packet analysis of Chapters
4
and
5

does not depend on the theory of initial value representations and can be read
independently from Chapters
2
and
3
. It propagates wave-packets through smooth crossings in two steps: using a rough diagonalisation of the Hamiltonian operator in the crossing region and
super-adiabatic projectors for the outside. Both constructions rely on pseudo-differential calculus
for matrix-valued symbols that is developed in Chapter
4
and complemented by
additional technical points in the appendices. The propagation of a wave packet through regions of small eigenvalue gap and the actual crossing are analyzed in
Chapter

5
and

Chapter

6
, respectively.

Notations and conventions.

All the functional sets that we shall consider can have values in
{\mathbb{C}}
(scalar-valued),
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
(vector-valued) or in
{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m}
(matrix-valued). We denote by by
v\cdot w=v_{1}w_{1}+\cdots+v_{m}w_{m}
the bi-linear products of vectors and by

\langle g,f\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f(x)\cdot\overline{g}(x)dx

the inner product of
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
.
For
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we will work with the spaces

(1.1)

\Sigma_{\varepsilon}^{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})=\left\{f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\;\;\forall\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d},\;\;|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq k,\;\;x^{\alpha}(\varepsilon\partial_{x})^{\beta}f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\right\}

endowed with the norm

\|f\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}=\sup_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq k}\|x^{\alpha}(\varepsilon\partial_{x})^{\beta}f\|_{L^{2}}.

We will use the abbreviation
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}^{k}:=\Sigma_{\varepsilon}^{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. We denote by

\{f,g\}=\nabla_{\xi}f\cdot\nabla_{x}g-\nabla_{x}f\cdot\nabla_{\xi}g

the Poisson bracket of differentiable functions
f
and
g
defined on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}={\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{x}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}_{\xi}
, and note that

\{f,g\}=-\nabla f\cdot J\nabla g
with

J=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbb{I}_{d}\\
-\mathbb{I}_{d}&0\end{pmatrix}.

Let
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
be the semi-classical parameter. For
a\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
being a smooth scalar-, vector- or matrix-valued function with adequate control on the growth of derivatives, the Weyl operator
\widehat{a}={\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(a)
is defined by

(1.2)

{\rm op}^{w}_{\varepsilon}(a)f(x):=\widehat{a}f(x):=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}a\!\left(\frac{x+y}{2},\xi\right){\rm e}^{i\xi\cdot(x-y)/\varepsilon}f(y)\,dy\,d\xi

for all Schwartz functions
f\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. In particular,
{\rm op}_{1}^{w}
denotes the pseudo-differential quantization with
\varepsilon=1
, that is, the standard non semi-classical one. We set
D_{x}={\rm op}_{1}(\xi)=\frac{1}{i}\partial_{x}
. If
\pi
is an orthogonal projector, then
\pi^{\perp}
denotes the projector
\pi^{\perp}=\mathbb{I}-\pi
.

1.2.
The setting

1.2.1.
The Schrödinger equation

For
\varepsilon>0
, we consider the Schrödinger equation

(1.3)

i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)=\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon}(t)\psi^{\varepsilon}(t),\;\;\psi^{\varepsilon}_{|t=t_{0}}=\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}.

in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
,
m\geq 2
, where
\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon}(t)
is the semi-classical quantization of a (time-dependent) Hermitian matrix symbol
H^{\varepsilon}(t,z)\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m}
. Here,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
z=(x,\xi)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. We are interested in an asymptotic description of the solution
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, in particular in asymptotic expansions of
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)
with precise error estimates of the order
\varepsilon
.

In full generality, we could assume that the map
(t,z)\mapsto H^{\varepsilon}(t,z)
is a semi-classical observable in the sense that the function
H^{\varepsilon}(t,z)
is an asymptotic sum of the form
\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}H_{j}(t,z)
. However,
in this asymptotic sum, the important terms are the principal symbol
H_{0}(t,z)
and the sub-principal one
H_{1}(t,z)
; the terms
H_{j}(t,z)
for
j\geq 2
only affect the solution beyond order
\varepsilon
, which is the order of the approximation we are looking for. Therefore, we assume that the self-adjoint matrix
H^{\varepsilon}
writes

H^{\varepsilon}(t,z):=H_{0}(t,z)+\varepsilon H_{1}(t,z).

1.2.2.
Assumptions on the Hamiltonian

We work on a time interval of the form

I:=[t_{0},t_{0}+T],\;\;t_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;T>0

and consider subquadratic matrix-valued Hamiltonians.

Definition 1.1

(Subquadratic)
.

The
\varepsilon
-dependent Hamiltonian

H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m})

is
subquadratic on the time interval
I
if and only if it has the property:

(1.4)

\forall j\in\{0,1\},\;\;\forall\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d},\;\;\exists C_{j,\gamma}>0,\;\;\sup_{(t,z)\in I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|\partial_{z}^{\gamma}H_{j}(t,z)|\leq C_{j,\gamma}\langle z\rangle^{(2-j-|\gamma|)_{+}}

Assuming that
H^{\varepsilon}
is subquadratic on the time interval
I
ensures that the system (
1.3
) is well-posed in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for
t\in I
, and, more generally also in the functional spaces
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
for
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We denote by
{\mathcal{U}}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})
the unitary propagator defined by

(1.5)

i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})=\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon}(t)\mathcal{U}_{H}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{0}),\;\;\mathcal{U}_{H}^{\varepsilon}(t_{0},t_{0})=\mathbb{I}_{m}.

It is a bounded operator of the
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}^{k}
spaces, uniformly in
\varepsilon
(see
[
52
]
): there exists
C_{T,k}>0
such that

\sup_{t\in I}\|{\mathcal{U}}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\|_{{\mathcal{L}}(\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})}\,\leq\,C_{T,k}.

Example 1.2
.

Take
m=2
,
d=1
,
H_{0}=\frac{|\xi|^{2}}{2}\mathbb{I}_{2}+{\rm Diag}(v_{1}(x),v_{2}(x))
with
v_{j}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, and
H_{1}(x)=M
a fixed Hermitian matrix. Assume the boundedness of the derivatives of the function
v_{j}
with order higher than 2. then
H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
is subquadratic.

We assume that the principal symbol
H_{0}(t,z)
of
H^{\varepsilon}(t,z)
has two distinct eigenvalues
h_{1}(t,z)
and
h_{2}(t,z)
that present a smooth crossing in the following sense, see also
[
23
]
.

Definition 1.3
.

(1)

(Smooth crossing).
The matrix
H_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m})
has a smooth crossing on the set
\Upsilon\subseteq I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
if there exists
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
and two orthogonal projectors
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m})
such that
H_{0}=h_{1}\pi_{1}+h_{2}\pi_{2}
and

h_{1}(t,z)=h_{2}(t,z)\;\Longleftrightarrow\;(t,z)\in\Upsilon.

(2)

Set

\displaystyle{f(t,z)=\frac{1}{2}\left(h_{1}(t,z)-h_{2}(t,z)\right)\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;v(t,z)=\frac{1}{2}\left(h_{1}(t,z)+h_{2}(t,z)\right)}
.

(a)

(Non-degenerate crossing). The crossing is non-degenerate at
(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})\in\Upsilon
if

d_{t,z}\left(H_{0}-v\,\mathbb{I}_{m}\right)(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})\not=0

where
d_{t,z}
is the one differential form in the variables
(t,z)
.

(b)

(Generic crossing points).
The crossing is generic at
(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})\in\Upsilon
if one has

(1.6)

(\partial_{t}f+\{v,f\})(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})\neq 0.

Note that there then exists an open set
\Omega\subset I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
containing
(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})
such that the set
\Upsilon\cap\Omega
is a manifold.

Example 1.4
.

Continuing with Example
1.2
, assume
v_{1}(x)=xw(x)
and
v_{2}(x)=-xw(x)
with
w(0)\not=0
. We then have
\Upsilon=\{x=0\}
and the non-degenerate generic crossing points are those included in
\{\xi\not=0\}
.

With these definitions at hand, we introduce one of the main assumptions on the crossing points of the Hamiltonian
H^{\varepsilon}
.

Assumption 1.5

(Crossing set)
.

The matrix
H_{0}
has a smooth crossing set
\Upsilon
and all the points of
\Upsilon
are non degenerate and generic crossing points.

To have well-defined unitary propagators
{\mathcal{U}}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{1}}(t,t_{0})
and
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{2}}(t,t_{0})
and adequate control on the eigenprojectors
\pi_{1}(t,z)
and
\pi_{2}(t,z)
, we shall make additional assumptions on the growth of the eigenvalues and of their gap function. Our setting will be the following:

Assumption 1.6

(Growth conditions for smooth crossings)
.

Let
H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
be
subquadratic on the time interval
I
and have a smooth crossing on the set
\Upsilon
.
We consider the two following assumptions :

(i)

The growth of
H_{0}(t,x)
is driven by the function
v(t,z)
,

(1.7)

\forall\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2d}\;,\;\;\exists C_{\gamma}>0,\;\;\forall(t,z)\in I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\;\;|\partial_{z}^{\gamma}(H_{0}-v\,\mathbb{I}_{m})(t,z)|+|f(t,z)|\leq C_{\gamma}.

(ii)

The gap is controlled at infinity, i.e. there exist
R>0
and
n_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

(1.8)

\forall t\in I,\;\;\forall|z|>R,\;\;|f(t,z)|\geq C\langle z\rangle^{-n_{0}},

and in the case
n_{0}\not=0
, the functions
z\mapsto\pi_{1}(t,z),\pi_{2}(t,z)
are assumed to have bounded derivatives at infinity.

(iii)

The eigenvalues
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are subquadratic, i.e. for
\ell\in\{1,2\}

(1.9)

\forall\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2d},\;|\gamma|\geq 2,\;\;\exists C_{\gamma}>0,\;\;\forall(t,z)\in I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\;\;\left|\partial_{z}^{\gamma}h_{\ell}(t,z)\right|\leq C_{\gamma}.

In the context of Assumption
1.6
, we shall say that a matrix
A
is diagonal if
A=\pi_{1}A\pi_{1}+\pi_{2}A\pi_{2}
and off-diagonal if
A=\pi_{1}A\pi_{2}+\pi_{2}A\pi_{1}
.

Remark 1.7
.

(1)

In (iii), the fact that the eigenvalues
h_{1}(t,z)
and
h_{2}(t,z)
are of subquadratic growth guarantees the existence
of the unitary propagators
{\mathcal{U}}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{j}}(t,t_{0})
for
j\in\{1,2\}
and of the classical quantities associated with the Hamiltonians
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
that we will introduce below.

(2)

The growth conditions of Assumption
1.6

imply that
the eigenprojectors
\pi_{j}(t)
,
j=1,2
, and their derivatives have at most polynomial growth. However, when
n_{0}\not=0
, they may actually grow. This is proved in Lemma
A.4
. It is for this reason that we assume that the projectors have bounded derivatives when
n_{0}\not=0
in (ii).

Example 1.8
.

(1)

The Hamiltonian of Example
1.2
and
1.4
, satisfies Assumption
1.5
in the set
\{\xi\not=0\}
. It also satisfies Assumption
1.6
(iii) in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. However, even it does not formally satisfy Assumption
1.6
(i) and (ii), the eigenprojectors
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are constant, and thus satisfy all the conditions required for our analysis.

(2)

Examples of matrix-valued Hamiltonian are given in molecular dynamics (as defined in
[
31
]
[Chapter 5]) by
Schrödinger operators with matrix-valued potential,

\widehat{H}_{S}=-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2}\Delta_{x}\,\mathbb{I}_{2}+V(x),\quad V\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{2,2}).

When
V
presents a codimension 1 crossing (as defined in
[
31
]
, then the crossing points
(x,\xi)
are non degenerate and generic when
\xi\not=0
.

(3)

Another class of examples appears in
solid state
physics in the context of Bloch band decompositions (see
[
69
,
12
]
for example) with Hamiltonians of the form

\widehat{H}_{A}=A(-i\varepsilon\nabla_{x})+W(x)\mathbb{I}_{2},\quad A\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{2,2}),\quad W\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}).

(4)

Finally, in
[
24
]
, the authors have considered the operator

\widehat{H}_{k,\theta}=\frac{\varepsilon}{i}\frac{d}{dx}\mathbb{I}_{2}+kx\begin{pmatrix}0&{\rm e}^{i\theta x}\\
{\rm e}^{-i\theta x}&0\end{pmatrix},

with
d=1
,
m=2
,
\theta\in{{\mathbb{R}}}_{+}
,
k\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
.

1.2.3.
Assumptions on the data

We consider vector-valued initial data
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
of the form

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\widehat{\vec{V}}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}

where
z\mapsto\vec{V}(z)
is a smooth
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
vector-valued function, bounded together with its derivatives, and

\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}})
is
frequency localized
in the sense of the next definition. For stating it, we denote the Gaussian of expectation
q
, variance
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
that oscillates along
p
according to

(1.10)

g^{\varepsilon}_{z}(x)=(\pi\varepsilon)^{-d/4}{\rm e}^{-\frac{(x-q)^{2}}{\varepsilon}+\frac{i}{\varepsilon}p\cdot(x-q)},\;\;\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\;{\rm with}\;z=(q,p).

Definition 1.9

(Frequency localized functions)
.

Let

(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a family of functions of
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. The family
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is frequency localized
if the family is bounded in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and if there exist
R_{0},C_{0},\varepsilon_{0}>0
and
N_{0}>d
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
,

(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d/2}\left|\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{z},\phi^{\varepsilon}\rangle\right|\leq C_{0}\,\langle z\rangle^{-N_{0}}\;\;\ \text{for all}\ z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\ \text{with}\ |z|>R_{0}.

One then says that
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is frequency localized.

We will introduce a more precise definition in Chapter
2
. We will also discuss in this Chpater the properties of frequency localized families. In particular, we will show that they can be written as continuous sum of Gaussian states with center
(q,p)
localized in a compact set (see Lemma
2.6
). The analysis of the examples given below is performed in Lemma
2.8
.

Example 1.10
.

(1)

The Gaussian wave packets
(g^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}})_{\varepsilon>0}
are frequency localized functions.

(2)

Define
({\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}(u))_{\varepsilon>0}
by

(1.11)

{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}(u)(x)=\varepsilon^{-d/4}{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}p_{0}\cdot(x-q_{0})}u\left(\frac{x-q_{0}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right),\;\;x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},

for
u\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
z_{0}=(q_{0},p_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
. They are frequency localized functions.

(3)

Lagrangian (or WKB) states

\varphi^{\varepsilon}(x)=a(x){\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(x)}

with
a\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}})
and
S\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{R}})
, are also frequency localized functions.

Our vector-valued initial data will have a scalar part consisting in a frequency localized family.

Assumption 1.11
.

The initial data
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
in (
1.3
) satisfies

(1.12)

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)=\widehat{\vec{V}}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x),\;\;x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

where

(i)

The family
(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{\varepsilon>0}
is frequency localized with constants
R_{0},N_{0},C_{0},\varepsilon_{0}
in Definition
1.9
.

(ii)

The function
z\mapsto\vec{V}(z)
is a function of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
, bounded together with its derivatives, and valued in the set of normalized vectors.

We point out that any vector-valued bounded family in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
writes as a sum of data of the form
\widehat{\vec{V}}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)
for bounded
(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{\varepsilon>0}
. As a consequence, assuming the initial datum
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
satisfies (
1.12
) is not really restrictive. Of course, for
k\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus\{0\}
,the vector valued function
\vec{V}
can be turned into
k\vec{V}

by changing
\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
into
k^{-1}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
.

1.3.
Classical quantities

In this section, we introduce classical quantities associated with the Hamiltonian
H^{\varepsilon}
. These quantities will be used to construct the approximations of the propagator
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})
that are the subject of this text. They are called
classical
because they do not depend on the semi-classical parameter
\varepsilon
and are obtained by solving
\varepsilon
-independent equations that mainly are ODEs instead of PDEs. Thus, the numerical realization of the resulting propagator’s approximations avoids the difficulties induced by the
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
-oscillations and is applicable in a high-dimensional setting, see
[
48
]
for a recent review on this topic. Besides their definition, we shall also recall well-known results about their role in the description of Schrödinger propagators.

In this section, we assume that
H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
is subquadratic on the time interval
I
(as defined in Definition
1.1
), with smooth eigenprojectors
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
, and eigenvalues
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
, the latter being subquadratic (as in (iii) of Assumption
1.6
).

1.3.1.
The flow map

Let
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, we associate with
h_{\ell}:I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
(t,z)\mapsto h_{\ell}(t,z)
the functions

z_{\ell}(t)=(q_{\ell}(t),p_{\ell}(t))

which denote the
classical Hamiltonian trajectory
issued from a phase space point
z_{0}
at time
t_{0}
, that is defined by the ordinary differential equation

\dot{z}_{\ell}(t)=J\partial_{z}h_{\ell}(t,z_{\ell}(t)),\;\;z_{\ell}(t_{0})=z_{0}

with

(1.13)

J=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbb{I}_{d}\\
-\mathbb{I}_{d}&0\end{pmatrix}.

We note that
J
is the matrix associated with the symplectic form

\sigma(z,z^{\prime})=\langle Jz,z^{\prime}\rangle=p\cdot q^{\prime}-p^{\prime}\cdot q,\qquad z=(q,p),\,z^{\prime}=(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}.

The trajectory
z_{\ell}(t)=z_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})
depends on the initial datum and defines
the associated
flow map

\Phi_{h_{\ell}}^{t,t_{0}}
of the Hamiltonian function
h_{\ell}
via

z_{0}\mapsto\Phi_{h_{\ell}}^{t,t_{0}}(z_{0}):=z_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0}),\qquad z_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}.

We will also use the trajectory’s
action integral

(1.14)

S_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\left(p_{\ell}(s)\cdot\dot{q}_{\ell}(s)-h_{\ell}(s,z_{\ell}(s))\right)ds,

and the
Jacobian matrix of the flow map
, also called
stability matrix

(1.15)

F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})=\partial_{z}\Phi_{h_{\ell}}^{t,t_{0}}(z_{0}).

Note that
F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})
is a symplectic
2d\times 2d
matrix, that satisfies the linearized flow equation

(1.16)

\partial_{t}F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})=J{\rm Hess}_{z}h_{\ell}(t,z_{\ell}(t))\,F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0}),\;\;\;\;F_{\ell}(t_{0},t_{0},z_{0})=\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}.

We denote its blocks by

(1.17)

F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})=\begin{pmatrix}A_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})&B_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})\\
C_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})&D_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})\end{pmatrix}.

Example 1.12
.

In the case of Example
1.2
, we have
\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)=(q_{\ell}(t-t_{0}),p_{\ell}(t-t_{0}))
for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, with

(1.18)

\dot{q}_{\ell}(t)=p_{\ell}(t),\;\;\dot{p}_{\ell}(t)=-\nabla v_{\ell}(q_{\ell}(t)),\;\;(q_{\ell}(0),p_{\ell}(0))=z.

The action satisfies
S_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)=\underline{S}_{\ell}(t-t_{0},z)
with
\underline{\dot{S}}_{\ell}(t)=\frac{|p_{\ell}(t)|^{2}}{2}-v_{\ell}(q_{\ell}(t))
and the flow map also is a function of
t-t_{0}
. Moreover, the system (
1.16
) simplifies since

{\rm Hess}\,h_{\ell}(t,z)=\begin{pmatrix}{\rm Hess}\,v_{\ell}(q)&0\\
0&\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\end{pmatrix}

1.3.2.
The metaplectic transform and Gaussian states

It is standard to
associate with the time-dependent symplectic map
F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},\cdot)
a unitary evolution operator, the
metaplectic transformation
that acts on square integrable functions in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})

as a unitary transformation.

{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]:\;u_{0}\mapsto u(t)

and
associates with an initial datum
u_{0}
the solution at time
t
of the Cauchy problem

i\partial_{t}u(t)={\rm op}_{1}\left({\rm Hess}_{z}h_{\ell}\left(t,\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z_{0})\right)z\cdot z\right)u(t),\;\;u(t_{0})=u_{0}.

This map is called the metaplectic transformation associated with the matrix
F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})
(see
[
52
]
).
It satisfies for all
\varepsilon>0
and for all symbol
a
compactly supported or polynomial

(1.19)

{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]^{-1}{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(a){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]={\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(a\circ F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})),

where
a\circ F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})
denotes the function
z\mapsto a\left(F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})z\right)
.

All these classical quantities are involved in the description of the propagation of
Gaussian states
by
{\mathcal{U}}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{\ell}}(t,t_{0})
, that are a generalization of the Gaussian families
(g^{\varepsilon}_{z})_{\varepsilon>0}
that we have already seen.
Gaussian states are wave packets
{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z}(g^{\Gamma})
with complex-valued Gaussian profiles
g^{\Gamma}
, whose
covariance matrix
\Gamma
is taken in the Siegel half-space
{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}(d)
of
d\times d
complex-valued symmetric matrices with positive imaginary part,

{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}(d)=\left\{\Gamma\in{\mathbb{C}}^{d\times d},\ \Gamma=\Gamma^{\tau},\ \text{\rm Im}\Gamma>0\right\}.

Here
\Gamma^{\tau}
is the transposed matrix of
\Gamma
and
\text{\rm Im}\Gamma>0
means that the matrix

\text{\rm Im}\Gamma
is positive definite.
The covariance matrices
\Gamma
are sometimes called
width
of the Gaussian state.

More precisely, the Gaussian profile
g^{\Gamma}
depends on
\Gamma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}(d)
according to

(1.20)

g^{\Gamma}(x):=c_{\Gamma}\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{2}\Gamma x\cdot x},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},

where

c_{\Gamma}\in{\mathbb{C}}
is a normalization constant such that
|c_{\Gamma}|=\pi^{-d/4}{\rm det}^{1/4}(\text{\rm Im}\Gamma)
. With these notations, the normal centered Gaussian is
g^{i\mathbb{I}_{d}}
and the Gaussian wave packets defined in (
1.10
) writes

g^{\varepsilon}_{z}={\rm WP}_{z}^{\varepsilon}(g^{i\mathbb{I}_{d}})
, according to (
1.11
).

The propagation of Gaussian states by a metaplectic transform is well-known
[
15
]
[Chapter 3]:
for
\Gamma_{0}\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}(d)
, we have

(1.21)

{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]g^{\Gamma_{0}}=g^{\Gamma_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})},

where the width
\Gamma_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})\in\mathfrak{S}^{+}(d)
and the corresponding normalization
c_{\Gamma_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})}
are determined by the initial width
\Gamma_{0}
and the Jacobian
F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})
according to

(1.22)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(C_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})+D_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})\Gamma_{0})(A_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})+B_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})\Gamma_{0})^{-1}

\displaystyle c_{\Gamma_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c_{\Gamma_{0}}\,{\rm det}^{-1/2}(A_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})+B_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})\Gamma_{0}).

The branch of the square root in
{\rm det}^{-1/2}
is determined by continuity in time.

A quick calculation based on Taylor formula shows that the relation

(1.23)

\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{\ell}}(t,t_{0}){\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}(\varphi)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z_{0})}(\varphi^{\varepsilon}(t))

if and only if
\varphi^{\varepsilon}(t)
solves

i\partial_{t}\varphi^{\varepsilon}(t)={\rm op}_{1}\left(\int_{0}^{1}{\rm Hess}_{z}h_{\ell}\left(t,\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z_{0})+\tau z)\right)z\cdot z(1-\tau)d\tau\right)\varphi^{\varepsilon},\;\;\varphi^{\varepsilon}(t_{0})=\varphi.

Therefore, a remarkable consequence of the fact that the metaplectic transform preserves the Gaussian structure according to (
1.21
) is that the Gaussian structure is preserved in the quantum evolution. Indeed,
the action of
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{\ell}}(t,t_{0})
on Gaussian wave packets
{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z}(g^{\Gamma})
obeys

\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{\ell}}(t,t_{0}){\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}(g^{\Gamma_{0}})={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z_{0})}(g^{\Gamma_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})})+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon})

in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

1.3.3.
Parallel transport

For systems, the wave function is valued in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
and thus vector-valued. The propagation then involves transformation of the vector part of the eigenfunctions that is called
parallel transport
.

Denoting by
\pi_{\ell}^{\perp}
the projector
\pi_{\ell}^{\perp}=\mathbb{I}_{m}-\pi_{\ell}
, we define self-adjoint matrices
H^{{\rm adia}}_{\ell,1}
by

(1.24)

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell}^{\perp}\,H_{\ell,1}^{\rm adia}\pi_{\ell}^{\perp}=0,\;\;\;\;\;\pi_{\ell}\,H_{\ell,1}^{\rm adia}\pi_{\ell}=\pi_{\ell}\left(H_{1}+\frac{1}{2i}\{H_{0},\pi_{\ell}\}\right)\pi_{\ell},

(1.25)

\displaystyle\pi_{\ell}^{\perp}\,H_{\ell,1}^{\rm adia}\pi_{\ell}=\pi_{\ell}^{\perp}\left(i\partial_{t}\pi_{\ell}+i\{h_{\ell},\pi_{\ell}\}\right)\pi_{\ell}.

One then introduces the map
{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})
defined for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
by -

(1.26)

i\partial_{t}{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})=H^{{\rm adia}}_{\ell,1}\left(t,\Phi_{h_{\ell}}^{t,t_{0}}(z_{0})\right){\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0}),\;\;{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{0},t_{0},z_{0})=\mathbb{I}_{m}.

The map
t\mapsto H^{{\rm adia}}_{\ell,1}\left(t,\Phi_{h_{\ell}}^{t,t_{0}}(z_{0})\right)
is a locally Lipschitz map valued in the set of self adjoint matrices. Therefore, the existence of
{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})
comes from solving a linear time dependent ODE by the Cauchy–Lipschitz Theorem.

Lemma 1.13
.

For all
(t_{0},z_{0})\in I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
and
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, the matrices

{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})
are unitary matrices. Besides, for all
\omega\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
, they satisfy

(1.27)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})=\pi_{\ell}\left(t,\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z_{0})\right){\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0}),

(1.28)

\displaystyle\omega\cdot\nabla_{z}\mathcal{R}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)=\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\mathcal{R}_{\ell}(t,s,z)(F(s,t_{0},z)\omega)\cdot\nabla_{z}H_{\ell,1}^{\rm adia}(s,\Phi^{s,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z))\mathcal{R}_{\ell}(s,t_{0},z)ds.

This Lemma is proved in Appendix
A
.
The relation (
1.27
) implies that
whenever a vector
\vec{V}_{0}
is in the eigenspace of
H_{0}(t_{0},z_{0})
for the eigenvalue
h_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})
, then the vector
{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}
is in the range of
\pi_{\ell}(t,\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z_{0}))
.
In other words, we have constructed a map that preserves the eigenspaces along the flow:

{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0}):{\rm Ran}\left(\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\right)\mapsto{\rm Ran}\left(\pi_{\ell}(t,\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z_{0}))\right).

The matrices
{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})
are sometimes referred to as Larmor precession (see
[
15
, §14.2]
).

For
(t,z)\in I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
,
the map
\vec{V}_{0}\mapsto{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z)\vec{V}_{0}
is a parallel transport in the Hermitian vector fiber bundle
(t,z)\mapsto{\rm Ran}(\pi_{\ell}(t,z))
over the phase space
I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}
,
associated with the curves
s\mapsto\gamma(s)=\left(s,\Phi^{s,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)\right)_{s\in I}
and the matrix
H^{\rm adia}_{\ell,1}
. Indeed, the covariant derivative along the curve
(\gamma(s))_{s\in I}
is given by

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}(s)}=\partial_{t}+Jdh_{\ell}\cdot\nabla_{z}

and the relation

\vec{X}\left(s,\Phi^{s,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)\right)={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z)\vec{V}_{0}
defines a smooth section along the path
\gamma
that satisfies

\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}(s)}\vec{X}(t,x)=-iH_{\ell,1}^{\rm adia}(t,x)\vec{X}(t,x)
.

Example 1.14
.

In the situation of Example
1.4
, for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, the operators
\mathcal{R}_{\ell}
are multiplications by
{\rm e}^{-itm_{\ell\ell}}
where we have set
M=(m_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}
.

1.3.4.
Classical quantities and wave-packets propagation

The classical maps introduced before, in particular the parallel transport
\mathcal{R}_{\ell}
,
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, play a role on
the quantum side in the propagation of
vector-valued wave packets
by systems. By vector-valued wave packets, we mean initial data colinear to fixed direction
\vec{V}_{0}
with scalar part that is a wave packet as in (
1.11
):

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)=\vec{V}_{0}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}\varphi_{0},\;\;x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\;\;\varphi_{0}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\;\;z_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\;\;\vec{V}_{0}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}.

We will say that
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
is
polarized
along the direction
\vec{V}_{0}
.

Under the assumptions made in this text, the propagation of such data is described at leading order by the classical trajectories and the classical quantities associated with them, including the parallel transport.

Proposition 1.15

(Vector-valued wave packets)
.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and assume that
H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
satisfies Assumptions
1.6
and
1.6
.
Let
\ell\in\{1,2\}
and
(t_{0},z_{0})\in I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
Then,
for any
\varphi_{0}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}})
and
\vec{V}_{0}\in{\rm Ran}\,\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})
, there exists a
constant
C>0
such that

\sup_{t\in I}\left\|{\mathcal{U}}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\,\widehat{\vec{V}_{0}}\,{{\rm WP}}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}\varphi_{0}-{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})}\,\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})\,{{\rm WP}}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi_{h_{\ell}}^{t,t_{0}}(z_{0})}\varphi(t)\right\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}\leq C\sqrt{\varepsilon},

where the profile function
\varphi(t)
and the vector of polarization
\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})
are given by

\varphi(t)={\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]\varphi_{0},\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}\left(t,t_{0},\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z_{0})\right)\vec{V}_{0}.

The proof of Proposition
1.15
can be found in
[
15
, Chapter 14]
for Hamiltonians without crossings, and in
[
23
]
or in Appendix
C
of this memoir for codimension 1 crossings. Recall that the functional spaces
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, are defined in (
1.1
).

1.4.
Thawed and frozen Gaussian approximations

Thawed and frozen Gaussian approximations have been introduced in the 80’s in theoretical chemistry
[
38
,
43
,
44
]
. The frozen one has become popular as the so-called Herman–Kluk approximation. They rely on the fact that the
family of wave packets
(g^{\varepsilon}_{z})_{z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}
forms a continuous frame and
provides for all square integrable functions
f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
the reconstruction formula

f(x)=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{z},f\rangle g^{\varepsilon}_{z}(x)dz.

The leading idea is then to write the unitary propagation of general, square integrable initial data
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
as

{\mathcal{U}}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{z},\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\rangle\,{\mathcal{U}}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})g^{\varepsilon}_{z}\,dz,

and to take advantage of the specific properties of the propagation of Gaussian states to obtain an integral representation that allows in particular for an efficient numerical realization of the propagator.

Such a program has completely been accomplished in the scalar case and
the mathematical proof of the convergence of this approximation is more recent
[
63
,
60
]
. It can be easily extended to the adiabatic setting
(see
[
23
]
).
We recall in the first subsections the scalar and adiabatic results and then we explain how we extend this approach to systems presenting smooth crossings via a hopping process. Surface hopping has been popularized in theoretical chemistry by the algorithm
of the fewest switches (see
[
67
]
). The first mathematical result proving convergence of such a hopping algorithm has been performed in
[
21
]
in the context of conical intersections. Surface hopping has
been combined with frozen Gaussian propagation
in various instances, see for example
[
70
,
49
]
. Here, it is the first time that the combination of a Gaussian approximation and a hopping process are achieved in a fully rigorous manner. All along this section, we use the Gaussian frame defined in (
1.10
).

1.4.1.
The scalar case

Let us assume for a while that
m=1
and select one of the scalar Hamiltonians
h_{\ell}(t)
that we assume to be subquadratic on
I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
. Define the operator

(1.29)

\mathcal{J}_{\ell,{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}(f)=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)}{\langle}g^{\varepsilon}_{z},f{\rangle}g_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(t,z)}^{\Gamma_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z),\varepsilon}dz.

As first proposed in
[
38
]
, it is also possible to get rid of the time-dependent variance matrices
\Gamma_{\ell}
by introducing the
Herman-Kluk prefactors for the
\ell
-th modes
,
a_{\ell}
, defined by

(1.30)

a_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)=2^{-d/2}{\rm det}^{1/2}\left(A_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)+D_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)+i(C_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)-B_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)\right),

where
A_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)
,
B_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)
,
C_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)
and
D_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)
are the
d\times d
matrices associated with the differential of the flow map according to (
1.15
).
One then sets

(1.31)

\mathcal{J}_{\ell,{\rm fr}}^{t,t_{0}}(f)=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)}{\langle}g^{\varepsilon}_{z},f{\rangle}a_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)g_{\Phi_{h_{\ell}}^{t,t_{0}}(z)}^{\varepsilon}dz

It is proved in
[
60
,
63
]
that the operators
\mathcal{J}_{{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}
and
\mathcal{J}_{{\rm fr}}^{t,t_{0}}
approximate the propagator
\mathcal{U}_{h_{\ell}}^{t,t_{0}}
according to

\left\|\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{\ell}}(t,t_{0})\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)-\mathcal{J}_{\ell,{\rm th/fr}}^{t,t_{0}}(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C_{T}\,\varepsilon\,\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}},

for all

\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
. Here,
C_{T}>0
is a constant depending on

T>0
where
t_{0},T
are chosen in
{\mathbb{R}}
with
[t_{0},T]\subset I
and
t_{0}<T
.

The arguments we detail in Chapter
2
give a proof of this result for frequency localized data. However, the result holds as soon as the initial data are uniformly bounded in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
(see
[
63
,
60
]
).

1.4.2.
The adiabatic situation

Whenever the eigenvalues are of constant multiplicity, the classical quantities that we have introduced above are enough to construct an approximation of the propagator. For
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, we define the first order thawed Gaussian approximation for the
\ell
-th mode as the operator
\mathcal{J}_{\ell,{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}
defined on functions of the form
\psi=\widehat{\vec{V}}f
for
\vec{V}
a smooth given vector-valued function and
f
any function in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,

(1.32)

\mathcal{J}_{\ell,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}(f)=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)}{\langle}g^{\varepsilon}_{z},f{\rangle}\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)g_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(t,z)}^{\Gamma_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z),\varepsilon}dz,

with

(1.33)

\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)=\mathcal{R}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z)\vec{V}(z).

The family of operators
f\mapsto\mathcal{J}_{\ell,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}(f)
has a Schwartz distribution kernel and defines a Fourier integral operator with an explicit complex phase. It is proved in Corollary
2.16
that it is a bounded family in
\mathcal{L}\left(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\right)
.

Theorem 1.16

(Thawed Gaussian approximation
[
43
,
60
,
63
,
23
]
)
.

Assume
h_{\ell}
is an eigenvalue of constant multiplicity of a matrix
H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
of subquadratic growth on the time interval
I
. Let
t_{0},T\in{\mathbb{R}}
with
[t_{0},T]\subset I
. Then, there exists
C_{T}>0
such that for all

\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,
\vec{V}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
bounded with bounded derivatives, for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T]

\left\|\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\left(\widehat{\pi_{\ell}(t_{0}){\vec{V}}}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)-\mathcal{J}_{\ell,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C_{T}\,\varepsilon\,\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}.

Remark 1.17
.

(1)

Note that in Theorem
1.16
, the family
(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{\varepsilon>0}
is not supposed to be frequency localized.

(2)

The approach we develop in this text yields as a by-product the convergence of the thawed propagator in the spaces
\Sigma^{\varepsilon}_{k}
for initial data
(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{\varepsilon>0}
which are frequency localized with
k
satisfying
N_{0}>k+d
(
N_{0}
being associated to
(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{\varepsilon_{0}}
by Definition
1.9
).

In a similar manner than in the scalar case, one
defines the first order frozen Gaussian approximation for the
\ell
-th mode as the operator
\mathcal{J}_{\ell,\vec{V},{\rm fr}}^{t,t_{0}}
defined by

(1.34)

\mathcal{J}_{\ell,\vec{V},{\rm fr}}^{t,t_{0}}(f)=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)}{\langle}g^{\varepsilon}_{z},f{\rangle}a_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)g_{\Phi_{h_{\ell}}^{t,t_{0}}(z)}^{\varepsilon}dz

where
\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)
is defined in (
1.33
) and
a_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)
is the Hermann-Kluk prefactor associated with the
\ell
-th mode as in (
1.30
).

Here again, this family of operators is bounded in the functional space
\mathcal{L}\left(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\right)
(see Corollary
2.16
).
The next result then is a consequence of Theorem
1.16
and Remark
1.17
also holds for this statement.

Theorem 1.18

(Frozen Gaussians approximation
[
43
,
60
,
63
,
23
]
)
.

Assume
h_{\ell}
is an eigenvalue of constant multiplicity of a matrix
H=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
of subquadratic growth on the time interval
I
. Let
t_{0},T\in{\mathbb{R}}
with
[t_{0},T]\subset I
. Then, there exists
C_{T}>0
such that for all

\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,
\vec{V}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
bounded with bounded derivatives, and for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
,

\left\|\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\left(\widehat{\pi_{\ell}(t_{0}){\vec{V}}}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)-\mathcal{J}_{\ell,\vec{V},{\rm fr}}^{t,t_{0}}(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq C_{T}\,\varepsilon\,\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}.

The terminology
thawed/frozen
for these Gaussian approximations was introduced by Heller
[
37
]
to put emphasis on the fact that, on the first case, the covariance of the matrix was evolving “naturally” by following the classical motion, while, on the other one, the covariance is “frozen” (constant). The possibility of freezing the covariance matrix was realized by Herman and Kluk (see
[
38
]
) by computing the kernel of the time dependent propagator.

In the next sections, we present our results and an extension of these statements to systems with crossings.
The method we develop also allows to prove the approximations of Theorems
1.18
and
1.16
in the spaces
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, with additional assumptions
on the initial data.

1.4.3.
Initial value representations for codimension
1
crossing at order
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
.

Our first result consists in an extension of the range of validity of Theorems
1.16
and
1.18
to Hamiltonians presenting smooth crossing and satisfying (
1.6
) at the prize of a loss in the accuracy of the approximation.

Theorem 1.19

(Leading order thawed/frozen Gaussian approximation)
.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Assume

H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
satisfies Assumptions
1.5
and
1.6
on the interval
I
.
Then, there exist constants
C_{T,k}>0
, such that for all initial data

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\vec{V}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
that satisfies Assumption
1.11
with frequency localization index
N_{0}>k+d
and constant
C_{0}
, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that
for all
t\in I
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, we have

\left\|\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\left(\widehat{\pi_{\ell}(t_{0}){\vec{V}}}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)-\mathcal{J}_{\ell,{\rm th}/{\rm fr}}^{t,t_{0}}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}\leq C_{T,k}\,\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\left(\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}+C_{0}\right).

The remarks below also hold for Theorems
1.23
,
1.24
and
1.25
.

Remark 1.20
.

(1)

Of course the result also holds for all initial data of the form

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)=\widehat{\vec{V}}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)+r^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x),\;\;x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

when the family
(r^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{\varepsilon>0}
satisfies
\|r^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
for the index
k
considered in the statement.

(2)

The fact of being frequency localized with
N_{0}>k+d+\frac{1}{2}
implies that
(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{\varepsilon>0}
is bounded in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
(see Section
2.2.6
). Thus,
(\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{\varepsilon>0}
also is bounded in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
and this space is the natural space where studying the approximation.

(3)

The control of the approximation in terms of the initial datum by
\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}+C_{0}
instead of
\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{\Sigma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}
is due to the method of the proof, which has to account for the presence of the crossing. The constant
C_{0}
(and the
L^{2}
-norm) controls the
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
-norm.

The loss of accuracy of the approximation, in
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
instead of
\varepsilon
, is due to the presence of the crossing set
\Upsilon
. Indeed, the crossing induces transitions between the modes that are exactly of order
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
and cannot be neglected. If the initial datum is frequency localized in a domain such that all the classical trajectories issued from its microlocal support at time
t_{0}
do not reach the crossing set before the time
t_{0}+T
, then an estimate in
\varepsilon
will hold. However, if these trajectories pass through the crossing, some additional terms of order
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
have to be added to obtain an approximation at order
\varepsilon
.
Let us now introduce the hopping trajectories that we will consider and the branching of classical quantities that we will use above the crossing set for treating these transitions.

1.4.4.
Hopping trajectories and branching process

Assume
H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
satisfies condition
1.6
and
1.6
on the interval
I
.
For considering initial data
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\widehat{\vec{V}}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
with
(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{\varepsilon>0}
frequency localized in a compact set
K\subset B(0,R_{0})
, we are going to make assumptions on the set
K
.

We consider sets
K
that are connected compact subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
and that do not intersect the crossing set
\Upsilon
. If one additionally assumes that the trajectories
\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)
issued from points
z\in K
intersect
\Upsilon
on generic crossing points,
then, because of their transversality to
\Upsilon
, a given
trajectory
\Phi_{h_{\ell}}^{t,t_{0}}(z)
issued from
z\in K
meets
\Upsilon
only a finite number of times.
We then denote by
(t^{\flat}_{\ell}(t_{0},z),\zeta^{\flat}_{\ell}(t_{0},z))
the first crossing point in
\Upsilon
:

(1.35)

\zeta_{\ell}^{\flat}(t_{0},z)=\Phi^{t^{\flat}_{\ell}(t_{0},z),t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z).

For the
\ell
-th mode and the compact
K
, we define

t_{\ell,{\rm max}}^{\flat}(t_{0},K)=\max\{t_{\ell}^{\flat}(t_{0},z),\;z\in K\}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;t_{\ell,{\rm min}}^{\flat}(t_{0},K)=\min\{t_{\ell}^{\flat}(t_{0},z),\;z\in K\}.

We shall assume that
K
is well-prepared for the mode
\ell
in the sense that all trajectories issued from
K
for this mode
\ell
have passed through
\Upsilon
(if they do) before the ones for the other mode start to reach
\Upsilon
.

Assumption 1.21

(Well-prepared frequency domain)
.

Let
K
be a connected compact subset
K
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
which does not intersect the crossing set
\Upsilon
. We say that
K
is a
well-prepared frequency domain on the time-interval
I
if for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, the trajectories
(\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z))_{t\in I}
issued from points
z\in K
intersect
\Upsilon
on generic crossing points and one has

t_{1,{\rm max}}^{\flat}(t_{0},K)<t_{2,{\rm min}}^{\flat}(t_{0},K).

A space-time crossing point
(t^{\flat}_{\ell}(t_{0},z),\zeta^{\flat}_{\ell}(t_{0},z))
is characterized by three parameters

\mu^{\flat}\in{\mathbb{R}},\qquad(\alpha^{\flat},\beta^{\flat})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}

given by

(1.36)

\displaystyle\mu^{\flat}(t_{0},z)=\partial_{t}f+\{v,f\}\left(t^{\flat}_{\ell}(t_{0},z),\zeta^{\flat}_{\ell}(t_{0},z)\right),

(1.37)

\displaystyle\left(\alpha^{\flat}(t_{0},z),\beta^{\flat}(t_{0},z)\right)=2\,J\nabla_{z}f\left(t^{\flat}_{\ell}(t_{0},z),\zeta^{\flat}_{\ell}(t_{0},z)\right).

Let us consider a trajectory for the mode
\ell=1
that reaches the hypersurface
\Upsilon
.
The hopping process is affected with a
transition coefficient

\tau_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)
that restricts the space time variables
(t,z)
to trajectories that have met the crossing set
\Upsilon

(1.38)

\tau_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)=\mathbb{I}_{t\geq t^{\flat}_{1}(t_{0},z)}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{i\mu^{\flat}(t_{0},z)}}.

Note that when
K
satisfies Assumption
1.21
, then if
t<t^{\flat}_{1,{\rm min}}(K)
and
z\in K
, then
\tau_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)=0
. Moreover, if
t\in\left(t^{\flat}_{1,{\rm max}}(K),t^{\flat}_{2,{\rm min}}(K)\right)
,
z\mapsto\tau_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)
is smooth.

One also considers a
matrix of change of polarization

(1.39)

W_{1\to 2}(t_{0},z)=W_{1}\!\left(t_{1}^{\flat}(t_{0},z),\zeta_{1}^{\flat}(t_{0},z)\right)^{*}

with

(1.40)

W_{1}=\pi_{1}H_{1}\pi_{2}+i\pi_{1}\left(\partial_{t}\pi_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\{h_{1}+h_{2},\pi_{1}\}\right)\pi_{2}

The matrix
W_{1\to 2}

maps
{\rm Ran}(\pi_{1}(\zeta_{1}^{\flat}(t_{0},z))
to
{\rm Ran}(\pi_{2}(\zeta_{1}^{\flat}(t_{0},z))
.

One then introduces
hopping trajectories
by setting

(1.41)

\Phi_{1,2}^{t,t_{0}}(z)=\Phi_{2}^{t,t^{\flat}_{1}(t_{0},z)}\left(\zeta_{1}^{\flat}(t_{0},z)\right),\;\;t>t^{\flat}_{1}(t_{0},z).

This trajectory
\left(\Phi_{1,2}^{t,t_{0}}(z)\right)_{t>t^{\flat}(t_{0},z)}
is the branch of a generalized trajectory that has hopped from the mode
\ell=1
to the mode
\ell=2

at the crossing point
(t^{\flat}_{1}(t_{0},z),\zeta^{\flat}_{1}(t_{0},z))
.
One could define similarly trajectories hopping from the mode
\ell=2
to
\ell=1
by exchanging the role of the indices
1
and
2
.

Along these trajectories, one defines classical quantites as follows:

(a)

The function
S_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)
is the action accumulated along the hopping trajectories, i.e. between times
t_{0}
and
t^{\flat}_{1}(t_{0},z)
on the mode
\ell=1
and then on the mode
\ell=2

(1.42)

S_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)=S_{1}(t^{\flat}_{1}(t_{0},z),t_{0},z)+S_{2}(t,t^{\flat}_{1}(t_{0},z),\zeta^{\flat}_{1}(t_{0},z)),

(b)

The matrix
\Gamma_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)
is generated according to (
1.22
) for the mode
\ell=2
along the trajectory

\left(\Phi_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)\right)_{t>t^{\flat}_{1}(t_{0},z)}
starting at time
t_{1}^{\flat}=t^{\flat}_{1}(t_{0},z)
from the matrix

(1.43)

\Gamma^{\flat}(t_{0},z)=\Gamma_{1}(t^{\flat}_{1},t_{0},z)-\frac{(\beta^{\flat}-\Gamma_{1}(t^{\flat}_{1},t_{0},z)\alpha^{\flat})\otimes(\beta^{\flat}-\Gamma_{1}(t^{\flat}_{1},t_{0},z))}{2\mu^{\flat}-\alpha^{\flat}\cdot\beta^{\flat}+\alpha^{\flat}\cdot\Gamma_{1}(t^{\flat}_{1},t_{0},z)\alpha^{\flat}},

(c)

The vector

\vec{V}_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)
is obtained by propagating the vector
\vec{V}_{1}\left(t_{1}^{\flat},t_{0},z\right)
for the mode
\ell=2
along the trajectory

\left(\Phi_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)\right)_{t>t^{\flat}(t_{0},z)}
starting at time
t_{1}^{\flat}
from the vector
\pi_{2}(t_{1}^{\flat},\zeta_{1}^{\flat})\vec{V}_{1}\left(t_{1}^{\flat},t_{0},z\right)
with
\zeta_{1}^{\flat}=\zeta_{1}^{\flat}(t_{0},z)
. One has

(1.44)

\vec{V}_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)=\mathcal{R}_{2}(t,t_{1}^{\flat},\zeta_{1}^{\flat})\pi_{2}\left(t_{1}^{\flat},\zeta_{1}^{\flat}\right)W_{1\rightarrow 2}(t_{0},z)\vec{V}_{1}\left(t_{1}^{\flat},t_{0},z\right).

(d)

The matrices
F_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)
are associated with the flow maps

(1.45)

F_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)=\partial_{z}\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{1,2}(z)=\begin{pmatrix}A_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)&B_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)\\
C_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)&D_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)\end{pmatrix}.

(e)

The transitional Herman–Kluk prefactors
depend on
\Gamma_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)
and
\tau_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)
according to

(1.46)

\displaystyle a_{1,2}

\displaystyle=\tau_{1,2}\frac{{\rm det}^{1/2}(C_{1,2}-iD_{1,2}-i(A_{1,2}-iB_{1,2}))}{{\rm det}^{1/2}(C_{1,2}-iD_{1,2}-\Gamma_{1,2}(A_{1,2}-iB_{1,2}))}

\displaystyle=\tau_{1,2}\frac{{\rm det}^{1/2}(A_{1,2}+D_{1,2}+i(C_{1,2}-B_{1,2}))}{{\rm det}^{1/2}(D_{1,2}+iC_{1,2}-i\Gamma_{1,2}(A_{1,2}-iB_{1,2}))}

where we have omitted to mark the dependence on
(t,t_{0},z)
for readability.

Example 1.22
.

With the notations of Example
1.4
, if
q_{1}(t^{\flat})=0
with
p^{\flat}:=p_{1}(t^{\flat})\not=0
, we have a generic crossing points with transition parameters

\mu^{\flat}=p^{\flat},\;(\alpha^{\flat},\beta^{\flat})=(0,-w(0)),\;\;\tau_{1,2}(t,0,z)=\mathbb{I}_{t\geq t_{1}^{\flat}(0,z)}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{ip^{\flat}}},\;\;W_{1\to 2}(t_{0},z)=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
\overline{m}_{12}&0\end{pmatrix}

where we have set
M=(m_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}
and used that
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are the orthogonal projectors on the vectors
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
respectively.

With these quantities in hands, we can define the correction terms of order
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
of the thawed and the frozen approximations and state our main results.

1.4.5.
Thawed Gaussian approximation at order
\varepsilon

With the notations of the preceding section, one defines the
thawed Gaussian correction term for the mode
\ell=1
as

(1.47)

\mathcal{J}_{1\to 2,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}(f)=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{z\in K}\tau_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z){\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)}\langle g_{z}^{\varepsilon},f\rangle\vec{V}_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)g_{\Phi_{1,2}^{t,t_{0}}(z)}^{\Gamma_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z),\varepsilon}dz

The formula (
1.47
) defines a family of operators that is bounded in
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
(see Corollary
2.16
).
The restriction
t>t_{1}^{\flat}(t_{0},z)
introduces a localization of the domain of integration on one side of the hypersurface
\{t=t_{1}^{\flat}(t_{0},z)\}
.

The thawed Gaussian correction term for the mode
\ell=2
, denoted by
\mathcal{J}_{2\to 1,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}
would be defined by exchanging the roles of the indices
1
and
2
. These correction terms allow to ameliorate the accuracy of the thawed Gaussian approximation and to obtain an approximation at order
\varepsilon
.

Theorem 1.23

(Thawed Gaussian approximation with hopping trajectories)
.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Assume

H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
satisfies Assumption
1.5
and s
1.6
on the interval
I
.
Let
K
be a compact satisfying Assumption
1.21
. Let
\varsigma\in(0,1)
.
Then, there exists constants
C_{T,k,K,\varsigma}>0
, such that for all initial datum

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\vec{V}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
that satisfies Assumption
1.11

with frequency localization index
N_{0}>k+d
and constants
C_{0}
,
R_{0}
with
K\subset B(0,R_{0})
, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that
for all
t\in I

we have for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
,

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}

\displaystyle-\mathcal{J}_{1,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)-\mathcal{J}_{2,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathcal{J}_{1\to 2,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)\Bigr\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}\leq\varepsilon^{1-\varsigma}\,C_{T,k,K,\varsigma}\left(C_{0}+\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\right).

This result emphasizes that for systems with smooth crossings, a term of order
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
is generated by the crossing.

1.4.6.
Frozen Gaussian approximation at order
\varepsilon

In order to freeze the covariance of the Gaussians
\Gamma_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)
that appear in the formula of the thawed Gaussian correction term (
1.47
), we use the correction prefactor
a_{1,2}
introduced in (
1.46
)
and define the frozen Gaussian correction term for the mode
\ell=1
as

(1.48)

\mathcal{J}_{1,2,\vec{V},{\rm fr}}^{t,t_{0}}(f)=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{z\in K}a_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z){\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)}\langle g_{z}^{\varepsilon},f\rangle\vec{V}_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)g^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi_{1,2}^{t,t_{0}}(z)}dz.

The formula (
1.48
) defines a Fourier-integral operator with a complex phase associated with the canonical transformations
\Phi_{1,2}^{t,t_{0}}
associated with the hopping flow. It is a family of operators that is bounded in
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
by Corollary
2.16
below.

We first state a point-wise approximation.

Theorem 1.24

(Point-wise time frozen Gaussian approximation with hopping trajectories)
.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Assume

H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
satisfies Assumptions
1.5
and
1.6
on the interval
I
.
Let
K
be a compact satisfying Assumption
1.21
. Let
\varsigma\in(0,1)
.
Then, there exists constants
C_{T,k,K,\varsigma}>0
, such that for all initial datum

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\vec{V}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
that satisfies Assumption
1.11

with frequency localization index
N_{0}>k+d
and constant
C_{0}
,
R_{0}
with
K\subset B(0,R_{0})
, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that
for all
t\in I
satisfying

t<t_{1,\rm min}^{\flat}(t_{0},K)\;\;\mbox{or}\;\;t^{\flat}_{1,{\rm max}}(t_{0},K)\leq t<t^{\flat}_{2,{\rm min}}(t_{0},K),

we have for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
,

\displaystyle\biggl\|\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}-\mathcal{J}_{1,\vec{V},{\rm fr}}^{t,t_{0}}

\displaystyle\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)-\mathcal{J}_{2,\vec{V},{\rm fr}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{J}_{1\to 2,\vec{V},{\rm fr}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)\Bigr\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}\leq\varepsilon^{1-\varsigma}\,C_{T,k,K,\varsigma}\,\left(\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}+C_{0}\right).

The proof of Theorem
1.24
is based on integration by parts and requires differentiability.
When
t<t^{\flat}_{1,{\rm min}}(K)
, then the transfer coefficient
\tau_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)=0
for all
z\in K
.
Moreover, on the interval
[t^{\flat}_{1,{\rm max}}(K),t^{\flat}_{2,{\rm min}}(K))
, then
z\mapsto\tau_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)
is smooth. It is for that reason, that we have to restrict the time validity of the approximation.

Averaging in time allows to overcome this difficulty and to obtain an approximation result that holds almost everywhere on intervals of time such that the classical trajectories issued from
K
and associated with the level
\ell=2
have not yet reached
\Upsilon
.

Theorem 1.25

(Time averaged frozen Gaussian approximation with hopping trajectories)
.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Assume

H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
is of subquadratic growth and satisfies Assumptions
1.5
and
1.6
on the interval
I
.
Let
K
be a compact satisfying Assumption
1.21
. Let
\varsigma\in(0,1)
.
Then, there exists constants
C_{T,k,K,\varsigma}>0
, such that for all initial datum

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\vec{V}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
that satisfies Assumption
1.11

in
K
with frequency localization index
N_{0}>k+d
and constant
C_{0}
, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that
for all
\chi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{0}\left(\left(t_{0},t^{\flat}_{2,{\rm min}}(t_{0},K)\right)\right)
,

\displaystyle\biggl\|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi(t)\biggl(\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}-\mathcal{J}_{1,\vec{V},{\rm fr}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)-\mathcal{J}_{2,\vec{V},{\rm fr}}^{t,t_{0}}\,\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathcal{J}_{1\to 2,\vec{V},{\rm fr}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)\biggr)dt\Bigr\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq\varepsilon^{1-\varsigma}\,C_{T,k,K,\varsigma}\,\|\chi\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\left(\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}+C_{0}\right).

To go beyond the time
t^{\flat}_{2,{\rm min}}(t_{0},K)
, one has to consider new transitions that would now go from the level
\ell=2
to the level
\ell=1
, each time a trajectory for the level
\ell=2
hits
\Upsilon
. The process can be understood as a random walk: each time a trajectory passes through
\Upsilon
a new trajectory arises on the other mode with a transition rate of order
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
.

The averaging in time can be understood as the result of a non-pointwise observation, that takes place over some time interval, that might even be a short one.

For proving Theorems
1.24
and
1.25
we use an accurate analysis for the propagation of individual wave-packets. We prove that a Gaussian wave-packet stays a generalized Gaussian wave packet modulo an error term of order
\varepsilon^{\mu}
, for any
\mu\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and in any space
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, as well before, or after, hitting the crossing hypersurface
\Upsilon
.

The proof then consists first in proving the thawed approximations and in deriving the frozen approximation from the thawed one. The arguments developed in Section
3.3
will show that one can “freeze” the Gaussian on any state
g^{\Gamma_{0},\varepsilon}_{z}
. The choice of some
\Gamma_{0}
instead of
i\mathbb{I}_{d}
will imply a slight modification of the definition of the Herman–Kluk prefactors
a_{\ell}
, and the
transitional ones
a_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}
,
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\{1,2\}
.

1.5.
Wave packets propagation at any order through generic smooth crossings

Our results crucially rely on the analysis of the propagation of wave-packets (including the ones with Gaussian amplitude functions) through smooth crossings.
We consider a Hamiltonian
H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
that satisfies Assumption
1.6
on the time interval
I
and presents a smooth crossing on a set
\Upsilon
. We fix a point
z_{0}=(q_{0},p_{0})\notin\Upsilon
and times
t_{0},T
such that

(1.49)

t_{0}<t^{\flat}_{1}(t_{0},z_{0})<t_{0}+T<t_{2}^{\flat}(t_{0},z_{0}).

We assume that the point
z^{\flat}_{1}:=\Phi^{t_{1}^{\flat}(t_{0},z_{0}),t_{0}}_{h_{1}}(z_{0})
is a non degenerate generic crossing point (see Definition
1.3
) that we denote by
\Phi^{t_{1}^{\flat},t_{0}}_{h_{1}}(z_{0})
for simplicity. We use the notations introduced in Section
1.4.5
(namely equations (
1.35
), (
1.36
), (
1.37
), (
1.41
) and (
1.42
)).
For
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, we set

z_{\ell}(t)=\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z_{0}).

Theorem 1.26
.

Let
(t_{0},z_{0})
satisfy (
1.49
), let
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
be a polarized wave packet

(1.50)

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\vec{V}_{0}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}(f_{0})\;\;\mbox{with}\;\;f_{0}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\vec{V}_{0}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}.

Let

\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)
be the solution of (
1.3
) with initial datum
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
.
There exist
\kappa_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and three families of differential operators
\left(\vec{B}_{\ell,j}(t)\right)_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
\ell\in\{1,2\}
and
\left(\vec{B}_{1\rightarrow 2,j}(t)\right)_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that setting
for
\delta>0
and
t\in I_{\delta}=[t_{0},t_{1}^{\flat}(t_{0},z_{0})-\delta]\cup[t_{1}^{\flat}(t_{0},z_{0})+\delta,t_{0}+T]

\displaystyle\psi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{1}(t,t_{0},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{1}(t)}\left(f^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t)\right),

\displaystyle\psi_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{2}(t,t_{0},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{2}(t)}\left(f^{\varepsilon}_{2}(t)\right)+{\mathbb{I}}_{t>t^{\flat}}{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z_{0})}\left(f^{\varepsilon}_{1\rightarrow 2}(t)\right),

with

\displaystyle f^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}(t)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]\sum_{0\leq j\leq N}\varepsilon^{j/2}\,\vec{B}_{\ell,j}(t)f_{0},\;\;\ell\in\{1,2\},

\displaystyle f^{\varepsilon}_{1\rightarrow 2}(t)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}_{2}(t,t^{\flat}_{1})\,{\mathcal{M}}[F_{2}(t,t^{\flat}_{1},z^{\flat}_{1})]\sum_{1\leq j\leq N}\varepsilon^{j/2}\,\vec{B}_{1\rightarrow 2,j}(t)f_{0},

one has the following property:
for all
k,N,M\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists
C_{M,N,k}>0
such for all
t\in I_{\delta}

\left\|\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)-\left(\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}(t)+\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}(t)\right)\right\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}\leq C_{M,N,k}\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{N+1}\delta^{-2\kappa_{0}-k}+\delta^{M}\right).

Moreover,
the operators
\vec{B}_{\ell,j}(t)
are differential operators of degree
\leq 3j
with time dependent smooth vector-valued coefficients and satisfy for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
,

(1.51)

\displaystyle\vec{B}_{\ell,0}(t)={\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})}\vec{V}_{0}\;\;\mbox{ and}\;\;\vec{B}_{\ell,j}(t_{0})=0\;\;\forall j\geq 1,

(1.52)

\displaystyle\vec{B}_{\ell,1}(t)=\biggl(\sum_{|\alpha|=3}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\partial_{z}^{\alpha}h_{\ell}(s,z_{\ell}(s))\,{\rm op}_{1}^{w}[(F_{\ell}(s,t_{0},z_{0})z)^{\alpha}]\,ds\,\mathbb{I}_{m}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\mathcal{R}(t_{0},s)\,\nabla_{z}H^{\rm adia}_{\ell,1}(s,z_{s})\cdot{\rm op}^{w}_{1}(F_{\ell}(s,t_{0},z_{0})z)\,\mathcal{R}(s,t_{0})\,ds\biggr){\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})}\vec{V}_{0}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\nabla_{z}\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\cdot\widehat{z}\,\vec{V}_{0},

(1.53)

\displaystyle\vec{B}_{{1\rightarrow 2},0}(t)=W_{1}(t^{\flat}_{1},\zeta_{1}^{\flat})^{*}\,\mathcal{T}^{\flat}_{1\rightarrow 2}\,\mathcal{M}[F_{1}(t^{\flat}_{1},t_{0},z_{1}^{\flat})]\pi_{1}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\delta)

as operator acting on the Schwartz class, and where the scalar transfer operator
\mathcal{T}^{\flat}_{1\rightarrow 2}
is defined by

(1.54)

{\mathcal{T}}^{\flat}_{1\rightarrow 2}\varphi(y)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}{\rm e}^{-i(\mu^{\flat}+\alpha^{\flat}\cdot\beta^{\flat}/2)s^{2}}{\rm e}^{is\beta^{\flat}\cdot y}\varphi(y-s\alpha^{\flat})ds,\;\;\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})

and the transfer matrix
W_{1}(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})
is given by (
1.40
).

In other words, Theorem
1.26
says that if
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
is a polarized wave packet, then, for
t\in I
,
t\not=t^{\flat}_{1}(t_{0},z_{0})
, the solution
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)
of (
1.3
) is asymptotic at any order to a sum of wave packets. Indeed, if
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
is fixed, choosing
\delta=\varepsilon^{1/2-c}
,
0<c<1/2
and
M,N
large enough will give an approximation in
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{n})
. This fact is crucial for the proofs of Theorems
1.23
,
1.24
and
1.25
.

The polarization of the wave packets
\psi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)
is first described by the vectors
\vec{B}_{\ell,j}(t_{0})
that evolves through
{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0})]
. Such evolution preserves the eigenmode. Secondly, in
\psi_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)
, one sees a
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
contribution that comes from a transfer from the mode
1
to the mode
2
with a change of polarization performed by the matrix
W_{1}^{*}
. This operator
\vec{B}_{1\to 2,0}(t)
coincides with a simple transfer operator given in (
1.53
) up to a term of order
\delta
. Higher order terms in
\epsilon
due to the transfer are more intricate.

Theorem
1.26
was proved in
[
23
]
up to order
o(\varepsilon)
. The notations are compatible. However,
in
[
23
]
, a coefficient
\gamma^{\flat}
appears in the definition of the transfer operator. It corresponds to a normalization process that we avoid here by using the projector
\pi_{2}(t^{\flat}_{1},\zeta_{1}^{\flat})
instead of taking the scalar product with a normalized eigenvector.

For proving the initial value representations of
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}
of Theorems
1.19
to
1.25
, we shall use two consequences of Theorem
1.26
:

(i)

the wave packet structure up to any order in
\varepsilon
of
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}\vec{V}_{0}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}(g^{i\mathbb{I}_{d}})
,

(ii)

the exact value of the action of
\vec{B}_{1,0}
,
\vec{B}_{2,0}
, and of
\vec{B}_{1\rightarrow 2}(t)
when
f_{0}
is the Gaussian
g^{i\mathbb{I}_{d}}
.

We recall that the action of the operators
{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,s)\,{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,s)]
on focalized Gaussians preserves the Gaussian structure and the focalization: in view of (
1.21
) and (
1.33
),

{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,s)\,{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,s,z)]\pi_{\ell}(s,z)\vec{V}_{0}g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}=\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,s,z)g^{\Gamma_{\ell}(t,s,z)}

where
\vec{V}_{\ell}\in{\rm Ran}(\pi_{\ell}(t,\Phi^{t,s}_{\ell}(z)))
and the matrix
\Gamma_{\ell}(t,s,z)
is given by (
1.22
) with
\Gamma_{0}=i\mathbb{I}_{d}
.
Besides, regarding the transfer term,
with the notations of Corollary 3.9 of
[
23
]
and those of (
1.36
), (
1.37
), (
1.43
) and (
1.44
)

\displaystyle\vec{B}_{1\rightarrow 2,0}(t^{\flat}_{1})g^{i\mathbb{I}_{d}}=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{i\mu^{\flat}(t_{0},z_{0})}}\,\vec{V}_{1,2}(t_{1}^{\flat},t_{0},z_{0})g^{\Gamma^{\flat}(t_{1}^{\flat},t_{0},z_{0})}.

These elements may enlighten the construction of the operator

\mathcal{J}_{1,2,\vec{V},{\rm fr/th}}^{t,t_{0}}
.

1.6.
Wave packet propagation, an example

In this section, we give a direct proof of Theorem
1.26
in a simple special case. It shows how the transfer terms of order
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
arise.
We assume that
d=1
and we consider the Hamiltonian

H(x,\xi)=\xi\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{2}}+xR_{\theta(x)}

where the
2
by
2
matrix
R_{\theta(x)}
is the
x
-dependent matrix

R_{\theta(x)}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta(x)&\sin\theta(x)\\
\sin\theta(x)&-\cos\theta(x)\end{pmatrix},\;\;\theta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}),

and
x\mapsto\theta(x)
has bounded derivatives.
The Hamiltonian
H
presents a smooth crossing along
\Upsilon=\{x=0\}
according to Definition
1.3
and satisfies Assumptions
1.6
. Moreover, we have

v=\xi,\;\;f=x,\;\;h_{1}=\xi+x,\;\;h_{2}=\xi-x.

The projector
\pi_{1}(x)
is the orthogonal projection on the vector
\vec{e}(x)=\Bigl(\cos\left({\theta(x)}/2\right),\,\sin\left({\theta(x)}/2\right)\Bigr)
,
and the projector
\pi_{2}(x)
on the vector
\vec{e}(x)^{\perp}=\left(-\sin\left({\theta(x)}/2\right),\,\cos\left({\theta(x)}/2\right)\right)
. We write

\pi_{1}(x)=|\vec{e}(x)\rangle\,\langle\vec{e}(x)|\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\pi_{2}(x)=\pi_{1}(x)^{\perp}=|\vec{e}(x)^{\perp}\rangle\,\langle\vec{e}(x)^{\perp}|.

We start by a rough diagonalisation of the system, which shows the role of the matrix
W_{1}
defined in (
1.40
).
Then, we will fix the function
\theta
and prove Theorem
1.26
with
N=1
in this case. The case
N=1
gives a good understanding of the mechanism of the transfer, even though
the approximation of the unitary propagator by an initial value representation
requires the result for any
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

1.6.1.
Rough diagonalisation and the role of the matrix
W_{1}

This matrix
W_{1}
appears as soon as one aims at decoupling the equations according to the modes.

Lemma 1.27
.

The functions

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}(t,x)=\pi_{\ell}(x)\psi^{\varepsilon}(t,x),\;\;(t,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}},\;\;\ell\in\{1,2\},

satisfy the system

(1.55)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t,x)=((\varepsilon D_{x}+x)\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{2}}+\varepsilon\Omega(x))\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t,x)+\varepsilon W_{1}(x)\psi^{\varepsilon}_{2}(t,x),\\
i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\psi^{\varepsilon}_{2}(t,x)=((\varepsilon D_{x}-x)\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{2}}+\varepsilon\Omega(x))\psi^{\varepsilon}_{2}(t,x)+\varepsilon W_{1}(x)^{*}\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t,x).\end{array}\right.

with

\Omega(x):=i\pi_{2}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{1}(x)\pi_{1}(x)-i\pi_{1}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{1}(x)\pi_{2}(x),\;\;W_{1}(x):=i\pi_{1}(x)\pi_{1}^{\prime}(x)\pi_{2}(x),\;\;x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

The arguments developed in Part 2 for proving Theorem
1.26
in full generality are based on such a ‘rough’ diagonalisation explained in Chapter
4
(see Section
4.2
).
The matrix-valued term
\Omega(x)
is also the first term obtained far from the crossing when one uses superadiabatic projectors as in Section
4.3
. The matrix
W_{1}
is a key element of the transfer between the modes. In particular, if the function
\theta
is constant,
W_{1}=0
, the equations are decoupled and there is no transfer. The transfer is mainly due to the
variations of the eigenprojectors.

Proof.

By projecting the equations, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}i\varepsilon\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t,x)=(\varepsilon D_{x}+x+i\varepsilon\pi_{2}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{1}(x)\pi_{1}(x))\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t,x)+i\varepsilon\pi_{1}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{1}(x)\pi_{2}(x)\psi^{\varepsilon}_{2}(t,x),\\
i\varepsilon\psi^{\varepsilon}_{2}(t,x)=(\varepsilon D_{x}-x+i\varepsilon\pi_{1}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{2}(x)\pi_{2}(x))\psi^{\varepsilon}_{2}(t,x)+i\varepsilon\pi_{2}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{2}(x)\pi_{1}(x)\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t,x).\end{array}\right.

In the computation above, we have used

\pi^{\prime}_{1}(x)=\pi_{1}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{1}(x)\pi_{2}(x)+\pi_{2}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{1}(x)\pi_{1}(x),\;\;x\in{\mathbb{R}}.

In order to view this system as governed by a self adjoint operator, we take advantage of the fact that
\pi_{2}(x)\pi_{1}(x)=0
to replace the matrix

i\pi_{2}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{1}(x)\pi_{1}(x)
by the self-adjoint matrix
\Omega(x)
.
Similarly, on the other mode, we replace
i\pi_{1}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{2}(x)\pi_{2}(x)
by
i\pi_{1}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{2}(x)\pi_{2}(x)-i\pi_{2}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{2}(x)\pi_{1}(x)
.
In view of
\pi_{2}(x)=1-\pi_{1}(x)
, we have

\displaystyle i\pi_{1}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{2}(x)\pi_{2}(x)-i\pi_{2}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{2}(x)\pi_{1}(x)=-i\pi_{1}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{1}(x)\pi_{2}(x)+i\pi_{2}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{1}(x)\pi_{1}(x)=\Omega(x),

\displaystyle i\pi_{2}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{2}(x)\pi_{1}(x)=-i\pi_{2}(x)\pi^{\prime}_{1}(x)\pi_{1}(x)=W_{1}(x)^{*},

which gives the result.
∎

1.6.2.
Propagation of wave packets

We now assume
\theta(x)=x
for all
x\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
We fix
\tau>0
and choose an interval of time
I=[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
given by

t_{0}=-\tau,\;\;t_{0}+T=\tau.

We are going to use
the
