classical trajectories

z_{1}(t):=\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t,-\tau}(-\tau,\tau)=\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t,0}(0,0)=(t,-t)\;\;\mbox{ and}\;\;z_{2}(t):=\Phi_{h_{2}}^{t,-\tau}(-\tau,-\tau)=\Phi_{h_{2}}^{t,0}(0,0)=(t,t).

These trajectories meet on
\Upsilon
at
(t^{\flat},z^{\flat})
with

t^{\flat}=0,\;\;z^{\flat}=(0,0).

As a consequence, the initial data is chosen as

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}(0)={\rm WP}_{z_{1}(-\tau)}(f_{1})\vec{e}(-\tau),\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\psi^{\varepsilon}_{2}(0)={\rm WP}_{z_{2}(-\tau)}(f_{2})\vec{e}(-\tau)^{\perp},

with
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}})
.
The actions associated with
z_{1}(t)
and
z_{2}(t)
respectively and given for
t\in[-\tau,\tau]
by

S_{1}(t)=\frac{t^{2}}{2}-\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;S_{2}(t)=-\frac{t^{2}}{2}+\frac{\tau^{2}}{2}.

In the statement of Theorem
1.26
, several quantities are of importance and have simple form for this example:

(1)

The
metaplectic operator
associated with the classical trajectories are identity:

\mathcal{M}[F_{\ell}(t,0,z^{\flat})]=\mathbb{I}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})},\;\;\ell=\{1,2\},\;\;t\in[-\tau,\tau].

(2)

The
parallel transports
of the vectors
\vec{e}(t)
(resp.
\vec{e}(t)^{\perp}
) along
z_{1}(t)
(resp.
z_{2}(t)
) are simple:

\mathcal{R}_{1}(t,0,z^{\flat})\,\vec{e}(0)=\vec{e}(t)\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\mathcal{R}_{2}(t,0,z^{\flat})\,\vec{e}(0)^{\perp}=\vec{e}(t)^{\perp}.

Indeed, we have for all
t\in[-\tau,\tau]

i\partial_{t}\vec{e}(t)=\Omega(t)\vec{e}(t)=\vec{e}(t)^{\perp}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;i\partial_{t}\vec{e}(t)^{\perp}=\Omega(t)\vec{e}(t)^{\perp}=-\vec{e}(t).

(3)

The
correction terms
inside each mode is given by vector-valued multiplication operators:

\vec{B}_{1,1}(t,y):=\frac{y}{i}\int_{-\tau}^{t}\Omega^{\prime}(s)\vec{e}(s)\,ds\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\vec{B}_{2,1}(t,y):=\frac{y}{i}\int_{-\tau}^{t}\Omega^{\prime}(s)\vec{e}(s)^{\perp}\,ds,\;\;\ell\in\{1,2\}\;\;y\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(4)

The
transfer operators

\mathcal{T}_{1\to 2}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2\to 1}
are given by multiplication operators (see the analysis of Section
6.4
):

\mathcal{T}_{1\to 2}=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}{\rm e}^{is^{2}-2isy}ds=\sqrt{i\pi}\;{\rm e}^{-iy^{2}}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\mathcal{T}_{2\to 1}=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}{\rm e}^{-is^{2}+2isy}ds=\sqrt{-i\pi}\;{\rm e}^{iy^{2}}.

In particular,

\mu^{\flat}=2,\;\;\alpha^{\flat}=0\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\beta^{\flat}=-2,

according to (
1.36
) and (
1.37
).
Moreover,

W_{1}(t)^{*}\vec{e}(t)=-i\vec{e}(t)^{\perp}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;W_{1}(t)\vec{e}(t)^{\perp}=i\vec{e}(t),\;\;t\in[-\tau,\tau].

As a consequence,
Theorem
1.26
with
N=1
is equivalent to the next Lemma
1.28
that we will prove directly.

Lemma 1.28
.

With the notation of points (3) and (4) above, define

(1.56)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1,\rm app}(t,x)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{1}(t)}{\rm WP}_{z_{1}(t)}\left(f_{1}\vec{e}(t)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\vec{B}_{1,1}(t)f_{1}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\tau^{2}}\,\mathbb{I}_{t>0}\mathcal{T}^{\flat}_{2\to 1}\,f_{2}\,\vec{e}(t)\right),\\
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{2,\rm app}(t,x)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{2}(t)}{\rm WP}_{z_{2}(t)}\left(f_{2}\,\vec{e}(t)^{\perp}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\vec{B}_{2,1}(t)f_{2}-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,{\rm e}^{-\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\tau^{2}}\,\mathbb{I}_{t>0}\,\mathcal{T}^{\flat}_{1\to 2}\,f_{1}\,\vec{e}(t)^{\perp}\right).\end{array}\right.

Then, for all
\delta\in(0,\sqrt{\varepsilon})
, there exists
C_{\delta}>0
such that

\sup_{\delta<|t|\leq\tau}\|\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)-(\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1,\rm app}(t)+\psi^{\varepsilon}_{2,\rm app}(t))\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}})}\leq C_{\delta}\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{2}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\delta\right).

Proof.

Let us investigate the construction of the approximate solution. We look for
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}(t)
and
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{2}(t)
, satisfying (
1.55
), and of the form

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}(t,x)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t)}{\rm WP}_{z_{\ell}(t)}(\vec{f}_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}(t)),\;\;\ell\in\{1,2\}

with
\vec{f}_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}(t)\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}^{2})
,

\vec{f}^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}(t,y)=\vec{f}_{\ell,0}(t,y)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\vec{f}_{\ell,1}(t,y)+o(\sqrt{\varepsilon}),\;\;t\in[-\tau,\tau],\;\;y\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Since
z_{1}(t),z_{2}(t),S_{1}(t),S_{2}(t)
are fixed, the map
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}(t)\mapsto\vec{f}_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}(t)
is one to one on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}^{2})
for each
\ell\in\{1,2\}
.
The idea is to plug the ansatz in the equation (
1.55
) and to choose the functions
\vec{f}_{1,0}
,
\vec{f}_{2,0}
,
\vec{f}_{1,1}
and
\vec{f}_{2,1}
so that there only remains terms of order
\varepsilon^{2}
in the equation (up to
\delta
-depending coefficients).

We start by considering the diagonal part of the system (
1.55
).
Injecting the ansatz in the equation leads to

(1.57)

\displaystyle\left(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-\varepsilon D_{x}-(-1)^{\ell}x-\varepsilon\,\Omega\right)\left({\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t)}{\rm WP}_{z_{\ell}(t)}(\vec{f}^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}(t))\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\varepsilon\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t)}{\rm WP}_{z_{1}(t)}\left(i\partial_{t}\vec{f}_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}(t)-\Omega(t+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y)\vec{f}^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}(t)\right).

This equation comes from the matching of the actions and trajectories with the Hamiltonians
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
. Such a discussion is developed in full generality in Sections (
C.3.1
) and (
C.3.2
).

Let us now use (
1.57
) to fix the first elements of the ansatz. We consider the mode
\ell=1
. The
function

\vec{f}_{1,0}(t,y)=f_{1}(y)\vec{e}(t),

satisfies

i\partial_{t}\vec{f}_{1,0}(t)-\Omega(t)\vec{f}_{1,0}(t)=0

and thus
contributes to suppressing the terms of degree
\varepsilon
in (
1.57
). For getting rid of the terms of order
\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}
, one asks for a first term in
\vec{f}_{1,1}
, that we denote by
f_{1,1}^{\rm cor}
, and that will satisfy

i\partial_{t}\vec{f}_{1,1}^{\rm cor}(t,y)=\Omega^{\prime}(t)y\vec{f}_{1,0}(t)=f_{1}(y)\Omega^{\prime}(t)\vec{e}(t).

This equation implies
\vec{f}_{1,1}^{\rm cor}(t)=\vec{B}_{1,1}(t)f_{1}
and gives the first term in
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
in the expansion (
1.56
). One argues similarly for the mode
\ell=2
and obtains the term
\vec{B}_{2,1}(t)f_{2}
in the expansion (
1.56
).

We now have to understand how to use a contribution in
\vec{f}_{1,1}(t)
and
\vec{f}_{2,1}(t)
for compensating the terms generated by the off-diagonal terms of the Hamiltonian in (
1.55
).
We look for a term of the form
\vec{f}_{1,1}^{\rm tr}(t)=f_{1}^{\rm tr}(t)\vec{e}(t)
so that up to
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
,

{\rm WP}_{z_{1}(t)}(i\partial_{t}f_{1}^{\rm tr}(t))\,\vec{e}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}(S_{2}(t)-S_{1}(t))}{\rm WP}_{z_{2}(t)}(W_{1}(t+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y)\vec{f}^{\varepsilon}_{2}(t,y)).

Or, equivalently

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}f_{1}^{\rm tr}(t,y)\,\vec{e}(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\rm e}^{-\frac{i}{\varepsilon}(t^{2}-\tau^{2})+\frac{2i}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}ty}W_{1}(t+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y)\vec{f}^{\varepsilon}_{2}(t,y))

\displaystyle\;=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\rm e}^{-\frac{i}{\varepsilon}(t^{2}-\tau^{2})+\frac{2i}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}ty}\left(f_{2}(y)W_{1}(t)\vec{e}(t)^{\perp}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}(yf_{2}(y)W_{1}(t)^{\prime}\vec{e}(t)^{\perp}+\vec{f}_{2,1}(t,y))\right)+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\;=\frac{{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\tau^{2}}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\rm e}^{-\frac{i}{\varepsilon}t^{2}+\frac{2i}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}ty}\left(i\vec{e}(t)f_{2}(y)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}(W_{1}(t)^{\prime}\vec{e}(t)^{\perp}yf_{2}(y)+\vec{f}_{2,1}(t,y))\right)+O(\sqrt{\varepsilon})

in the Schwartz class.
It is thus important to understand the action of the operator defined on
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}([-\tau,\tau],\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}))
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{\varepsilon}(t):\varphi\mapsto

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\int_{-\tau}^{t}{\rm e}^{-\frac{i}{\varepsilon}s^{2}+\frac{2i}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}ys}\varphi(s,y)ds.

Let
\delta\in(0,\sqrt{\varepsilon})\subset(0,\tau)
.
If
t\in[-\tau,-\delta]
, an integration by parts shows that
\mathcal{T}^{\varepsilon}\varphi(t,\cdot)
is of order
\sqrt{\varepsilon}\delta^{-1}
in the Schwartz class.
This corresponds to the adiabatic situation, when the classical trajectories have not yet reached the crossing point. However, if
t\in[\delta,\tau]
, this operator is no longer negligible.
Indeed,
performing again integration by parts, we obtain that in the Schwartz space

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{\varepsilon}\varphi(t,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}{\rm e}^{-\frac{i}{\varepsilon}s^{2}+\frac{2i}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}ys}\varphi(s,y)ds+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\delta^{-1})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}{\rm e}^{-i\varepsilon\sigma^{2}+{2i}{y\sigma}}\varphi(0,y)d\sigma+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\delta^{-1}).

A detailed argument for analyzing these asymptotics is given in Section
6.2
for the general case.
We deduce

\displaystyle f_{1}^{\rm tr}(t,y)

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\tau^{2}}(\mathcal{T}^{\varepsilon}f_{2})\vec{e}(t)+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon\delta^{-1}).

We can now conclude the proof.
Setting
\vec{f}_{\ell,1}(t)=\vec{f}_{\ell,1}^{\rm cor}(t)+\vec{f}_{\ell,1}^{\rm tr}(t)
, and arguing similarly for the mode
\ell:=2
, yields to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}i\varepsilon\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1,{\rm app}}(t,x)=((\varepsilon D_{x}+x)\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{2}}+\varepsilon\Omega(x))\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1,{\rm app}}(t,x)+\varepsilon W_{1}(x)\psi^{\varepsilon}_{2,{\rm app}}(t,x)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2}\delta^{-1}+\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}\delta),\\
i\varepsilon\psi^{\varepsilon}_{2,{\rm app}}(t,x)=((\varepsilon D_{x}-x)\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{2}}+\varepsilon\Omega(x))\psi^{\varepsilon}_{2,{\rm app}}(t,x)+\varepsilon W_{1}(x)^{*}\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1,{\rm app}}(t,x)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2}\delta^{-1}+\varepsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}\delta).\end{array}\right.

in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{C}}^{2})
, which concludes the proof.
∎

1.7.
Detailed overview

The main results of this paper are Theorems
1.23
,
1.24
,
1.25
, and
1.26
.

We prove Theorems
1.23
,
1.24
and
1.25
in Chapters
2
and
3
. These proofs rely on Theorem
1.26
that is proved later.
Chapter
2
starts with Section
2.1
that recalls elementary facts about the Bargmann transform. Then, in Section
2.2
, we analyze the notion of frequency localization that we have first introduced in this text and that is crucial in the setting of frozen and thawed initial value representations. Indeed, these approximations rely on a class of operators that is studied in Section
2.3
. Endowed with these results, we are able to prove the approximations of Theorems
1.23
,
1.24
,
1.25
in Chapter
3
. We describe the general proof strategy in Section
3.1
and develop the proof of Theorem
1.23
in Section
3.2
. We explain in Section
3.3
how to pass from a thawed to a frozen approximation, and thus obtain Theorems
1.24
and
1.25
.

Chapters
4
and
5
are devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.26
. These two chapters are independent of the preceding ones, and one can start reading them, skipping Chapters
2
and
3
.
In Chapter
4
, we construct the different diagonalisations of the Hamiltonian
H^{\varepsilon}
that we are going to use. In the crossing region, we use a rough diagonalisation (see Section
4.2
), and outside this region we use super-adiabatic projectors as proposed in
[
5
,
51
,
65
]
(see Section
4.3
). These constructions rely on a symbolic calculus that we present in Section
4.1
. The analysis of the propagation of wave-packets in region of small gap is then performed in Chapter
5
. We carefully analyze the dependence of the approximation in term of the gap’s parameter. The propagation through the crossing region itself is performed in Chapter
6
, it is based on Duhamel formula and the use of Dyson series.

The Appendices are devoted to the proof of some technical points used in the proofs of
Chapters
4
and
5
.

Part I
Initial value representations

Chapter 2
Frequency localized families

In this chapter, we study frequency localized families. We shall use a more precise definition than the one of the Introduction. We use tha Gaussian wave packets
g^{\varepsilon}_{z}
introduced in (
1.10
),

g^{\varepsilon}_{z}={\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z}(g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}})
with the notation (
1.20
).

Definition 2.1

(Frequency localized functions of order
\beta
)
.

Let
\beta\geq 0
. Let

(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a family of functions of
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. The family
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is frequency localized of order
\beta

if the family is bounded in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and if there exist
R_{\beta},C_{\beta},\varepsilon_{\beta}>0
and
N_{\beta}>d
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\beta}]
,

(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d/2}\left|\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{z},\phi^{\varepsilon}\rangle\right|\leq C_{\beta}\,\varepsilon^{\beta}\,\langle z\rangle^{-N_{\beta}}\;\;\ \text{for all}\ z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\ \text{with}\ |z|>R_{\beta}.

One then says that
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is frequency localized of order
\beta

on the ball
B(0,R_{\beta})
.

With this terminology, the notion introduced in Definition
1.9
in the Introduction consists in frequency localized functions at order
0
.

In other words, being frequency localized of order
\beta
expresses in terms of the Bargmann transform

(2.1)

\mathcal{B}[f](z):=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{z},f\rangle,\;\;z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}

of the family. More precisely, if

(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
satisfies Definition
2.1
, its Bargmann transform has polynomial decay at infinity with control in
\varepsilon^{\beta}
outside a ball
B(0,R_{\beta})
.

In Section
2.1
below,
we recall some facts about the Bargmann transform, i.e. the map

z\mapsto\mathcal{B}[f](z).

Then, in Section
2.2
, we study some properties of frequency localized families and give some examples.
Indeed, the thawed/frozen approximations that we aim at studying are constructed thanks to the Bargmann transform. They belong to a class of
operators obtained by integrating the Bargmann transform against adapted families of the form

\left(z\mapsto\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}\right)\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d}_{z},L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})).

We then denote by
\mathcal{J}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}]
the operator acting on
\phi\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
according to

(2.2)

\mathcal{J}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}](\phi)(x)=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\mathcal{B}[\phi](z)\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}(x)dz=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{z},\phi\rangle\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}(x)dz,\;\;x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.

The thawed/frozen operators of equations (
1.32
), (
1.34
), (
1.47
) and (
1.48
) are of that form.
We study the properties of these operators in Section
2.3
.

2.1.
The Bargmann transform

Recall that the Bargman transform is the map
\mathcal{B}

\mathcal{B}:\;\;L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\ni f\mapsto\mathcal{B}[f]\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d}),

defined by (
2.1
)
This map is an isometry and one has

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|\mathcal{B}[f](z)|^{2}dz=\|f\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

Indeed, the Gaussian frame identity writes

(2.3)

f(x)=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{z},f\rangle g^{\varepsilon}_{z}(x)dz=\mathcal{J}[g^{\varepsilon}_{z}]f,

and equation (
2.3
) is equivalent to

f(x)=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\mathcal{B}[f](z)g^{\varepsilon}_{z}(x)dz,\;\;\forall f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}).

More generally, the Bargmann transform characterizes the
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
spaces according to the next result that we prove in Section
2.2.6
below.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists a constant
c_{k}
such that for all
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,

\|f\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}\leq c_{k}\|\langle z\rangle^{k}\mathcal{B}[f]\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})}.

For proving Lemma
2.2
and along the next sections of this chapter, we shall use properties of wave packets that we sum-up here.

Lemma 2.3
.

(1)

Let
f,g\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
z,z^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
, then

(2.4)

\langle{\rm WP}_{z}^{\varepsilon}(f),{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z^{\prime}}(g)\rangle={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}p^{\prime}\cdot(q-q^{\prime})}W[f,g]\left(\frac{z^{\prime}-z}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)

where the function
W[f,g]
is the Schwartz function on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
defined by

W[f,g](\zeta)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\overline{f}(x)g(x-q){\rm e}^{ip\cdot x}dx,\;\;\zeta=(q,p).

Moreover, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists a constant
C_{n}>0
such that

(2.5)

\forall\zeta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\;\;\langle\zeta\rangle^{n}\left|W[f,g](\zeta)\right|\leq C_{n}\|f\|_{\Sigma_{1}^{n}}\|g\|_{\Sigma_{1}^{n}}.

(2)

Let
z=(q,p)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
and
\alpha,\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
, then

(2.6)

g_{\varepsilon,z}^{\alpha,\gamma}:=x^{\alpha}(\varepsilon D_{x})^{\gamma}g^{\varepsilon}_{z}={\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z}\left((q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y)^{\alpha}(p+\sqrt{\varepsilon}D_{y})^{\gamma}g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}})\right).

Moreover, for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists
c_{k}>0
such that for all
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}

and for all
\alpha,\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
,

(2.7)

\|g_{\varepsilon,z}^{\alpha,\gamma}\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}\leq c_{k}\langle z\rangle^{k+|\alpha|+|\beta|}.

Proof.

(1) The formula for
\langle{\rm WP}_{z}^{\varepsilon}(f),{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z^{\prime}}(g)\rangle
comes from a simple computation. Then,
for
\alpha,\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
and
z=(q,p)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
, we observe

\displaystyle\left|q^{\gamma}p^{\alpha}W[f,g](z)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|q^{\gamma}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}D_{x}^{\alpha}(\overline{f}(x)g(x-q)){\rm e}^{ix\cdot p}dx\right|

\displaystyle\leq\langle q\rangle^{|\gamma|}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left|D_{x}^{\alpha}(\overline{f}(x)g(x-q))\right|dx

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\frac{|\gamma|}{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\langle x\rangle^{|\gamma|}\langle x-q\rangle^{|\gamma|}\left|D_{x}^{\alpha}(\overline{f}(x)g(x-q))\right|dx

where we have used Peetre inequality

(2.8)

\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}},\;\;\forall\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}},\;\;\frac{\langle t\rangle^{\ell}}{\langle t^{\prime}\rangle^{\ell}}\leq 2^{\frac{|\ell|}{2}}\langle t-t^{\prime}\rangle^{|\ell|}.

The conclusion then follows.

(2) Equation (
2.6
) comes from the relation

{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(a){\rm WP}_{z}^{\varepsilon}(\varphi)={\rm WP}_{z}^{\varepsilon}({\rm op}_{1}(a(z+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot))\varphi)

and (
2.7
) follows.
∎

We are now in position of proving Lemma
2.2
.

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\alpha,\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
such that
|\alpha|+|\gamma|=k
.
we consider the operator

T_{\alpha,\gamma}=\mathcal{B}\circ(x^{\alpha}(\varepsilon D_{x})^{\gamma})\circ\mathcal{B}^{-1}\circ\langle z\rangle^{-k}.

The kernel of this operator is the function

{\mathbb{R}}^{4d}\ni(X,Y)\mapsto k^{\varepsilon}(X,Y)=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{X},x^{\alpha}(\varepsilon D_{x})^{\gamma}g^{\varepsilon}_{Y}\rangle\langle Y\rangle^{-k}.

Therefore, with the notation of (
2.6
) and by (
2.4
),

\displaystyle\int_{Y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\sup_{X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|k^{\varepsilon}(X,Y)|dY

\displaystyle=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{Y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\sup_{X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\left|W[g^{i{\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}},g^{\alpha,\gamma}_{\varepsilon,Y}]\left(\frac{X-Y}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)\right|\langle Y\rangle^{-k}dY

\displaystyle=\int_{\zeta\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\sup_{X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\left|W[g^{i{\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}},g^{\alpha,\gamma}_{\varepsilon,Y}]\left(\zeta\right)\right|\langle X-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\zeta\rangle^{-k}d\zeta.

We deduce from (
2.5
) and (
2.7
) the existence of a constant
c_{k}>0
such that

\int_{Y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\sup_{X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|k^{\varepsilon}(X,Y)|dY\leq c_{k}.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\int_{X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\sup_{Y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|k^{\varepsilon}(X,Y)|dX

\displaystyle=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\sup_{Y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\left|W[g^{i{\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}},g^{\alpha,\gamma}_{\varepsilon,Y}]\left(\frac{X-Y}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)\right|\langle Y\rangle^{-k}dX\leq c_{k}.

Therefore, the Schur test yields the boundedness of
T_{\alpha,\gamma}
. One then deduces that for
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, one has

\displaystyle\|x^{\alpha}(\varepsilon D_{x})^{\gamma}f\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\|\mathcal{B}[x^{\alpha}(\varepsilon D_{x})^{\gamma}f]\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})}

\displaystyle=\left\|T^{\alpha,\gamma}\left(\langle z\rangle^{k}\mathcal{B}[f]\right)\right\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})}\leq c_{k}\|\langle z\rangle^{k}\mathcal{B}[f]\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})},

which concludes the proof.
∎

2.2.
Frequency localized families

We investigate here the properties of families that are frequency localized in the sense of Definition
2.1
.

2.2.1.
First properties of frequency localized functions

The first properties are straightforward.

Proposition 2.4
.

The set of frequency localized function is a subspace of
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. Moreover, we have the following properties:

(1)

If
(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{1})_{\varepsilon>0}
and
(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{2})_{\varepsilon>0}
are two frequency localized families at the scales
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
respectively, then for all
a,b\in{\mathbb{C}}
, the family
(a\phi^{\varepsilon}_{1}+b\phi^{\varepsilon}_{2})_{\varepsilon>0}
is frequency localized at the scale
{\rm min}(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
.

(2)

If
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is frequency localized at the scale
\beta\geq 0
, then
it is also frequency localized at the scale
\beta^{\prime}
for all
\beta^{\prime}\in[0,\beta]
.

Moreover, the notion of frequency localized functions is microlocal. Indeed, defining the

\varepsilon
-Fourier transform by

\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}f(\xi)=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\xi\cdot x}\phi^{\varepsilon}(x)dx=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\widehat{f}\left(\frac{\xi}{\varepsilon}\right),\;\;f\in\mathcal{S}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),

we have the following result.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a bounded family in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. Then,
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is frequency localized at the scale
\beta\geq 0
if and only if

(\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is frequency localized at the scale
\beta\geq 0
.

Proof.

This comes from the observation that for all
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
,

\left|\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{z},\phi^{\varepsilon}\rangle\right|=\left|\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{Jz},\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}\phi^{\varepsilon}\rangle\right|

where
J
is the matrix defined in (
1.13
). Thus it is equivalent to state the fact of being frequency localized for a family or for the family of its
\varepsilon
-Fourier transform.
∎

2.2.2.
Frequency localized families and Bargmann transform

The Gaussian frame identity (
2.3
) allows to decompose a function of
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
into a (continuous) sum of Gaussians. After discretization of the integral, this sum may be turned into a finite one, which opens the possibility of approximation’s strategies (see
[
47
]
where this observation is used for numerical purposes).
It is thus important to identify assumptions that allow to compactify the set of integration in
z
. The notion of frequency localized families plays this role according to the next result.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a frequency localized family at the scale
\beta\geq 0
. Let
R_{\beta},C_{\beta}
and
N_{\beta}
be the constants associated to
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
according to Definition
2.1
. Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
N_{\beta}>d+k
. Then, for all
\chi\in L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
supported in
[0,2]
and equal to
1
on
[0,1]
, there exists
C>0
such that for
R>R_{\beta}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,

\left\|\phi^{\varepsilon}-\phi^{\varepsilon}_{<,R}\right\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C\,C_{\beta}\,\varepsilon^{\beta}\left(\int_{|z|>R}\langle z\rangle^{-2(N_{\beta}-k)}{dz}\right)^{1/2},

where

(2.9)

\phi^{\varepsilon}_{R,<}:=\mathcal{J}\left[g^{\varepsilon}_{z}\chi\left(\frac{|z|}{R}\right)\right](\phi^{\varepsilon})=\mathcal{B}^{-1}\left(\mathbb{I}_{|z|<R}\mathcal{B}[\phi^{\varepsilon}](z)\right).

Remark 2.7
.

Lemma
2.6
can be interpreted in different manners.

(1)

If
\beta>0
, then
\phi^{\varepsilon}_{R,<}
approximates
\phi^{\varepsilon}
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
as
\varepsilon
goes to
0
in any space
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
with
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
N_{\beta}>k+d
, and uniformly with respect to
R>R_{\beta}
.

(2)

If
\beta\geq 0
(which includes
\beta=0
), then the same approximation holds by letting
R
go to
+\infty
, and it is uniform with respect to
\varepsilon
. In particular, when
\beta=0
we have

\limsup_{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\|\phi^{\varepsilon}-\phi^{\varepsilon}_{R,<}\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq CR^{-(N_{\beta}-k-d)}.

Proof.

We set

r^{\varepsilon}(x)=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{|z|\geq R}\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{z},\varphi^{\varepsilon}\rangle g^{\varepsilon}_{z}(x)dz

and consider
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
For
R>R_{\beta}
,
\alpha,\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
with
|\alpha|+|\gamma|=k
, we have

\displaystyle\|x^{\alpha}(\varepsilon D_{x})^{\gamma}r^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\;\leq(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-2d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\int_{|z|>R}\int_{|z^{\prime}|>R}\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{z},\varphi^{\varepsilon}\rangle\,\overline{\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{z^{\prime}},\varphi^{\varepsilon}\rangle}g_{\varepsilon,z}^{\alpha,\gamma}(x)\,\overline{g_{\varepsilon,z^{\prime}}^{\alpha,\gamma}(x)}\,dx\,dz\,dz^{\prime}

with the notation (
2.6
).
By (
2.4
) and the frequency localization of order
{}_{b}eta
of
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\|x^{\alpha}(\varepsilon D_{x})^{\gamma}r^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{\beta}^{2}\,\varepsilon^{2\beta}\,(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{|z|>R}\int_{|z^{\prime}|>R}\langle z\rangle^{-N_{\beta}}\langle z^{\prime}\rangle^{-N_{\beta}}\,W[g_{\varepsilon,z}^{\alpha,\gamma},g_{\varepsilon,z^{\prime}}^{\alpha,\gamma}]\left(\frac{z-z^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)dz\,dz^{\prime}.

Besides, by (
2.5
) and (
2.7
), there exists a constant
C^{\prime}_{n,k}
such that

\left|W[g_{\varepsilon,z}^{\alpha,\gamma},g_{\varepsilon,z^{\prime}}^{\alpha,\gamma}](\zeta)\right|\leq C^{\prime}_{n,k}\,\langle\zeta\rangle^{-n}\langle z\rangle^{k}\langle z^{\prime}\rangle^{k}.

We deduce the existence of
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\|x^{\alpha}(\varepsilon D_{x})^{\gamma}r^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq c\,C_{\beta}^{2}\,\,\varepsilon^{2\beta}\,\varepsilon^{-d}\int_{|z|>R}\int_{|z^{\prime}|>R}\langle z\rangle^{-N_{\beta}+k}\langle z^{\prime}\rangle^{-N_{\beta}+k}\left\langle\frac{z-z^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right\rangle^{-n}dzdz^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq c\,C_{\beta}^{2}\,\varepsilon^{2\beta}\,\int_{|z|>R}\int\langle z\rangle^{-N_{\beta}+k}\langle z+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\zeta\rangle^{-N_{\beta}+k}\langle\zeta\rangle^{-n}dzd\zeta.

Since
-N_{\beta}+k\leq 0
, Peetre inequality (
2.8
) gives

\langle z+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\zeta\rangle^{-N_{\beta}+k}\leq 2^{\frac{N_{\beta}-k}{2}}\langle\sqrt{\varepsilon}\zeta\rangle^{N_{\beta}-k}\langle z\rangle^{-N_{\beta}+k}\leq 2^{\frac{N_{\beta}-k}{2}}\langle\zeta\rangle^{N_{\beta}-k}\langle z\rangle^{-N_{\beta}+k},

by restricting ourselves to
\varepsilon\leq 1
.
Therefore, there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\displaystyle\|x^{\alpha}(\varepsilon D_{x})^{\gamma}r^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq c\,C_{\beta}^{2}\,\varepsilon^{2\beta}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\langle\zeta\rangle^{-n+N_{\beta}-k}d\zeta\right)\left(\int_{|z|>R}\langle z\rangle^{-2(N_{\beta}-k)}dz\right)

and we conclude the proof by choosing
n=N_{\beta}+k+2d+1
.
∎

2.2.3.
Examples

In this section, we analyze the examples given in the Introduction.

Lemma 2.8
.

(1)

Let
u\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
z_{0}=(q_{0},p_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
. Then, the family
({\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}(u))_{\varepsilon>0}
is frequency localized at the scale
\beta
for any
\beta\geq 0
.

(2)

Let
a\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and
S\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. Then, the family
({\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(x)}a)_{\varepsilon>0}
is frequency localized at the scale
\beta
for any
\beta\geq 0
.

Proof.

1- By (
2.4
), we have for
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}

\mathcal{B}[{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}(u)](z)=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\langle{\rm WP}_{z}^{\varepsilon}(g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}),{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}(u)\rangle=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}p_{0}\cdot(q-q_{0})}\,W[g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}},u]\left(\frac{z_{0}-z}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right).

Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the estimate (
2.5
) implies the existence of a constant
C_{N}^{\prime}
such that

|\mathcal{B}[{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}(u)](z)|\leq C_{N}^{\prime}\,\varepsilon^{-\frac{d}{2}}\,\left\langle\frac{z_{0}-z}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right\rangle^{-N}.

Choosing
|z|>\max(2|z_{0}|,1)
, we have
2|z_{0}-z|\geq 2(|z|-|z_{0}|)\geq|z|
and we deduce

\left\langle\frac{z_{0}-z}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right\rangle^{-N}=\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon+|z-z_{0}|^{2}}\right)^{\frac{N}{2}}\leq\left(\frac{4\varepsilon}{4\varepsilon+|z|^{2}}\right)^{\frac{N}{2}}\leq{(4\varepsilon)^{\frac{N}{2}}}\,{|z|^{-N}},

whence
the existence of a constant
c_{N}>0
such that for
|z|>\max(2|z_{0}|,1)
and
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

|\mathcal{B}[{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}(u)](z)|\leq c_{N}\,\varepsilon^{\frac{N-d}{2}}\,\langle z\rangle^{-N}.

2- One has for
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}[{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(x)}a](z)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-d/2}(\pi\varepsilon)^{-d/4}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}a(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y){\rm Exp}\left(-\frac{i}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}p\cdot y+\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(q+y\sqrt{\varepsilon})\right){\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}dy.

This term has a very specific structure involving the symbol
y\mapsto a(y)
, an exponentially decaying function
y\mapsto{\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}
and an oscillating phase

y\mapsto\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y):=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}p\cdot y+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}S(q+y\sqrt{\varepsilon}).

We are going to show that the terms defined for
j\in\{1,\cdots,d\}
by

A_{j}^{\varepsilon}:=q_{j}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}a(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y){\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}{\rm e}^{i\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y)}dy\;\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\;B_{j}^{\varepsilon}:=p_{j}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}a(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y){\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}{\rm e}^{i\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y)}dy,

have the same structure. Then, it will be enough to consider only one of these terms and to prove that they are controlled by a power of
\varepsilon
, this will imply an adequate control on
|\mathcal{B}[{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(x)}a](z)|
by a recursive argument.

Let us first transform
A_{j}^{\varepsilon}
and
B_{j}^{\varepsilon}
.
Indeed, we have

\displaystyle A_{j}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}(q_{j}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y_{j})a(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y){\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}{\rm e}^{i\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y)}dy-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}a(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y)\left(y_{j}{\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}\right){\rm e}^{i\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y)}dy.

The first integral of the right hand side has the same structure with the symbol
y\mapsto a(y)
and the second one with the exponentially decaying function
y\mapsto y_{j}{\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}
.
Besides, observing

p_{j}{\rm e}^{i\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y)}=-i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\partial_{y_{j}}({\rm e}^{i\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y)})-\partial_{y_{j}}S(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y){\rm e}^{i\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y)},

we obtain with an integration by parts

\displaystyle B_{j}^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\partial_{y_{j}}S(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y)a(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y){\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}{\rm e}^{i\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y)}dy

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;+i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\partial_{y_{j}}\left(a(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y){\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}\right){\rm e}^{i\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y)}dy.

Here again the right hand side has the same structure with different symbols and rapidly decaying term.

We now focus in proving that one typical term written for
\varphi
a polynomial function as

(2.10)

L^{\varepsilon}:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}a(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y)\varphi(y){\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}{\rm e}^{i\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y)}dy

is of order
\varepsilon^{N}
for all
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
The decay of
y\mapsto{\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}
allows to reduce the set of integration. Indeed, we have

\left|\int_{|y|>\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{4}}}a(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y){\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}{\rm e}^{i\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y)}\varphi(y)dy\right|\leq{\rm e}^{-\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{4}}\|a\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{4}}|\varphi(y)|dy.

Therefore, there exists a constant
c>0
such that

|L^{\varepsilon}|\leq c\left(\left|\int_{|y|\leq\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{4}}}a(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y){\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}{\rm e}^{i\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y)}\varphi(y)dy\right|+{\rm e}^{-\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}\right).

We now use the oscillations of the phase for treating the integral with domain
\{|y|\leq\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{4}}\}
. We observe that there exists
R_{0}>0
such that if
|z|>R_{0}
, then

z\notin\{|p-\nabla S(q)|\leq 1,\;{\rm dist}(q,{\rm supp}(a))\leq 1\}.

We
choose
|z|>R_{0}
and we have the following alternative:

\mbox{either}\;{\rm dist}(q,{\rm supp}(a))>1,\;\mbox{ or}\;\left({\rm dist}(q,{\rm supp}(a))\leq 1\;\mbox{ and}\;|p-\nabla S(q)|>1\right).

If

{\rm dist}(q,{\rm supp}(a))>1
, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that if
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
and
|y|\leq\varepsilon^{1/4}
, then
q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y\notin{\rm supp}(a)
. The integral thus is zero and we are reduced to the case where
{\rm dist}(q,{\rm supp}(a))\leq 1
and
|p-\nabla S(q)|>1
. One can find
\varepsilon_{1}>0
such that for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1}]
and
|y|\leq\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{4}}
,

\left|\nabla S(q+y\sqrt{\varepsilon})-p\right|>\frac{1}{2}.

We then consider the differential operator

L^{\varepsilon}=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\frac{\nabla S(q+y\sqrt{\varepsilon})-p}{|\nabla S(q+y\sqrt{\varepsilon})-p|^{2}}\cdot\nabla_{y}

and we write

\displaystyle\int_{|y|\leq\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{4}}}a(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y){\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}{\rm e}^{i\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y)}\varphi(y)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{|y|\leq\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{4}}}a(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y){\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}\varphi(y)(L^{\varepsilon})^{N}\left({\rm e}^{i\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y)}\right)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{|y|\leq\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{4}}}{(L^{\varepsilon})^{N}}^{*}\left(a(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y)|\nabla S(q+y\sqrt{\varepsilon})-p|^{-2N}{\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}\varphi(y)\right){\rm e}^{i\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y)}dy.

There exists a constant
C>0
independent of
z
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1}]
and
|y|\leq\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{4}}
,

\left|{(L^{\varepsilon})^{N}}^{*}\left(a(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y)|\nabla S(q+y\sqrt{\varepsilon})-p|^{-2N}{\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}\varphi(y)\right)\right|\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{N}{2}}{\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}.

We deduce

\displaystyle\left|\int_{|y|\leq\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{4}}}a(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}y){\rm e}^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{2}}{\rm e}^{i\Lambda^{\varepsilon}(y)}\varphi(y)dy\right|\leq C\varepsilon^{\frac{N}{2}}

and (
2.10
) writes

|L^{\varepsilon}|\leq c\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{N}{2}}+{\rm e}^{-\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}\right)

for some constant
c>0
.
This terminates the proof.
∎

2.2.4.
Characterization of frequency localized families

The characterization of frequency localized families can be done by using other families of wave packets than Gaussian ones and the cores
z
can be distributed in different manners.

Proposition 2.9
.

The family
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is frequency localized at the scale
\beta\geq 0
if and only if for all
\mathcal{C}^{1}
-diffeomorphism
\Phi
satisfying

\exists a,b>0,\;\;\forall z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\;\;a|z|\leq\Phi(z)\leq b|z|,

for all
\theta\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,
there exists
C_{\beta}
,
N_{\beta}
,
R_{\beta}
and
\varepsilon_{\beta}
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\beta}]
and for
|z|>R_{\beta}

(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-\frac{d}{2}}|\langle{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi(z)}(\theta),\phi^{\varepsilon}\rangle|\leq C_{\beta}\,\varepsilon^{\beta}\,\langle z\rangle^{-N_{\beta}}\,\max\left(1,\frac{1}{a}\right)^{N_{\beta}}\,\|\theta\|_{\Sigma^{2d+1+N_{\beta}}}.

Moreover, for all family
(\lambda^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
bounded in
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\beta}]
and
R>R_{\beta}
,

\left\|\mathcal{J}\left[{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\lambda^{\varepsilon}(z)}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi(z)}(\theta)\mathbb{I}_{|z|>R}\right](\phi^{\varepsilon})\right\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}\leq C\,C_{\beta}\,\varepsilon^{\beta}\left(\int_{|z|>R}\langle z\rangle^{-2(N_{\beta}-k)}dz\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

We only have to prove that if
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is frequency localized at the scale
\beta\geq 0
, then the property holds for some given profile
\theta
and diffeomorphism
\Phi
. Then, the equivalence will follow.
We consider the constants
C_{\beta}
,
N_{\beta}
,
R_{\beta}
and
\varepsilon_{\beta}
given by Definition
2.1
and we take
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{\beta}]
.
We observe

\displaystyle(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-\frac{d}{2}}\langle{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi(z)}(\theta),\phi^{\varepsilon}\rangle

\displaystyle=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-\frac{3d}{2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\langle{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi(z)}(\theta),g^{\varepsilon}_{z^{\prime}}\rangle\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{z^{\prime}},\phi^{\varepsilon}\rangle dz^{\prime}=I_{1}+I_{2}

with

I_{1}=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-\frac{3d}{2}}\int_{|z^{\prime}|>R_{\beta}}\langle{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi(z)}(\theta),g^{\varepsilon}_{z^{\prime}}\rangle\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{z^{\prime}},\phi^{\varepsilon}\rangle dz^{\prime}.

Let us study
I_{1}
. Using (
2.4
), (
2.5
) and that
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is frequency localized at the scale
\beta\geq 0
, we deduce the existence of
c_{\beta},N_{\beta}>0
such that we have

\displaystyle|I_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq c_{\beta}\,\varepsilon^{\beta}\,(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{|z^{\prime}|>R_{\beta}}\left|W[\theta,g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}]\left(\frac{z^{\prime}-\Phi(z)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)\right|\langle z^{\prime}\rangle^{-N_{\beta}}dz^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\beta}\,\|\theta\|_{\Sigma^{n}}\,\varepsilon^{\beta}\,(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left\langle\frac{z^{\prime}-\Phi(z)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right\rangle^{-n}\langle z^{\prime}\rangle^{-N_{\beta}}dz^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq c_{\beta}\,\|\theta\|_{\Sigma^{n}}\,\varepsilon^{\beta}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left\langle\zeta\right\rangle^{-n}\langle\Phi(z)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\zeta\rangle^{-N_{\beta}}d\zeta,

where the constant
c_{\beta}
may have changed between two successive lines.
We observe that Peetre’s inequality (
2.8
) yields, assuming
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,

\langle\Phi(z)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\zeta\rangle^{-N_{\beta}}\leq 2^{\frac{N_{\beta}}{2}}\langle\Phi(z)\rangle^{-N_{\beta}}\langle\sqrt{\varepsilon}\zeta\rangle^{N_{\beta}}\leq 2^{\frac{N_{\beta}}{2}}\langle\Phi(z)\rangle^{-N_{\beta}}\langle\zeta\rangle^{N_{\beta}},

whence by choosing
n=2d+1+N_{\beta}
,

\displaystyle|I_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq c_{\beta}\,\|\theta\|_{\Sigma^{2d+1+N_{\beta}}}\,\varepsilon^{\beta}\,\langle\Phi(z)\rangle^{-N_{\beta}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\left\langle\zeta\right\rangle^{-(2d+1)}d\zeta,

for some new constant
c_{\beta}>0
. We conclude by observing that

\langle\Phi(z)\rangle^{-N_{\beta}}\leq\max\left(1,\frac{1}{a}\right)^{N_{\beta}}\langle z\rangle^{-N_{\beta}},

whence, by modifying
c_{\beta}
,

|I_{1}|\leq c_{\beta}\,\|\theta\|_{\Sigma^{2d+1+N_{\beta}}}\,\varepsilon^{\beta}\,\max\left(1,\frac{1}{a}\right)^{N_{\beta}}\langle z\rangle^{-N_{\beta}}.

We now study
I_{2}
. Using (
2.4
), (
2.5
), we write for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}

|I_{2}|\leq\|\phi^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-\frac{3d}{2}}\int_{|z^{\prime}|\leq R_{\beta}}\left\langle\frac{z^{\prime}-\Phi(z)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right\rangle^{-n}dz^{\prime}.

We observe that if
|z|>2aR_{\beta}
, then for
|z^{\prime}|\leq R_{\beta}\leq\frac{1}{2a}|z|
, we have

|z^{\prime}-\Phi(z)|\geq|\Phi(z)|-|z^{\prime}|\geq\frac{1}{2a}|z|.

Therefore

\left\langle\frac{z^{\prime}-\Phi(z)}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right\rangle^{-n}=\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon+|z^{\prime}-\Phi(z)|^{2}}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}\leq{(2a)^{n}}{\varepsilon^{\frac{n}{2}}}|z|^{-n}.

Using that
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is a bounded family in
L^{2}
, we obtain that there exists a constant
c^{\prime}
such that for
|z|>2aR_{\beta}
and any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\displaystyle|I_{2}|

\displaystyle\leq c^{\prime}\varepsilon^{\frac{n-3d}{2}}|z|^{-n}.

The proof of the last property follows the line of the proof of Lemma
2.6
combined with adapted change of variables.
This terminates the proof.
∎

2.2.5.
Frequency localized families and semi-classical pseudodifferential calculus

Frequency localized families also enjoy properties with respect to pseudodifferential calculus.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a frequency localized family at the scale
\beta\geq 0
.

(1)

For all semi-classical symbol
a\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
, the family
\bigl(\widehat{a}\,\phi^{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon>0}
is frequency localized at the scale
\beta\geq 0
.

(2)

For all subquadratic Hamiltonian
h\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{R}})
, for all
t,t_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, the vector-valued family
\bigl(\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h}(t,t_{0})\phi^{\varepsilon}\bigr)_{\varepsilon>0}
is frequency localized at the scale
\beta\geq 0
.

Proof.

(1) We can assume without loss of generality that
a
is real-valued. We write

\mathcal{B}[\widehat{a}\phi^{\varepsilon}]=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d/2}\langle\widehat{a}g^{\varepsilon}_{z},\phi^{\varepsilon}\rangle.

Since
g^{\varepsilon}_{z}
is a wave packet, we have

\widehat{a}g^{\varepsilon}_{z}=\widehat{a}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z}(g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}})={\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z}(\mathfrak{g}^{\varepsilon}_{a}),\;\;\mathfrak{g}^{\varepsilon}_{a}={\rm op}_{1}(a(z+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot))g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}.

The function
\mathfrak{g}^{\varepsilon}_{a}
is of Schwartz class on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and its Schwartz semi-norms are uniformly bounded in
\varepsilon
because
a
is compactly supported. We deduce from Proposition
2.9
,

\left|\mathcal{B}[\widehat{a}\phi^{\varepsilon}]\right|\leq C_{\beta}\,\varepsilon^{\beta}\,\langle z\rangle^{-N_{\beta}}\|\mathfrak{g}^{\varepsilon}_{a}\|_{2d+1+N_{\beta}},

which concludes the proof.

(2) We write

\mathcal{B}\left[\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h}(t,t_{0})\phi^{\varepsilon}\right]=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d/2}\langle\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h}(t,t_{0})g^{\varepsilon}_{z},\phi^{\varepsilon}\rangle.

Since
g^{\varepsilon}_{z}
is a wave packet, we have

(2.11)

\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h}(t,t_{0})g^{\varepsilon}_{z}={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(-t,z)}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{-t,0}_{h}(z)}(g^{\Gamma(-t,z)}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,r^{\varepsilon}_{z}(t))

with the notations of the introduction. Besides, for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists a constant
C=C(n,t)
such that

\|r^{\varepsilon}_{z}(t)\|_{\Sigma^{n}}\leq C(n,t)
.
We deduce from Proposition
2.9
,

\left|\mathcal{B}\left[\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h}(t,t_{0})\phi^{\varepsilon}\right]\right|\leq C_{\beta}\,\varepsilon^{\beta}\,\langle z\rangle^{-N_{\beta}}\|g^{\Gamma(t,z)}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}r^{\varepsilon}_{z}(t)\|_{2d+1+N_{\beta}},

which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.11
.

(1)

The proof of Proposition
2.10
(1) extends to smooth functions
a
with polynomial growth

\exists N_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}},\;\;\forall\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d},\;\;\forall z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\;\;\left|\partial^{\gamma}a(z)\right|\leq\langle z\rangle^{N_{0}-|\gamma|}

provided the integer
N_{\beta}
associated with the frequency localisation at the scale
\beta\geq 0
of the family
(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{\varepsilon>0}
verifies
N_{\beta}>d+N_{0}
.

(2)

The proof of Proposition
2.10
(2) also extends to adiabatic smooth matrix-valued Hamiltonian
H
that are subquadratic according to Definition
1.1
.
However, it is not clear whether the same result holds for Hamiltonians with crossings, either they are smooth as in this article or conical as in the Appendix of
[
20
]
. Indeed, even though
one knows that
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})(g^{\varepsilon}_{z}\vec{V})
is asymptotic to a
wave packet, it is not clear that the remainder of the approximation has a wave-packet structure as in (
2.11
).

2.2.6.
Frequency localized families and
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
-regularity

The size of
N_{\beta}
in Definition
2.1
gives an information about the regularity of the family.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a frequency localized family at the scale
\beta\geq 0
, let
C_{\beta},N_{\beta},\varepsilon_{\beta}
are the constants associated by Definition
2.1
. Assume that
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
is such that
N_{\beta}>d+k
, then
(\phi^{\varepsilon})_{0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{\beta}}
is uniformly bounded in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
and there exists
c>0
independent of
\varepsilon

\|\phi^{\varepsilon}\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}\leq c(C_{\beta}+\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}).

Proof.

This Lemma is a simple consequence of
Lemma
2.2
.
Since
N_{\beta}>d+k
, we have for
|z|>R_{\beta}
,

\langle z\rangle^{k}|\mathcal{B}[\phi^{\varepsilon}](z)|\leq\varepsilon^{\beta}C_{\beta}\langle z\rangle^{-N_{\beta}+k}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d}).

Moreover,
z\mapsto\langle z\rangle^{2k}|\mathcal{B}[\phi^{\varepsilon}](z)|^{2}
is locally integrable and we can write

\displaystyle\|\langle z\rangle^{k}\mathcal{B}[\phi^{\varepsilon}](z)\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\langle R_{\beta}\rangle^{2k}\int_{|z|\leq R_{\beta}}|\mathcal{B}[\phi^{\varepsilon}](z)|^{2}dz+\varepsilon^{2\beta}C_{\beta}^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\langle z\rangle^{-2(N_{\beta}-k)}dz

\displaystyle\leq\langle R_{\beta}\rangle^{2k}\|\mathcal{B}[\phi^{\varepsilon}](z)\|^{2}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}+\varepsilon^{2\beta}C_{\beta}^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\langle z\rangle^{-2(N_{\beta}-k)}dz

\displaystyle\leq\langle R_{\beta}\rangle^{2k}\|\phi^{\varepsilon}\|^{2}_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\varepsilon^{2\beta}C_{\beta}^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\langle z\rangle^{-2(N_{\beta}-k)}dz,

whence the conclusion since the right hand side is bounded for
2(N_{\beta}-k)>2d
.
∎

2.3.
Operators built on Bargmann transform

We investigate here the properties of the operators defined in (
2.2
).
We shall investigate two cases :

(a)

The case where the family
(\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z})_{\varepsilon>0}
is only uniformly bounded in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, which is a light assumption, but with uniform bounds in
z
on adequate semi-norms or norms.

(b)

The case where the family
(\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z})_{\varepsilon>0}
is a wave packet (up to a phase), which is a stronger assumption on the family.

The thawed/frozen approximation operators belong to the type (b). We will consider operators of type (a) in the proofs of
Theorems
1.19
and
1.23
, when taking for the family
(\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z})_{\varepsilon>0}
a term of rest appearing in the expansion of the action of the propagator on a Gaussian wave packet.

In Section
2.3.1
, we analyze the action of these operators on
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
spaces. In Section
2.3.2
, we prove special properties of the operators corresponding to families of the type
(b) involving classical quantities linked with the propagation of Gaussian wave packets by a Schrödinger evolution.

2.3.1.
Action in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
of operators built on Bargmann transform

This section is devoted to the proof of the following result.

Theorem 2.13
.

Let
\varepsilon_{0}>0
.

(1)

Let
R>0
.
There exists
c_{0}>0
such that for all measurable
z
-dependent family
(\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z})_{\varepsilon>0}
, for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
, for all
\phi\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})

\|\mathcal{J}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}\mathbb{I}_{|z|<R}](\phi)\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}\leq(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\,c_{0}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}}\,R^{2d}\sup_{|z|\leq R}\|\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}.

(2)

Assume
\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}=\lambda^{\varepsilon}(z){\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi(z)}(\theta)
with
\theta\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,
(\lambda^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
a bounded family in
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}})
and
\Phi
a smooth diffeomorphism of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
such that

\exists c>0,\;\exists\ell\in{\mathbb{N}},\;\;\forall z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\;\;|J_{\Phi}(z)|+|J_{\Phi}(z)^{-1}|\leq c\langle z\rangle^{\ell}.

Then, there exists
c^{\prime}_{0}>0
such that for all
\phi\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
,

\|\mathcal{J}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}](\phi)\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}\leq c^{\prime}_{0}\,\|\lambda_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\phi\|_{L^{2}}\|\theta\|_{\Sigma^{k+\ell+2d+1}}.

These results also hold for the adjoint of
{\mathcal{J}}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}]
:

(2.12)

{\mathcal{J}}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}]^{*}:\phi\mapsto(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\langle\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z},\phi\rangle g^{\varepsilon}_{z}dz.

Corollary 2.14
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2.13
(2), the family of operators
{\mathcal{J}}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}]^{*}
given by in (
2.12
)
satisfies the same kind of estimates than the family
{\mathcal{J}}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}]
.

A straightforward consequence of Theorem
2.13
and of Lemme
2.6
is given in the next statement.

Corollary 2.15
.

Assume
(\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z})_{\varepsilon>0}
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
2.13
. Let
(\Phi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
be a frequency localized family at the scale
\beta\geq 0
and
C_{\beta}>0
,
N_{\beta}\in{\mathbb{N}}
be the constants associated with Definition
2.1
. Then, for all
k>0
such that
N_{\beta}>k+d
, there exists a constant
c_{k}
such that for all
R>0
,

\|\mathcal{J}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}](\phi^{\varepsilon}-\phi^{\varepsilon}_{R,<})\|_{\Sigma^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\leq c_{k}\,C_{\beta}\,\varepsilon^{\beta}R^{-(N_{\beta}-k-d)}.

where the family
(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{R,<})_{\varepsilon>0}
is introduced in (
2.9
).

Proof of Theorem
2.13
.

Let us first prove the
L^{2}
-estimate (
k=0
) in both cases.

(1) By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, for
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{J}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}\mathbb{I}_{|z|\leq R}](\phi^{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-2d}\|\phi^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\int_{|z|,|z^{\prime}|\leq R}\left|\int_{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}(x)\overline{\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z^{\prime}}}(x)dx\right|\,dz\,dz^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-2d}\|\phi^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\int_{|z|,|z^{\prime}|\leq R}\|\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}\|_{L^{2}}\|\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z^{\prime}}\|_{L^{2}}\,dz\,dz^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}R^{4d}(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-2d}\|\phi^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\left(\sup_{|z|\leq 2R}\|\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}\|_{L^{2}}\right)^{2}

where
c_{1}>0
is a universal constant.

(2) Let
(x,y)\mapsto k^{\varepsilon}(x,y)
be the integral kernel of the operator
\mathcal{J}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}]
.
Since the Bargmann transform is an isometry, it is equivalent to consider the operator
\mathcal{B}\circ\mathcal{J}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}]\circ\mathcal{B}^{-1}
, the kernel of which is the function

({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})^{2}\ni(X,Y)\mapsto k^{\varepsilon}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)
defined by

\displaystyle k^{\varepsilon}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)

\displaystyle=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\overline{g}^{\varepsilon}_{X}(x)g^{\varepsilon}_{Y}(y)k^{\varepsilon}(x,y)dxdy=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-2d}\int_{z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{z},g^{\varepsilon}_{Y}\rangle\langle g^{\varepsilon}_{X},\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}\rangle dz.

Therefore, by (
2.4
),
k^{\varepsilon}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)
satisfies

|k^{\varepsilon}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)|\leq(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-2d}\int_{z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\left|\lambda^{\varepsilon}(z)W[g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}},g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}]\left(\frac{Y-z}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)W[g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}},\theta]\left(\frac{\Phi(z)-X}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right)\right|dz.

We deduce

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|k^{\varepsilon}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)|dX

\displaystyle\leq M\|\lambda^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|W[g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}},g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}](z)|dz\right)\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|W[g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}},\theta](X)|dX\right),

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|k^{\varepsilon}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)|dY

\displaystyle\leq M\|\lambda^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|W[g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}},g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}](Y)|dY\right)\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|W[g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}},\theta](z)J_{\Phi}^{-1}(z)|dz\right),

with
M=\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\|\lambda^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}
, and, by equations (
2.5
), we deduce the existence of
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|k^{\varepsilon}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)|dX+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|k^{\varepsilon}_{\mathcal{B}}(X,Y)|dY

\displaystyle\leq CM\|\theta\|_{\Sigma^{2d+\ell+1}}.

We then conclude by Schur Lemma
and obtain

\|\mathcal{B}\circ\mathcal{J}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}]\circ\mathcal{B}^{-1}\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d}))}\leq CM\ \|\lambda^{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}}\,\|\theta\|_{\Sigma^{2d+\ell+1}},

and so it is for
\mathcal{J}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}]
.

(3) For concluding the proof when
k\not=0
, we use that for
\alpha,\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{d}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,

x^{\alpha}(\varepsilon\partial_{x})^{\gamma}\mathcal{J}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}]=\mathcal{J}[x^{\alpha}(\varepsilon\partial_{x})^{\gamma}\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}],

and the additional observation

x^{\alpha}(\varepsilon\partial_{x})^{\gamma}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z}(\theta)={\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}\left((q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}x)^{\alpha}(p+\sqrt{\varepsilon}D_{x})^{\gamma}\theta\right).

We then conclude by observing that, as in the estimate (
2.7
), we have for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\|(q+\sqrt{\varepsilon}x)^{\alpha}(p+\sqrt{\varepsilon}D_{x})^{\gamma}\theta\|_{\Sigma^{n}}\leq\langle z\rangle^{k}\|\theta\|_{\Sigma^{n+k}}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem
2.13
has consequences for the thawed/frozen approximation operators introduced in Chapter
1
.
We extend the notation
\mathcal{J}[\vec{\theta}^{\varepsilon}_{z}]
to vector valued functions
\vec{\theta}^{\varepsilon}_{z}
by considering the operator coordinate by coordinate.

Corollary 2.16
.

Assume the Hamiltonian
H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
satisfies Assumptions
1.6
and
1.6
. Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in I
.

(1)

The families of operators
\left(\mathcal{J}_{\ell,\vec{V},{\rm th/fr}}^{t,t_{0}}\right)_{\varepsilon>0}

defined in (
1.32
) and (
1.34
)
are bounded families in

\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m}))
.

(2)

Assume moreover that the compact
K
satisfies Assumptions
1.21
. Then, the family of operators
\left(\mathcal{J}_{1,2,\vec{V},{\rm th/fr}}^{t,t_{0}}\right)_{\varepsilon>0}
defined in (
1.47
) and (
1.48
) are bounded families in the space

\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m}))
.

Remark 2.17
.

If one assumes that
(t,z)\mapsto\partial_{t}f+\{v,f\}
is bounded from below and
\partial_{t}f
is bounded, then one can replace the compact
K
by
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
in the definition of
\mathcal{J}_{1,2,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}
and one obtains a bounded family in

\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m}))
.

Proof.

Let
\ell\in\{1,2\}
. Let us first discuss
\mathcal{J}_{\ell,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}
.
We write
\mathcal{J}^{t,t_{0}}_{\ell,\vec{V},{\rm th}}=\mathcal{J}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}]
with

\displaystyle\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{\varepsilon}(z){\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi_{\ell}^{t,t_{0}}(z)}(g^{\Gamma}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z))\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\lambda^{\varepsilon}(z)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)}\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z).

We observe that for all
t\in I
and
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
,

\|\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)\|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m}}=\|\vec{V}_{\ell}(t_{0},t_{0},z)\|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m}}=\|\pi_{\ell}(t_{0})\vec{V}\|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m}}\leq\|\vec{V}\|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m}}

which is independent of
z
.
Therefore, the family
(\lambda^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
.

Besides, by Proposition
A.4
, the flow map
(t,z)\mapsto\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)
satisfies the assumptions of (2) of Theorem
2.13
. Similarly, the map
(t,z)\mapsto\Gamma_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)
is bounded on
I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
. Therefore, for any
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists
c_{N,t_{0},T}>0
such that

\forall t\in I,\;\;\|x^{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\beta}g^{\Gamma}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},\cdot)\|_{\Sigma_{1}^{N}}\leq c_{N,t_{0},T}.

We then conclude by
(2) of Theorem
2.13
.
The proof for
\mathcal{J}_{\ell,{\rm fr}}^{t,t_{0}}
follows exactly the same lines.

The proof for
\mathcal{J}_{1,2,\vec{V},{\rm th/fr}}^{t,t_{0}}
requires additional observations.
We need to consider the transition coefficient map
(t,z)\mapsto\tau_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)
(see (
1.38
)) and the matrix-valued maps
z\mapsto\Gamma^{\flat}(t_{0},z)
(see (
1.43
)), which requires the analysis of the function parametrizing the crossing (see (
1.36
) and (
1.37
)),

(2.13)

z\mapsto\left(\alpha^{\flat}(t_{0},z),\beta^{\flat}(t_{0},z),\mu^{\flat}(t_{0},z)\right).

By the condition (
1.7
) of Assumption
1.6
, with
n_{0}=0
, the derivatives of
(t,z)\mapsto f(t,z)
are uniformly bounded in
z
. Moreover, if one takes
z
in a compact
K
that satisfies Assumptions
1.21
, one has the additional properties that
\partial_{t}f
and
\mu^{\flat}
are bounded below.
As a consequence
z\mapsto\alpha^{\flat}(t_{0},z)
,
z\mapsto\beta^{\flat}(t_{0},z)
and
z\mapsto\mu^{\flat}(t_{0},z)
are bounded functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
for all
t\in I
,
the map defined in (
2.13
) is smooth. One then argues as before by including the coefficient
\tau_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)
in the definition of
\lambda^{\varepsilon}

and the result follows from Theorem
2.13
(2).
∎

2.3.2.
Some properties of operators built on Bargmann transform via families with wave packet structure

In this section we analyze the properties of the operators
\mathcal{J}[\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}]
when
(\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z})_{\varepsilon>0}
is of the form

(2.14)

\theta^{\varepsilon}_{z}={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(z)}u(z){\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi(z)}(\theta(z,\cdot)),

with
\theta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
,
S\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{R}})
,
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}})
and
\Phi
a smooth diffeomorphism satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
2.13
. We are interested in the case where
S
and
\Phi
are linked in the same manner as when they are the flow map and the action associated with classical trajectories.
Therefore, we consider the following set of Assumptions.

Assumption 2.18
.

Let
S\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d}_{z},{\mathbb{R}})
,
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d}_{z},{\mathbb{C}})
and
\Phi
a smooth diffeomorphism. We assume the following properties:

(i)

There exists
c>0
and
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\forall z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\;\;|J_{\Phi}(z)|+|J_{\Phi}(z)^{-1}|\leq c\langle z\rangle^{\ell}.

(ii)

Setting
\Phi(z)=(\Phi_{q}(z),\Phi_{p}(z))
and

\partial_{z}\Phi=\begin{pmatrix}A(z)&B(z)\\
C(z)&D(z)\end{pmatrix},

we have

\nabla_{q}S(z)=-p+A(z)\Phi_{p}(z)\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\nabla_{p}S(z)=B(z)\Phi_{p}(z),\;\;z=(q,p).

(iii)

For all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the quantities
\sup_{z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\|\theta(z,\cdot)\|_{\Sigma^{k}}
,

\sup_{|\alpha|\leq k}\|\partial_{z}^{\alpha}S\|_{L^{\infty}}
and
\sup_{|\alpha|\leq k}\|\partial_{z}^{\alpha}u\|_{L^{\infty}}
,
are uniformly bounded in
z
.

The next technical lemma will be useful for proving our main results. It contains all the information needed to pass from the thawed approximation to the frozen one.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
\mathfrak{d}=\partial_{q}-i\partial_{p}
.
Let
\theta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
,
S\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{R}})
,
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}})
and
\Phi
a smooth diffeomorphism satisfying Assumptions
2.18
.
Then, the following equality between operators in
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})
holds for
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
:

\mathcal{J}\left[u\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}\left((\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}x-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}D_{x})\theta\right)\right]=-i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{J}\left[\mathfrak{d}u\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}(\theta)\right]-i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{J}\left[u\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}(\mathfrak{d}\theta)\right].

Note that with the notation of Lemma
2.19
and Assumptions
2.18
, we have

(2.15)

\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}(z)=C(z)-iD(z)\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}(z)=A(z)-iB(z).

Besides, condition (ii) of Assumption
2.18
implies that the equality of Lemma
2.19
holds formally. The conditions (i) and (iii) ensure the boundedness of the operators involved in the estimates.

Proof.

The integral kernel of the operator
\mathcal{J}\left[u\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}(\theta)\right]
is the
function

(x,y)\mapsto\int_{z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}k(z,x,y)dz

defined by

k(z,x,y)=u(z,x){\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(z)}\overline{g_{z}^{\varepsilon}(y)}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi(z)}(\theta(z,\cdot))(x),\;\;(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\;\;z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}.

We aim at calculating
\mathfrak{d}k
. We observe for
z=(q,p)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
,
y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}S(z)

\displaystyle=-p+(A(z)-iB(z))\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}(z),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}\left(\overline{g^{\varepsilon}_{z}(y)}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\varepsilon}[(\mathfrak{d}p+i\mathfrak{d}q)(y-q)+p\mathfrak{d}q]g^{\varepsilon}_{z}(y)=\frac{i}{\varepsilon}p\,g^{\varepsilon}_{z}(y),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}\left({\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi(z)}(\theta(z,\cdot))\right)

\displaystyle={\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi(z)}(\mathfrak{d}\theta(z,\cdot))+\frac{i}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi(z)}\left((\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}(z)x-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}(z)D_{x})\theta(z,\cdot)\right)

\displaystyle\;\;-\frac{i}{\varepsilon}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi(z)}\left((A(z)-iB(z))\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}(z)\theta(z,\cdot)\right).

We obtain

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}k(z,x,y)

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(z)}\Bigl(\mathfrak{d}u(z,x)\,\,\overline{g_{z}^{\varepsilon}(y)}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi(z)}(\theta(z,\cdot))(x)+u(z,x)\,\overline{g_{z}^{\varepsilon}(y)}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi(z)}(\mathfrak{d}\theta(z,\cdot))(x)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{i}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}u(z,x)\,\overline{g_{z}^{\varepsilon}(y)}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi(z)}\left((\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}(z)x-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}(z)D_{x})\theta(z,\cdot)\right)(x)\Bigr)

The result then follows from the integration in
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
.
∎

The case of Gaussian functions
\theta
is of particular interest. Indeed, if
\theta(z,\cdot)=g^{\Theta(z)}
with
\Theta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\mathfrak{S}^{+}(d))
, we have for
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
,

(2.16)

(\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}(z)x-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}(z)D_{x})g^{\Theta(z)}(x)=(\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}(z)-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}(z)\Theta(z))x\,g^{\Theta(z)}(x).

We set

M_{\Theta}(z):=\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}(z)-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}(z)\Theta(z).

By (
2.15
), we have the equality between matrix-valued functions

(2.17)

M_{\Theta}=(C-iD)-(A-iB)\Theta=(A-iB)\left[(A-iB)^{-1}(C-iD)-\Theta\right].

Note that this matrix
M_{\Theta}
is invertible because
(A+iB)^{-1}(C+iD)-\overline{\Theta}\in\mathfrak{S}^{+}(d)
(as the sum of two elements of
\mathfrak{S}^{+}(d)
).
These observations are in the core of the proof of the next result which is a corollary of Lemma
2.19
, when applied to Gaussian profiles.

Corollary 2.20
.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
\Theta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\mathfrak{S}^{+}(d))
such that
M_{\Theta}
is bounded together with its inverse,
let
S\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{R}})
,
u\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}})
and
\Phi
a smooth diffeomorphism satisfying Assumptions
2.18
. Then,
in
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
, we have

(2.18)

\mathcal{J}\left[u\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}\left(xg^{\Theta}\right)\right]=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon}).

Besides, for all
L\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}}^{d,d})
, in
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
, we have

(2.19)

\mathcal{J}\left[u\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}\left(Lx\cdot xg^{\Theta}\right)\right]=\frac{1}{i}\,\mathcal{J}\left[u\,{\rm Tr}\left(L\,M_{\Theta}^{-1}\,\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}\right)\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}\left(g^{\Theta}\right)\right]+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon).

with

LM_{\Theta}^{-1}\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}=L\left[(A-iB)^{-1}(C-iD)-\Theta\right]^{-1}.

Remark 2.21
.

(1)

This result has an interesting consequence concerning the pseudodifferential calculus. Indeed, for
a
real-valued and with bounded derivatives, in view of (
2.12
)

{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(a)={\mathcal{J}}({\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(a)g^{\varepsilon}_{z})={\mathcal{J}}[a(z)g^{\varepsilon}_{z}]+\sqrt{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{J}}\left({\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z}\left(\nabla a(z)\cdot\begin{pmatrix}x\\
D_{x}\end{pmatrix}g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}\right)\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon),

where we have used the properties of wave packets.
The equality
\nabla g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}=xg^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}
allows to conclude by Corollary
2.20

{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(a)={\mathcal{J}}[a(z)g^{\varepsilon}_{z}]+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)

in any space
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
. This was already proved in
[
63
]
.

(2)

More can be said about the
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
-order term on the right-hand side of (
2.18
). By revisiting the proof below, one sees that there exists a real-valued smooth function
z\mapsto c(z)
such that

\mathcal{J}\left[u\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}\left(xg^{\Theta}\right)\right]=-i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathcal{J}\left[u\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}\left(c(z)g^{\Theta}\right)\right]+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon).

Proof of Corollary
2.20
.

One uses (
2.16
) and the first relation of Lemma
2.19
that we apply to
\theta=g^{\Theta}
. It gives that in
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\left[u\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}\left(xg^{\Theta}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}\left[u\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}\left(M_{\Theta}^{-1}(\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}x-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}D_{x})g^{\Theta}\right)\right]=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon}),

whence (
2.18
).

Secondly, with
L\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}}^{d,d})
, we associate the matrix
L^{\prime}
such that

L=\,^{t}L^{\prime}M_{\Theta}
. We
have

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}x-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}D_{x})\cdot(L^{\prime}xg^{\Theta})

\displaystyle=\left((\,^{t}L^{\prime}(\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}\Theta))x\cdot x-{\rm Tr}(\,^{t}L^{\prime}\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q})\right)g^{\Theta}.

It remains to prove that in
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
, we have

(2.20)

\mathcal{J}\left[u\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}\left((\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}x-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}D_{x})\cdot(L^{\prime}xg^{\Theta})\right)\right]=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon).

We first apply
Lemma
2.19
to the function
\theta=L^{\prime}xg^{\Theta}
and we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\left[u\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}\left((\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}x-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}D_{x})\cdot(L^{\prime}xg^{\Theta})\right)\right]

\displaystyle\;\;=-i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathcal{J}\left[\mathfrak{d}u\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}\left(L^{\prime}xg^{\Theta}\right)+u{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}\left(L^{\prime}x\,\mathfrak{d}(g^{\Theta})\right)\right].

We use the relation (
2.18
) and obtain in
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
,

(2.21)

\mathcal{J}\left[u\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}\left((\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}x-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}D_{x})\cdot(L^{\prime}xg^{\Theta})\right)\right]=-i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathcal{J}\left[u{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}\left(L^{\prime}x\,\mathfrak{d}(g^{\Theta})\right)\right]+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon).

We calculate

\mathfrak{d}(g^{\Theta})=\frac{\mathfrak{d}c_{\Theta}}{c_{\Theta}}g^{\Theta}+(\mathfrak{d}\Theta x\cdot x)g^{\Theta},

with

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{d}\Theta x\cdot x)\,x\,g^{\Theta}

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{d}\Theta x\cdot x)M_{\Theta}^{-1}(\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}x-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}D_{x})g^{\Theta}

\displaystyle=M_{\Theta}^{-1}(\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}x-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}D_{x})\left((\mathfrak{d}\Theta x\cdot x)g^{\Theta}\right)-2M_{\Theta}^{-1}\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}\mathfrak{d}\Theta\,x\,g^{\Theta}.

Therefore, there exists matrices
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
such that, setting
\tilde{\theta}=(\mathfrak{d}\Theta x\cdot x)g^{\Theta}
, we have

L^{\prime}x\,\mathfrak{d}(g^{\Theta})=L_{1}xg^{\Theta}+L_{2}(\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}x-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}D_{x})\tilde{\theta}.

We deduce

\mathcal{J}\left[u\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi}\left((\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}x-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}D_{x})\cdot(L^{\prime}xg^{\Theta})\right)\right]=-i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathcal{J}[L_{1}xg^{\Theta}]-i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathcal{J}[L_{2}(\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}x-\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{q}D_{x})\tilde{\theta}]

and we obtain (
2.20
) by
Lemma
2.19
applied to the function
\tilde{\theta}
, and by the relation (
2.18
),
which concludes the proof
∎

2.3.3.
Operators built on Bargmann transform via classical quantities

We now apply the results of the preceding section to the diffeomorphism
\Phi
given by a flow map associated to a Hamiltonian
h
. We are going to derive the results induced by Lemma
2.19
and Corollary
2.20
for time dependent quantities after integration in time. We will use the resulting formula for the Hamiltonians
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
associated with the matrix-valued Hamiltonian
H^{\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 2.22
.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
h
be a subquadratic Hamiltonian on
I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
,
I=[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
. We consider

(1)

the classical quantities associated to
h
as in Section
1.3
on the interval
I
:

z\mapsto S(t,z),\Phi^{t,t_{0}}(z),F(t,t_{0},z),

(2)

a smooth function defined on
I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
, bounded and with bounded derivatives,

(t,z)\mapsto u(t,z)
,

(3)

a smooth map from
I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
into
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,
(t,z)\mapsto\theta(t,z)
,

(4)

a smooth function from
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
into
I
,

z\mapsto t^{\flat}(z)
.

Then, for all
\chi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{0}(I)
, we have the following equality between operators in
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
,

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\chi(t)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\left[\mathbb{I}_{t>t^{\flat}(z)}u(t)\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(t)}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}}\left((\mathfrak{d}\Phi_{p}^{t}x-\mathfrak{d}\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{q}D_{x})\theta(t)\right)\right]dt

\displaystyle=i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\mathcal{J}\left[\mathbb{I}_{t>t^{\flat}(z)}\mathfrak{d}u(t)\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(t)}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}}(\theta(t))\right]dt

\displaystyle+i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\chi(t)\mathcal{J}\left[\mathbb{I}_{t>t^{\flat}(z)}u(t)\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(t)}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}}(\mathfrak{d}\theta(t))\right]dt

\displaystyle-i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathcal{J}\left[\chi(t^{\flat})\,\mathfrak{d}t^{\flat}\,u(t^{\flat})\,\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(t^{\flat})}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t^{\flat},t_{0}}}(\theta(t^{\flat}))\right].

Note that the result of this lemma is an equality. Thus, we have not emphasized assumptions that make these operators bounded. One could for example assume global boundedness of all the quantities involve and of their derivatives, or, what would be enough, that
\theta
is compactly supported in
z
.

We also emphasize that the functions denoted by
(\chi\circ t^{\flat})\,u(t^{\flat})\,\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(t^{\flat})}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t^{\flat}}}(\theta(t^{\flat}))
is the map

z\mapsto\chi\left(t^{\flat}(z)\right)\,u\left(t^{\flat}(z),z\right)\,\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S\left(t^{\flat}(z),z\right)}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t^{\flat}(z),t_{0}}(z)}\left(\theta\left(t^{\flat}(t),z\right)\right).

Note also that, by construction, the flow map
\Phi^{t,t_{0}}
and the action
S
satisfy Assumptions
2.18
(see
[
15
,
60
,
48
]
).

Proof.

The proof follows the lines of the one of Lemma
2.19
, using the relation

(2.22)

\mathfrak{d}\left(\mathbb{I}_{t>t^{\flat}(z)}\right)=\mathfrak{d}t^{\flat}(z)\delta(t-t^{\flat}(z))

that produces an additional term.
∎

As a Corollary, for Gaussian profiles, we have the following Corollary.

Corollary 2.23
.

With the same assumptions as in Lemma
2.22
, we additionally assume
\theta(t)=g^{\Theta(t)}
, with
\Theta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\mathcal{S}^{+}(d))
. Then, for all
L\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}}^{d,d})
, we have the following equality in
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
,

\displaystyle\int\chi(t)\mathcal{J}\left[\mathbb{I}_{t>t^{\flat}(z)}u\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(t,t_{0})}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}}\left(Lx\cdot xg^{\Theta(t,t_{0})}\right)\right]dt

\displaystyle\;\;=\frac{1}{i}\,\int\chi(t)\mathcal{J}\left[\mathbb{I}_{t>t^{\flat}(z)}\tilde{u}(t,t_{0})\,{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(t,t_{0})}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}}\left(g^{\Theta(t,t_{0})}\right)\right]dt+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)

with

\widetilde{u}(t,t_{0})={\rm Tr}\left(L\left[(A(t,t_{0})-iB(t,t_{0}))^{-1}(C(t,t_{0})-iD(t,t_{0}))-\Theta(t,t_{0})\right]^{-1}\right)
.

Proof.

The proof follows the lines of the one of Corollary
2.20
, using the relation
(
2.22
).
∎

Chapter 3
Convergence of the thawed and the frozen Gaussian approximations

Our aim in this chapter is to prove the initial value representations of Theorems
1.19
,
1.23
,
1.24
and
1.25
.
In Section
3.1
, we explain the strategy in the context of Theorem
1.19
, and prove the thawed approximation at order
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. Then, in Section
3.2
, we prove the thawed Gaussian approximation with transfer’s term of Theorem
1.23
. Finally Section
3.3
is devoted to the passage from thawed approximations to frozen ones, which allow to terminate the proof of Theorem
1.19
, and to obtain Theorems
1.24
and
1.25
.

3.1.
Strategy of the proofs

Our aim in this section is to prove the initial value representation of Theorems
1.19
for the thawed Gaussian approximation. It is also the opportunity to explain the overall strategy that is also used for proving Theorems
1.23
,
1.24
and
1.25
.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Let
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\widehat{\vec{V}}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
be as in Assumption
1.11
with
(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{\varepsilon>0}
a frequency localized family of order
\beta\geq 0
with
N_{\beta}>d+k
. By Lemma
2.12
, the latter condition which implies the boundedness of the family
(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{\varepsilon>0}
in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
.
Without loss of generality, we assume

\vec{V}(z)=\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z)\vec{V}(z),\;\;\forall z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},

for some
\ell\in\{1,2\}
that is now fixed.

We start with the Gaussian frame equality (
2.3
)

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}{\langle}g^{\varepsilon}_{z},\widehat{\vec{V}}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}{\rangle}g^{\varepsilon}_{z}\,dz.

Writing

{\langle}g^{\varepsilon}_{z},\widehat{\vec{V}}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}{\rangle}={\langle}\widehat{\vec{\overline{V}}}g^{\varepsilon}_{z},\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}{\rangle}
and formula (
2.12
), we have in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
,

\displaystyle\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}[\widehat{\vec{\overline{V}}}g^{\varepsilon}_{z}]^{*}(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}).

By Remark
2.21
(1), we obtain in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
,

\displaystyle\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}[\vec{\overline{V}}(z)g^{\varepsilon}_{z}]^{*}(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}})

In view of the formula (
2.12
), we also have

\displaystyle\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}[\vec{V}(z)g^{\varepsilon}_{z}](\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}).

Corollary
2.15
yields that, in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
, we have

\displaystyle\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}[\vec{V}(z)g^{\varepsilon}_{z}]((\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{R,<})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{\beta}C_{\beta}R^{-n_{\beta}})

with the notations of Corollary
2.15
and setting
n_{\beta}=N_{\beta}-k-d>0
.

Now that the data has been written in a convenient form, we apply the propagator
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})
and we take advantage of its boundedness in
\mathcal{L}(\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})
to write

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}

\displaystyle=(2\pi\varepsilon)^{-d}\int_{|z|\leq R}{\langle}g^{\varepsilon}_{z},\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}{\rangle}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\vec{V}(z)g^{\varepsilon}_{z}\right)dz+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{\beta}\,C_{\beta}\,R^{-n_{\beta}})
