\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}\left[\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0}){\vec{V}}(z)g^{\varepsilon}_{z}\right]((\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{R,<})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{\beta}\,C_{\beta}\,R^{-n_{\beta}}).

We then use the
description of the propagation of wave packets by
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})
, as stated in Theorem
1.26
. We choose
\varsigma\in(0,1)
,
\delta=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}-\varsigma}
,
M
and
N
such that

(3.1)

\left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{N+1}\delta^{-2\kappa_{0}-k}+\delta^{M}\leq\varepsilon^{N^{\prime}}

where
N^{\prime}\in N
with
N^{\prime}\geq d+1
.
Then, in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, we have

\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0}){\vec{V}}(z)g^{\varepsilon}_{z}=\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N^{\prime}}).

Therefore, by (1) of Theorem
2.13
in
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m}))
,

(3.2)

\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\mathcal{J}\left[\mathbb{I}_{|z|\leq R}\,\,\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)\right]+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N-d}\,R^{d}\,\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{\beta}\,C_{\beta}\,R^{-n_{\beta}})

For simplifying notation, we write
N^{\prime}=N
.

Using that
\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)
is a linear combination of wave packets and considering the explicit formula of Theorem
1.26
, Point (2) of Theorem
2.13
implies that in
\mathcal{L}(\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m}))
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}_{\ell,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}\left((\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{R,<}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N-d}\,R^{d}\,\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{\beta}\,C_{\beta}\,R^{-n_{\beta}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}_{\ell,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N-d}\,R^{d}\,\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{\beta}\,C_{\beta}\,R^{-n_{\beta}})

where we have used again Corollary
2.15
.
If
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
, we perform
an appropriate choice of
R
and
N
: we choose
R=\varepsilon^{-\gamma}
with
\gamma\geq\frac{1}{n_{\beta}}(\frac{1}{2}-\beta)
, and
N\geq\frac{1}{2}+d(1+\gamma)
.

At this stage of the description, the thawed Gaussian approximation of Theorem (
1.19
) is proved. For obtaining the frozen one, we shall argue as in the scalar case considered in
[
60
]
(Lemma 3.2 and Lemma 3.4). We will detail this argument later in Section
3.3
below.

The proofs of the order
\varepsilon
approximations of Theorems
1.23
,
1.24
and
1.25
start with the same lines. However, one includes in the approximation the two first terms of the asymptotic expansion of
\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)
: the one of order
\varepsilon^{0}
and the one of order
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}
. The terms of order
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
are twofold:

(i)

The one along the same mode as the initial data, here denoted by
\ell
. This term will be proved to be of lower order because its structure allows to use the first part of Corollary
2.20
.

(ii)

The one generated by the crossing along the other mode. This one is not negligible and involves the transfer’s term.

At that stage of the proofs, one will be left with the thawed approximation.

The derivation of the frozen approximation from the thawed one involves the second part of Corollary
2.20
. However, complications are induced in the treatment of the transfer’s term described in (ii) above because of the singularity in time that it contains. This difficulty is overcome by averaging in time and using Corollary
2.23
.
We implement this strategy in the next sections.

3.2.
Thawed Gaussian approximations with transfer terms

We prove here the higher order approximation of Theorem
1.23
for initial data
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\widehat{\vec{V}}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
with
(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{\varepsilon>0}
frequency localized at the scale
\beta\geq 0
in a compact set
K
. As in the preceding section, we assume
\vec{V}=\pi_{\ell}(t_{0})\vec{V}
and, without loss of generality, we suppose
\ell=1
.

We start as in the preceding section and transform equation (
3.2
) by taking the terms of order
\varepsilon^{0}
and
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}
in the expansion of
\psi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}
(see Theorem
1.26
).
We obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\left[\psi^{\varepsilon,1}_{1}(t)+\psi^{\varepsilon,1}_{2}(t)\right]((\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{R,<})+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}\,C_{\beta}\,R^{-n_{\beta}}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N-d}R^{d}\|\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon\|\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}).

The rest in
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N-d}R^{d}\|\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}})
comes from the remainder of the approximation of
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})g^{\varepsilon}_{z}
(case (1) of Theorem
2.13
), while the term
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon\|\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}})
is generated by the terms of order
\varepsilon^{j}
for
j\geq 1
of the approximation, these terms having a wave packet structure (case (2) of Theorem
2.13
).

We write for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, taking
N=1
in Theorem
1.26
,

\psi^{\varepsilon,N=1}_{\ell}(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{1}\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}\psi^{\varepsilon,N=1}_{\ell,j}(t).

We make the following observations:

(i)

Because the assumptions on
K
induce that there is only one passage through the crossing, and because
\vec{V}=\pi_{1}(t_{0})\vec{V}
, Theorem
1.26
implies
that
\psi^{\varepsilon,N=1}_{2,0}(t)=0
and
\psi^{\varepsilon,N=1}_{2,1}(t)
only depends on the transfer profile
f^{\varepsilon}_{1\rightarrow 2}
.

(ii)

For the mode

\ell=1
, the term of order
\sqrt{\varepsilon}

involves

\psi^{\varepsilon,N=1}_{\ell=1,j=1}(t)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{\ell}(t)}\left({\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})\,{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]\,\vec{B}_{\ell,j}(t)g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}\right).

We first prove that the operator
\mathcal{J}\left[\psi^{\varepsilon,1}_{1,1}(t)\right]
has a contribution of lower order, i.e. of order
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
.
In that purpose,
we analyze the form
\psi^{\varepsilon,1}_{1,1}(t)
and we show that we can use Corollary
2.20
. These ideas are inspired from
[
61
]
[section 3] and the reader can also refer to the book
[
15
]
.
We consider the term

{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]\,\vec{B}_{\ell,1}(t)g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}.

The idea is to analyze the effect of each of the operation performed on the Gaussian
g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}
.
The operator
\vec{B}_{\ell,1}
is given by (
1.52
). It is a differential operators which is a linear combination of terms of the form
x^{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\beta}
with
|\alpha|+|\beta|=3
, and of operators of multiplication by
x_{j}
or of derivation
\partial_{x_{j}}
,
1\leq j\leq d
. When applied to
g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}
, we obtain a linear combination of terms of the form
x_{j}g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}(x)
for
1\leq j\leq d
, or
x^{\alpha}g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}(x)
for
|\alpha|=3
. One then applies the metaplectic operator
{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]
to this function. By (
1.19
), if
P
is a polynomial function,

{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})](Pg^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}})(x)=P(A_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})x){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}(x)

where
A_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})
is the upper block-diagonal coefficient of
F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})
(see (
1.17
)).
We deduce that

{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})\,{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]\,\vec{B}_{\ell,1}(t)g^{i\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}}(x)=\vec{a}(t)xg^{\Gamma_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})}(x)

for some smooth and bounded vector-valued map
(t,z)\mapsto\vec{a}(t,z)
.
Therefore, we can apply Corollary
2.20
. It yields

\mathcal{J}\left[\psi^{\varepsilon,1}_{1,1}(t)\right]((\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{R,<})=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}).

We can now conclude the proof. Indeed, we
have now only two terms in the expansion and we are left with

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}\left[\psi^{\varepsilon,1}_{1,0}(t)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\psi^{\varepsilon,1}_{2,1}(t)\right]((\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{R,<})+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}\,C_{\beta}\,R^{-n_{\beta}}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N-d}R^{d}\|\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}\left[\psi^{\varepsilon,1}_{1,0}(t)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\psi^{\varepsilon,1}_{2,1}(t)\right](\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0})+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}\,C_{\beta}\,R^{-n_{\beta}}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N-d}R^{d}\|\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}).

We can now identify the terms, using in particular the formula for the transfer terms given in (
1.53
). Since we have chosen
\delta=\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}-\varsigma}
at the beginning of the proof (see around (
3.1
)), we obtain

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}_{1,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{J}_{1,2,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{1-\varsigma}\|\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}})+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}\,C_{\beta}\,R^{-n_{\beta}}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N-d}R^{d}\|\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}).

If
\beta<1
, we choose
R=\varepsilon^{-\gamma}
,
N=1+d(\gamma+1)
with

\gamma\geq\frac{1}{n_{\beta}}(1-\beta).

This gives
Theorem
1.23
.

Remark 3.1
.

More generally, if we had started with
\vec{V}=\pi_{1}(t_{0})\vec{V}+\pi_{2}(t_{0})\vec{V}
, we would have obtained

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}_{1,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)+\mathcal{J}_{2,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{J}_{1,2,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{1-\varsigma}\|\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}})+\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon^{\beta}\,C_{\beta}\,R^{-n_{\beta}}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N-d}R^{d}\|\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}).

3.3.
Frozen Gaussian approximations with transfer terms

It remains to pass from the thawed to the frozen approximation. As we have already mentioned, we follow the arguments developed in Lemma 3.2 and 3.4 of
[
60
]
that we detail explicitly, in the same spirit than in
[
24
]
. It is based on an evolution argument which crucially uses Corollary
2.20
. We now explain that step.

End of the proof of Theorem
1.19
.

We start from the approximation given by the first part of Theorem
1.19
: in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, we have

\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x)=\mathcal{J}_{1,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon}(C_{\beta}+\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}))

and our aim is to prove that in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})

\mathcal{J}_{1,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)=\mathcal{J}_{1,\vec{V},{\rm fr}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon(C_{\beta}+\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}))).

Of course, a remainder of size
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon})
would be enough for proving Theorem
1.19
; however, it will be usefull to have a reaminder of size
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
in order to prove Theorems
1.24
and
1.25
.

We set for
s\in[0,1]

\Theta(s,z)=(1-s)\Gamma_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)+is\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}

where
\Gamma_{\ell}
is given by (
1.22
).
We consider the partially normalised Gaussian function

\widetilde{g}(t,s)=(\pi)^{-d/4}{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{2}\Theta(s,z)x\cdot x},\;x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

and we set

\widetilde{g}^{\,\Theta(s),\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)}(x)={\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)}\left(\widetilde{g}(t,s)\right),\\

The aim
is to construct a map

s\mapsto a(s,z)
such that for all
s\in[0,1]
in
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})
,

\frac{d}{ds}\mathcal{J}\left[a(s,z)\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)\widetilde{g}^{\,\Theta(s),\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)}\right]=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon).

Choosing
a(0,z)=1
,
we have

\mathcal{J}^{t,t_{0}}_{\ell,\vec{V},{\rm th}}=\mathcal{J}\left[a(0,z)\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)\widetilde{g}^{\,\Theta(0),\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)}\right],

and we will obtain that
for any
f\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, we have in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{t,t_{0}}_{\ell,\vec{V},{\rm th}}(f)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}\left[a(1,z)\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)\widetilde{g}^{\,\Theta(1),\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)}\right](f)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}^{t,t_{0}}_{\ell,\vec{V},{\rm fr}}(f)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)

provided
a(1,z)=a_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)
as defined in (
1.30
).

For constructing the map
s\mapsto a(s,z)
, we compute

\displaystyle\frac{d}{ds}\mathcal{J}\left[a(s,z)\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)\widetilde{g}^{\,\Theta(s),\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{J}\left[\partial_{s}a(s,z)\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)\widetilde{g}^{\,\Theta(s),\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{i}{2}\mathcal{J}\left[a(s,z)\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z){\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)}\left(\partial_{s}\Theta(s)x\cdot x\widetilde{g}^{\,\Theta(s),\varepsilon}\right)\right].

We use equation (
2.19
) of Corollary
2.20
to transform the second term of the right-hand side and obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}\left[a(s,z)\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z){\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)}(\partial_{s}\Theta(s)x\cdot xg^{\,\Theta(s)})\right]

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{1}{i}\mathcal{J}\left[a(s,z)\vec{V}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z){\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)}({\rm Tr}(\Theta_{1}(s))g^{\,\Theta(s)})\right]+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)

in
\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})
and
with

\Theta_{1}(s)=\partial_{s}\Theta(s)\left[(A_{\ell}-iB_{\ell})^{-1}(C_{\ell}-iD_{\ell})-\Theta\right]^{-1}

where
M_{\ell}(s,z)
is associated to
\Theta(s,z)
according to (
2.17
). In particular, we have

\partial_{s}M(s)=-(A_{\ell}-iB_{\ell})\partial_{s}\Theta(s).

We deduce

\Theta_{1}(s)=-(A_{\ell}-iB_{\ell})^{-1}\partial_{s}M(s)M(s)^{-1}(A_{\ell}-iB_{\ell})

and

{\rm Tr}(\Theta_{1}(s))=-{\rm Tr}(\partial_{s}M(s)M(s)^{-1})=-{\rm det}M(s)^{-1}\,\partial_{s}\left({\rm det}M(s)\right).

Therefore, the condition

\partial_{s}a(s,z)-\frac{1}{2}{\rm Tr}(\partial_{s}M(s,z)M(s)^{-1})a(s,z)=0

that we have to fulfilled, is realized by

a(s,z)=\frac{{\rm det}M(s)}{{\rm det}M(0)}a(0,z)=a_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z).

∎

Proof of Theorem
1.24
.

We now start from the result of Theorem
1.23
, that is equation (
3.3
). In view of what has been done in the end of the proof of Theorem
1.19
, we only have to prove

\mathcal{J}_{1,2,\vec{V},{\rm th}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)=\mathcal{J}_{1,2,\vec{V},{\rm fr}}^{t,t_{0}}\left(\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\sqrt{\varepsilon}(C_{\beta}+\|\phi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}\right).

As noticed in the introduction, when
t<t_{1,{\rm min}}(K)
, then
\tau_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)=0
for all
z\in K
and when
t\in[t_{1,{\rm max}}(K),t_{2,{\rm min}}(K))
,
z\mapsto\tau_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)
is smooth. Therefore, one can use the perturbative argument allowing to froze the covariances of the Gaussian terms as in the proof of Theorem
1.19
and one obtains the formula (
1.46
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.25
.

We now have to deal with
the discontinuity of the transfer coefficient
\tau_{1,2}(t,t_{0},z)
, we use Lemma
2.22
.
∎

Part II
Wave-packet propagation through smooth crossings

Chapter 4
Symbolic calculus and diagonalization of Hamiltonians with smooth crossings

In this section, we revisit the diagonalization of Hamiltonians in the case of the smooth crossings in which we are interested. We
settle the algebraic setting that we will use in Section
5
for the propagation of wave packets.

We will use the
Moyal product
about which we recall some facts:
if
A^{\varepsilon},B^{\varepsilon}
are semi-classical series, their Moyal product is the formal series

\displaystyle C^{\varepsilon}:=A^{\varepsilon}\circledast B^{\varepsilon}\;\;\mbox{where}\;\;\displaystyle{C^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}C_{j}}

(4.1)

\displaystyle C_{j}(x,\xi)=\frac{1}{2^{j}}\sum_{|\alpha+\beta|=j}\frac{(-1)^{|\beta|}}{\alpha!\beta!}(D^{\beta}_{x}\partial^{\alpha}_{\xi}A).(D^{\alpha}_{x}\partial^{\beta}_{\xi}B)(x,\xi),\;\;j\in{\mathbb{N}}.

We also introduce the
Moyal bracket

(4.2)

\{A^{\varepsilon},B^{\varepsilon}\}_{\circledast}:=A^{\varepsilon}\circledast B^{\varepsilon}-B^{\varepsilon}\circledast A^{\varepsilon}.

Let us now consider a smooth matrix-valued symbol
H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
, where the principal symbol
H_{0}=h_{1}\pi_{1}+h_{2}\pi_{2}
has two smooth eigenvalues
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
with smooth eigenprojectors
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
. We allow for a non-empty crossing set
\Upsilon
as in Definition
1.3
.
By standard symbolic calculus with smooth symbols, we have for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
the relations

(4.3)

\pi_{\ell}\circledast(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{\varepsilon})=(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-h_{\ell})\circledast\pi_{\ell}=O(\varepsilon).

We are going to see two manners to replace the projector
\pi_{\ell}
and the Hamiltonian
h_{\ell}
by asymptotic series so that the relation above holds at a better order.

We call “rough” the first diagonalization process that we propose. It will hold everywhere, including
\Upsilon
and is comparable to the reduction performed in
[
4
]
for avoided crossings. It is the subject of Section
4.2
.

The second one, more sophisticated, will require to work in a domain that does not meet
\Upsilon
. Based on the use of superadiabatic projectors, as developed in
[
5
,
51
,
62
,
65
]
. This strategy is implemented in Section
4.3
. The new element comparatively to the references that we have mentioned, is that we keep a careful memory of the dependence of the constructed elements with respect to the distance of their support from
\Upsilon
(which will be monitored by the size of the gap between the eigenfunctions of the Hamiltonian). For this reason, we will use a pseudodifferential setting that we precise in the next Section
4.1
.

4.1.
The pseudodifferential setting

We consider formal semi-classical series

A^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}A_{j}

where all the functions
A_{j}
are smooth (matrix-valued) in an open set
\mathcal{D}\subset{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
, that is,
A_{j}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{D},{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m})
.

Notation.

If
A^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}A_{j}
is a formal series and
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we denote by
A^{\varepsilon,N}
the function

(4.4)

A^{\varepsilon,N}=\sum_{0\leq j\leq N}\varepsilon^{j}A_{j}.

The formal series that we will consider in Section
4.3
will present two small parameters: the semi-classical parameter
\varepsilon>0
and another parameter
\delta>0
that controls the growth of the symbol and of its derivatives. For our intended application,
\delta
is related to the size of the gap
between the eigenvalues of the Hamiltonian’s symbol.

Definition 4.1

(Symbol spaces)
.

Let
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}

and
\delta\in(0,1]
.

(i)

We denote by
\boldsymbol{S}^{\mu}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
the set of smooth (matrix-valued) functions in
\mathcal{D}
such

|\partial^{\gamma}_{z}A(t,z)|\leq C_{\gamma}\delta^{\mu-|\gamma|},\;\;\forall(t,z)\in\mathcal{D}.

Notice that the set
\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D}):=\boldsymbol{S}^{0}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
has the algebraic structure of a ring.

(ii)

We shall say that a formal series
\displaystyle{A^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}A_{j}}
is in
\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{\mu}(\mathcal{D})
if

A_{j}\in\boldsymbol{S}^{\mu-2j}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
for all
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
We set
\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(\mathcal{D}):=\boldsymbol{S}^{0}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(\mathcal{D})
.

Remark 4.2
.

(1)

If
A\in\mathbf{S}^{\mu}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
and
B\in\mathbf{S}^{\mu}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
, then
AB\in\mathbf{S}^{\mu+\mu^{\prime}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
while
\{A,B\}\in\mathbf{S}^{\mu+\mu^{\prime}-2}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
. Besides, if
A\in\boldsymbol{S}^{\mu}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
, then
\partial_{z}^{\gamma}A\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}^{\mu-|\gamma|}(\mathcal{D})
.

(2)

When
\delta=1
, as in the next Section
4.2
, then for all
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\mathbf{S}^{\mu}_{1}(\mathcal{D})=\mathbf{S}^{0}_{1}(\mathcal{D})
coincides with the standard class of Calderón–Vaillancourt symbols, those smooth functions that are bounded together with their derivatives. Similarly,
\mathbf{S}^{\mu}_{\varepsilon,1}(\mathcal{D})=\mathbf{S}^{0}_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{D})
coincides with asymptotic series of symbols.

(3)

The parameter
\delta
can be understood as quantifying the loss that appears at each differentiation. However, in the asymptotic series, one looses
\delta^{-2}
when passing from some
j
-th term of to the
(j+1)
-th one. In Section
4.3
,
\delta
will monitor the size of the gap function
f
in the domain
\mathcal{D}
.

The Moyal bracket (
4.1
) satisfies the property stated in the next lemma.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\delta_{A},\,\delta_{B}\in]0,1]
.
If
A^{\varepsilon}
and
B^{\varepsilon}
are formal series of
{\bf S}_{\varepsilon,\delta_{A}}(\mathcal{D})
and
{\bf S}_{\varepsilon,\delta_{B}}(\mathcal{D})
, respectively. then
A^{\varepsilon}\circledast B^{\varepsilon}
is a formal series of
{\bf S}_{\varepsilon,\min(\delta_{A},\delta_{B})}(\mathcal{D})
. Besides, for
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

A^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast B^{\varepsilon,N}=\sum_{0\leq j\leq N}\varepsilon^{j}C_{j}+\varepsilon^{N+1}R_{A,B}^{\varepsilon,N}

where for all
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2d}
, there exists
C_{N,\gamma}
independent on
\delta_{A,B}
and
\varepsilon
such that

|\partial_{z}^{\gamma}R_{A,B}^{\varepsilon,N}(t,z)|\leq C_{N,\gamma}\,[\min(\delta_{A},\delta_{B})]^{-N-\kappa_{0}},\;\forall\varepsilon\in]0,1],\;\;\forall(t,z)\in\mathcal{D},

where
\kappa_{0}>0
is a universal constant depending only on the dimension
d
.

Proof.

The estimate is a direct consequence of
[
6
, Theorem A1]
. We give a detailed proof of this result in Appendix
B
, see Theorem
B.1
.
∎

When
\delta_{A}=1
and
\delta_{B}=\delta\in(0,1]
,
\min(\delta_{A},\delta_{B})=\delta
. This shows that

{\bf S}_{\varepsilon,1}(\mathcal{D})\circledast{\bf S}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(\mathcal{D})\subset{\bf S}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(\mathcal{D}).

Let us conclude this section by comments on the quantization of symbols of the classes
{\bf S}_{\delta}^{\mu}(\mathcal{D})
. Further results are discussed in Appendix
B.2
.
The Calderón-Vaillancourt Theorem for pseudo-differential operators (see
[
16
,
71
,
26
]
) states that there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
a\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
,

\|{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(a)\|_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))}\leq C\sup_{0\leq|\gamma|\leq 2d+1}\,\varepsilon^{\frac{|\gamma|}{2}}\sup_{z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\partial_{z}^{\gamma}a(z)|.

Actually, the original article
[
9
]
treats the case
\varepsilon=1
and the estimate in the semi-classical case comes from the observation that

{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(a)=\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{*}{\rm op}_{1}(a(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot))\Lambda_{\varepsilon}

where
\Lambda_{\varepsilon}
is the
L^{2}
-unitary scaling operator defined on function
f\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
by

\Lambda_{\varepsilon}f(x)=\varepsilon^{-\frac{d}{4}}f\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right),\;\;x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.

One can derive an estimate in the sets
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
by observing

\left(x^{\alpha}(\varepsilon\partial_{x})^{\beta}\right)\circ{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(a)=\sum_{|\gamma_{1}|+|\gamma_{2}|+|\gamma_{3}|\leq k}\varepsilon^{\frac{|\gamma_{1}|}{2}}c_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}}(\varepsilon)\,{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(\partial_{z}^{\gamma_{1}}a)\circ\left({x^{\gamma_{2}}}(\varepsilon\partial_{x})^{\gamma_{3}}\right)

for some coefficients
c_{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3}}(\varepsilon)
, uniformly bounded with respect to
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
. This implies
the boundedness of
{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(a)
in weighted Sobolev spaces: for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists a constant
C_{k}>0
such that for all
a\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
,

(4.5)

\|{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(a)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})}\leq C_{k}\sum_{0\leq|\gamma|\leq 2d+k+1}\varepsilon^{\frac{|\gamma|}{2}}\,\sup_{z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\partial_{z}^{\gamma}a(z)|.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
A\in\mathbf{S}^{\mu-2j}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
for
\mu\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then, for
k\in{\mathbb{N}}

\|{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(A)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})}\leq c_{k}\ \delta^{\mu-k-2j}\sup_{0\leq|\gamma|\leq 2d+1}\left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{|\gamma|},

where the constant
c_{k}>0
depends on the symbol
A
and
k
, but is independent of
\varepsilon,\delta,j,\mu
.
In particular, if
\delta\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, then

(4.6)

\|{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(A)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})}\leq c_{k}\,\delta^{\mu-k-2j}.

Proof.

For any
\gamma
, there exists
c_{\gamma}=c_{\gamma}(A)>0
such that

|\partial^{\gamma}_{z}A(z)|\leq c_{\gamma}\,\delta^{\mu-2j-|\gamma|}
.
Hence,

\|{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(A)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})}\leq C_{k}\sum_{0\leq|\gamma|\leq 2d+k+1}\varepsilon^{\frac{|\gamma|}{2}}c_{\gamma}\,\delta^{\mu-2j-|\gamma|}\leq c_{k}\ \delta^{\mu-2j-k}\sup_{0\leq|\gamma|\leq 2d+1}\left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{|\gamma|}

for some symbol dependent constant
c_{k}>0
.
∎

4.2.
The ‘rough’ reduction

The next result gives a reduction of the Hamiltonian in a block diagonalized form. We will use this reduction on small interval of times.

Theorem 4.5
.

Assume a Hamiltonian
H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
with values in the set of
m\times m
matrices such that
H_{0}
has smooth eigenprojectors and eigenvalues.
There exist matrix-valued asymptotic series

\displaystyle\pi^{\varepsilon}_{1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{1}+\sum_{j\geq 1}\varepsilon^{j}\pi_{1,j},\;\;h^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}=h_{\ell}\,{\mathbb{I}_{m}}+\sum_{j\geq 1}\varepsilon^{j}h_{\ell,j},\;\;W^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{j\geq 1}\varepsilon^{j}W_{j},\;\;\ell\in\{1,2\}

such that for all
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}
and
\pi_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}=\mathbb{I}_{m}-\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}
are approximate projectors

(4.7)

\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}\circledast\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}=\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+1}),\;\;\ell\in\{1,2\}

and
H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
reduces according to

(4.8)

\displaystyle\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}\circledast(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-h^{\varepsilon,N}_{1})\circledast\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}+W^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast\pi_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+1}),

(4.9)

\displaystyle\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}\circledast(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-h^{\varepsilon,N}_{2})\circledast\pi_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}+(W^{\varepsilon,N})^{*}\circledast\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+1}).

Moreover, for all
\ell\in\{1,2\}
and
j\geq 1
, the symbols
\pi_{\ell,j}
and
h_{\ell,j}
are self-adjoint, the matrices
W_{j}
are off-diagonal, with
W_{1}
given by (
1.40
), and

(4.10)

\displaystyle h_{\ell,1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\ell}H_{1}\pi_{\ell}+(-1)^{\ell}\frac{i}{2}(h_{1}-h_{2})\pi_{\ell}\{\pi_{1},\pi_{1}\}\pi_{\ell}.

If
H^{\varepsilon}
also satisfies Assumption
1.6
on the time interval
I
, then the
2m\times 2m
matrix-valued Hamiltonian

\underline{H}^{\varepsilon}:=\begin{pmatrix}{h_{1}^{\varepsilon}}&W^{\varepsilon}\\
(W^{\varepsilon})^{*}&{h_{2}^{\varepsilon}}\end{pmatrix}

is subquadratic according to Definition
1.1
.

Note that in
\underline{H}^{\varepsilon}
, the off-diagonal blocks are of lower order than the diagonal ones since the asymptotic series
W^{\varepsilon}
has no term of order
0
.

Theorem
4.5
allows to put the equation (
1.3
) in a reduced form by setting

\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon}=\,^{t}(\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon}_{1},\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon}_{2})\;\;\mbox{with}\;\;\underline{\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}}=\widehat{\pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon}}\psi^{\varepsilon}.

Indeed, we then have

(4.11)

i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon}=\widehat{\underline{H}}^{\varepsilon}\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{\infty}),\;\;\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon}_{|t=0}=\,^{t}\left(\widehat{\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon}}\,\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0},\widehat{\pi_{2}^{\varepsilon}}\,\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\right).

We deduce the following corollary.

Corollary 4.6
.

Formally, we have for
t\in I
,

\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\underline{\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}}+\underline{\psi^{\varepsilon}_{2}},

where
\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon}
solves (
4.11
).

Proof of Theorem
4.5
.

The proof relies on a recursive argument.

The case
N=0
is equivalent to (
4.3
)

The case
N=1
has been proved in Lemma B.2 in
[
23
]
.
However, we revisit the proof in order to have a justification of the value of
W_{1}
in this text.
We first compute
\pi_{\ell,\ell}
by requiring

\pi^{\varepsilon,(\ell)}\circledast\pi^{\varepsilon,(\ell)}=\pi^{\varepsilon,(\ell)}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
, which admits the solution for
\ell=1

\pi_{1,1}=-\pi_{2,1}=-\frac{1}{2i}\pi_{1}\{\pi_{1},\pi_{1}\}\pi_{1}+\frac{1}{2i}\pi_{2}\{\pi_{1},\pi_{1}\}\pi_{2}.

We recall that
\{\pi_{1},\pi_{1}\}
is diagonal and skew-symmetric (see Lemma B.1 in
[
23
]
).
Then, we observe

\pi_{\ell}\circledast(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{\varepsilon})=(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-h_{\ell})\circledast\pi_{\ell}+\varepsilon\Theta_{\ell}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

with

\Theta_{\ell}=-\frac{1}{2i}\{\pi_{\ell},H_{0}\}-\pi_{\ell}H_{1}-i\partial_{t}\pi_{\ell}+\frac{1}{2i}\{h_{\ell},\pi_{\ell}\}

or, equivalently

\displaystyle\Theta_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2i}(h_{2}-h_{1})\{\pi_{1},\pi_{1}\}-i\partial_{t}\pi_{1}-\pi_{1}H_{1}+\frac{1}{i}\{h_{1},\pi_{1}\}\pi_{1}+\frac{1}{2i}\{h_{1}+h_{2},\pi_{1}\}\pi_{2},

\displaystyle\Theta_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2i}(h_{1}-h_{2})\{\pi_{2},\pi_{2}\}-i\partial_{t}\pi_{2}-\pi_{2}H_{1}+\frac{1}{i}\{h_{2},\pi_{2}\}\pi_{2}+\frac{1}{2i}\{h_{1}+h_{2},\pi_{2}\}\pi_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2i}(h_{2}-h_{1})\{\pi_{1},\pi_{1}\}+i\partial_{t}\pi_{1}-\pi_{2}H_{1}-\frac{1}{i}\{h_{2},\pi_{1}\}\pi_{2}-\frac{1}{2i}\{h_{1}+h_{2},\pi_{2}\}\pi_{1}

We observe

\Theta_{2}^{*}=-\frac{1}{2i}(h_{2}-h_{1})\{\pi_{1},\pi_{1}\}-i\partial_{t}\pi_{1}-H_{1}\pi_{2}+\frac{1}{i}\pi_{2}\{h_{2},\pi_{1}\}+\frac{1}{2i}\pi_{1}\{h_{1}+h_{2},\pi_{2}\}

and

(4.12)

\pi_{1}\Theta_{2}^{*}\pi_{2}=\pi_{1}\Theta_{1}\pi_{2}.

Thus, we have to solve

\displaystyle-\pi_{1,1}H_{0}

\displaystyle=-h_{1,1}\pi_{1}-h_{1}\pi_{1,1}+i\partial_{t}\pi_{1}+\Theta_{1}+W_{1}\pi_{2},

\displaystyle-\pi_{2,1}H_{0}

\displaystyle=-h_{2,1}\pi_{2}-h_{2}\pi_{2,1}+i\partial_{t}\pi_{2}+\Theta_{2}+W_{1}^{*}\pi_{1}.

Multiplying on the right the first equation by
\pi_{1}
and the second by
\pi_{2}
, we obtain that
h_{1,1}
and
h_{2,1}
have to satisfy

h_{1,1}\pi_{1}=i\partial_{t}\pi_{1}\pi_{1}+\Theta_{1}\pi_{1}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;h_{2,1}\pi_{2}=i\partial_{t}\pi_{2}\pi_{2}+\Theta_{2}\pi_{2}

which is solved by taking the self-adjoint matrices

\displaystyle h_{1,1}

\displaystyle=i\partial_{t}\pi_{1}\pi_{1}+\Theta_{1}\pi_{1}-i\pi_{1}\partial_{t}\pi_{1}\pi_{2}+\pi_{1}\Theta_{1}^{*}\pi_{2},

\displaystyle h_{2,1}

\displaystyle=i\partial_{t}\pi_{2}\pi_{2}+\Theta_{2}\pi_{2}-i\pi_{2}\partial_{t}\pi_{2}\pi_{1}+\pi_{2}\Theta_{2}^{*}\pi_{1}.

Multiplying on the right the first equation by
\pi_{2}
and the second by
\pi_{1}
, we obtain that
W_{1}
has to verify

W_{1}\pi_{2}=-(h_{2}-h_{1})\pi_{1,1}\pi_{2}-\Theta_{1}\pi_{2}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;W_{1}^{*}\pi_{1}=(h_{2}-h_{1})\pi_{2,1}\pi_{1}-\Theta_{2}\pi_{1}.

Using the relations
\pi_{1,1}^{*}=\pi_{1,1}=-\pi_{2,1}
,
\pi_{1}\partial_{t,z}\pi_{1}=\partial_{t,z}\pi_{1}\pi_{2}
and (
4.12
), we obtain

W_{1}\pi_{2}=\pi_{1}W_{1}=\pi_{1}\left(H_{1}+i\partial_{t}\pi_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\{h_{1}+h_{2},\pi_{1}\}\right)\pi_{2},

whence (
1.40
).

One can now perform the recursive argument. Assume that we have obtained (
4.7
), (
4.8
) and (
4.9
) for some
N\geq 1
and let us look for
\pi_{1,N+1}
,
h_{1,N+1}
,
h_{2,N+1}
and
W_{N+1}
such that the relations for
N+1
too.

Construction of
\pi_{1,N+1}
. We start with
\pi_{1,N+1}
. We write

\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}=\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}+\varepsilon^{N+1}R^{\varepsilon},

where
R^{\varepsilon}
is an asymptotic series with first term
R_{N}
.
We first observe that
R_{N}
is diagonal. Indeed, we have

(1-\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N})\circledast\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}=\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast(1-\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N})

and

(1-\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N})\circledast\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}=-\varepsilon^{N+1}R^{\varepsilon}\circledast\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N},\;\;\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast(1-\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N})=-\varepsilon^{N+1}\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast R^{\varepsilon}.

This yields
\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast R^{\varepsilon}=R^{\varepsilon}\circledast\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}
and imply
\pi_{1}R_{N}=R_{N}\pi_{1}
.
We now look to
\pi_{1,N+1}
that must satisfy

\pi_{1,N+1}=R_{N}+\pi_{1,N+1}\pi_{1}+\pi_{1}\pi_{1,N+1}.

This relation fixes the diagonal part of
\pi_{1,N+1}
according to

\pi_{1}\pi_{1,N+1}\pi_{1}=-\pi_{1}R_{N}\pi_{1}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\pi_{2}\pi_{1,N+1}\pi_{2}=\pi_{2}R_{N}\pi_{2},

We will see later that we do not need to prescribe
off-diagonal components to
\pi_{1,N+1}
.

Compatibility relations.
For constructing
h_{1,N+1}
,
h_{2,N+1}
and
W_{N+1}
, we need information about the asymptotic series
\Theta^{\varepsilon}_{1}
defined by

\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}\circledast(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{\varepsilon})=(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-h^{\varepsilon,N}_{1})\circledast\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}+W^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast\pi_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}+\varepsilon^{N+1}\Theta^{\varepsilon}_{1}.

Let us denote by
\Theta_{1,N}
the first term of the asymptotic series
\Theta^{\varepsilon}_{1}
.
For obtaining information about
\Theta_{1,N}
, we compute

\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}\circledast(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{\varepsilon})\circledast\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell^{\prime}}

for different choices of
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in\{1,2\}
.

(i)

Taking
\ell\not=\ell^{\prime}
gives two relations

\displaystyle\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}

\displaystyle\circledast(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{\varepsilon})\circledast\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}

\displaystyle=\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}\circledast W^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}+\varepsilon^{N+1}\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}\circledast\Theta^{\varepsilon}_{1}\circledast\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2})

\displaystyle\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}

\displaystyle\circledast(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{\varepsilon})\circledast\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}

\displaystyle=\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}\circledast(W^{\varepsilon,N})^{*}\circledast\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}+\varepsilon^{N+1}\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}\circledast\Theta^{\varepsilon}_{2}\circledast\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2}),

from which we deduce

(4.13)

\pi_{2}\Theta_{2,N}\pi_{1}=\left(\pi_{1}\Theta_{1,N}\pi_{2}\right)^{*}=\pi_{2}\Theta_{1,N}^{*}\pi_{1}.

(ii)

Taking
\ell=\ell^{\prime}
gives the relations

\displaystyle\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}

\displaystyle\circledast(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{\varepsilon})\circledast\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}

\displaystyle=\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}\circledast(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-h^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell})\circledast\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}+\varepsilon^{N+1}\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}\circledast\Theta^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\circledast\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2}),

whence the self-adjointness of the diagonal part of
\Theta^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}
.

Construction of
h_{1,N+1}
and
h_{2,N+1}
, first properties of
W_{N+1}
.

We write the asymptotic series

\displaystyle\pi^{\varepsilon,N+1}_{1}\circledast(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{\varepsilon})=\pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}\circledast(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{\varepsilon})-\varepsilon^{N+1}\pi_{1,N+1}H_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2}),

\displaystyle(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-h^{\varepsilon,N+1}_{1})\circledast\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N+1}+W^{\varepsilon,N+1}\circledast\pi_{2}^{\varepsilon,N+1}=(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-h^{\varepsilon,N}_{1})\circledast\pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}+W^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast\pi_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\varepsilon^{N+1}\left(i\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}-h_{1,N+1}\pi_{1}-h_{1}\pi_{1,N+1}+W_{N+1}\pi_{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2}).

Therefore, the terms
h_{1,N+1}
and
W_{N+1}
have to satisfy

-\pi_{1,N+1}H_{0}=i\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}-h_{1,N+1}\pi_{1}-h_{1}\pi_{1,N+1}+W_{N+1}\pi_{2}+\Theta_{1,N}

or equivalently

0=i\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}-h_{1,N+1}\pi_{1}+(h_{2}-h_{1})\pi_{1,N+1}\pi_{2}+W_{N+1}\pi_{2}+\Theta_{1,N}.

By multiplying on the right by
\pi_{1}
, then
\pi_{2}
, we are left with the two equations

\displaystyle h_{1,N+1}\pi_{1}=\Theta_{1,N}\pi_{1}+i\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}\pi_{1},

(4.14)

\displaystyle W_{N+1}\pi_{2}=\Theta_{1,N}\pi_{2}+(h_{2}-h_{1})\pi_{1,N+1}\pi_{2}+i\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}\pi_{2}.

Considering similarly the conditions for the mode
h_{2}
, we obtain that
h_{2,N+1}
and
W_{N+1}^{*}
have to satisfy

h_{2,N+1}\pi_{2}=\Theta_{2,N}\pi_{2}+i\partial_{t}\pi_{2,N}\pi_{2}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;W_{N+1}^{*}\pi_{1}=\Theta_{2,N}\pi_{1}-(h_{2}-h_{1})\pi_{2,N+1}\pi_{1}+i\partial_{t}\pi_{2,N}\pi_{1}.

Since
\pi_{2,N}=-\pi_{1,N}
for
N\geq 1
, we are left with the relation

\displaystyle h_{2,N+1}\pi_{2}=\Theta_{2,N}\pi_{2}-i\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}\pi_{2},

(4.15)

\displaystyle W_{N+1}^{*}\pi_{1}=\Theta_{2,N}\pi_{1}+(h_{2}-h_{1})\pi_{1,N+1}\pi_{1}-i\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}\pi_{1}.

We set

\displaystyle h_{1,N+1}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{1,N}\pi_{1}+i\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}\pi_{1}+\pi_{1}\Theta_{1,N}^{*}\pi_{2}-i\pi_{1}\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}\pi_{2},

\displaystyle h_{2,N+1}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{2,N}\pi_{2}+i\partial_{t}\pi_{2,N}\pi_{2}+\pi_{2}\Theta_{2,N}^{*}\pi_{1}-i\pi_{2}\partial_{t}\pi_{2,N}\pi_{1}.

Then,
h_{1,N+1}
and
h_{2,N+1}
are self-adjoint and satisfy the desired properties.

End of the construction of
W_{N+1}
.
The construction of
W_{N+1}
requires to be more careful because there is a compatibility condition that has to be satisfied in order to have simultaneously (
4.14
) and (
4.15
).
Let us look for
W_{N+1}
of the form

W_{N+1}=\Theta_{1,N}\pi_{2}+(h_{2}-h_{1})\pi_{1,N+1}\pi_{2}+i\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}\pi_{2}+U_{N+1}\pi_{1}.

This guarantees (
4.14
).
Then, one has

W_{N+1}^{*}=\pi_{2}\Theta_{1,N}^{*}+(h_{2}-h_{1})\pi_{2}\pi_{1,N+1}-i\pi_{2}\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}+\pi_{1}U_{N+1}^{*}

and

\displaystyle W_{N+1}^{*}\pi_{1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}\Theta_{1,N}^{*}\pi_{1}-i\pi_{2}\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}\pi_{1}+\pi_{1}U_{N+1}^{*}\pi_{1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}\Theta_{2,N}\pi_{1}-i\pi_{2}\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}\pi_{1}+\pi_{1}U_{N+1}^{*}\pi_{1}

where we have used the first property of the matrices
\Theta_{1,N}
and
\Theta_{2,N}
that we have exhibited (see (
4.13
)), together with the fact that
\pi_{1,N+1}
is diagonal, as noticed in its construction.
It is then enough to choose

U_{N+1}=\pi_{1}\left(\Theta_{2,N}^{*}+(h_{2}-h_{1})\pi_{1,N+1}+i\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}\right)\pi_{1}

since it implies

\displaystyle W_{N+1}^{*}\pi_{1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}\Theta_{2,N}\pi_{1}-i\pi_{2}\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}\pi_{1}+\pi_{1}(\Theta_{2,N}+i\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}+(h_{2}-h_{1})\pi_{1,N+1})\pi_{1}

\displaystyle=\Theta_{2,N}\pi_{1}+(h_{2}-h_{1})\pi_{1,N+1}\pi_{1}-i\pi_{2}\partial_{t}\pi_{1,N}\pi_{1},

where we have used that
\pi_{1,N+1}
is diagonal (whence
\pi_{1,N+1}\pi_{1}=\pi_{1}\pi_{1,N+1}\pi_{1}
). As a consequence,
the second part of (
4.15
) is satisfied.
This concludes the recursive argument and the proof of the Theorem
4.5
since the growth properties of the matrices that we have constructed come with the recursive equations.
∎

4.3.
Superadiabatic projectors and diagonalization

We now want to get rid of the off-diagonal elements
\widehat{W}^{\varepsilon}
that appears in the reduction of Theorem
4.5
. This is only possible outside
\Upsilon
. We are going to take into account how far from the crossing set we are by introducing a gap assumption.

Assumption 4.7

(Gap assumption)
.

Let
t_{0}<t_{1}
,
I
an open interval of
{\mathbb{R}}
containing
[t_{0},t_{1}]
and
\Omega
an open subset of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
.
We say that the eigenvalue
h
has a gap larger than
\delta\in(0,1]
in
\mathcal{D}:=I\times\Omega
if one has

({NC}_{\delta})

\displaystyle{d\left(h(t,z),{\rm Sp}(H_{0}(t,z))\right)\geq\delta,\;\;\forall(t,z)\in\mathcal{D}.}

The construction of superadiabatic projectors dates to
[
5
]
which was inspired by the paper
[
19
]
. It has then been carefully developed in
[
51
]
and
[
62
]
(see also the book
[
65
]
).
We revisit here the construction of superadiabatic projectors, in order to control their norms with respect to the parameter
\delta
.

We follow the construction of the Section 14.4 of the latest edition of
[
15
]
(2021), that we adapt to our context.
One proceeds in two steps: first by defining the formal series for the projectors and then for the Hamiltonians.
In order to simplify the notations in the construction, we just consider an eigenvalue
h
and we will then apply the result to the eigenvalues
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
.

4.3.1.
Formal superadiabatic projectors

Theorem 4.8

(semiclassical projector evolution)
.

Assume the eigenvalue
h
of the Hamiltonian
H_{0}
satisfies Assumption
4.7
in
\mathcal{D}
.
Then, there exists a
unique formal series
\sum_{j\geq 1}\varepsilon^{j}\Pi_{j}
in
\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}(\mathcal{D})
such that setting
\Pi_{0}(t,z)=\pi(t,z)
,
the formal series

\displaystyle{\Pi^{\varepsilon}(t,z)=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\Pi_{j}(t,z)}

is a formal projection
and

(4.16)

i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\Pi^{\varepsilon}(t)=\{H^{\varepsilon}(t),\Pi^{\varepsilon}(t)\}_{\circledast}.

Moreover the sub-principal term
\Pi_{1}(t)
is an Hermitian matrix given by the following formulas:

(4.17)

\displaystyle\pi(t)\Pi_{1}(t)\pi(t)=-\frac{1}{2i}\pi(t)\{\pi(t),\pi(t)\}\pi(t),

\displaystyle\pi(t)^{\perp}\Pi_{1}(t)\pi(t)^{\perp}=\frac{1}{2i}\pi(t)^{\perp}\{\pi(t),\pi(t)\}\pi(t)^{\perp},

\displaystyle\pi(t)^{\perp}\Pi_{1}(t)\pi(t)=\pi(t)^{\perp}(H_{0}(t)-h(t))^{-1}\pi(t)^{\perp}R_{1}(t)\pi(t),

where

R_{1}(t)=i\partial_{t}\pi(t)-\frac{1}{2i}\left(\{H_{0}(t),\pi(t)\}-\{\pi(t),H_{0}(t)\}\right)-[H_{1}(t),\pi(t)].

Proof.

With Notations
4.4
, and working in space time variable,

(4.18)

\displaystyle\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}-\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{N+1}S_{N+1}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2}),

(4.19)

\displaystyle-\left\{\tau+H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1},\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}\right\}_{\circledast}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{N+1}R_{N+1}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2}),

where the Moyal bracket has been defined in (
4.2
) and
\left\{\tau,\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}\right\}_{\circledast}=\partial_{t}\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}
.

Step 1.
We start with
N=0
. We have
\Pi^{(0)}=\pi\in\boldsymbol{S}^{0}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
. Since
\pi^{2}=\pi
and
[H_{0},\pi]=0
, we obtain

S_{1}=\tfrac{1}{2i}\{\pi,\pi\}\;\mbox{and}\;R_{1}=i\partial_{t}\pi-\tfrac{1}{2i}(\{H_{0},\pi\}-\{\pi,H_{0}\})-[H_{1},\pi],

and we have
R_{1},S_{1}\in\boldsymbol{S}^{0}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
. Before constructing
S_{1}
,
two structural observations are in order:

(1)

The matrix
S_{1}
is symmetric and satisfies
\pi S_{1}\pi^{\perp}=\pi^{\perp}S_{1}\pi=0
.

(2)

The matrix
R_{1}
is skew-symmetric. It satisfies

\pi R_{1}\pi=0\quad\text{and}\quad\pi^{\perp}R_{1}\pi^{\perp}=[H_{0},\pi^{\perp}S_{1}\pi^{\perp}].

If
H_{0}
has only two eigenvalues,
H_{0}
expresses only in terms of
\pi
and
the expression of
R_{1}
given above shows that
R_{1}
is off-diagonal. The situation is more complicated if
H_{0}
has strictly more than two distinct eigenvalues. For verifying (2) in that case, one uses two times
the Poisson bracket rule

\{A,BC\}-\{AB,C\}=\{A,B\}C-A\{B,C\}.

We obtain

\displaystyle\{H_{0},\pi\}-\{\pi,H_{0}\}

\displaystyle=\{H_{0},\pi^{2}\}-\{\pi^{2},H_{0}\}

\displaystyle=\{h\pi,\pi\}+\{H_{0},\pi\}\pi-H_{0}\{\pi,\pi\}-\{\pi,h\pi\}+\{\pi,\pi\}H_{0}-\pi\{\pi,H_{0}\}

\displaystyle=\pi\{h,\pi\}-\{\pi,h\}\pi+[\{\pi,\pi\},H_{0}]+\{H_{0},\pi\}\pi-\pi\{\pi,H_{0}\},

which implies

\pi^{\perp}(\{H_{0},\pi\}-\{\pi,H_{0}\})\pi^{\perp}=\pi^{\perp}[\{\pi,\pi\},H_{0}]\pi^{\perp}.

For determining the
\pi
-diagonal component, we choose
A=\pi
,
B=H_{0}\pi^{\perp}
, and
C=\pi

to obtain

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\{\pi,H_{0}\pi^{\perp}\}\pi-\pi\{H_{0}\pi^{\perp},\pi\}

\displaystyle=\{\pi,H_{0}\}\pi-\{\pi,h\pi\}\pi-\pi\{H_{0},\pi\}+\pi\{h\pi,\pi\}

\displaystyle=\{\pi,H_{0}\}\pi-\{\pi,h\}\pi-\pi\{H_{0},\pi\}+\pi\{h,\pi\}.

This relation implies

\pi(\{H_{0},\pi\}-\{\pi,H_{0}\})\pi=0.

For constructing the matrix
\Pi_{1}
that defines
\Pi^{(1)}=\pi+\varepsilon\Pi_{1}
, we need to satisfy

\pi\Pi_{1}+\Pi_{1}\pi-\Pi_{1}=-S_{1}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;-[H_{0},\Pi_{1}]=-R_{1}.

The first of these two equations uniquely determines the diagonal blocks of
\Pi_{1}
, while the second
equation uniquely determines the off-diagonal blocks. We obtain

\displaystyle\pi\Pi_{1}\pi=-\pi S_{1}\pi\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\pi^{\perp}\Pi_{1}\pi^{\perp}=\pi^{\perp}S_{1}\pi^{\perp},

\displaystyle\pi\Pi_{1}\pi^{\perp}=-\pi R_{1}\pi^{\perp}(H_{0}-h)^{-1}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\pi^{\perp}\Pi_{1}\pi=(H_{0}-h)^{-1}\pi^{\perp}R_{1}\pi.

For concluding this first step, we deduce from
R_{1},S_{1}\in\boldsymbol{S}^{0}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
that

\Pi_{1}\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}^{-1}(\mathcal{D})
.

Step
N\geq 1
.

Next we proceed by induction and assume that we have constructed the matrices
\Pi_{j}(t)\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}^{1-2j}(\mathcal{D})
for
1\leq j\leq N
such that (
4.18
) and (
4.19
) hold. Note that by Lemma
4.3
, this implies

R_{N+1}(t)\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}^{-2N}(\mathcal{D})\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;S_{N+1}(t)\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}^{-2N}(\mathcal{D}).

Indeed,

-\left\{\tau+H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1},\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}\right\}_{\circledast}
is a formal series of
\varepsilon\,\boldsymbol{S}^{-2}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(\mathcal{D})
while
\Pi^{N}\circledast\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}-\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}
is a formal series of
\varepsilon\boldsymbol{S}^{-1}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(\mathcal{D})
. In order to go one step further, we see that
\Pi_{N+1}
has to
be chosen so that first

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2})

\displaystyle=-\left\{\tau+H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1},\Pi^{\varepsilon,N+1}\right\}_{\circledast}

\displaystyle=-\left\{\tau+H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1},\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}\right\}_{\circledast}-\varepsilon^{N+1}[H_{0},\Pi_{N+1}]+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{N+1}R_{N+1}-\varepsilon^{N+1}[H_{0},\Pi_{N+1}]+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2}),

and second

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2})

\displaystyle=\Pi^{N+1}\circledast\Pi^{\varepsilon,N+1}-\Pi^{\varepsilon,N+1}

\displaystyle=\Pi^{N}\circledast\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}-\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}+\varepsilon^{N+1}(\pi\Pi_{N+1}+\Pi_{N+1}\pi-\Pi_{N+1})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{N+1}S_{N+1}+\varepsilon^{N+1}(\pi\Pi_{N+1}-\Pi_{N+1}\pi^{\perp})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2})

As a conclusion, we have to find
\Pi_{N+1}
such that

(4.20)

\pi\Pi_{N+1}-\Pi_{N+1}\pi^{\perp}=-S_{N+1}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;[H_{0},\Pi_{N+1}]=R_{N+1}.

The first relation in (
4.20
) determines the diagonal part of
\Pi_{N+1}
:

\pi\Pi_{N+1}\pi=-\pi S_{N+1}\pi\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\pi^{\perp}\Pi_{N+1}\pi^{\perp}=-\pi^{\perp}S_{N+1}\pi^{\perp}

provided a first compatibility relation is satisfied, namely

(4.21)

\pi S_{N+1}\pi^{\perp}=\pi^{\perp}S_{N+1}\pi=0.

Then the second equation in (
4.20
) fixes the off diagonal part of
\Pi_{N+1}
, via

\pi H_{0}(\pi\Pi_{N+1}\pi^{\perp})=\pi R_{N+1}\pi^{\perp}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\pi^{\perp}H_{0}(\pi^{\perp}\Pi_{N+1}\pi)=\pi^{\perp}R_{N+1}\pi,

up to a second compatibility about the diagonal part of the commutators that are now fixed: in view of
[\pi H_{0},\pi\Pi_{N+1}\pi]=-[\pi H_{0},\pi S_{N+1}\pi]
and
[\pi^{\perp}H_{0},\pi^{\perp}\Pi_{N+1}\pi^{\perp}]=[\pi^{\perp}H_{0},\pi^{\perp}S_{N+1}\pi^{\perp}]
, one must have

(4.22)

[\pi H_{0},\pi S_{N+1}\pi]=-\pi R_{N+1}\pi\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;[\pi^{\perp}H_{0},\pi^{\perp}S_{N+1}\pi^{\perp}]=\pi^{\perp}R_{N+1}\pi^{\perp}.

This process allows to to construct
\Pi_{N+1}(t)\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}^{-2N-1)}(\mathcal{D})
and we will also obtain
R_{N+2}(t)\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}^{-2N-2}(\mathcal{D})
,
S_{N+2}(t)\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}^{-2N-2}(\mathcal{D})
because of equations (
4.18
), (
4.19
) and Lemma
4.3
.

It remains to prove the two compatibility relations (
4.21
) and (
4.22
).
For proving (
4.21
), we take advantage of the fact that

Z:=\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast\left((\Pi^{\varepsilon,N})^{2\circledast}-\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}\right)\circledast(\mathbb{I}-\Pi^{\varepsilon,N})=\varepsilon^{N+1}\pi S_{N+1}\pi^{\perp}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2}),

while one also has by construction

Z=\left((\Pi^{\varepsilon,N})^{2\circledast}-\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}\right)\circledast\left(\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}-(\Pi^{\varepsilon,N})^{2\circledast}\right)={\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{2N+2}).

This implies that

\pi S_{N+1}\pi^{\perp}=0
and, using that
S_{N+1}
is Hermitian, we deduce that it is diagonal, whence (
4.21
).

For proving the first relation in (
4.22
), we argue similarly with

Z^{\prime}:=-\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast\left(\left\{\tau+H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1},\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}\right\}_{\circledast}\right)\circledast\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}=\varepsilon^{N+1}\pi R_{N+1}\pi+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2}),

which also satisfies

\displaystyle Z^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast(\tau+H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1})\circledast(\Pi^{\varepsilon,N})^{2\circledast}+(\Pi^{\varepsilon,N})^{2\circledast}\circledast(\tau+H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1})\circledast\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}

\displaystyle=-\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast(\tau+H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1})\circledast(\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}+\varepsilon^{N+1}S_{N+1})

\displaystyle\qquad+(\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}+\varepsilon^{N+1}S_{N+1})\circledast(\tau+H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1})\circledast\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2})

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{N+1}\left(-\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast(\tau+H_{0})\circledast S_{N+1}+S_{N+1}\circledast(\tau+H_{0})\circledast\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2}).

This implies

\pi R_{N+1}\pi=-\pi H_{0}S_{N+1}+S_{N+1}H_{0}\pi.

Since
[S_{N+1},\pi]=0
, we deduce

\pi R_{N+1}\pi=-[\pi H_{0},\pi S_{N+1}\pi].

One argues similarly with
\mathbb{I}-\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}
for obtaining the other relation in (
4.22
). This finishes the proof.
∎

4.3.2.
Formal adiabatic decoupling

The second (and decisive) part of the analysis is a formal adiabatic decoupling using the superadiabatic projectors introduced before.

Theorem 4.9

(formal adiabatic decoupling)
.

Assume the eigenvalue
h
of the Hamiltonian
H_{0}
satisfies Assumption
4.7
in
\mathcal{D}
.
There exists a formal time dependent Hermitian Hamiltonian in
\mathcal{D}
,

\displaystyle{H^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}H^{\rm adia}_{j}}

such that

(4.23)

\Pi^{\varepsilon}\circledast(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{\varepsilon})=(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon})\circledast\Pi^{\varepsilon}

with the following properties:

(1)

The principal symbol is
H^{\rm adia}_{0}=h\,\mathbb{I}_{m}
.

(2)

The subprincipal term
H_{1}^{\rm adia}
is a Hermitian matrix satisfying

\displaystyle\pi^{\perp}\,H_{1}^{\rm adia}\pi=\pi^{\perp}\left(i\partial_{t}\pi+i\{h,\pi\}\right)\pi\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\pi\,H_{1}^{\rm adia}\pi

\displaystyle=\pi H_{1}\pi+\frac{1}{2i}\pi\{H_{0},\pi\}\pi

(see (
1.24
)) and we can choose

\pi^{\perp}H^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon}_{1}\pi^{\perp}=0.

(3)

We have

\varepsilon^{-2}\left(H^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon}-h\mathbb{I}_{m}-\varepsilon H^{\rm adia}_{1}\right)\in{\bf S}_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}(\mathcal{D}).

(4)

Finally,
\pi(t)
satisfies a transport equation along the classical flow for
h(t)
.

(4.24)

\partial_{t}\pi+\{h,\pi\}=\frac{1}{i}[H^{{\rm adia}}_{1},\pi].

Remark 4.10
.

Note that equations (
1.24
) imply
that
H^{\rm adia}_{1}(t,z)
is smooth everywhere, also on the crossing set.

The above construction singles out a smooth eigenvalue
h
with smooth eigenprojector
\pi
. If it is applied to the Hamiltonian
H^{\varepsilon}
for two smooth eigenvalues
(h_{1},h_{2})
and two smooth eigenprojectors
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
, then we obtain two pairs of formal series

(4.25)

\Pi^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\Pi_{\ell,j}\;\;{\rm and}\;\;H^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon}_{\ell}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}H^{\rm adia}_{\ell,j}.

We note, that if
\pi_{1}^{\perp}=\pi_{2}
, then
\Pi^{\varepsilon}_{2}=\mathrm{Id}-\Pi^{\varepsilon}_{1}
.

Corollary 4.11
.

At the level of the evolution operator, the result implies

\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})=\widehat{\Pi_{1}^{\varepsilon}}(t)\mathcal{U}^{\rm adia,\varepsilon}_{1}(t,t_{0})\widehat{\Pi_{1}^{\varepsilon}}(t_{0})+\widehat{\Pi_{2}^{\varepsilon}}(t)\mathcal{U}^{\rm adia,\varepsilon}_{2}(t,t_{0})\widehat{\Pi_{2}^{\varepsilon}}(t_{0})

where for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
,
\mathcal{U}^{\rm adia,\varepsilon}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})
are the evolution operators associated with the Hamiltonian
H^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon}_{\ell}

Proof of Theorem
4.9
.

This result is Theorem 80 of Chapter 14 in
[
15
]
combined with Lemma
4.3
. We first observe that
equation (
4.23
) reduces to proving

(4.26)

\Pi^{\varepsilon}\circledast(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{\varepsilon})=(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon})\circledast\Pi^{\varepsilon}.

For proving the latter relation, one first observes that if
H_{0}^{\rm adia}=h
, then we have

\displaystyle(H^{\rm adia,\varepsilon}-H_{0}-\varepsilon H_{1})\circledast\Pi^{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\left((h-H_{0})\Pi_{1}+(H_{1}^{\rm adia}-H_{1})\pi+\frac{1}{2i}\{h-H_{0},\pi\}\right)+i\partial_{t}\pi+\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{2}).

Therefore,

H_{1}
has to be chosen so that

(H_{1}^{\rm adia}-H_{1})\pi=(H_{0}-h)\Pi_{1}+\frac{1}{2i}\{H_{0}-h,\pi\}+i\partial_{t}\pi.

In view of (
4.17
), this requires

\pi(H_{1}^{\rm adia}-H_{1})\pi=\frac{1}{2i}\pi\{H_{0},\pi\}\pi,

which is given by the second relation of (
1.24
), and, using again (
4.17
)

\pi^{\perp}(H_{1}^{\rm adia}-H_{1})\pi=\pi^{\perp}(R_{1}+\frac{1}{2i}\{H_{0}-h,\pi\}+i\partial_{t}\pi)\pi=\pi^{\perp}(i\partial_{t}\pi+\frac{1}{2i}\{\pi,H_{0}\}-\frac{1}{2i}\{h,\pi\}-H_{1})\pi

which is also given by the first relation of (
1.24
) in view of
the observation that

\pi^{\perp}\{H_{0},\pi\}\pi=-\pi^{\perp}\{h,\pi\}\pi.

For proving this relation, one uses the Poisson bracket rule

\{A,BC\}-\{AB,C\}=\{A,B\}C-A\{B,C\}

several times. First, one gets

\pi\{\pi,\pi\}\pi^{\perp}=0=\pi^{\perp}\{\pi,\pi\}\pi.

Then, taking
A=\pi^{\perp}
,
B=\pi
,
C=H_{0}
,
one gets

\displaystyle\{\pi^{\perp},h\pi\}-0

\displaystyle=\{\pi^{\perp},\pi\}H_{0}-\pi^{\perp}\{\pi,H_{0}\},

whence

-\pi^{\perp}\{\pi,h\}\pi=-\pi^{\perp}\{\pi,H_{0}\}\pi.

Finally, for concluding the construction of
H_{1}^{\rm adia}
, It remains to check that

\left((H_{0}-h)\Pi_{1}+\frac{1}{2i}\{H_{0}-h,\pi\}+i\partial_{t}\pi\right)\pi^{\perp}=0

which comes from the latter observation about
\{H_{0},\pi\}
.

Now that
H_{0}^{\rm adia}
and
H_{1}^{\rm adia}
are constructed, one uses a recursive argument: assume that one has constructed
H_{j}^{\rm adia}
for
0\leq j\leq N
with
H_{j}^{\rm adia}\in\boldsymbol{S}^{-2j}_{\delta}
for
j\in\{2,\cdots,N\}
and such that has (
4.26
) holds up to
\mathcal{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{N+1})
. Let us construct
H_{N+1}^{\rm adia}
. Setting as in Notation
4.4

H^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{j}H_{j}^{\rm adia}

we write

(H^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}-H_{0}-\varepsilon H_{1})\circledast\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}=\varepsilon^{N+1}T_{N}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+2})

with
T_{N}\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}^{-2N-3}
and we look for
H^{\rm adia}_{N+1}
such that

\pi H^{\rm adia}_{N+1}=T_{N}
. This is doable as long as
\pi^{\perp}T_{N}=0
, which comes form the observation that

(H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}-H^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N})\circledast\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}\circledast(1-\Pi^{\varepsilon,N})=\varepsilon^{N+1}\pi^{\perp}T_{N}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+1})=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+1})

by the properties of superadiabatic projectors. Besides,
H^{\rm adia}_{N+1}\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}^{-2N-3}
, which fits with (ii) of Definition
4.1

For concluding the proof, we observe that Point (3) comes from Lemma
4.3
, and Point
(4) from (
1.24
).
∎

Chapter 5
Propagation in adiabatic regimes with small gap

In this section, we prove Theorem
1.26
outside the gap region, i.e. the following statement:

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
(t_{0},z_{0})
and
T>0
be such that for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, we have
\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}\in{|f(t,z)|>\delta}
on the interval
[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
. Let
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
be a polarized wave packet as in (
1.50
) and

\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)
be the solution of (
1.3
) with initial datum
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
.
There exist
\kappa_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and two families of differential operators of degree
\leq 3j
with time dependent smooth vector-valued coefficients,
\left(\vec{B}_{\ell,j}(t)\right)_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}
,
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, satisfying (
1.51
) and (
1.52
), such that setting
for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
,

(5.1)

\displaystyle\psi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{\ell}(t)}\left(f^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}(t)\right),

with

\displaystyle f^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}(t)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0})]\sum_{0\leq j\leq N}\varepsilon^{j/2}\,\vec{B}_{\ell,j}(t)f_{0},\;\;\ell\in\{1,2\},

one has the following property:
for all
k,N,M\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists
C_{M,N,k}>0
such for all
t\in I_{\delta}

\left\|\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)-\left(\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}(t)+\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}(t)\right)\right\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}\leq C_{M,N,k}\left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{N+1}\delta^{-2\kappa_{0}}.

In the next chapter, we will prove Theorem
1.26
, by applying this result on the intervals
[t_{0},t_{1}^{\flat}(t_{0},z_{0})-\delta]
and on
[t_{1}^{\flat}(t_{0},z_{0}]+\delta,t_{0}+T]
, with different data at
t=t_{1}^{\flat}(t_{0},z_{0}]+\delta
that will be calculated in the next chapter. Assumption (
1.49
) guarantees that the hypothesis of Theorem
5.1
are satisfied on these intervals. The parameter
\delta
will be taken afterwards as
\delta\approx\varepsilon^{\alpha}
with
\alpha<\frac{1}{2}
as suggested by the statement.

The proof relies on the use of the superadiabatic projectors constructed in Section
4.3
.
In order to explain carefully each step of the proof, we start by proving the propagation
faraway from the crossing area in Section
5.1
. That allows us to settle the arguments, before doing it precisely close to
\Upsilon
in Section
5.2
.
All along Section
5
, we will use that in the zone where the wave packets
(\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t))_{\varepsilon>0}
are microlocalized, the Assumption
4.7
is satisfied. This comes from the following dynamical Assumption
5.2
that will be satisfied in different domains of interest.

Assumption 5.2

(Dynamical assumption)
.

We say that
\Omega_{1}
and
t_{1}
satisfy the dynamical assumption
(DA)
for the mode
h_{\ell}
and the set
\Omega
if we have

(DA)

\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(\Omega_{1})\subset\Omega

for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
.

5.1.
Propagation far away from the crossing area

In this section, we analyze the propagation of wave packets in a region where the gap is bounded from below. It gives the opportunity to introduce the method that we shall use in the next section for a small gap region. So,
we fix
\delta=\delta_{0}
,
\delta_{0}>0
small but independent on
\varepsilon
and we
work in the open set

\Omega:=\{z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\;|h_{2}(t,z)-h_{1}(t,z)|>\delta_{0},\;\;\forall t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]\},\;\;\mathcal{D}=I\times\Omega

where
I
is an open interval of
{\mathbb{R}}
containing
[t_{0},t_{1}]
and where the gap condition is also satisfied.
We associate with
H^{\varepsilon}
the formal series of Theorems
4.9
and
4.8
for each of the modes:

\Pi^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\Pi_{\ell,j}\;\;{\rm and}\;\;H^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon}_{\ell}=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}H^{\rm adia}_{\ell,j}

and we will use the notation introduced in (
4.4
).

With
z_{0}\in\Omega
, we associate the open sets
\Omega_{0}
,
\Omega_{1}
,
\Omega_{2}
and
\Omega_{3}
such that

z_{0}\in\Omega_{0}\Subset\Omega_{2}\Subset\Omega_{1}\Subset\Omega_{3}\Subset\Omega

where
\Omega_{0}
and
\Omega_{2}
are constructed so that
for any initial data
z\in\Omega_{0}
the flows
are staying in
\Omega_{2}
:

\Phi_{h_{\ell}}^{t,t_{0}}(z)\in\Omega_{2},\;\;\forall t\in[t_{0},t_{1}],\;\;\forall z\in\Omega_{0},\;\;\forall\ell\in\{1,2\}.

We associate cut-offs to these subsets. We take

\chi_{0}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega_{0})
with
\chi_{0}=1
near
z_{0}
. Then, we choose
K_{0}
a compact neighborhood of
z_{0}
in
\Omega_{0}
, which implies that
\Omega_{2}
is a neighborhood of

(5.2)

\widetilde{K}_{\ell,0}:=\{\Phi_{h_{\ell}}^{t,t_{0}}(K_{0}),\;t_{0}\leq t\leq t_{1}\},\;\;\ell\in\{1,2\}.

So we can choose
\chi_{2}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega)
with
\chi_{2}=1
on
\widetilde{K}_{0}=\widetilde{K}_{1,0}\cup\widetilde{K}_{2,0}\subset\Omega_{2}
.
Finally, we take
\chi_{1}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega_{1})
and
\chi_{3}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\Omega_{3})
with
\chi_{1}=1
on
{\rm supp}(\chi_{2})\subset\Omega_{2}
and
\chi_{3}=1
on
{\rm supp}(\chi_{1})\subset\Omega_{1}
.

For
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, we set

\widetilde{H}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)=\chi_{3}\,H^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t),

which is a smooth subquadratic Hamitonian,
and we consider
{\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,s)
the propagator associated with the Hamiltonian
\chi_{3}\,H_{\ell}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}(t)
.

The next result is the usual adiabatic decoupling that results from the analysis of Chapter
4
. We sometimes shorten
{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(\chi_{j})
in
\widehat{\chi}_{j}
,
j\in\{0,1,\cdots,3\}
.

Proposition 5.3

(adiabatic decoupling - I)
.

Let
k,N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
.

(i)

For any
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, we have in
\mathcal{L}(\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})
,

(5.3)

\displaystyle\left(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon}(t)\right){\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{1}\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)\right)\,{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(\chi_{2})=

\displaystyle\qquad{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{1}\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)\right)\,\left(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\widetilde{H}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)\right)\right)\,{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(\chi_{2})+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{N+1}).

(ii)

Let
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\in\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
such that

\widehat{\chi}_{0}\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{N+1})
in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
.
Set

\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)={\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{2}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)\right)\,{\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{0}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t_{0})\right)\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0},\;\;\ell\in\{1,2\}.

Then we have in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
,

(5.4)

{\mathcal{U}}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}(t)+\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}(t)+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{N+1}),\;\forall t\in[t_{0},t_{1}].

Remark 5.4
.

(1)

The assumption satisfied by
(\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0})_{\varepsilon>0}
in (ii) of Proposition
5.3
is sometimes referred in the literature as having a compact semi-classical wave front set, or being mcrolocalized in
\Omega_{0}
.

(2)

In the proof below, the reader will notice that we do not need to assume that
H^{\varepsilon}(t)
is sub-quadratic, we only need to know that
\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon}(t)
defines a unitary Schrödinger propagator in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
. However, we use the boundedness of the derivatives of the projectors.

Proof.

(i) We fix
\ell\in\{1,2\}
. Using Theorem
4.9
and the fact that
\chi_{3}\chi_{1}=\chi_{1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{\varepsilon}(t))\circledast\left(\chi_{1}\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\chi_{1}(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{\varepsilon}(t))\circledast\left(\chi_{1}\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)\right)+\varepsilon^{N+1}\widehat{R}^{\varepsilon}_{N}

\displaystyle=\chi_{1}\left(\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)\right)\circledast\left(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-H^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)\right)+\varepsilon^{N+1}\widehat{R}^{\varepsilon}_{N}

\displaystyle=\left(\chi_{1}\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)\right)\circledast\left(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-\widetilde{H}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)\right)+\varepsilon^{N+1}\widehat{R}^{\varepsilon}_{N}

where the operators
\widehat{R}_{N}^{\varepsilon}
-s are rest terms that may change from one line to the other one. Their symbols all satisfy
\chi_{2}R_{N}^{\varepsilon}=0
. When quantizing the symbols, the relation (
5.3
) follows from Theorem
B.1
and Corollary
B.2
(with
\delta=1
).

(ii) We calculate for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
the quantity
(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon})\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)
. By Point (i), and because
\chi_{2}\chi_{1}=\chi_{2}
, we have in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon})\,\widehat{\chi_{2}\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}}

\displaystyle=(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon})\,\widehat{\chi_{1}\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}}\,\widehat{\chi}_{2}+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{N+1})

\displaystyle=\widehat{\chi_{1}\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}}\,(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-\widetilde{H}_{\ell}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N})\,\widehat{\chi}_{2}+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{N+1}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon})\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)=\widehat{\chi_{1}\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}}\,(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-\widetilde{H}_{\ell}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N})\,\widehat{\chi}_{2}\,{\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,\widehat{\chi_{0}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}}\,\psi_{0}^{\varepsilon}.

We now study
\widehat{\chi}_{2}\,{\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,\widehat{\chi_{0}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}}
.
We recall that

\chi_{2}
is identically equal to
1
on the compact set
\widetilde{K}_{0}
, defined in (
5.2
), with
K_{0}={\rm supp}(\chi_{0})
.
Hence, using Egorov Theorem of Appendix
C.1
and its notation, we have for
\ell\in\{1,2\}

\displaystyle\widehat{\chi}_{2}\,{\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,\widehat{\chi_{0}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}}

\displaystyle\,={\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,{\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t_{0},t)\,\widehat{\chi}_{2}\,{\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},N,\varepsilon}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,\widehat{\chi_{0}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}}

\displaystyle\,={\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,\,\widehat{\chi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}(t,t_{0})}\,\widehat{\chi_{0}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}}+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{N+1})

(5.5)

\displaystyle\,={\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,\,\widehat{\chi_{0}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}}+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{N+1}).

We are left with

\displaystyle(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon})\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\chi_{1}\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}}(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-\widetilde{H}_{\ell}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N})\,{\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,\widehat{\chi_{0}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}}\,\psi_{0}^{\varepsilon}+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{N+1})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{N+1}).

Since
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)
solves
(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon})\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)=0
, we now compare the data at
t=t_{0}
.
By Theorem
4.9
and because
\chi_{0}\chi_{2}=\chi_{0}
, we have

\widehat{\chi}_{0}={\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{2}\Pi_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}+\chi_{2}\Pi_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}\right)\widehat{\chi}_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{N+1}).

Therefore,

\psi^{\varepsilon}(t_{0})=\widehat{\chi}_{0}\psi^{\varepsilon}(t_{0})+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{N+1})=\psi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t_{0})+\psi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t_{0})+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{N+1}).

We deduce

\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)=\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}(t)+(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon})\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}(t)+{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{N+1}),

which concludes the proof.
∎

The adiabatic decoupling of Proposition
5.3
and Egorov Theorem (see Proposition
C.1
) allow to give an explicit description at any order of the solutions of equation (
1.3
) for initial data that are focalized wave packets.

5.1.1.
Application to adiabatic propagation of wave packets

We use the techniques of Appendix
C
that are classic when
\delta=1
, see for example the recent edition of
[
15
]
. One constructs
two maps
{\mathcal{R}}_{1}(t,t_{0},z)
and
{\mathcal{R}}_{2}(t,t_{0},z)
as introduced in (
1.26
) and obtains
the following result, see also Proposition
C.6
).

Theorem 5.5
.

Assume that
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
is a polarized wave packet:

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}={\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}(f_{0})\,\vec{V}_{0},\;\;\mbox{with}\;\;f_{0}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\vec{V}_{0}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}.

Let
N\geq 1
and
k\geq 0
. Then, there exists a constant
C_{N,k}>0
such that
the solution
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)
of (
1.3
) satisfies for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
,

\left\|\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)-\left(\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}(t)+\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}(t)\right)\right\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}\leq C_{N,k}\,\varepsilon^{\frac{N+1}{2}},\;

with for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
and for all
N\geq 0
,

(5.6)

\psi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{\ell}(t)}\left({\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0})]\sum_{0\leq j\leq N}\varepsilon^{j/2}\vec{B}_{\ell,j}(t)f_{0}\right),

where
\vec{B}_{\ell,j}(t)
are differential operators of degree
\leq 3j
with vector-valued time-dependent coefficients satisfying (
1.51
) and (
1.52
).

Theorem
5.5
is a simple version of Theorem
5.1
in the case where we have no crossing in the time interval
[t_{0},t_{1}]
. However, the main ideas of the proof of Theorem
5.1
are already there.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.3
, we have

\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)=\mathcal{U}_{H}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}=\sum_{\ell=1,2}{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{2}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)\right){\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{0}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t_{0})\right)\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\varepsilon^{N+1}\right).

It is thus enough to prove that for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
,
\psi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)
given by (
5.6
) satisfies

\psi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)={\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{2}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)\right){\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{0}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t_{0})\right)\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\varepsilon^{N+1}\right).

The micro-localization of wave packets implies

{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{0}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t_{0})\right)\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}={\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}(\vec{f}^{\varepsilon}_{\ell})+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\varepsilon^{N+\frac{1}{2}}\right)

with

\displaystyle\vec{f}^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}=\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}\vec{f}_{\ell,j},\;\;\vec{f}_{\ell,j}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m}),

\displaystyle\vec{f}_{\ell,0}=f_{0}\,\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\vec{f}_{\ell,1}=\nabla\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\cdot\widehat{z}\,f_{0}\,\vec{V}_{0}.

Applying Theorem
C.6
with
k=h_{\ell}
and
K_{1}=H^{\rm adia}_{\ell,1}
, we obtain that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})

\displaystyle\,{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{0}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t_{0})\right)\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}={\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}(\vec{f}^{\varepsilon}_{\ell})+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\varepsilon^{N+\frac{1}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{\ell}(t)}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0})]\vec{U}_{\ell,j}(t)\right)+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\varepsilon^{\frac{N+1}{2}}\right),

with

\displaystyle\vec{U}_{\ell,0}(t)

\displaystyle=\vec{f}_{\ell,0}=\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0}=\vec{B}_{\ell,0}f_{0},

using (
1.51
), and

\displaystyle\vec{U}_{\ell,1}(t)

\displaystyle=\vec{f}_{\ell,1}+{\bf b}_{1}(t,t_{0})\vec{f}_{\ell,0}=\nabla\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\cdot\widehat{z}\,f_{0}\,\vec{V}_{0}+{\bf b}_{1}(t,t_{0})\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0}

where
{\bf b}_{1}(t,t_{0})
is given

\displaystyle{\bf b}_{1}(t,t_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i}\sum_{|\alpha|=3}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\partial_{z}^{\alpha}h_{\ell}(s,z_{\ell}(s)){\rm op}_{1}^{w}((F_{\ell}(s,t_{0})z)^{\alpha})ds\,\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\mathcal{R}(t_{0},s)\,\partial_{z}H_{\ell,1}^{\rm adia}(s,z_{\ell}(s)\,{\rm op}_{1}^{w}(F_{\ell}(s,t_{0})z)\,\mathcal{R}(s,t_{0})\,ds.

In view of (
1.52
), we obtain

\vec{U}_{\ell,1}(t)=\vec{B}_{\ell,1}(t)f_{0},

whence

{\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,t_{0})\,{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{0}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t_{0})\right)\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}=\psi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\varepsilon^{N+\frac{1}{2}}\right)

and we conclude using again the microlocalisation of wave packets and observing that

{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{2}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)\right)\psi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)=\psi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\varepsilon^{N+\frac{1}{2}}\right).

∎

5.2.
Propagation close to the crossing area

Our goal in this section is to extend the analysis of the preceding section
up to a time
t^{\flat}-c\delta
for some
c>0
and
\delta\ll 1
.
We follow the same strategy as in the preceding section and carefully check the dependence in
\delta
of the estimates.

For proving Theorem
5.1
, we consider a wave packet at initial time
t_{0}
that is focalized along the mode
h_{1}
at some point
z_{0}
.
We let it evolve along that mode according to Theorem
5.5
, up to
(t_{1},z_{1})
conveniently chosen and we consider
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t_{1})
as a new initial data, knowing that it is a wave-packet, modulo
{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{\infty})
.
We choose a point
(t_{1},z_{1})
close enough to
(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})
such that
|t_{1}-t^{\flat}|+|z_{1}-\zeta^{\flat}|\leq\eta_{0}
where
\eta_{0}
is defined in the next Lemma.

Lemma 5.6
.

Assume
(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})
is a generic smooth crossing point as in Definition
1.3
and consider
\delta\in(0,1]
.

(1)

There exist

\eta_{0}>0
and
c_{0}>0
such that we have

|f(t,\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t,t_{1}}(z))|\geq c_{0}|t-t^{\flat}|\;\mbox{ if}\;\;|t-t^{\flat}|+|z-\zeta^{\flat}|\leq\eta_{0}.

(2)

There exists
c,M>0
such that for all
(t,z)
satisfying
\left|z-\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t,t_{1}}(z_{1})\right|\leq c\delta
, we have

|f(t,z)|\geq c_{0}\delta-Mc\delta\geq\frac{c_{0}}{2}\delta.

Proof.

The result comes readily from the transversality of the curve
t\mapsto\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t,t_{1}}(z)
to the set
\Upsilon=\{f=0\}
. Recall that this transversality is due to Point (b) of Assumption
1.3
.
∎

Thanks to Lemma
5.6
, we now work close to the point
(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})
and our goal is to prove accurate estimates for the evolution of the solution
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)
of the Schrödinger equation with the initial data
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t_{1})
,
for
t\in[t_{1},t^{\flat}-c\delta]
.

We follow the strategy of the previous section. Therefore, in order to obtain the analogue of Proposition
5.3
, we need to be able to evaluate the rest term in the Egorov Theorem in terms of
\delta
, and to construct adapted
\delta
-dependent cut-off functions. For estimating the remainders in the small parameter
\delta
, we use the symbolic calculus in the classes
{\bf S}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
, in particular
the Moyal product rules as stated in Lemma
4.3
and the Egorov Theorem of Proposition
C.1
. Finally, the construction of the cut-off functions relies on the fact that due to Point (b) of Assumption
1.3
, we can apply a straightening theorem for the Hamiltonian vector fields associated with the modes.

5.2.1.
Localization up to the crossing region

We construct the cut-off functions by using thin tubes along the classical trajectories.
We use a straightening theorem for non singular vector fields. We set
D(z_{1},\rho_{1})=\{|z-z_{1}|\leq\rho_{1}\}
and consider a branch of trajectory

{\mathcal{T}}_{1}:=\{\Phi_{h}^{t,t_{1}}(z_{1}),\;t\in[t_{1},t_{1}^{+}]\},\;\;t_{1}^{+}>t^{\flat}.

Lemma 5.7
.

[
2
]

Let be
P_{1}
a transverse hyperplane to the curve
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}
in
z_{1}
.
There exist
\rho_{1}>0
and
t_{1}^{-}<t_{1}<t^{\flat}<t_{1}^{+}
such that the map

(t,z)\mapsto\Phi^{t,t_{1}}_{h}(z)

is a diffeomorphism from

]t_{1}^{-},t_{1}^{+}[\times D(z_{1},\rho_{1})
onto a neighborhood
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
of
{\mathcal{T}}_{1}
in
P_{1}
.

Hence for any
z
in the tube
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
, we have

z=\Phi_{h}^{\tau(z),t_{1}}(Y(z))

where
\tau
and
Y
are smooth functions of
z
,
\tau(z)\in[t_{1},t_{1}^{+}]
,
Y(z)\in D(z_{1},\rho_{1})
.

We then define the cut-off functions as follows: consider

•

\zeta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(]-2,2[)
equal to 1 in
[-1,1]
,

•

\theta\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
with
\theta(t)=0
if
t\leq-1
and
\theta(t)=1
if
t\geq 1
,

we set for
\delta>0
,

\chi^{\delta}(z)=\theta\left(\frac{\tau(z)-t_{1}^{-}}{\eta}\right)\left(1-\zeta\right)\left(\frac{\tau(z)-t^{\flat}}{c\delta}\right)\zeta\left(\frac{|z-\Phi_{h}^{\tau(z),t_{1}}(z_{1})|^{2}}{(C\delta)^{2}}\right),

where
c>0,C>0
, and
\eta>0
is a small enough constant.

By choosing adapted constants
c_{j}
and
C_{j}
for
j\in\{1,2,3\}
,
conveniently,
we construct some functions

\chi^{\delta}_{j}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},[0,1]),\;\;j\in\{1,2,3\},

such that

(1)

\chi^{\delta}_{j}=1
on
\displaystyle{\bigcup_{t_{0}\leq t\leq t_{1}}B\left(\Phi_{h}^{t_{0},t}(z_{0}),c_{j}\delta\right)}

and
\chi^{\delta}_{j}
is supported in
\displaystyle{\bigcup_{t_{0}\leq t\leq t_{1}}B\left(\Phi_{h}^{t_{0},t}(z_{0}),2c_{j}\delta\right)}
,

(2)

for all
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2d}
, there exists
