C_{\gamma}
such that for all
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}

|\partial_{z}^{\gamma}\chi_{j}^{\delta}(z)|\leq C_{\gamma}\,\delta^{-|\gamma|},

(3)

\chi_{3}^{\delta}=1
on supp
\chi_{1}^{\delta}
and

\chi_{1}^{\delta}=1
on supp
\chi_{2}^{\delta}
.

Finally, with
\chi_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},[0,1])
satisfying
\chi_{0}=1

on
B(0,1)
and
\chi_{0}(z)=0
for
|z|\geq 2
, we associate

\chi^{\delta}_{0}(z)=\chi_{0}\left(\frac{z_{1}-z}{\delta}\right).

And we consider
\chi_{4}
a smooth fixed cut-off (
\delta
-independent).

5.2.2.
Adiabatic decoupling close to the gap

Omitting the mode index, we set

(5.7)

\widetilde{H}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)=\chi_{4}\left(h_{\ell}(t)+\varepsilon H_{\ell,1}^{\rm adia}(t)\right)+\chi_{3}^{\delta}\left(\sum_{2\leq j\leq N}\varepsilon^{j}H^{\rm adia}_{\ell,j}(t)\right).

Notice that, because the crossing is smooth, the eigenvalues
h_{\ell}
,
\pi_{\ell}
and the first adiabatic correctors
H_{\ell,1}^{{\rm adia}}
are smooth, even in a neighborhood of
(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat}
).

Let

{\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,s)
be the quantum propagator
associated with the Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)
.
The following result is a consequence of the sharp estimates given in
[
6
]
concerning propagation of quantum observables and proved in the Appendix, see (ii) of the Egorov Theorem
C.1
.

Proposition 5.8
.

Consider the cut-off functions
\chi_{0}^{\delta}
and
\chi_{2}^{\delta}
defined above and set for
t\in[t_{1},t^{\flat}-c\delta]

{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(\chi_{0}^{\delta,\varepsilon}(t,t_{1})):={\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t_{1},t)\,{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(\chi^{\delta}_{0})\;{\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,t_{1}).

Then, for any
M\geq 1
,
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
and
t\in[t_{1},t^{\flat}-c\delta]
, we have:

(1-\chi_{2}^{\delta})\circledast\chi_{0}^{\delta,\varepsilon,M}(t,t_{1})=\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta^{2}}\right)^{M}\zeta_{M}(t)\;\;\mbox{with}\;\;\zeta_{M}(t,\cdot)\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta^{2}}(\mathcal{D}).

Revisiting the proof of Proposition
5.3
, using Lemma
4.3
for the formal series
\Pi^{\varepsilon}_{\ell}\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta^{2}}^{0}(\mathcal{D})
and using (3) of Theorem
4.9
about
H^{\varepsilon,{\rm adia}}_{\ell}
, we obtain the following result.

Proposition 5.9

(adiabatic decoupling - II)
.

With the previous notations, we have the following properties.

(ii) For
t_{1}\leq t\leq t^{\flat}-\delta
, we have

(5.8)

\displaystyle\left(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon}(t)\right)\,{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{1}^{\delta}\,\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)\right)\,{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(\chi^{\delta}_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad={\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{1}^{\delta}\,\Pi^{\varepsilon,N}(t)\right)\left(i\varepsilon\partial_{t}-{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(\widetilde{H}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t))\right)\,{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(\chi_{2}^{\delta})+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta^{2}}\right)^{N+1}\delta^{-\kappa_{0}}\right)

where
\kappa_{0}\in{\mathbb{N}}
is
N
-independent.

(ii) Consider

\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)={\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{2}^{\delta}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)\right){\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,t_{1})\,{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{0}^{\delta}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t_{1})\right)\psi^{\varepsilon}(t_{1})

where
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t_{1})={\mathcal{U}}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t_{1},t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}(t_{0})
.
Then we have, for
N\geq 2
and for all
t\in[t_{1},t^{\flat}-\delta]
,

{\mathcal{U}}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{0})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}(t)+\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}(t)+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta^{2}}\right)^{N+1}\delta^{-\kappa_{0}}\right).

where

{\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}
is the propagator associated with the Hamiltonian
\widetilde{H}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}(t)
.

The Remark
5.4
is valid also for this Proposition.
Note that the integer
\kappa_{0}
stems from the symbolic calculus estimates of Theorem
B.1
.

5.2.3.
Proof of Theorem
5.1

The previous results have consequences for wave packets propagation and allow to prove Theorem
5.1
, which provides
an asymptotic expansion modulo
{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{\infty})
for any
\alpha<1/2
if

\delta\approx\varepsilon^{\alpha}
. In other words,
the super-adiabatic approximation is valid for times
t
such that
|t-t^{\flat}|\geq\varepsilon^{1/2-\eta}
,
for any
\eta>0
.

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

We shall use for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
that since

\Phi_{h_{\ell}}^{t_{1},t_{0}}(z_{0})=z_{\ell}(t_{1})=z_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{h_{\ell}}^{t,t_{1}}(z_{1})=\Phi_{h_{\ell}}^{t,t_{0}}(z_{0})=z_{\ell}(t)\qquad,

\displaystyle S_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1})=S_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0}),

(5.9)

\displaystyle F_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1})F_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})=F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0}).

We start from
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)=\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{1})\psi^{\varepsilon}(t_{1})
. By Theorem
5.5
,
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t_{1})=\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+\frac{1}{2}})
with

\displaystyle\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{\ell}(t_{1})}\left(\sum_{0\leq j\leq 2N}\varepsilon^{j/2}\vec{g}_{\ell,j}(t_{1})f\right),

\displaystyle\vec{g}_{\ell,j}(t_{1})={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})]\vec{B}_{\ell,j}(t_{1})f_{0},

where
\vec{B}_{\ell,0}(t_{1})
and
\vec{B}_{\ell,1}(t_{1})
are given by (
1.51
) and (
1.52
) for
t=t_{1}
.
Therefore, for
t\in[t_{1},t^{\flat}-\delta)
,

\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)=\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{1})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+\frac{1}{2}})

and
we have to analyze the propagation of the wave packet
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}
from time
t_{1}
to
t\in(t_{1},t^{\flat}-\delta)
by
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{1})
.

We follow the arguments of the proof of Theorem
5.5
. The only difference is the size of the rest terms. We deduce

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H}(t,t_{1})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1,2}{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{1}^{\delta}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)\right){\mathcal{U}}^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t,t_{1})\,{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}\left(\chi_{0}^{\delta}\Pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t_{1})\right)\psi^{\varepsilon}_{1}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta^{2}}\right)^{N+1}\delta^{-\kappa_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}(t)+\widetilde{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}(t)+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta^{2}}\right)^{N+1}\delta^{-\kappa_{0}}\right)

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1})}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{\ell}(t)}\left({\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1})]\sum_{0\leq j\leq 2N}\varepsilon^{j/2}\vec{U}_{\ell,j}(t)\right),

where

\displaystyle\vec{U}_{\ell,0}(t)=\pi_{\ell}(t_{1},z_{1})\vec{g}_{\ell,0}(t_{1}),

(5.10)

\displaystyle\vec{U}_{\ell,1}(t)=(a)+(b)+(c)+(d),

with

\displaystyle\;\;(a)=\pi_{\ell}(t_{1},z_{1})\vec{g}_{\ell,1}(t_{1}),

\displaystyle\;\;(b)=\sum_{|\alpha|=3}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\partial_{z}^{\alpha}h_{\ell}(s,z_{\ell}(s))\,{\rm op}_{1}^{w}[(F_{\ell}(s,t_{1},z_{1})z)^{\alpha}]\,ds\,\pi_{\ell}(t_{1},z_{1})\vec{g}_{\ell,0}(t_{1}),

\displaystyle\;\;(c)=\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\mathcal{R}(t_{1},s,z_{1})\,\nabla_{z}H^{\rm adia}_{\ell,1}(s,z_{s})\cdot(F_{\ell}(s,t_{1},z_{1})\widehat{z})\,\mathcal{R}(s,t_{1},z_{1})\,ds\,\pi_{\ell}(t_{1},z_{1})\vec{g}_{\ell,0}(t_{1}),

\displaystyle\;\;(d)=\widehat{z}\cdot\nabla\pi_{\ell}(t_{1},z_{1})\,\vec{g}_{\ell,0}(t_{1}),

and the
\vec{U}_{\ell,j}(t)
for
j\geq 2
are linear combinations of differential operators of degree
\leq 3k
with time dependent smooth vector-valued coefficients, applied to the
\vec{g}_{\ell,k^{\prime}}(t_{1})
, with
k+k^{\prime}=j
. Thus they can be written in the adequate form after application of the (exact) Egorov Theorem in (
1.19
).

We now study more precisely
\vec{U}_{\ell,0}(t)
. We have

\displaystyle\vec{U}_{\ell,0}(t)=\pi_{\ell}(t_{1},z_{1})\vec{g}_{\ell,0}(t_{1})

\displaystyle=\pi_{\ell}(t_{1},z_{1}){\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})]\vec{B}_{\ell,0}(t_{1})f_{0}

(5.11)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})]\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})]B_{\ell,0}(t)f_{0},

where we have used the commutation property of the matrix
{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})
stated in Lemma
1.13
, the value of
\vec{B}_{\ell,0}(t)={\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})}\vec{V}_{0}
, which is independent of
t
, and the fact that the scalar operator
{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z)]
commutes with the constant matrix
{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})
.

Let us then focus on

\vec{U}_{\ell,1}(t)
. We analyze each of the four terms in (
5.10
). The first term is is

\displaystyle(a)

\displaystyle=\pi_{\ell}(t_{1},z_{1}){\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})]\vec{B}_{\ell,1}(t_{1})f_{0}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})]

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{|\alpha|=3}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\partial_{z}^{\alpha}h_{\ell}(s,z_{\ell}(s))\,{\rm op}_{1}^{w}[(F_{\ell}(s,t_{0},z_{0})z)^{\alpha}]\,ds\,\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0}\right.

\displaystyle\;+\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mathcal{R}(t_{0},s,z_{0})\,\nabla_{z}H^{\rm adia}_{\ell,1}(s,z_{s})\cdot{\rm op}^{w}_{1}(F_{\ell}(s,t_{0},z_{0})z)\,\mathcal{R}(s,t_{0},z_{0})\,ds\,\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0}

\displaystyle\;+\left.\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\widehat{z}\cdot\nabla\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\,\vec{V}_{0}f_{0}\right),

where we have used again Lemma
1.13
and the precise form of
B_{\ell,1}(t_{1})
as given in (
1.52
). We apply
{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1})]
to
(a)
.
Using the group properties

{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1}){\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1})]{\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})]={\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})],

we obtain

(5.12)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1})](a)={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]

\displaystyle\Bigr(\sum_{|\alpha|=3}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\partial_{z}^{\alpha}h_{\ell}(s,z_{\ell}(s))\,{\rm op}_{1}^{w}[(F_{\ell}(s,t_{0},z_{0})z)^{\alpha}]\,ds\,\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\mathcal{R}(t_{0},s,z_{0})\,\nabla_{z}H^{\rm adia}_{\ell,1}(s,z_{s})\cdot{\rm op}^{w}_{1}(F_{\ell}(s,t_{0},z_{0})z)\,\mathcal{R}(s,t_{0},z_{0})\,ds\,\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0}

\displaystyle+\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\widehat{z}\cdot\nabla\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\,\vec{V}_{0}f_{0}\Bigr).

At this stage, we observe that (
5.12
) involves two integrals that might combine with
(b)
and
(c)
in (
5.10
), while the third term might match with
(d)
. We first analyze
(b)
and write

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1})](b)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1})]\sum_{|\alpha|=3}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\partial_{z}^{\alpha}h_{\ell}(s,z_{\ell}(s))\,{\rm op}_{1}^{w}[(F_{\ell}(s,t_{1},z_{1})z)^{\alpha}]\,ds\,\pi_{\ell}(t_{1},z_{1})\vec{g}_{\ell,0}(t_{1})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]\sum_{|\alpha|=3}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\partial_{z}^{\alpha}h_{\ell}(s,z_{\ell}(s))\,{\rm op}_{1}^{w}[(F_{\ell}(s,t_{0},z_{0})z)^{\alpha}]\,ds\,\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0},

where we first have used (
5.11
) and then applied the exact Egorov theorem
1.19
and the group property (
5.9
). We then observe that the first integral in (
5.12
) and the above one combine to an integral from
t_{0}
to
t
as claimed. An analogous argument also
brings the term (c) in the appropriate form, since

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1})](c)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1})]\ \frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\mathcal{R}(t_{1},s,z_{1})\,\nabla_{z}H^{\rm adia}_{\ell,1}(s,z_{s})\cdot(F_{\ell}(s,t_{1},z_{1})\widehat{z})\,\mathcal{R}(s,t_{1},z_{1})\,ds

\displaystyle\qquad{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})]\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]\ \frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{1}}^{t}\mathcal{R}(t_{0},s,z_{0})\,\nabla_{z}H^{\rm adia}_{\ell,1}(s,z_{s})\cdot(F_{\ell}(s,t_{0},z_{0})\widehat{z})\,\mathcal{R}(s,t_{0},z_{0})\,ds

\displaystyle\qquad\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0}.

We finally consider
(d)
. By the exact Egorov Theorem (
1.19
),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1})](d)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1})]\nabla\pi_{\ell}(t_{1},z_{1})\cdot\widehat{z}\,{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})]\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]\nabla\pi_{\ell}(t_{1},z_{1})\cdot(F_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})\widehat{z})\,{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0}.

Next we apply
Lemma

1.13
to pull the matrix
{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})
to the left. We differentiate
(
1.27
) and use the result for
t=t_{1}
and
z_{1}=\Phi^{t_{1},t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z_{0})
. We thus obtain that for any
\omega\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
,

\displaystyle\omega\cdot\nabla{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})+{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})\omega\cdot\nabla\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})

\displaystyle=(F_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})\omega)\cdot\nabla\pi_{\ell}\left(t_{1},z_{1}\right){\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})+\pi_{\ell}\left(t,z_{1}\right)\omega\cdot\nabla{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0}).

Using (
1.28
), we arrive at

\displaystyle(F_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})\omega)\cdot\nabla\pi_{\ell}(t_{1},z_{1})\,{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})-{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})\,\omega\cdot\nabla\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})

\displaystyle=\omega\cdot\nabla{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})-\pi_{\ell}(t_{1},z_{1})\omega\cdot\nabla{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},t_{0},z_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},s,z_{0})(F_{\ell}(s,t_{0},z_{0})\omega)\cdot\nabla H_{\ell,1}^{\rm adia}(s,\Phi^{s,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z_{0})){\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(s,t_{0},z_{1})\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\,ds

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}\pi_{\ell}(t_{1},z_{1}){\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},s,z_{0})(F_{\ell}(s,t_{0},z_{0})\omega)\cdot\nabla H_{\ell,1}^{\rm adia}(s,\Phi^{s,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z_{0})){\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(s,t_{0},z_{1})\,ds.

Therefore, after quantization,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1})](d)={\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]\,\widehat{z}\cdot\nabla\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0}

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]\left(1-\pi_{\ell}(t_{1},z_{1})\right)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{0}}^{t_{1}}{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t_{1},s,z_{0})(F_{\ell}(s,t_{0},z_{0})\omega)\cdot\nabla H_{\ell,1}^{\rm adia}(s,\Phi^{s,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z_{0})){\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(s,t_{0},z_{1})\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0}\,ds

Using that

\widehat{z}\cdot\nabla\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})+\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\widehat{z}\cdot\nabla\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})=\widehat{z}\cdot\nabla\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0}),

we see that the last term in (
5.12
) combines with the first one above to produce

{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0}){\mathcal{M}}[F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})]\,\widehat{z}\cdot\nabla\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0},

which matches the last term in the final formula (
1.52
) defining
\vec{B}_{\ell,1}(t)
.

In view of
S_{\ell}(t,t_{1},z_{1})=S_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z_{0})
, we finally obtain
\widetilde{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)=\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)
, for all
t\in[t_{0},t^{\flat}-\delta)
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Chapter 6
Propagation of wave packets through the crossing set

In this chapter, we aim at concluding the proof of Theorem
1.26
. For this, it remains to analyze the propagation of a wave packet through the smooth crossing set
\Upsilon
. We consider an incoming wave packet at time
t^{\flat}-\delta
along a trajectory intersecting
\Upsilon
at time
t^{\flat}
, and the aim is to describe the outgoing wave packet at time
t^{\flat}+\delta
.

We use the rough reduction of Section
4.2
to treat the zone around the crossing. We fix the point
(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})\in\Upsilon
and consider trajectories
z_{1}(t)
and
z_{2}(t)
reaching simultaneously at time
t^{\flat}
in the point
\zeta^{\flat}
.
We fix
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
large enough and we set, with the notations of Theorem
4.5
,

\underline{\psi}_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)=\widehat{\pi_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)}\psi^{\varepsilon}(t),\;\;\ell\in\{1,2\}.

By Theorem
4.5
, if
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the solution

\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)
of the Schrödinger equation (
1.3
) satisfies in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
,

(6.1)

\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)=\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}(t)+\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}(t)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{N+1}).

Our aim in this section is to determine
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t^{\flat}+\delta)
in terms of
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t^{\flat}-\delta)
by using the description (
6.1
) of
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)
.

The family

\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}=\,^{t}(\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{1},\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{2})

satisfies

(6.2)

i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}=\widehat{\underline{H}^{\varepsilon,N}}(t)\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}

with

\underline{H}^{\varepsilon,N}(t,z):=\begin{pmatrix}h_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}(t,z)&0\\
0&h_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}(t,z)\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0&W^{\varepsilon,N}(t,z)\\
W^{\varepsilon,N}(t,z)^{*}&0\end{pmatrix},

where

h_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}=h_{\ell}{\mathbb{I}}_{m}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\varepsilon^{j}h_{\ell,j}\quad\text{and}\quad W^{\varepsilon,N}=\sum_{j=1}^{N+1}\varepsilon^{j}W_{j}

with matrix-valued
h_{\ell,j}
and
W_{j}
for
j\geq 1
. Note that writing
W^{\varepsilon,N}
, we are making a small abuse of notation comparatively with (
4.4
).
According to Theorem
4.5
,
the Hamiltonian
\underline{H}^{\varepsilon,N}
is subquadratic (see Definition
1.1
). Thus, there exists a constant
C_{k,N}>0
such that for all
\varepsilon>0
and
t\in I
,

(6.3)

\|\widehat{W}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\Sigma^{k+1}_{\varepsilon},\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})}\leq C_{k,N}.

Here we have used that the asymptotic series
W^{\varepsilon}
starts with
\varepsilon W_{1}
, and we recall that

W_{1}=\pi_{1}H_{1}\pi_{2}+i\pi_{1}\left(\partial_{t}\pi_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\{h_{1}+h_{2},\pi_{1}\}\right)\pi_{2},

see (
1.40
). Thus, it is appropriate to write

\underline{H}^{\varepsilon,N}=H_{\rm diag}^{\varepsilon,N}+\varepsilon H_{\rm adiag}^{\varepsilon,N}

with

(6.4)

H_{\rm diag}^{\varepsilon,N}(t,z):=\begin{pmatrix}h_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}(t,z)&0\\
0&h_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}(t,z)\end{pmatrix},\qquad\varepsilon H_{\rm adiag}^{\varepsilon,N}(t,z):=\begin{pmatrix}0&W^{\varepsilon,N}(t,z)\\
W^{\varepsilon,N}(t,z)^{*}&0\end{pmatrix}

Let us summarize the information about the data that comes from the preceding section. Let
\delta>0
, for all
s\in(t^{\flat}-\delta,t^{\flat}-\frac{\delta}{2})
,

(6.5)

\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}(s)=\,^{t}\left({\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{1}(s)}(\varphi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}(s)),{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{2}(s)}(\varphi^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}(s))\right),

with for
\ell=1,2
,

\varphi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(s)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}\varphi_{j,\ell}(s),\;\;\varphi_{j,\ell}(s)\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}).

Our aim is to prove that the description of
\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}(s)
given in Equation (
6.5
) extends to
s=t^{\flat}+\delta
and to derive precise formula for
\varphi_{j,\ell}(t^{\flat}+\delta)
when
j=\{0,1\}
and
\ell\in\{1,2\}
. This entails a precise analysis on how the wave packets leave the crossing set, in particular with respect
to the exchanges between modes
\varphi_{1,1}
and
\varphi_{1,2}
at the crossing.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
k,N,M\in{\mathbb{N}}
with
M\leq N
.
We denote by
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\underline{H}}(t,s)
and
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\rm diag}(t,s)
the propagators associated with

\underline{H}^{\varepsilon,N}(t)
and
H^{\varepsilon,N}_{\rm diag}(t)
, respectively.
Then, there exists
C>0
,
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a universal constant
\kappa_{0}
, and an operator
\Theta^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{M}
such that
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\delta>0
with
\delta\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}
,

\Theta^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{M}=\mathbb{I}+\sum_{1\leq m\leq M}\,\Theta^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{m,M}

and

(6.6)

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\underline{H}}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat}-\delta)-\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\rm diag}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat})\;\Theta^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{M}\;\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\rm diag}(t^{\flat},t^{\flat}-\delta)\right\|_{\mathcal{L}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon}^{k+\ell},\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\leq C\left(\delta^{M+1}+(\varepsilon\delta^{-2})^{\frac{M+1}{2}}\delta^{-2\kappa_{0}-k}\right).

Moreover, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,
and a family of operators
(\mathcal{T}_{m,M}^{\varepsilon,\delta})_{m\geq 1}
and constants
c_{k,m,M}>0

such that for all
\vec{\varphi}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{2})
,
m\geq 1
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
,

(6.7)

\Theta^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{m,M}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}(\vec{\varphi})={\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}\left(\mathcal{T}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{m,M}\vec{\varphi}+\varepsilon^{\frac{M+1}{2}}\vec{r^{\varepsilon}}_{m,M}\right),

with
\vec{r^{\varepsilon}}_{m,M}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
and

(6.8)

\|\mathcal{T}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{m,M}\vec{\varphi}\|_{\Sigma^{k}}\leq c_{k,m,M}\,\varepsilon^{\frac{m}{2}}|\log\varepsilon|^{\max(0,m-1)}\,\|\vec{\varphi}\|_{\Sigma^{k+2m+1}}.

Besides, the operator
\mathcal{T}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1,M}
maps
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
into itself and

(6.9)

\mathcal{T}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1,M}=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\begin{pmatrix}0&W_{1}(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})\mathcal{T}^{\flat}_{2\rightarrow 1}\\
W_{1}(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})^{*}\mathcal{T}^{\flat}_{1\rightarrow 2}&0\end{pmatrix}+\mathcal{O}(\delta\sqrt{\varepsilon}),

where the transfer operators
\mathcal{T}^{\flat}_{1\rightarrow 2}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\flat}_{2\rightarrow 1}
are defined according to (
1.54
).

A few remarks are in order. First, we
point out that some additional action effects will appear when applying
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\underline{H}}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat}-\delta)
to a wave packet via the operators
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\rm diag}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat})

and
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\rm diag}(t^{\flat},t^{\flat}-\delta)
. When applied to a Gaussian wave packets, i.e. when
\varphi_{\ell,1}=g^{\Gamma}_{\ell}
in (
6.5
), the leading order correction term at time
t^{\flat}+\delta
due to the crossing is

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}\begin{pmatrix}{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{1}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})+\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{2}(t^{\flat},t_{0},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{1}(t^{\flat}+\delta)}(\varphi_{1})\\
{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{2}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})+\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{1}(t^{\flat},t_{0},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{2}(t^{\flat}+\delta)}(\varphi_{2})\end{pmatrix}

with

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}[F_{1}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})]W_{1}(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})\mathcal{T}^{\flat}_{2\rightarrow 1}\mathcal{M}[F_{2}(t^{\flat},t_{0},z_{0})]g^{\Gamma}_{2},

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}[F_{2}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})]W_{1}(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})^{*}\mathcal{T}^{\flat}_{1\rightarrow 2}\mathcal{M}[F_{1}(t^{\flat},t_{0},z_{0})]g^{\Gamma}_{1}.

Recall that
W_{1}
is the off-diagonal matrix described in (
1.40
).

The remainder of this section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
6.1
. In Section
6.1
, we use Dyson series to construct the interaction operators
\Theta^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{m,M}
, obtain the decomposition (
6.6
). We prove that their action on wave packets obey to (
6.7
). Then, in Section
6.2
, we study the operators
\mathcal{T}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{m,M}
, proving the estimate (
6.8
) and calculating the quantity
\mathcal{T}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1,M}
, as in (
6.9
) (which was already done in
[
23
]
).

6.1.
Construction of the interaction operators

6.1.1.
Dyson expansions

We perform a Dyson expansion via the Duhamel formula and uses (
6.4
). A first use of Duhamel formula gives for
t_{1},t_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

(6.10)

\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\underline{H}}(t_{2},t_{1})=\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(t_{2},t_{1})+\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\underline{H}}(t_{2},s_{1})\widehat{H^{\varepsilon,N}_{\rm adiag}}(s_{1})\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(s_{1},t_{1})ds_{1}.

With one iteration of the Duhamel formula, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\underline{H}}(t_{2},t_{1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(t_{2},t_{1})+\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(t_{2},s_{1})\widehat{H^{\varepsilon,N}_{\rm adiag}}(s_{1})\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(s_{1},t_{1})ds_{1}

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{s_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\underline{H}}(t_{2},s_{2})\widehat{H^{\varepsilon,N}_{\rm adiag}}(s_{2})\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(s_{2},s_{1})\widehat{H^{\varepsilon,N}_{\rm adiag}}(s_{1})\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(s_{1},t_{1})ds_{2}ds_{1}.

With two iterations, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\underline{H}}(t_{2},t_{1})=\;

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(t_{2},t_{1})+\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(t_{2},s_{1})\widehat{H^{\varepsilon,N}_{\rm adiag}}(s_{1})\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(s_{1},t_{1})ds_{1}

\displaystyle\;-\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{s_{1}}^{t_{2}}\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\rm diag}(t_{2},s_{2})\widehat{H^{\varepsilon,N}_{\rm adiag}}(s_{2})\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(s_{2},s_{1})\widehat{H^{\varepsilon,N}_{\rm adiag}}(s_{1})\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(s_{1},t_{1})ds_{1}ds_{2}

\displaystyle\;-\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{s_{1}}^{t_{2}}\int_{s_{2}}^{t_{2}}\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\underline{H}}(t_{2},s_{3})\widehat{H^{\varepsilon,N}_{\rm adiag}}(s_{3})\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\rm diag}(s_{3},s_{2})\widehat{H^{\varepsilon,N}_{\rm adiag}}(s_{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(s_{2},s_{1})\widehat{H^{\varepsilon,N}_{\rm adiag}}(s_{1})\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(s_{1},t_{1})ds_{3}ds_{2}ds_{1}

and the last term of the right hand side contains three integrations in time, which implies that any of its (adequate) operator norms is of size
|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{3}
.

Our aim is to transform
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\underline{H}}
for
t_{1}=t^{\flat}-\delta
,
t_{2}=t^{\flat}+\delta
by performing
M
iterations. For simplifying the notation, we set

(6.11)

E(s)=\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(t^{\flat},s)\widehat{H^{\varepsilon}_{\rm adiag}}(s)\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(s,t^{\flat}).

In doing that, we choose the time
t^{\flat}
as a reference time (an initial time in terms of Cauchy data) for the evolution operators. This choice will be present in all the arguments of the proof.
In that manner, after
M
iterations,
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have the Dyson formula

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\underline{H}}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat}-\delta)=\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat})\Bigl(\mathbb{I}\,+

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq m\leq M}(i)^{-m}\int_{t^{\flat}-\delta}^{t^{\flat}+\delta}ds_{1}\int_{s_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+\delta}ds_{2}\cdots\int_{s_{m-1}}^{t^{\flat}+\delta}ds_{m}E(s_{m})E(s_{m-1})\cdots E(s_{1})\Bigr)\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(t^{\flat},t^{\flat}-\delta)

\displaystyle\qquad+R_{M}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(t^{\flat})

and
R^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{M}(t^{\flat})

satisfies

\|R^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{M}(t^{\flat})\|_{\mathcal{L}(\Sigma_{\varepsilon}^{k+M+1},\Sigma^{k})}\leq C\delta^{M+1}.

This gives

(6.12)

\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\underline{H}}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat}-\delta)=\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat})\;\widetilde{\Theta}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{M}\;\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{\rm diag}}(t^{\flat},t^{\flat}-\delta)+R^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{M}(t^{\flat})

with

\widetilde{\Theta}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{M}=\mathbb{I}+\sum_{m=1}^{M}\widetilde{\Theta}_{m}(t^{\flat}+\delta)

where the family
\widetilde{\Theta}_{m}(s)
is given for
s\in[t^{\flat}-\delta,t^{\flat}+\delta]
,
m\geq 1
by

(6.13)

\widetilde{\Theta}_{m}(s)=(i)^{-m}\int_{t^{\flat}-\delta}^{s}ds_{1}\int_{s_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+\delta}ds_{2}\cdots\int_{s_{m-1}}^{t^{\flat}+\delta}ds_{m}E(s_{m})E(s_{m-1})\cdots E(s_{1})

The family of operators
\widetilde{\Theta}_{m}(s)
contains all the information about the interactions between the modes
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
modulo
\mathcal{O}(\delta^{\infty})
when
M
goes to
+\infty
. We prove in the next section that by adequate linear combination of the

\widetilde{\Theta}_{m}(t^{\flat}+\delta)
, one can find operators
\Theta_{m,M}^{\varepsilon,\delta}
satisfying (
6.6
) with (
6.7
), (
6.8
) and (
6.9
).

6.1.2.
Action of the interaction operator on wave packets

We focus now in understanding the action of
\widetilde{\Theta}^{\varepsilon}_{m}(s)
on wave packets of the form

{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}(\vec{\varphi})=\begin{pmatrix}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}(\varphi_{1})\\
{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}(\varphi_{2})\end{pmatrix}.

This will allow to define the operators
\Theta^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{m,M}(s)
with the expected properties.

Let us start by analyzing the function
E(s)
involved in the definition of
\Theta_{m}(s)
and introduced in (
6.11
).
According to (
6.4
), we have

E(s)=\begin{pmatrix}0&{I}(s)\\
{I}^{*}(s)&0\end{pmatrix},\;\;s\in[t^{\flat}-\delta,t^{\flat}+\delta]

with

(6.14)

{I}(s):=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}}(t^{\flat},s)W^{\varepsilon,N}(s)\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}}(s,t^{\flat}).

Note that
I(s)
, as
E(s)
, depends on
\varepsilon
,
\delta
and
N
.

The operator
I(s)
combines conjugation of the pseudo-differential operator
\chi_{\delta}(s)W^{\varepsilon,N}(s)
by the propagator
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}}(s,t^{\flat})
and composition by two different propagators
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}}(t^{\flat},s)
and
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}}(t^{\flat},s)
.
Indeed, we can write,

{I}(s)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\left(\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}}(t^{\flat},s)W^{\varepsilon,N}(s)\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}}(s,t^{\flat})\right)\circ\left(\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}}(t^{\flat},s)\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}}(s,t^{\flat})\right).

Similarly, for
{I}^{*}(s)
, one writes

(6.15)

{I}^{*}(s)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\left(\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}}(t^{\flat},s)W^{\varepsilon,N}(s)^{*}\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}}(s,t^{\flat})\right)\circ\left(\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}}(t^{\flat},s)\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}}(s,t^{\flat})\right).

Both operations are well understood:

(1)

First,
the conjugation of a pseudo-differential operator by a propagator, that is generated by a subquadratic, matrix-valued Hamiltonian with scalar principal symbol, is described by the Egorov Theorem of Appendix
C
(see Proposition
C.1
). Since
\widehat{W^{\varepsilon,N}(s)}
has a vanishing principal symbol,
we have an asymptotic expansion of the form

\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}}(t^{\flat},s)\widehat{W^{\varepsilon,N}(s)}\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{\ell}^{\varepsilon,N}}(s,t^{\flat})=\varepsilon\,\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\widehat{\underline{W}_{j,\ell}(s)},\qquad\ell\in\{1,2\}.

(2)

Second,
the action on wave packets of two different propagators acting one backwards and the other one forwards has been studied in
[
23
]
[Section 5.2]. The
precise computation of the asymptotic behavior of the action of the operator
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}}(t^{\flat},s)\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}}(s,t^{\flat})
on wave packets is detailed in Appendix
C.4
. This involves the canonical transformation of the phase space
z\mapsto\Phi_{1}^{t^{\flat},s}\circ\Phi_{2}^{s,t^{\flat}}(z)
) (see Lemma
C.7
).

As a consequence,
one obtains the
analogue of Lemma 5.3 of
[
23
]
, which writes in our context as follows.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Using notation (
1.36
),

-

A smooth real-valued map
s\mapsto\Lambda(s)
with

\Lambda(0)=0
,
\dot{\Lambda}(0)=0
, and satisfying the relation

\frac{1}{2}(\ddot{\Lambda}(0)-\dot{p}(0)\cdot\dot{q}(0))=-\mu^{\flat},

-

A smooth vector-valued map
s\mapsto z(s)=(q(s),p(s))
with

z(0)=0
,
\dot{z}(0)=(\alpha^{\flat},\beta^{\flat})
,

-

A smooth map
s\mapsto{Q}^{\varepsilon}(s)
of pseudo-differential operators,
that maps Schwartz functions to Schwartz functions,

such that for all
s\in[t^{\flat}-\delta,t^{\flat}+\delta]
and all
\vec{\varphi}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
,

{I}^{*}(s){\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}(\vec{\varphi})(y)={\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}\left(Q^{\varepsilon}(s)\vec{\varphi}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left((\varepsilon\delta^{-2})^{\frac{M+1}{2}}\delta^{-2\kappa_{0}-k}\right).

Moreover,
Q^{\varepsilon}(s)=\sum_{j=0}^{M}\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}Q^{\varepsilon}_{j}(s)
,
and, with the scaling notation
z_{\varepsilon}(s)=z(s)/\sqrt{\varepsilon}:=(q_{\varepsilon}(s),p_{\varepsilon}(s))
, we have

(6.16)

\displaystyle Q^{\varepsilon}_{j}(s)\vec{\varphi}(y)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\Lambda(s-t^{\flat})}{Q}_{j}(s-t^{\flat}){\rm e}^{ip_{\varepsilon}(s-t^{\flat})\cdot(y-q_{\varepsilon}(s-t^{\flat}))}\vec{\varphi}(y-q_{\varepsilon}(s-t^{\flat})),

(6.17)

\displaystyle Q_{0}(0)=W_{1}(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})^{*},

where the
\varepsilon
-independent operators
Q_{j}(s)
map
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
into itself, uniformly in
s\in[t^{\flat}-\delta,t^{\flat}+\delta]
.

Proof.

We start from (
6.15
). We first use the Egorov theorem of Proposition
C.1
(see also point (1) above). In
\mathcal{L}(\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})
, we have

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}}(t^{\flat},s)\widehat{W^{\varepsilon,N}(s)}^{*}\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}}(s,t^{\flat})=\sum_{j=0}^{M}\varepsilon^{j}\widehat{\underline{W}_{j,2}(s)}^{*}+\mathcal{O}((\varepsilon\delta^{-2})^{M+1}\delta^{-2\kappa_{0}-k}),

with
\underline{W}_{1,2}(0)=W_{1}(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})
.
Moreover, by Lemma
C.7
and with its notation, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{2}^{\varepsilon,N}}(t^{\flat},s)\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{h_{1}^{\varepsilon,N}}(s,t^{\flat})\right){\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}(\vec{\varphi})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{M}\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}\left({\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\widetilde{\Lambda}(s)}{\rm e}^{i\widetilde{p}_{\varepsilon}(s)\cdot(y-\widetilde{q}_{\varepsilon}(s))}\vec{\varphi}_{j}(s,y-\widetilde{q}_{\varepsilon}(s))\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathcal{O}((\varepsilon\delta^{-2})^{\frac{M+1}{2}}\delta^{-k-2\kappa_{0}}).

Injecting these relations in (
6.15
), we obtain

\displaystyle I^{*}(s){\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}(\vec{\varphi})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j_{1}=0}^{M}\sum_{j_{2}=0}^{M}\varepsilon^{j_{1}}\varepsilon^{\frac{j_{2}}{2}}\widehat{\underline{W}_{j_{1},2}(s)}^{*}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}\left({\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\widetilde{\Lambda}(s)}{\rm e}^{i\widetilde{p}_{\varepsilon}(s)\cdot(y-\widetilde{q}_{\varepsilon}(s))}\vec{\varphi}_{j_{2}}(s,y-\widetilde{q}_{\varepsilon}(s))\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathcal{O}((\varepsilon\delta^{-2})^{M+1}\delta^{-k-2\kappa_{0}})

in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
.
We observe

\displaystyle\widehat{\underline{W}_{j_{1},2}(s)}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}\left({\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\widetilde{\Lambda}(s)}{\rm e}^{i\widetilde{p}_{\varepsilon}(s)\cdot(y-\widetilde{q}_{\varepsilon}(s))}\vec{\varphi}_{j_{2}}(s,y-\widetilde{q}_{\varepsilon}(s))\right)

\displaystyle\qquad={\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}\left({\rm op}_{1}\left(\underline{W}_{j_{1},2}(s,\zeta^{\flat}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}z)^{*}\right){\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\widetilde{\Lambda}(s)}{\rm e}^{i\widetilde{p}_{\varepsilon}(s)\cdot(y-\widetilde{q}_{\varepsilon}(s))}\vec{\varphi}_{j_{2}}(s,y-\widetilde{q}_{\varepsilon}(s))\right)

\displaystyle\qquad={\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}\left({\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\widetilde{\Lambda}(s)}{\rm e}^{i\widetilde{p}_{\varepsilon}(s)\cdot(y-\widetilde{q}_{\varepsilon}(s))}\underline{\vec{\varphi}}_{j_{1},j_{2}}(s,y-\widetilde{q}_{\varepsilon}(s))\right)

with
\varphi_{j_{1},j_{2}}(s)={\rm op}_{1}\left(\underline{W}_{j_{1},2}(s,\tilde{z}_{\varepsilon}(s)+\zeta^{\flat}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}z)^{*}\right)\vec{\varphi}_{j_{2}}
.
Reorganizing the terms and
Taylor expanding in
z
the symbol of the preceding pseudodifferential operator, we obtain the asymptotics with the terms
Q^{\varepsilon}_{j}(s)
, as in (
6.16
).
We conclude the proof with the relation

\varphi_{1,0}(0,y)={\rm op}_{1}\left(\underline{W}_{1,2}(0,\zeta^{\flat}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}z)^{*}\right)\vec{\varphi}_{0}(0)=W_{1}(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})^{*}\vec{\varphi},

whence (
6.17
)
∎

We now go back to the action of the operators
\widetilde{\Theta}_{m}(s)
(see (
6.13
)) on wave packets. By Lemma
6.2
,
for all
\vec{\varphi}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
, we have in the Schwartz class,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Theta}_{1}(s){\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}(\vec{\varphi})

\displaystyle=-i\sum_{0\leq j\leq M}\varepsilon^{j-1}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}\left(\int_{t^{\flat}-\delta}^{s}\begin{pmatrix}0&Q_{j}^{\varepsilon}(s_{1})^{*}\\
Q_{j}^{\varepsilon}(s_{1})&0\end{pmatrix}\vec{\varphi}ds_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathcal{O}((\varepsilon\delta^{-2})^{\frac{M+1}{2}}\delta^{-2\kappa_{0}-k}).

More generally, after reorganization of the terms, we can write for all
M\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
and
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
,

(6.18)

\sum_{1\leq m\leq M}\widetilde{\Theta}_{m}(s)=\sum_{1\leq m\leq M}\Theta_{m,M}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(s),\qquad s\in[t^{\flat}-\delta,t^{\flat}+\delta]

with

(6.19)

\displaystyle\Theta_{m^{\prime},M}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(s){\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}(\vec{\varphi})=\sum_{m=1}^{M}\;\sum_{j_{1}+\cdots+j_{m}+m=m^{\prime}}\varepsilon^{\frac{j_{1}+\cdots+j_{m}}{2}}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}\,({\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{j_{1},\cdots,j_{m}}\vec{\varphi}),

(6.20)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{j_{1},\cdots,j_{m}}(s)\vec{\varphi}=(-i)^{m}\int_{t^{\flat}-\delta}^{s}ds_{1}\int_{s_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+\delta}ds_{2}\cdots\int_{s_{m-1}}^{t^{\flat}+\delta}ds_{m}T_{j_{m}}(s_{m})\cdots T_{j_{2}}(s_{2})T_{j_{1}}(s_{1}),

and

\displaystyle T_{j}(s)=\begin{pmatrix}0&Q_{j}^{\varepsilon}(s)^{*}\\
Q_{j}^{\varepsilon}(s)&0\end{pmatrix}.

Note that when
m^{\prime}=1
, there is only one term in the sum of (
6.19
), corresponding to
m=1
and
j_{1}=0
.
The reorganization of the terms is made in view of the claim

(6.21)

\|{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{j_{1},\cdots,j_{m}}(s)\vec{\varphi}\|_{\Sigma^{k}}\leq C_{j_{1},\cdots,j_{m},\varphi}\,\varepsilon^{\frac{m}{2}}\,|\log\varepsilon|^{\max(0,m-1)},\;\;\forall\vec{\phi}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m}).

Then, we define the transfer operators of (
6.7
) by setting

(6.22)

\mathcal{T}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{m^{\prime},M}=\sum_{m=1}^{M}\;\sum_{j_{1}+\cdots+j_{m}+m=m^{\prime}}{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{j_{1},\cdots,j_{m}},\;\;1\leq m^{\prime}\leq M,

and (
6.21
) implies
that (
6.8
) and (
6.9
) hold.
Proving (
6.21
) (and thus (
6.8
) and (
6.9
)) is the subject of the next section.

6.2.
The transfer operators

In this section, we analyze the operators
{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{j_{1},\cdots,j_{m}}(s)
introduced in (
6.20
). The key argument is the next Lemma.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let us fix
M\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

(1)

For all
1\leq j\leq M
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists
c_{k,j}>0
and
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all time-dependent Schwartz function
\vec{\varphi}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m}))
,
s\in[t^{\flat}-\delta,t^{\flat}+\delta]
,
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\int_{t^{\flat}-\delta}^{s}T_{j}(s^{\prime})\vec{\varphi}(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime}\Bigr\|_{\Sigma^{k}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq c_{k,j}\sqrt{\varepsilon}\Bigl(\sup_{s^{\prime}\in[t^{\flat}-\delta,t^{\flat}+\delta]}\|\vec{\varphi}(s^{\prime})\|_{\Sigma^{k+\ell+1}}+|\log|\varepsilon||\sup_{s^{\prime}\in[t^{\flat}-\delta,t^{\flat}+\delta]}\|\partial_{s}\vec{\varphi}(s^{\prime})\|_{\Sigma^{k+\ell}}\Bigr).

(2)

There exists an operator

\tilde{T}_{1}^{\varepsilon,\delta}
such that for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there are constants
C_{k}
,
c_{1,k}
and
\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that for all
\vec{\varphi}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
,

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{t^{\flat}-\delta}^{s}T_{1}(s^{\prime})\vec{\varphi}ds^{\prime}-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\begin{pmatrix}0&W_{1}(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})\mathcal{T}^{\flat}_{2\rightarrow 1}\\
W_{1}(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})^{*}\mathcal{T}^{\flat}_{1\rightarrow 2}&0\end{pmatrix}\vec{\varphi}-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\tilde{T}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}\vec{\varphi}\right\|_{\Sigma^{k}}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C_{k}\sqrt{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{M+1}\|\vec{\varphi}\|_{\Sigma^{k+\ell}}.

where
\mathcal{T}^{\flat}_{1\rightarrow 2}
is the transfer operator defined in (
1.54
), and with

\|\tilde{T}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}\vec{\varphi}\|_{\Sigma^{k}}\leq c_{1,k}\,\delta\,\|\vec{\varphi}\|_{\Sigma^{k+\ell}}.

Proof of
the claim (
6.21
), and
equations (
6.8
) and (
6.9
).

Note that in view of

{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{j_{1}}(s)\,\vec{\varphi}=-i\int_{t^{\flat}-\delta}^{s}T_{j}(s^{\prime})\vec{\varphi}(s^{\prime})ds^{\prime},

Lemma
6.3
(2) applies directly to the first transfer operator.
Besides, for
\vec{\varphi}
independent on
s
, we have

\|{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{j_{1}}(s)\,\vec{\varphi}\|_{\Sigma^{k}}\leq c_{k,0}\,\sqrt{\varepsilon}\|\vec{\varphi}\|_{\Sigma^{k+\ell(M,k)}}.

We deduce that (
6.21
) holds for
m=1
.
We can now conclude the proof of the claim for all
m\geq 1
by recursion. We observe

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{j_{1},\cdots,j_{m}}(s)\vec{\varphi}

\displaystyle=-i\int_{t^{\flat}-\delta}^{s}T_{j}(s_{1})\vec{\varphi}(s_{1})ds_{1}

with

\displaystyle\vec{\varphi}(s)={\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{j_{2},\cdots,j_{m}}(t^{\flat}+\delta)\vec{\varphi}-{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{j_{2},\cdots,j_{m}}(s)\vec{\varphi},

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\vec{\varphi}(s)=-i{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{j_{3},\cdots,j_{m}}(t^{\flat}+\delta)\vec{\varphi}-{\mathcal{T}}^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{j_{3},\cdots,j_{m}}(s)\vec{\varphi},

and Lemma
6.3
(1) allows to conclude the recursion argument and to obtain (
6.21
).
∎

The proof of Lemma
6.3
relies on the analysis of the (scalar) integrals defined for
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\vec{\varphi}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}},S({\mathbb{R}}^{d}))
scalar-valued by

(6.23)

I_{\varphi(.)}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(s):=\int_{t^{\flat}-\delta}^{s}{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\Lambda(s^{\prime}-t^{\flat})}{Q}_{j}(s^{\prime}-t^{\flat}){\rm e}^{ip_{\varepsilon}(s^{\prime}-t^{\flat})\cdot(y-q_{\varepsilon}(s^{\prime}-t^{\flat}))}\vec{\varphi}(s^{\prime},y-q_{\varepsilon}(s^{\prime}-t^{\flat}))ds^{\prime}.

We will us this preparatory Lemma that simplifies the general form of the terms
I^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{\varphi(\cdot)}(s)
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Set

L=\beta^{\flat}\cdot y-\alpha^{\flat}\cdot D_{y}
.
Then, in Schwartz space,

I_{\varphi(.)}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(s)=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\int_{-\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}^{(s-t^{\flat})/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\rm e}^{i\phi^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}{Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{iL\sigma}\vec{\varphi}(t^{\flat}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma)d\sigma+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\delta)

with

\displaystyle\phi^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Lambda(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma)-\frac{1}{2}p_{\varepsilon}(\sigma)q_{\varepsilon}(\sigma).

In the following, we will use that by Lemma
6.2
,

\displaystyle\phi^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\ddot{\Lambda}(0)-\dot{p}(0)\dot{q}(0))\sigma^{2}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma^{3}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{1}(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma)

\displaystyle=-\mu^{\flat}\sigma^{2}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma^{3}\phi^{\varepsilon}_{1}(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma)

where
\sigma\mapsto\phi^{\varepsilon}_{1}(\sigma)
is a smooth family of operators bounded in the Schwartz class and with bounded derivatives on any compact of
{\mathbb{R}}
.
In particular, we have on one hand,

\left|\frac{d}{d\sigma}\phi^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)\right|\geq c_{0}|\sigma|,

for some constant
c_{0}>0
; and on the other hand, we have

{\rm e}^{i\phi^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}=\frac{1}{\sigma}b^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)\partial_{\sigma}{\rm e}^{i\phi^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}\;\;\mbox{with}\;\;b^{\varepsilon}(\sigma):=\frac{\sigma}{i\partial_{\sigma}\phi^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}\;\;\mbox{and}\ \left|\frac{d}{d\sigma}b^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)\right|\leq\frac{c_{1}}{|\sigma|}

for some constant
c_{1}>0
as above.
Finally, we will also take advantage of the fact that the operator
{\rm e}^{i\sigma L}
maps
\Sigma^{k}
into itself continuously for all
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Let us now prove Lemma
6.4
.

Proof.

We first transform the integral by the change of variables
s^{\prime}=t^{\flat}+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}
:

\displaystyle I_{\varphi(.)}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(s)=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\varepsilon}\int_{-\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}^{(s-t^{\flat})/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\Lambda(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})}Q_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{ip_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})\cdot(y-q_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}))}\vec{\varphi}(t^{\flat}+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon},y-q_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}))d\sigma.

One sees that the change of variable has exhibited a power of
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. However, even though the integrand is bounded, the size of the support of the integral is large: it is of size
2\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}
, which spoils that gain of
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. This integral will turn out to be smaller because of the oscillations of the phase outside
s=0
. The proof then consists in integrations by parts. Before entering into the proof in the next sections, we make some preparatory work.

We start by studying the phase present in the integral close to
\sigma=0
.
Following
[
23
]
,
we set

L^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)=p_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})\cdot y-q_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})\cdot D_{y},

and we observe

{\rm e}^{ip_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})\cdot(y-q_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}))}\vec{\varphi}(t^{\flat}+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon},y-q_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}))={\rm e}^{-\frac{i}{2}p_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})\cdot q_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{\rm e}^{iL^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}\vec{\varphi}.

Therefore, we have

{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\Lambda(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{\rm e}^{ip_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})\cdot(y-q_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}))}\vec{\varphi}(t^{\flat}+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon},y-q_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}))={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\Lambda(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})-\frac{i}{2}p_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})\cdot q_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{\rm e}^{iL^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}\vec{\varphi}.

Using Taylor expansion in
\sigma=0
, we obtain

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\Lambda(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})-\frac{1}{2}q_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})\cdot p_{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})=\mu^{\flat}\sigma^{2}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}s^{3}f_{1}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})

with
s\mapsto f_{1}(s)
bounded, together with its derivatives, for
s\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
. In the following, the notation
f_{j}
will denote functions that have the same property.
Then, with

L=\beta^{\flat}\cdot y-\alpha^{\flat}\cdot D_{y}
,
we have

L^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)=\sigma L+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma^{2}L^{\varepsilon}_{1}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}).

The family of operator
s\mapsto L^{\varepsilon}_{1}(s)
maps
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
into itself, for
s\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
.
Moreover,
the commutator
[L,L_{1}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})]
is a scalar so that we have

\frac{1}{2}[L,L_{1}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})]=f_{2}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})

with the notation we have just introduced.
Therefore, by Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula

{\rm e}^{iL^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}={\rm e}^{i\sigma L}{\rm e}^{i\sigma^{2}\sqrt{\varepsilon}L_{1}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{\rm e}^{i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma^{3}f_{2}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})}.

We will also use

{\rm e}^{i\sqrt{\varepsilon}s^{2}L_{1}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})}={\rm Id}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma^{2}\Theta(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})

where the operator-valued map
s\mapsto\Theta(s)
is smooth and such that for all
\sigma\in[t_{0},t_{0}+T]
, the operator
\Theta(s)
and its derivatives maps
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
into itself.
Setting
f_{3}=f_{1}+f_{2}
, we deduce that
I^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{\varphi(\cdot)}(s)
writes

\displaystyle I^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{\varphi(\cdot)}(s)=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\int_{-\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}^{(s-t^{\flat})/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\rm e}^{i\mu^{\flat}\sigma^{2}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma^{3}f_{3}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{isL}ds+R^{\varepsilon,\delta}(s)

with

\displaystyle R^{\varepsilon,\delta}(s)=\varepsilon\int_{-\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}^{(s-t^{\flat})/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\rm e}^{i\mu^{\flat}\sigma^{2}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma^{3}f_{3}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{i\sigma L}\sigma^{2}\Theta^{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})d\sigma.

Let us analyze
R^{\varepsilon,\delta}(s)
. For this, we perform an integration by parts. Indeed,

\partial_{\sigma}(\mu^{\flat}\sigma^{2}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma^{3}f_{3}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}))=2\mu^{\flat}\sigma(1+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}f_{4}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}))

for some smooth bounded function
f_{4}
with bounded derivatives. Moreover, since
\delta
is small,
we have
1+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}f_{4}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})>1/2

for all
\sigma\in]-\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon},+\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon}[
.
Therefore, we can write

\displaystyle R^{\varepsilon,\delta}(s)=

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\varepsilon\sigma}{2i\mu^{\flat}(1+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}f_{4}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}))}{\rm e}^{i\mu^{\flat}\sigma^{2}+i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma^{3}f_{3}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{i\sigma L}\right]_{-\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}^{\frac{s-t^{\flat}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}

\displaystyle\qquad-\frac{\varepsilon}{2i\mu^{\flat}}\int_{-\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}^{\frac{s-t^{\flat}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}{\rm e}^{i\mu^{\flat}\sigma^{2}+i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma^{3}f_{3}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})}\frac{d}{d\sigma}\left(\frac{\sigma}{1+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}f_{4}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{i\sigma L}\right)d\sigma.

We deduce that for all
\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, we have in the Schwartz class

R^{\varepsilon,\delta}(s)\varphi=O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\delta)+R^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}(s)\varphi

with

R^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}\varphi(s)=-\frac{\varepsilon}{2i\mu^{\flat}}\int_{-\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}^{\frac{s-t^{\flat}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}{\rm e}^{i\mu^{\flat}\sigma^{2}+i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma^{3}f_{3}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})}\left(\frac{\sigma}{1+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}f_{4}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})}{Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{i\sigma L}L\varphi\right)d\sigma.

We then need another integration by parts to obtain that in Schwartz class

R^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}(s)\varphi=O(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\delta)
. Note that this additional integration by parts is required by the presence of a
\sigma
without a coefficient
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
in the integrand. We write

\displaystyle R^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{1}(s)\varphi

\displaystyle=-\frac{\varepsilon}{(2i\mu^{\flat})^{2}}\left[{\rm e}^{i\mu^{\flat}\sigma^{2}+i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma^{3}f_{3}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})}\left(\frac{1}{(1+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}f_{4}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}))^{2}}{Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{i\sigma L}L\varphi\right)\right]_{-\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}^{\frac{s-t^{\flat}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;+\frac{\varepsilon}{(2i\mu^{\flat})^{2}}\int_{-\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}^{\frac{s-t^{\flat}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}{\rm e}^{i\mu^{\flat}\sigma^{2}+i\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma^{3}f_{3}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})}\frac{d}{d\sigma}\left(\frac{1}{(1+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}f_{4}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}))^{2}}{\mathcal{Q}}^{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{i\sigma L}L\varphi\right)d\sigma

\displaystyle=O(\delta\sqrt{\varepsilon})

This terminates the proof of Lemma
6.4
.
∎

We devote the next three subsections to the proof of each part of Lemma
6.3
, taking advantage of Lemma
6.4
and focusing on the main part of
I^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{\varphi(\cdot)}(s)
exhibited in this statement.

6.2.1.
Estimates on the transfer operators

Proof of Lemma
6.3
(1).

Because the phase
\phi^{\varepsilon}
is oscillating far away from
0
, we
use a smooth cut-off function
\chi
identically equal to
1
close to
0
and to
1
outside a ball of radius
1
, and we write in Schwartz space

I_{\varphi(.)}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(s)=I^{1}(s)+I^{2}(s)+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\delta)

with

\displaystyle I^{1}(s)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\int_{-\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}^{(s-t^{\flat})/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\rm e}^{i\phi^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}\chi(\sigma){Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{iL\sigma}\phi(t^{\flat}+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})d\sigma,

\displaystyle I^{2}(s)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\int_{-\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}^{(s-t^{\flat})/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\rm e}^{i\phi^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}(1-\chi)(\sigma){Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{iL\sigma}\phi(t^{\flat}+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})d\sigma.

Of course, we have also used Lemma
6.4
.

The compactly supported term
.
The term
I^{1}
is the easiest to deal with since it has compact support, independently of
\varepsilon
and
\delta
. Therefore, using that
Q_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})
maps
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
into itself for each
1\leq j\leq M
, we obtain the existence of
\ell=\ell(M,k)
such that

\displaystyle\|I^{1}(s)\|_{\Sigma^{k}}\

\displaystyle\leq C\,\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sup_{s^{\prime}\in[t^{\flat}-\delta,t^{\flat}+\delta]}\|\vec{\varphi}(s^{\prime})\|_{\Sigma^{k+\ell(M,k)}}.

The oscillating term
.
For dealing with the term
I^{2}
, we take advantage of the oscillating phase for compensating the fact that the support is large and we perform integration by parts.
We write

\displaystyle I^{2}(s)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\int_{-\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}^{(s-t^{\flat})/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\partial_{\sigma}\left({\rm e}^{i\phi^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}\right)\frac{1}{\sigma}b^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)(1-\chi)(\sigma){Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{iL\sigma}\vec{\varphi}(t^{\flat}+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})d\sigma

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\int_{-\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}^{(s-t^{\flat})/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\rm e}^{i\phi^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}\partial_{\sigma}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma}b^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)(1-\chi)(\sigma){Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{iL\sigma}\vec{\varphi}(t^{\flat}+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})\right)d\sigma

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\left.\left({\rm e}^{i\phi^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}\frac{1}{\sigma}b^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)(1-\chi)(\sigma){Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{iL\sigma}\vec{\varphi}(t^{\flat}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma)\right)\right|_{\sigma=-\frac{\delta}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}^{\sigma=\frac{s-t^{\flat}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sum_{\varsigma=1}^{3}\int_{-\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}^{(s-t^{\flat})/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\rm e}^{i\phi^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}b^{\varepsilon}_{\varsigma}(\sigma)\vec{\varphi}(t^{\flat}+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})d\sigma+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{-1}\varepsilon\sup_{s^{\prime}\in[t^{\flat}-\delta,t^{\flat}+\delta]}\|\vec{\varphi}(s^{\prime})\|_{\Sigma_{k+\ell(M,k)}})

with

\displaystyle b^{\varepsilon}_{1}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\left(-\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}b^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)(1-\chi)(\sigma)+\frac{1}{\sigma}\partial_{\sigma}b^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)(1-\chi)(\sigma)-\frac{1}{\sigma}b^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)\chi^{\prime}(\sigma)\right){Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{iL\sigma},

\displaystyle b^{\varepsilon}_{2}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\sigma}b^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)(1-\chi)(\sigma)\left(\partial_{s}{Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{iL\sigma}+{Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{iL\sigma}\partial_{s}\right),

\displaystyle b^{\varepsilon}_{3}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{\sigma}b^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)(1-\chi)(\sigma){Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{iL\sigma}L.

We deduce from the integrability of the coefficient of
{Q}_{j}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})
in
b^{\varepsilon}_{1}

that

\int_{-\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}^{(s-t^{\flat})/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\rm e}^{i\phi^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}b^{\varepsilon}_{1}(\sigma)\vec{\varphi}(t^{\flat}+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})d\sigma=\mathcal{O}(\sup_{s^{\prime}\in[t^{\flat}-\delta,t^{\flat}+\delta]}\|\vec{\varphi}(s^{\prime})\|_{\Sigma_{k+\ell(M,k)}}).

Then, using the size of the support of the integral, we obtain

\int_{-\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}^{(s-t^{\flat})/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\rm e}^{i\phi^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}b^{\varepsilon}_{2}(\sigma)\vec{\varphi}(t^{\flat}+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})d\sigma=\mathcal{O}(\delta\sup_{s^{\prime}\in[t^{\flat}-\delta,t^{\flat}+\delta]}(\|\vec{\varphi}(s^{\prime})\|_{\Sigma_{k+\ell(M,k)}}+\|\partial_{s}\vec{\varphi}(s^{\prime})\|_{\Sigma_{k+\ell(M,k)}}).

Finally, the last term satisfies

\int_{-\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}^{(s-t^{\flat})/\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\rm e}^{i\phi^{\varepsilon}(\sigma)}b^{\varepsilon}_{3}(\sigma){\rm e}^{iL\sigma}\vec{\varphi}(t^{\flat}+\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})d\sigma=\mathcal{O}(|\log\varepsilon|)\sup_{s^{\prime}\in[t^{\flat}-\delta,t^{\flat}+\delta]}(\|\vec{\varphi}(s^{\prime})\|_{\Sigma_{k+1+\ell(M,k)}},

which terminates the proof of (1).
∎

6.2.2.
The leading order term of the transfers operator.

Proof of Lemma
6.3
(2).

We now study
I_{\varphi}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(s)
for some function
\vec{\varphi}
independent of
s
and
we want to calculate the leading order term when
s=t^{\flat}+\delta
and
j=1
.
We set

\mathfrak{I}_{\varphi}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}I_{\varphi}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(t^{\flat}+\delta)-W_{1}(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})\mathcal{T}^{\flat}_{1\rightarrow 2}.

Following
[
23
]
Section 5.3, we first transform the expression
I_{\varphi}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(t^{\flat}+\delta)
by performing the change of variable

z=\sigma(1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\sigma\tilde{\phi}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})/\mu^{\flat})^{1/2}

and we observe that

\sigma=z(1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}zg_{1}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon}))\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\partial_{\sigma}z=1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}zg_{2}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon})

for some smooth bounded functions
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
with bounded derivatives.
Note that
we have used that
\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon}
is of order
\delta
, thus small, in the domain of the integral.
Besides, there exists a family of operators
\widetilde{Q}^{\varepsilon}(z)
such that

{Q}^{\varepsilon}(\sigma\sqrt{\varepsilon})=\widetilde{Q}^{\varepsilon}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon})

with
\widetilde{Q}^{\varepsilon}(0)={Q}^{\varepsilon}(0)
and
Q^{0}(0)=W_{1}(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})

\displaystyle I_{\varphi}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(t^{\flat}+\delta)=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\int_{z_{-}}^{z_{+}}{\rm e}^{-i\mu^{\flat}z^{2}}\widetilde{Q}^{\varepsilon}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{iz(1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}zg_{1}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon}))\,L}\vec{\varphi}\frac{dz}{1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}zg_{2}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon})}+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\delta)

(the
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\varepsilon}\delta)
coming from Lemma
6.4
).
Moreover
z_{-}=-\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon}+o(1)
and
z_{+}=\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon}+o(1)
.
A Taylor expansion allows to write

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}^{\varepsilon}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{i\sqrt{\varepsilon}z^{2}g_{1}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon})\,L}\frac{1}{1+\sqrt{\varepsilon}zg_{2}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon})}={Q}_{0}(0)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}z(\widetilde{Q}^{\varepsilon}_{1}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon})+z\widetilde{Q}^{\varepsilon}_{2}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon}))

for some smooth operator-valued maps
z\mapsto\widetilde{Q}^{\varepsilon}_{j}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon})
mapping
\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
into itself, such that for all
\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
the family

\|{\widetilde{Q}^{\varepsilon}_{j}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon})}\varphi\|_{\Sigma^{k}}\leq c_{j}\|\varphi\|_{\Sigma^{k+1+\ell(M,k)}}
(because of the loss of regularity involved by
L
and the
Q^{\varepsilon}_{j}
-s).
We obtain in Schwartz class

\mathfrak{I}_{\varphi}=\mathfrak{I}_{\varphi}^{1}+\mathfrak{I}_{\varphi}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\delta)

with

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}^{1}_{\varphi}=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\int_{z_{-}}^{z_{+}}z\,{\rm e}^{-i\mu^{\flat}z^{2}}(\widetilde{Q}^{\varepsilon}_{1}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon})+z\widetilde{Q}^{\varepsilon}_{2}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon})){\rm e}^{izL}\,\vec{\varphi}dz,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{\varphi}^{2}=Q_{0}(0)\int_{{\mathbb{R}}\setminus[z_{-},z_{+}]}{\rm e}^{-i\mu^{\flat}z^{2}}\,{\rm e}^{izL}\ \vec{\varphi}dz.

Let us study
\mathfrak{I}_{\varphi}^{1}
.
Arguing by integration by parts as previously, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}^{1}_{\varphi}=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{2i\mu^{\flat}}\int_{z_{-}}^{z_{+}}\,{\rm e}^{-i\mu^{\flat}z^{2}}{\partial_{z}}\left(\widetilde{Q}^{\varepsilon}_{1}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon})+z\widetilde{Q}^{\varepsilon}_{2}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{izL}\right)\,\vec{\varphi}\,dz+\mathcal{O}(\delta\|\vec{\varphi}\|_{\Sigma^{k+\ell(M,k)}})

\displaystyle=\frac{\varepsilon}{2i\mu^{\flat}}\int_{z^{-}}^{z_{+}}\,{\rm e}^{-i\mu^{\flat}z^{2}}\partial_{z}\widetilde{Q}^{\varepsilon}_{1}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{izL}\,\vec{\varphi}dz

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{2i\mu^{\flat}}\int_{z_{-}}^{z_{+}}\,{\rm e}^{-i\mu^{\flat}z^{2}}\partial_{z}\left(\widetilde{Q}^{\varepsilon}_{2}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon})+\sqrt{\varepsilon}z\partial_{z}\widetilde{Q}^{\varepsilon}_{2}(z\sqrt{\varepsilon}){\rm e}^{izL}\right)\,\vec{\varphi}\,dz+\mathcal{O}(\delta\|\vec{\varphi}\|_{\Sigma^{k+\ell(M,k)}}).

Since
|z\sqrt{\varepsilon}|\leq\delta
on the support of the integral, we obtain

\mathfrak{I}^{1}_{\varphi}=\mathcal{O}(\delta\|\vec{\varphi}\|_{\Sigma^{k+\ell(M,k)}}).

It remains to analyze
\mathfrak{I}_{\varphi}^{2}
. For this, we focus on

\mathfrak{I}_{\varphi}^{2,\infty}:=\int_{-\infty}^{z_{-}}{\rm e}^{-i\mu^{\flat}s^{2}}{\rm e}^{isL}\,\vec{\varphi}\,ds.

We claim that for
any
k\geq 0
, and any
N\geq 1
,
there exists a constant
C_{k,N}>0
and an integer
\ell(N,k)
such that

(6.24)

\|\mathfrak{I}_{\varphi}^{2,\infty}\|_{\Sigma^{k}}\leq C_{k,N}\|\varphi\|_{\Sigma^{\ell(N,k)}}\lambda^{-N},\;\;\lambda=\delta/\sqrt{\varepsilon}.

This estimate will conclude the proof, since a similar estimate holds when integrating from
z_{+}
to
+\infty
.
For proving (
6.24
), we
use a Littlewood-Paley decomposition defined as follows (see
[
1
, p. 91]
):
Let
\chi_{0}\in C_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
be such that
\chi_{0}(u)=1
if
|u|\leq 1/2
and
\chi_{0}(u)=0
if
|u|\geq 1
. Define
\chi_{1}(u)=\chi_{0}(u/2)-\chi_{0}(u)
(supported in

\{1/2\leq|u|\leq 2\}
). Then we have

1=\chi_{0}(u)+\sum_{j\geq 0}\chi_{1}(2^{-j}u),\,\,\forall u\in{\mathbb{R}}.

We then write

(6.25)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{\varphi}^{2,\infty}=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{z_{-}}\chi_{0}\left(\frac{s}{\lambda}\right){\rm e}^{-i\mu^{\flat}s^{2}}{\rm e}^{isL}\,\vec{\varphi}\,ds+\sum_{j\geq 0}\int_{-\infty}^{z_{-}}\chi_{1}\left(\frac{s}{2^{j}\lambda}\right){\rm e}^{-i\mu^{\flat}s^{2}}{\rm e}^{isL}\,\vec{\varphi}\,ds.

We deduce that there exists
j_{0}
such that for
j\geq j_{0}
,

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{z_{-}}\chi_{1}\left(\frac{s}{2^{j}\lambda}\right){\rm e}^{-i\mu^{\flat}s^{2}}{\rm e}^{isL}\,\vec{\varphi}\,ds=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\chi_{1}\left(\frac{s}{2^{j}\lambda}\right){\rm e}^{-i\mu^{\flat}s^{2}}{\rm e}^{isL}\,\vec{\varphi}\,ds.

The first
(j_{0}+1)
-th. terms in the right-hand side of (
6.25
) they can be treated as
\mathfrak{I}^{1}_{\varphi}
via a unique integration by parts, generating boundary terms. We obtain

\mathfrak{I}_{\varphi}^{2,\infty}=\sum_{j\geq j_{0}+1}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\chi_{1}\left(\frac{s}{2^{j}\lambda}\right){\rm e}^{-i\mu^{\flat}s^{2}}{\rm e}^{isL}\,\vec{\varphi}\,ds+\mathcal{O}(\delta\|\vec{\varphi}\|_{\Sigma^{k+\ell(M,k)}}).

We then
integrate by parts using the differential operator
P:=\frac{i}{2\mu^{\flat}s}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}
. We have for
j\geq 1
,

\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\chi_{1}\left(\frac{s}{2^{j}\lambda}\right){\rm e}^{-i\mu^{\flat}s^{2}}{\rm e}^{isL}\,\vec{\varphi}\,ds=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}{\rm e}^{-i\mu^{\flat}s^{2}}(P^{*})^{M}\left(\chi_{1}\left(\frac{s}{2^{j}\lambda}\right){\rm e}^{isL}\right)\,\vec{\varphi}\,ds.

Besides, for any
N\in{\mathbb{R}}
, there exists a constant
C_{N}>0
such that if
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{0}({\mathbb{R}})
,

|(P^{*})^{N}f(s)|\leq C_{N}\langle s\rangle^{-N}\sup_{1\leq p\leq N}|f^{(p)}(s)|.

Therefore,
noticing that
|s|
is of order
2^{j}\lambda
on the support of the integrals, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\chi_{1}\left(\frac{s}{2^{j}\lambda}\right){\rm e}^{-i\mu^{\flat}s^{2}}{\rm e}^{isL}\,\vec{\varphi}\,ds\right\|_{\Sigma^{k}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\vec{\varphi}\|_{\Sigma^{k+N}}\int_{|s|\sim 2^{j}\lambda}|s|^{-N}ds

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\|\vec{\varphi}\|_{\Sigma^{k+N}}(2^{j}\lambda)^{-N+1}\int_{1/2}^{2}\frac{ds}{s^{2}}.

Therefore, taking
N
large enough, the series is convergent and we have (
6.24
).∎

6.2.3.
Conclusion of the proof of Theorem
6.1

We can now conclude the proof of
Theorem
6.1
. Via a Dyson series, we have constructed interaction operators in Section
6.1
, leading to the relation (
6.12
). After resummation as in (
6.18
), we are left with operators
\Theta^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{m,M}
satisfying (
6.6
). Moreover, their action on wave packets expresses as (
6.19
), which is studied in details in Section
6.2
.
By Lemma
6.3
, we obtain that the operators
\Theta^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{m,M}
satisfy (
6.7
) with the properties (
6.8
)
and (
6.9
).

6.3.
Propagation of wave packets

We prove Theorem
1.26
. Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and let be
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
a polarized wave packet as in (
1.50
):

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\vec{V}_{0}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{0}}(f_{0})\;\;\mbox{with}\;\;f_{0}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\vec{V}_{0}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}.

Let
\delta>0
.
By Theorem
5.1
, for
t\in[t_{0},t^{\flat}-\delta]
, and in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
,
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)
is an asymptotic sum of wave packets and writes

\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)=\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}(t)+\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}(t)+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{N+1}\delta^{-2\kappa_{0}}\right).

with
\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t)
given by (
5.1
).

We now take the vector

\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}(t^{\flat}-\delta)=\,^{t}\left(\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}(t^{\flat}-\delta),\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}(t^{\flat}-\delta)\right),\;\;\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t^{\flat}-\delta)=\psi^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t^{\flat}-\delta),\;\ell=1,2,

as initial data in the system (
6.2
). It is a sum of
N
wave packets.
By construction, in particular because of the linearity of the equation, we have for all
t\in[t^{\flat}-\delta,t^{\flat}+\delta]
,

\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)=\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}(t)+\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}(t)+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{N+1}\delta^{-2\kappa_{0}}\right)

in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
. When
t=t^{\flat}+\delta
, we deduce from Theorem
6.1
that in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
,

\displaystyle\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}(t^{\flat}+\delta)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\rm diag}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat})\;\left(\mathbb{I}+\sum_{1\leq m\leq M}\Theta^{\varepsilon,\delta}_{m,M}\right)\;\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{\rm diag}(t^{\flat},t^{\flat}-\delta)\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}(t^{\flat}-\delta)

\displaystyle\;\;+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{M+1})+\mathcal{O}\left((\varepsilon\delta^{-2})^{\frac{M+1}{2}}\delta^{-2\kappa_{0}-k}\right).

By Proposition
C.6
of the Appendix,
\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t^{\flat}+\delta)
is a sum of wave packets

\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{\ell}(t^{\flat}+\delta)=\sum_{0\leq m\leq M}\varepsilon^{\frac{m}{2}}\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,m,M,N}_{\ell}(t^{\flat}+\delta)

where each term
\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,m,M,N}_{\ell}(t^{\flat}+\delta)
involves a term of action. When
m=0
and
m=1
, these terms have been computed precisely:

(i)

If
m=0
, for
\ell=1,2
,

\displaystyle\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,0,M,N}_{\ell}(t^{\flat}+\delta)

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{\ell}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat}-\delta,\zeta^{\flat})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t^{\flat}+\delta,t_{0}}(z_{0})}\left(\mathcal{M}[F_{\ell}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t_{0},z_{0})]\pi_{\ell}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0}\right)

where we have used the property of the scalar propagation of wave packets.

(ii)

If
m=1
,
only the term with
\ell=2
contributes and

\displaystyle\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,1,M,N}_{2}(t^{\flat}+\delta)

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{2}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})+\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S_{1}(t^{\flat},t_{0},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\Phi^{t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat}}_{2}(\zeta^{\flat})}(\varphi_{2})

with

\varphi_{2}=\mathcal{M}[F_{2}(t^{\flat}+\delta,t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})]W_{1}(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})^{*}\mathcal{T}^{\flat}_{1\rightarrow 2}\mathcal{M}[F_{1}(t^{\flat},t_{0},z_{0})]\pi_{1}(t_{0},z_{0})\vec{V}_{0}f_{0},

where
W_{1}
is the off-diagonal matrix computed in (
1.40
).

At that stage of the proof, we choose
M\geq N
and have obtained that
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t^{\flat}+\delta)
is a sum of wave packets up to
\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{N+1}\delta^{-2\kappa_{0}-k}+\delta^{M+1}\right)
in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
. Moreover, we know precisely the terms of order
\varepsilon^{0}
and
\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

For concluding, we take the vector

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\rm app}(t^{\flat}+\delta):=\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{1}(t^{\flat}+\delta)+\underline{\psi}^{\varepsilon,N}_{2}(t^{\flat}+\delta)

as initial data at time
t=t^{\flat}+\delta
in the equation (
1.3
). The function
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\rm app}(t^{\flat}+\delta)
is an approximation of
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t^{\flat}+\delta)
at order
\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{N+1}\delta^{-2\kappa_{0}-k}+\delta^{M+1}\right)
in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
.
By construction and because of the linearity of the equation, for all times
t\in[t^{\flat}+\delta,t_{0}+T]
,

\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)=\mathcal{U}_{H}(t,t^{\flat}+\delta)\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\rm app}(t^{\flat}+\delta)+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{N+1}\delta^{-2\kappa_{0}-k}+\delta^{M+1}\right).

We then applies Theorem
5.1
between times
t^{\flat}+\delta
and
t
. Indeed, the classical trajectories involved in the construction do not meet
\Upsilon
again and we are in an adiabatic regime, as in Theorem
5.1
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
1.26
.

6.4.
Transfer operator for Gaussian states

In this section, we study the action of the transfer operator
\mathcal{T}_{1\to 2}^{\flat}
defined in (
1.54
) on Gaussian states. This will justify the transfer coefficient (
1.38
) used in the initial value representations.

Following
[
23
]
, for
(\mu,\alpha,\beta)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
and
\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, we set

(6.26)

{\mathcal{T}}_{\mu,\alpha,\beta}\varphi(y)=\left(\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}{\rm e}^{i\mu s^{2}}{\rm e}^{is(\beta\cdot y-\alpha\cdot D_{y})}ds\right)\varphi(y).

The operator
\mathcal{T}_{1\to 2}^{\flat}
then satisfies

\mathcal{T}_{1\to 2}^{\flat}=\mathcal{T}_{-\mu^{\flat},\alpha^{\flat},\beta^{\flat}}.

Indeed, by the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula, we have

{\rm e}^{is\beta\cdot y}{\rm e}^{-is\alpha\cdot D_{y}}={\rm e}^{is\beta\cdot y-is\alpha\cdot D_{y}+is^{2}\alpha\cdot\beta/2},

and we deduce the equivalent representation

(6.27)

{\mathcal{T}}_{\mu,\alpha,\beta}\varphi(y)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}{\rm e}^{i(\mu-\alpha\cdot\beta/2)s^{2}}{\rm e}^{is\beta\cdot y}\varphi(y-s\alpha)ds.

An explicit computation also gives the following useful connection with the Fourier transform

(6.28)

{\mathcal{F}}{\mathcal{T}}_{\mu,\alpha,\beta}={\mathcal{T}}_{\mu+\alpha\cdot\beta,\beta,-\alpha}{\mathcal{F}}.

The next proposition sums up the main information that we will use about these operators.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
(\mu,\alpha,\beta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d+1}
.

(1)

The operator
\mathcal{T}_{\mu,\alpha,\beta}
maps
{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
into itself if and only if
\mu\not=0
.

(2)

Moreover, if
\mu\not=0
,
\mathcal{T}_{\mu,\alpha,\beta}
is a metaplectic transformation in the Hilbert space

L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
multiplied by a complex number:

(6.29)

{\mathcal{T}}_{\mu,\alpha,\beta}=\sqrt{\frac{i\pi}{\mu}}{\rm e}^{-\frac{i}{4\mu}(\beta\cdot y-\alpha\cdot D_{y})^{2}}.

(3)

If
\mu\not=0
,
\Gamma\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}(d)
and
P\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
is a polynomial function then there exists

\Gamma_{\mu,\alpha,\beta,\Gamma}\in{\mathfrak{S}}^{+}(d)
such that

{\mathcal{T}}_{\mu,\alpha,\beta}({\rm op}^{w}_{1}(P)g^{\Gamma})=\sqrt{\frac{i\pi}{\mu}}{\rm op}^{w}_{1}(P\circ\Phi_{\alpha,\beta}((4\mu)^{-1})g^{\Gamma_{\mu,\alpha,\beta,\Gamma}}

where
\Phi_{\alpha,\beta}
is the symplectic
2d\times 2d
matrix given by

(6.30)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\Phi_{\alpha,\beta}(t)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{I}-2t\beta\otimes\alpha&2t\alpha\otimes\alpha,\\
-2t\beta\otimes\beta&\mathbb{I}+2t\alpha\otimes\beta\end{pmatrix},\;\;t\in{\mathbb{R}},

and

(6.31)

\Gamma_{\mu,\alpha,\beta,\Gamma}=\Gamma-\frac{(\beta-\Gamma\alpha)\otimes(\beta-\Gamma\alpha)}{2\mu-\alpha\cdot\beta+\alpha\cdot\Gamma\alpha}.

Remark 6.6
.

The matrix
\Gamma_{\mu,\alpha,\beta,\Gamma}
is in
\mathfrak{S}^{+}(d)
since
g^{\Gamma_{\mu,\alpha,\beta,\Gamma}}
is proved to be Schwartz class.
It is also important to notice that

2\mu-\alpha\cdot\beta+\alpha\cdot\Gamma\alpha
is non zero because the imaginary part of
\Gamma
is positive definite.
We note that the normalisation of the Gaussian
g^{\Gamma_{\mu,\alpha,\beta,\Gamma}}
follows (
1.22
).

Proof.

Point (1) is linked with Point (2) and comes from the formula (
1.54
) and (
6.26
). Indeed, when
\mu\not=0
,
equation (
6.29
) is an application of relation (
6.26
) and of functional calculus on the self-adjoint operator
(\beta\cdot y-\alpha\cdot D_{y})^{2}
and the Fourier-transform formula of complex Gaussian functions:

(6.32)

\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}{\rm e}^{is^{2}\mu}{\rm e}^{is\tau}ds=\sqrt{\frac{i\pi}{\mu}}{\rm e}^{\frac{\tau^{2}}{4i\mu}}\;{\rm with}\;\arg(i\mu)\in]-\pi,\pi[.

It remains to analyze the case where
\mu=0
. The computations are different whether
\alpha\cdot\beta=0
or not. We assume
\alpha\not=0
and we set

\hat{\alpha}=\frac{\alpha}{|\alpha|},\;\;y=(y\cdot\hat{\alpha})\,\hat{\alpha}+y_{\perp}.

Similar formulas can be obtained when
\beta\not=0
using (
6.28
).
Let us first assume
\alpha\cdot\beta=0
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{0,\alpha,\beta}\varphi(y)

\displaystyle=\int{\rm e}^{is\beta\cdot y_{\perp}}\varphi(y\cdot\hat{\alpha}\,\hat{\alpha}-s\alpha+y_{\perp})ds

\displaystyle=|\alpha|^{-1}\int{\rm e}^{i|\alpha|^{-1}(y\cdot\hat{\alpha}-\sigma)(\beta\cdot y_{\perp})}\varphi(\sigma\hat{\alpha}+y_{\perp})d\sigma

\displaystyle=|\alpha|^{-1}{\rm e}^{i|\alpha|^{-1}(y\cdot\hat{\alpha})(\beta\cdot y_{\perp})}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\varphi\left(\frac{\beta\cdot y_{\perp}}{|\alpha|},y_{\perp}\right)

where
\varphi\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
,
y_{\perp}=y-{\hat{\alpha}\cdot y}\,\hat{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}
is the partial Fourier transform in the direction
\alpha
.

In the case where
\alpha\cdot\beta\not=0
, we write

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{0,\alpha,\beta}\varphi(y)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-1}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\rm e}^{-is^{2}\frac{\alpha\cdot\beta}{2}+is\beta\cdot y+i\eta(y\cdot\hat{\alpha}-s|\alpha|)}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\varphi(\eta,y_{\perp})d\eta ds

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{1}{2i\pi\beta\cdot\alpha}}\int{\rm e}^{i\frac{(\beta\cdot y-\eta|\alpha|)^{2}}{2\alpha\cdot\beta}+i\eta y\cdot\hat{\alpha}}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\varphi(\eta,y_{\perp})d\eta

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{1}{2i\pi\beta\cdot\alpha}}{\rm e}^{i\frac{(\beta\cdot y)^{2}}{2\beta\cdot\alpha}}\int{\rm e}^{-i\eta\frac{\beta_{\perp}\cdot y_{\perp}}{\beta\cdot\hat{\alpha}}}{\rm e}^{i\frac{\eta^{2}|\alpha|^{2}}{2\beta\cdot\alpha}}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\varphi(\eta,y_{\perp})d\eta

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{1}{2i\pi\beta\cdot\alpha}}{\rm e}^{i\frac{(\beta\cdot y)^{2}}{2\beta\cdot\alpha}}\int{\rm e}^{-i\eta\frac{\beta_{\perp}\cdot y_{\perp}}{\beta\cdot\hat{\alpha}}}\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}\left(e^{i\frac{(D_{y}\cdot\alpha)^{2}}{2\beta\cdot\alpha}}\varphi\right)(\eta,y_{\perp})d\eta

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{i\beta\cdot\alpha}}{\rm e}^{i\frac{(\beta\cdot y)^{2}}{2\beta\cdot\alpha}}\left(e^{i\frac{(D_{y}\cdot\alpha)^{2}}{2\beta\cdot\alpha}}\varphi\right)\left(-\frac{\beta_{\perp}\cdot y_{\perp}}{\beta\cdot\hat{\alpha}}\hat{\alpha}+y_{\perp}\right)

This concludes the proof of Points (1) and (2).

Point (3) derives from the formulation of
\mathcal{T}_{\mu,\alpha,\beta}
as a metaplectic transform,
