\mathcal{T}_{\mu,\alpha,\beta}=\sqrt{\frac{i\pi}{\mu}}{\rm e}^{-\frac{i}{4\mu}\hat{K}}

associated with the quadratic Hamiltonian

K(y,\eta)=(\beta\cdot y-\alpha\cdot\eta)^{2}.

We use general results concerning the action of a metaplectic transformation on a Gaussian
g^{\Gamma}
(for details see
[
15
]
[Chapter 3] and in particular
[
15
]
[§3.2, Theorem 16]). With the quadratic Hamiltonian
\hat{K}
one associates the linear flow
\Phi_{\alpha,\beta}(t)=(\Phi_{ij}(t))_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}
(in a
d\times d
block form) given by (
6.30
).
Besides, the Egorov theorem and the classical propagation of the Gaussian are both exact: we have for
P
a smooth polynomial function

{\rm e}^{-it\hat{K}}({\rm op}_{1}(P)g^{\Gamma})={\rm op}_{1}(P\circ\Phi_{\alpha,\beta}(t)){\rm e}^{-it\hat{K}}g^{\Gamma}=({\rm op}_{1}(P\circ\Phi_{\alpha,\beta}(t))g^{\Gamma_{t}},

where, in view of (
1.22
), the matrix
\Gamma_{t}\in\mathfrak{S}^{+}(d)
is given by

(6.33)

\Gamma_{t}=(\Phi_{21}(t)+\Phi_{22}(t)\Gamma)(\Phi_{11}(t)+\Phi_{12}(t)\Gamma)^{-1},\;\;c_{\Gamma_{t}}=c_{\Gamma}\,{\rm det}^{-1/2}(\Phi_{11}(t)+\Phi_{12}(t)\Gamma),

This induces the existence of the matrix
\Gamma_{\mu,\alpha,\beta,\Gamma}\in\mathfrak{S}^{+}(d)
of
Point (3) of the Proposition. Formula (
6.31
) follows either by computing the inverse in (
6.33
) or by applying
(
6.27
) to get

{\mathcal{T}}_{\mu,\alpha,\beta}g^{\Gamma}(y)=c_{\Gamma}\sqrt{\frac{2i\pi}{2\mu-\alpha\cdot\beta+\alpha\cdot\Gamma\alpha}}\;{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{2}\left(y\cdot\Gamma y-\frac{(y\cdot(\beta-\Gamma\alpha))^{2}}{2\mu-\alpha\cdot\beta+\alpha\cdot\Gamma\alpha}\right)}.

∎

Appendix A
Matrix-valued Hamiltonians

We explore here the implications of the technical assumptions on the Hamiltonian
H^{\varepsilon}
. We aim at motivating the set of Assumptions
1.6
and deriving their
consequences. Roughly speaking, these assumptions ensure the existence of the propagators associated with the full matrix-valued Hamiltonian and with its eigenvalues. Secondly, they guarantee adequate properties of growth at infinity which are used in our analysis.

In this section, we work with
m\times m
(
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
) matrix-valued Hamiltonians
H
that are subquadratic:

(A.1)

\forall\beta\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2d},\;\;\exists C_{\beta}>0,\;\;\forall(t,z)\in I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\;\;|\partial^{\beta}H(t,z)|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m}}\leq C_{\beta}\langle z\rangle^{(2-|\beta|)+}.

For their eigenvalues we can deduce the following growth properties.

Lemma A.1
.

Assume that the matrix-valued function
H\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m})
satisfies (
A.1
).
Assume that for all
(t,z)\in I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
,
H(t,z)

has a smooth eigenvalue
h(t,z)
with a smooth eigenprojector
\pi(t,z)
of constant rank for
|z|>R
,
R>0
. Then,
there exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
(t,z)\in I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
with
|z|>R
,

|h(t,z)|\leq C\langle z\rangle^{2},\;\;|\nabla h(t,z)|\leq C\langle z\rangle.

Proof.

The relation
H\pi=h\pi
implies
|h(t,z)|\leq C\langle z\rangle^{2}
.
Moreover, writing

H=h\pi+H(1-\pi)=h\pi+(1-\pi)H

and differentiating these two relations, we obtain for all
j\in\{1,\cdots 2d\}
, denoting
\partial_{z_{j}}
by
\partial_{j}

(A.2)

\partial_{j}H=\partial_{j}h\,\pi+h\partial_{j}\pi+\partial_{j}H(1-\pi)-H\partial_{j}\pi=\partial_{j}h\,\pi+h\partial_{j}\pi+(1-\pi)\partial_{j}H-\partial_{j}\pi H.

Multiplying from the left and the right with
\pi
and using that
\partial\pi
is off-diagonal,
we obtain the relation
\pi\partial_{j}H\pi=\partial_{j}h\pi
, whence with
c={\rm Rank}(\pi)=cte
,

\partial_{j}h=c\,{\rm Tr}(\pi\partial_{j}H\pi).

This implies
|\partial_{j}h(t,z)|\leq C\langle z\rangle
.
∎

This proof shows that the study of higher derivatives of the eigenvalues requires a control on the derivatives of the eigenprojectors. The following example shows that the situation may become very intricate, and one can have smooth subquadratic eigenvalues while the derivatives of the projectors are unbounded.

Example A.2
.

Assume
d=1
. Let
\rho,\theta\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
such that
\rho
has bounded derivatives and with

\rho(x)=\frac{1}{x^{3}}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\theta(x)=x\ln(x)-x\;\;\mbox{for}\;\;|x|>1.

Define

H(x)=\rho(x)\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\theta(x))&\sin(\theta(x))\\
\sin(\theta(x))&-\cos(\theta(x))\end{pmatrix}.

The eigenvalues of
H
are
\pm\rho
and the eigenprojector associated with the eigenvalue
\rho
is

\pi(x)=\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\theta(x))&\sin(\theta(x))\\
\sin(\theta(x))&-\cos(\theta(x))\end{pmatrix}\right).

Its derivative

\pi^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\prime}(x)\begin{pmatrix}-\sin(\theta(x))&\cos(\theta(x))\\
\cos(\theta(x))&\sin(\theta(x))\end{pmatrix}

is not bounded. On the other side,
\rho
and
H
are sub-quadratic. Indeed, for
|x|>1
, the derivatives of the coefficients of
H
are of the form

\frac{1}{x^{3}}\left(p_{1}\left(\frac{1}{x},\ln x\right)\cos\theta(x)+p_{2}\left(\frac{1}{x},\ln x\right)\sin\theta(x)\right)

for
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
two polynomial functions of two variables. Thus, they are bounded.

A manner of controlling the growth of the derivatives of the eigenprojectors consists in requiring a lower bound on the gap function
f
at infinity.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
\ell\in\{1,2\}
.
Assume that the matrix-valued function
H\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m})
is bounded together with its derivatives and
has a smooth bounded eigenvalue
h
with smooth associated eigenprojectors
\pi(t,z)
of constant rank for
|z|>R
,
R>0
.
Assume there exists
C,n_{0}>0
such that for
(t,z)\in I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
with
|z|>R
,

{\rm dist}\left(h(t,z),{\rm Sp}(H(t,z))\setminus\{h(t,z)\}\right)\geq C\langle z\rangle^{-n_{0}}.

Then, for all
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2d}
with
|\gamma|\geq 2
, there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}>0
such that

\forall(t,z)\in I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\;\;|\partial_{z}^{\gamma}\pi(t,z)|\leq C_{\gamma}\langle z\rangle^{|\gamma|n_{0}}.

Proof.

We work for
|z|>R
and set
\delta=\langle z\rangle^{-n_{0}}
for this proof only. We use the formula from complex analysis

\pi(z)=-\frac{1}{2i\pi}\oint_{\mathcal{C}}(H(t,z)-\zeta)^{-1}d\zeta

where
{\mathcal{C}}
is a closed contour in the complex plane with winding number one around
h(t,z)
. More precisely, the contour
{\mathcal{C}}
is constructed as follows.
We set
a=\inf\,h(t,z)
,
b=\sup\,h(t,z)
and for
\eta>0
, we consider the rectangles
\mathcal{R}_{\eta}
based on the points
(a-\eta,-1-\eta)
,
(a-\eta,1+\eta)
,
(b+\eta,1+\eta)
and
(b+\eta,-1-\eta)
.
We choose a smooth curve
{\mathcal{C}}
, strictly included in
(\mathcal{R}_{\delta}\setminus\mathcal{R}_{0})
and consisting in four connected branches,
{\mathcal{C}}_{\ell}
,
1\leq\ell\leq 4
, that are two by two disjoint with

{\mathcal{C}}_{1}=\{\zeta=a-\delta/2-it,\,-1\leq t\leq 1\},\;\;{\mathcal{C}}_{3}=\{\zeta=b+\delta/2+it,\,-1\leq t\leq 1\}

and
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}\subset\{1\leq\text{\rm Im}(\zeta)\leq 1+\delta\}
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{4}\subset\{-1-\delta\leq\text{\rm Im}(\zeta)\leq-1\}
.

By resolvant estimates and Pythagoras’ theorem, there exists a constant
c>0
such that,

\forall\zeta\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{C}}_{3},\;\;|(H(t,z)-\zeta)^{-1}|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m}}\leq c\frac{1}{\sqrt{t^{2}+\delta^{2}}},

while for all
\zeta\in{\mathcal{C}}_{2}\cup{\mathcal{C}}_{4}
, this norm is uniformly bounded in
\delta
.
Similarly,

\forall\zeta\in{\mathcal{C}}_{3},\;\;|(H(t,z)-\zeta)^{-1}|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m}}\leq c\frac{1}{\sqrt{t^{2}+\delta^{2}}},

while for all
\zeta\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{C}}_{2}\cup{\mathcal{C}}_{4}
, this norm is uniformly bounded in
\delta
.

We deduce from the boundedness of the derivatives of
H(t,z)
that for all
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2d}
, there exists
c_{\gamma}>0
such that

\displaystyle\forall\zeta\in{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{C}}_{3},\;\;|\partial^{\gamma}_{z}\left((H(t,z)-\zeta)^{-1}\right)|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m}}\leq c_{\gamma}\frac{1}{\left(\sqrt{t^{2}+\delta^{2}}\right)^{|\gamma|+1}},

while these derivatives are uniformly bounded, independently of
\delta
on the other branches of
{\mathcal{C}}
.
As a consequence, there exists
C_{\gamma}>0
associated with
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2d}
, and such that

\displaystyle|\partial^{\gamma}_{z}\pi|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\gamma}\int_{-1}^{+1}\frac{dt}{\left(\sqrt{t^{2}+\delta^{2}}\right)^{|\gamma|+1}}C_{\gamma}\delta^{-|\gamma|}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{du}{\left(\sqrt{u^{2}+1}\right)^{|\gamma|+1}}=\mathcal{O}(\delta^{-|\gamma|}),

which concludes the proof.
∎

These two Lemmata allow to derive the consequences of Assumption
1.6
for a Hamiltonian
H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
.

Proposition A.4
.

Assume that
H^{\varepsilon}=H_{0}+\varepsilon H_{1}
satisfies Assumption
1.6
. Then, for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
we have the following properties:

(1)

For all
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2d}
with
|\gamma|\geq 2
, there exists a constant
C_{\gamma}>0
such that

\forall(t,z)\in I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\;\;|\partial_{z}^{\gamma}\pi_{\ell}(t,z)|\leq C_{\gamma}\langle z\rangle^{|\gamma|n_{0}}.

(2)

The Hamiltonian trajectories
\Phi^{t_{0},t}_{h_{\ell}}(z)
are globally defined for all
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
. Besides, there exists
C^{\prime}>0
such that

|\Phi^{t_{0},t}_{h_{\ell}}(z)|\leq C|z|{\rm e}^{C|t-t_{0}|}

and the Jacobian matrices
F_{\ell}(t,z)=\partial_{z}\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)
(see (
1.15
)) satisfy

\|F_{\ell}(t,z)\|_{{\mathbb{C}}^{2d,2d}}\leq C{\rm e}^{C|t-t_{0}|}.

Remark A.5
.

Recall the representation
H_{0}=v\mathbb{I}+f(\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})
with gap
f
and average
v
defined
Definition

1.3
.
Under the assumptions of Proposition
A.4
, for all
j\in\{1,\cdots,2d\}
,
the matrices

f\partial_{z_{j}}\pi_{1}=-f\partial_{z_{j}}\pi_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{z_{j}}(H_{0}-v)-\partial_{z_{j}}f(\pi_{2}-\pi_{1})\right)

are bounded.

Proof of Proposition
A.4
.

The eigenprojectors
\pi_{\ell}
are those of
H_{0}-v\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m}}
. By (i) and (ii) of Assumptions
1.6
, the Hamiltonian
H_{0}-v\mathbb{I}_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m}}
satisfies the properties required for applying Lemma
A.3
, whence Point 1 of Proposition
A.4
.
Moreover, Point 2 of Proposition
A.4
comes from (iii) of Assumptions
1.6
.
∎

We close this Section with the proof of Lemma
1.13
.

Proof of Lemma
1.13
.

The map
t\mapsto\mathcal{R}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)
is valued in the set of unitary maps because
the matrix
H^{{\rm adia}}_{\ell,1}
is self adjoint.
Besides,
the map

(t,z)\mapsto Z_{\ell}(t,z)=\pi_{\ell}(t,\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)){\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)\pi_{\ell}^{\perp}(t_{0},z)

satisfies the ODE

i\partial_{t}Z_{\ell}(t,z)=\left(-i\left(\pi_{\ell}(\partial_{t}\pi_{\ell}+\{h,\pi_{\ell}\})\right)\circ\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}\right)\!(z)\,Z_{\ell}(t,z),\;\;\;\;Z_{\ell}(t_{0},z)=0,

and thus coincides with the solution
Z_{\ell}(t,z)=0
.

For proving (
1.28
), we observe that
\omega\cdot\nabla_{z}\mathcal{R}(t,t_{0},z)
satisfies the ODE

\displaystyle i\partial_{t}(\omega\cdot\nabla_{z}\mathcal{R}(t,t_{0},z)=

\displaystyle H_{\ell,1}^{\rm adiab}(t,\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z))(\omega\cdot\nabla_{z}{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z))

\displaystyle\;\;+(F_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z)\omega)\cdot\nabla_{z}H_{\ell,1}^{\rm adiab}(t,\Phi^{t,t_{0}}_{h_{\ell}}(z)){\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}(t,t_{0},z),

with initial data
\omega\cdot\nabla_{z}\mathcal{R}(t,t_{0},z)=0
.
The formula then comes from Duhamel formula and the definition of
\mathcal{R}(t,t_{0},z)
.
∎

Appendix B
Elements of symbolic calculus :
the Moyal product

In this section, we revisit results about symbolic calculus, in particular regarding composition formula and the remainder estimate for the Moyal product. We aim at their extension to the setting of the symbol classes
\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}^{\mu}(\mathcal{D})
and
\mathbf{S}_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{\mu}(\mathcal{D})
that we have introduced in Definition
4.1
.

B.1.
Formal expansion

We first recall the formal product rule for quantum observables
with Weyl quantization. Let
A,B\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m})
. The Moyal product
C:=A\circledast B
is the
semi-classical observable

C
such that
\widehat{A}\circ\widehat{B}=\widehat{C}
. Some computations with
the Fourier transform give the following well known formula
[
39
, Theorems 18.1.8]

[
71
, Theorem 4.11(i)]

(B.1)

C(x,\xi)=\exp\left(\frac{i\varepsilon}{2}\sigma(D_{q},D_{p};D_{q^{\prime}},D_{p^{\prime}})\right)A(q,p)B(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})|_{(q,p)=(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})=(x,\xi)},

where
\sigma
is the symplectic bilinear form
\sigma((q,p),(q^{\prime},p^{\prime}))=p\cdot q^{\prime}-p^{\prime}\cdot q
and

D=i^{-1}\nabla
.
By expanding the exponential term, we obtain

(B.2)

C(x,\xi)=\sum_{j\geq 0}\frac{\varepsilon^{j}}{j!}\left(\frac{i}{2}\sigma(D_{q},D_{p};D_{q^{\prime}},D_{p^{\prime}})\right)^{j}A(q,p)B(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})|_{(q,p)=(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})=(x,\xi)}.

So that
C=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}C_{j}
is a formal power series in
\varepsilon
with coefficients
given by (
4.1
).

B.2.
Symbols with derivative bounds

For
\mu\geq 0
denote by
{\bf P}(\mu)
the linear space of matrix-valued
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
symbols
A:{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m}
such that for any
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2d}
with
|\gamma|\geq\mu
, there exists
C_{\gamma}>0
such that

|\partial_{z}^{\gamma}A(z)|\leq C_{\gamma}\;\;\forall z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}.

Assuming that
A\in{\bf P}(\mu_{A})
,
B\in{\bf P}(\mu_{B})
it is known that, for
\varepsilon
fixed,
A\circledast B=C
where
C\in{\bf P}(\mu_{C})
with
\mu_{C}\geq\max\{\mu_{A},\mu_{B}\}

(see e.g. the proof
[
39
, Theorem 18.1.8]
)

We aim at having better estimates for small
\varepsilon>0
and a control of the derivatives of
C
in terms of those of
A
and
B
. The following estimate and its proof are a particular case of
[
6
, Theorem A.1]
.

Theorem B.1
.

For every
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\gamma\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2d}
, there exists a constant
K_{N,\gamma}
such that for any

A\in{\bf P}(\mu_{A})
,
B\in{\bf P}(\mu_{B})
the Moyal remainder

(B.3)

R_{N}(A,B;z;\varepsilon):=(A\circledast B)(z)-\sum_{0\leq j\leq N}\varepsilon^{j}C_{j}(z)

satisfies for every
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,

(B.4)

\left|\partial_{z}^{\gamma}R_{N}(A,B;z;\varepsilon)\right|\leq\varepsilon^{N+1}K_{N,\gamma}\sum_{N+1\leq|\alpha|,|\beta|\leq N+\kappa_{0}+|\gamma|}\|\partial_{z}^{\alpha}A\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\partial_{z}^{\beta}B\|_{L^{\infty}},

with
\kappa_{0}=4d+2
.

The estimate (
4.5
) allows to evaluate the norms of the operators involved in Theorem
B.1
when considering symbols of the classes

\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}^{\mu}(\mathcal{D})
and
\mathbf{S}_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{\mu}(\mathcal{D})
of Definition
4.1
.

Corollary B.2
.

If
A\in\mathbf{S}_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{\mu}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
and
B\in\mathbf{S}_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{\mu^{\prime}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
, then
A\circledast B\in\mathbf{S}^{\mu+\mu^{\prime}}_{\varepsilon,\delta}
(see Remark
4.2
) and for all
N,k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists a constant
C_{N,k}>0
such that

\|{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(R_{N}(A,B;z;\varepsilon))\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}\leq C_{N,k}\,\varepsilon^{N+1}\,\delta^{\mu+\mu^{\prime}-2(N+1+k+\kappa_{0})}.

The proof of Theorem
B.1
relies on the technical next lemma, the proof of which we postpone at the end of this paragraph.

Lemma B.3
.

There exists a constant
C_{d}>0
such that for any
F\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m})

the integral

(B.5)

I(\lambda)=\lambda^{2d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\exp[-i\lambda\sigma(u,v)]F(u,v)dudv.

satisfies

(B.6)

|I(\lambda)|\leq C_{d}\sup_{u,v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\atop|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 4d+1}|\partial^{\alpha}_{u}\partial^{\beta}_{v}F(u,v)|.

We are now in position of proving Theorem
B.1
.

Proof of Theorem
B.1
.

The application of Taylor formula at order
N
gives the following formula for the remainder

\displaystyle R_{N}(A,B;z;\varepsilon)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N!}\left(\frac{i\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{N+1}\left(\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)^{N}\exp\left(\frac{it\varepsilon}{2}\sigma(D_{u};D_{v})\right)dt\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\,\sigma^{N+1}(D_{u};D_{v})A(u)B(v)|_{u=v=(x,\xi)}.

By Fourier transform computations (see
[
71
, Theorem 4.8(iii) or Theorem 4.11(ii)]
, we obtain

(B.7)

R_{N}(A,B;z;\varepsilon)=\frac{1}{N!}\left(\frac{i\varepsilon}{2}\right)^{N+1}\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)^{N}R_{N,t}(z;\varepsilon)dt,

where
R_{N,t}(z;\varepsilon)
is given by the oscillating integral

\displaystyle R_{N,t}(z;\varepsilon)=(2\pi\varepsilon t)^{-2d}\int\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\exp\left(-\frac{i}{2t\varepsilon}\sigma(u;v)\right)\sigma^{N+1}(D_{u};D_{v})A(u+z)B(v+z)dudv.

We now use Lemma
B.3
for
A,B\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
with the integrand

F_{N,\gamma}(z;u,v)=\pi^{-2d}\ \partial_{z}^{\gamma}\left(\sigma^{N+1}(D_{u};D_{v})A(u+z)B(v+z)\right)

and the parameter
\lambda=1/(2t\varepsilon)
. We then have

|\partial_{z}^{\gamma}R_{N,t}(z;\varepsilon)|\leq C_{d}\sup_{u,v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\atop|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq 4d+1}|\partial^{\alpha}_{u}\partial^{\beta}_{v}F_{N,\gamma}(z;u,v)|.

Moreover, there holds the elementary estimate

|\sigma^{N+1}(D_{u},D_{v})A(u)B(v)|\leq(2d)^{N+1}\sup_{|\alpha|+|\beta|=N+1}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{\xi}^{\beta}A(x,\xi)\partial_{y}^{\beta}\partial_{\eta}^{\alpha}B(y,\eta)|.

Together with the Leibniz formula, we then get the claimed results with universal constants.
For symbols
A\in{\bf P}(\mu_{A})
and
B\in{\bf P}(\mu_{B})
we argue by localisation. We use

A_{\eta}(u)={\rm e}^{-\eta u^{2}}A(u)
and

B_{\eta}(v)={\rm e}^{-\eta v^{2}}B(v)
for
\eta>0
and
pass to the limit
\eta\rightarrow 0
.
∎

It remains to prove Lemma
B.3
.

Proof of Lemma
B.3
.

The lemma is proved in a standard way, using integration by parts and stationary phase argument.
For the sake of completeness, we give here a proof.
We introduce a cut-off
\chi_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{0}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
such that

\chi_{0}(x)=1\;\;\mbox{ for}\;\;|x|\leq 1/2\;\;\mbox{ and}\;\;\chi_{0}(x)=0\;\;\mbox{for }\;\;|x|\geq 1.

We split
I(\lambda)
into
too pieces and write

I(\lambda)=I_{0}(\lambda)+I_{1}(\lambda)
with

\displaystyle I_{0}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2d}\int\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\exp[-i\lambda\sigma(u,v)]\chi_{0}(u^{2}+v^{2})F(u,v)dudv,

\displaystyle I_{1}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{2d}\int\!\!\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}\exp[-i\lambda\sigma(u,v)](1-\chi_{0})(u^{2}+v^{2}))F(u,v)dudv.

We notice that
(u,v)\mapsto\sigma(u,u)
is a quadratic non-degenerate real form on
{\mathbb{R}}^{4d}
.

Let us estimate
I_{1}(\lambda)
. For
I_{1}(\lambda)
, the integrand is supported outside the ball of radius
1/\sqrt{2}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{4d}
. Therefore, we can integrate by parts with the
differential operator

L=\frac{i}{|u|^{2}+|v|^{2}}\left(Ju\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial v}-Jv\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\right),

using that
L{\rm e}^{-i\lambda\sigma(u,v)}=L{\rm e}^{-i\lambda Ju\cdot v}=\lambda{\rm e}^{-i\lambda\sigma(u,v)}
.
Performing
4d+1
integrations by parts for gaining enough decay to ensure integrability in
(u,v)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4d}
, we get a constant
c_{d}
such that

|I_{1}(\lambda)|\leq c_{d}\sup_{u,v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\atop|\mu|+|\nu|\leq 4d+1}|\partial^{\mu}_{u}\partial^{\nu}_{v}F(u,v)|.

To estimate
I_{0}(\lambda)
we apply the stationary phase.
The symmetric matrix of the quadratic form
\sigma(u,v)
is

A_{\sigma}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-J\\
J&0\end{pmatrix}.

So, by the stationary phase Theorem (
[
39
]
, Vol.I, section 7.7),
we obtain the existence of two constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
such that

(B.8)

|I_{0}(\lambda)-\lambda^{-2d}c_{1}|\leq c_{2}\sup_{u,v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\atop|\alpha|\leq 2}|\partial^{\alpha}(\chi_{0}(u^{2}+v^{2})F(u,v)|.

∎

Appendix C
Propagation of wave packets by perturbation of scalar systems

In this Appendix, we revisit several well-known results concerning a Hamiltonian
\widehat{K}(t)
with its symbol
K(t)

valued in the set
{\mathbb{C}}^{m,m}
of self-adjoint
m\times m
matrices (
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
), and which is a perturbation of a scalar function
k(t)
.
We consider an interval
I_{\delta}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
that may depend on
\delta>0
, which is small, and assume that
K(t)
is defined on
I_{\delta}
and of the form

(C.1)

K(t)=k(t){\mathbb{I}}_{m}+\varepsilon K_{1}(t)+\cdots+\varepsilon^{N}K_{N}(t)

with
k
scalar-valued and
k(t){\mathbb{I}}_{m}+\varepsilon K_{1}(t)
subquadratic on the time interval
I_{\delta}
according to Definition
1.1
.

We will work on an adequate domain
\mathcal{D}\subseteq I_{\delta}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
and use the symbol class
\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}(\mathcal{D})
, where the additional parameter
\delta>0
controls the growth of derivatives, see
Definition

4.1
. We assume

(C.2)

\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\left(K(t,\cdot)-k(t,\cdot)\mathbb{I}_{m}-\varepsilon K_{1}(t,\cdot)\right)\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{-1}(\mathcal{D}).

In particular, we assume
K_{2}(t)\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}^{-1}
,
(\mathcal{D})
,
K_{3}(t)\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}^{-3}(\mathcal{D})
and more generally
K_{j}(t)\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}^{3-2j}(\mathcal{D})
for
j\geq 2
.
We revisit standard propagation results and take care of the loss in
\delta^{-1}
and control all the classical estimates with respect to this parameter.

These properties are satisfied by the Hamiltonians that we consider in the adiabatic region. Indeed, the formally constructed adiabatic symbol
H^{{\rm adia},\varepsilon}(t)
satisfies (
C.2
), see property (3) of of Theorem
4.9
. Truncating the series at
N
and introducing adequate temporal cut-offs,
the Hamiltonians
\widetilde{H}_{\ell}^{{\rm adia},N,\varepsilon}(t)
defined for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
in (
5.7
)
satisfy both assumptions (
C.1
) and (
C.2
) on the intervals
[t_{0},t^{\flat}-\delta]
and

[t^{\flat}+\delta,t_{0}+T]
.

C.1.
Egorov Theorem

We denote by
\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})
the unitary propagator associated with
\widehat{K}(t)
. It satisfies

(C.3)

i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})=\widehat{K}(t)\,\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in}),\;\;\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t_{\rm in},t_{\rm in})=\mathbb{I}_{m}.

Moreover

\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})^{*}=\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t_{\rm in},t),\;t_{\rm in},t\in I_{\delta}.

It is also important that
\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})
maps
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
into itself for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Indeed, for all

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}\in\Sigma^{1}_{\varepsilon}
and all
1\leq j\leq d
, the function
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)=\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{0}
satisfies

\displaystyle i\varepsilon\partial_{t}(x_{j}\psi^{\varepsilon}(t))-\widehat{K}(t)(x_{j}\psi^{\varepsilon}(t))=f^{\varepsilon}_{j}(t),

\displaystyle i\varepsilon\partial_{t}(\varepsilon D_{x_{j}}\psi^{\varepsilon}(t))-\widehat{K}(t)(\varepsilon D_{x_{j}}\psi^{\varepsilon}(t))=g^{\varepsilon}_{j}(t),

where

\displaystyle f^{\varepsilon}_{j}(t)=[x_{j},\widehat{K}(t)]\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)=-\tfrac{\varepsilon}{i}\mathrm{op}^{w}_{\varepsilon}(\partial_{\xi_{j}}K(t))\psi^{\varepsilon}(t),

\displaystyle g^{\varepsilon}_{j}(t)=[\varepsilon D_{x_{j}},\widehat{K}(t)]\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)=\tfrac{\varepsilon}{i}\mathrm{op}^{w}_{\varepsilon}(\partial_{x_{j}}K(t))\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)

are uniformly bounded in
L^{2}
. Therefore,
w^{\varepsilon}(t)={}^{t}(x_{j}\psi^{\varepsilon}(t),\varepsilon D_{x_{j}}\psi^{\varepsilon}(t))
satisfies

\|w^{\varepsilon}(t)\|_{L^{2}}\leq\|w^{\varepsilon}(t_{\rm in})\|_{L^{2}}+C\int_{t_{\rm in}}^{t}\|w^{\varepsilon}(s)\|_{L^{2}}\,ds

for some constant
C>0
. By the Gronwall Lemma, one then deduces the
L^{2}
-boundedness of the families
(x_{j}\psi^{\varepsilon}(t))
and
(\varepsilon D_{x_{j}}\psi^{\varepsilon}(t))
, whence the boundedness of
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)
in
\Sigma^{1}_{\varepsilon}
for all
t\in I_{\delta}
. The corresponding recursive process will give the boundedness of
\psi^{\varepsilon}(t)
in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

The Egorov Theorem describes the evolution of an observable when it is conjugated by the propagator
\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})
.
Our aim is to consider the evolution of
\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t_{\rm in},t)\widehat{A}\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,t_{\rm in})
for matrix-valued observables
A\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
and in spaces
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
, with a precise estimate of the remainders.

Proposition C.1
.

With the above assumptions on
K(t)
, for any matrix-valued symbol
A\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
,
there exists a formal series

(t,t_{\rm in})\mapsto\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}A_{j}(t,t_{\rm in})

defined on
I_{\delta}\times I_{\delta}
such that for any
J\geq 1
, we have for all
t,t_{\rm in}\in I_{\delta}
,

\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})^{*}\,\widehat{A}\;\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,t_{\rm in})=\sum_{0\leq j\leq J}\varepsilon^{j}\widehat{A}_{j}(t,t_{\rm in})+\varepsilon^{J+1}\widehat{R}^{\varepsilon}_{J}(t,t_{\rm in})

with
A_{j}(t,t_{\rm in})\in\boldsymbol{S}^{-2j}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
.
Besides, for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists a constant
C_{k,J}>0
such that

\|\widehat{R^{\varepsilon}_{J}}(t,t_{\rm in})\|_{\mathcal{L}(\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})}\leq C_{k,J}\delta^{-2(J+1+\kappa_{0})-k},

and the matrix
A_{0}(t,t_{\rm in})
is given by

(C.4)

A_{0}(t,t_{\rm in},z)=\left({\mathcal{R}}(t,t_{\rm in})^{*}A{\mathcal{R}}(t,t_{\rm in})\right)\circ(\Phi_{k}^{t,t_{\rm in}})(z),

where the unitary matrices
{\mathcal{R}}(t,t_{\rm in},z)
solve the transport equation

(C.5)

i\partial_{t}{\mathcal{R}}(t,t_{\rm in},z)=K_{1}\left(t,\Phi_{k}^{t,t_{\rm in}}(z)\right){\mathcal{R}}(t,t_{\rm in},z),\;{\mathcal{R}}(t_{\rm in},t_{\rm in},z)=\mathbb{I}_{m}.

Remark C.2
.

(1)

Note that in the setting of Proposition
C.1
,
we have the propagation law of the supports:

{\rm supp}(A_{j}(t,t_{\rm in}))=\Phi_{k}^{t_{\rm in},t}({\rm supp}(A))\;\;\mbox{ for any}\;\;j\geq 0.

(2)

In the scalar time-independent case, that is for
K=k(z)
and
K_{j}=0
for
j\geq 1
, and with
\delta=1
, the Egorov theorem

\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t_{\rm in},t)\widehat{A}\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,t_{\rm in})=\widehat{A\circ\Phi_{k}^{t,t_{\rm in}}}+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

is well-known (see
[
15
,
16
,
71
]
for example) and has a vanishing sub-principal symbol
A_{1}(t,t_{\rm in})=0
.

In the time-dependent matrix-valued case considered here,
the dynamics on the observable is driven by the classical flow twisted by the precession
{\mathcal{R}}
(see also Section
1.3.3
where such terms appear).

Proof.

We perform a recursive argument. The starting point comes from the analysis of an auxiliary map.
We consider the operator
\mathcal{L}^{t,\tau}
defined by

\mathcal{L}^{t,\tau}a(z)=a(\Phi_{k}^{t,\tau}(z)),\;\;z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},

for any smooth function
a
.
Then, the function
\tilde{a}(\tau,z)=\mathcal{L}^{t,\tau}a(z)
satisfies
\tilde{a}(\tau,\Phi_{k}^{\tau,t}(z))=a(z)
and does not depend on
\tau
. Using that
\Phi^{t,\tau}_{k}
is a diffeomorphism, we deduce that

\partial_{\tau}\mathcal{L}^{t,\tau}a=-\{k(\tau),\mathcal{L}^{t,\tau}a\},\;\;\mathcal{L}^{t,t}=a.

We now introduce
for
\tau,t\in I_{\delta}
the auxiliary map valued in
\mathbf{S}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
and defined by

A\mapsto A(t,\tau):=\mathcal{L}^{t,\tau}(\mathcal{R}(\tau,t)A\mathcal{R}(\tau,t)^{*})

We deduce from the analysis of
\mathcal{L}^{t,\tau}
the equation

\partial_{\tau}A(t,\tau)=-\{k(\tau),A(t,\tau)\}+\mathcal{L}^{t,\tau}(\mathcal{\partial}_{\tau}R(\tau,t)A\mathcal{R}(\tau,t)^{*})+\mathcal{L}^{t,\tau}(\mathcal{R}(\tau,t)A\partial_{\tau}\mathcal{R}(\tau,t)^{*})

In view of (
C.5
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{t,\tau}(\mathcal{\partial}_{\tau}R(\tau,t)A\mathcal{R}(\tau,t)^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i}\mathcal{L}^{t,\tau}(K_{1}(\tau,\Phi_{k}^{\tau,t}(z))R(\tau,t)A\mathcal{R}(\tau,t)^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i}K_{1}(\tau,\Phi_{k}^{\tau,t}\circ\Phi_{k}^{t,\tau}(z))\mathcal{L}^{t,\tau}(R(\tau,t)A\mathcal{R}(\tau,t)^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i}K_{1}(\tau,z)A(t,\tau).

Arguing similarly for the last term, we obtain that
A(t,\tau)
solves

\partial_{\tau}A(t,\tau,z)=-\{k(\tau),A(t,\tau)\}(z)+\frac{1}{i}[K_{1}(\tau,z),A(t,\tau,z)],\;\;A(t,t)=A.

Let us now start with the proof of the result for
J=0
. We choose
s,\tau,t\in I_{\delta}
and consider the quantity

Q^{\varepsilon}_{A}(t,s,\tau)=\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau,s)^{*}\,\widehat{A}(t,\tau)\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau,s).

The times
s,\tau,t
can be understood as
s\leq\tau\leq t
with
s
an initial time (that will be taken as
s=t_{\rm in}
) and
t
the time at which we want to prove the property.
We then have the boundary properties

Q^{\varepsilon}_{A}(t,s,t)=\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,s)^{*}\widehat{A}\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,s)\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;Q^{\varepsilon}_{A}(t,s,s)=\widehat{A}(t,s).

Differentiating in
\tau
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\tau}Q^{\varepsilon}_{A}(t,s,\tau)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau,s)^{*}\left(\left[-\frac{1}{i\varepsilon}\widehat{K}(\tau),\widehat{A}(t,\tau)\right]+\partial_{\tau}\widehat{A}(t,\tau)\right)\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau,s)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau,s)^{*}\left(\left[-\frac{1}{i\varepsilon}\widehat{K}(\tau),\widehat{A}(t,\tau)\right]-\widehat{\{k,A(t,\tau)\}}+\frac{1}{i}\widehat{[K_{1}(\tau),A(t,\tau)]}\right)\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau,s)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau,s)^{*}\,\widehat{B_{1}^{\varepsilon}}(t,\tau)\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau,s)

where the matrix
B^{\varepsilon}_{1}\in\mathbf{S}^{-2-2\kappa_{0}}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(\mathcal{D})
stems from the Moyal product (see Corollary
B.2
).
We deduce by integration between the times
s
and
\tau

(C.6)

Q^{\varepsilon}_{A}(t,s,\tau)=Q^{\varepsilon}_{A}(t,s,s)+\varepsilon\int_{s}^{\tau}\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau^{\prime},s)^{*}\,\widehat{B_{1}^{\varepsilon}}(t,\tau^{\prime})\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau^{\prime},s)d\tau^{\prime},

which implies when
\tau=t
,

\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,s)^{*}\widehat{A}\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,s)=\widehat{A}(t,s)+\varepsilon\int_{s}^{t}\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau,s)^{*}\,\widehat{B_{1}^{\varepsilon}}(t,\tau)\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau,s)d\tau.

This gives the first step of the recursive argument.

We now assume that we have obtained for
J\geq 0

\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,s)^{*}\,\widehat{A}\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,s)=\sum_{j=0}^{J}\varepsilon^{j}\widehat{A}_{j}(t,s)+\varepsilon^{J+1}\int_{s}^{t}\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau,s)^{*}\,\widehat{B_{J+1}^{\varepsilon}}(t,\tau)\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau,s)d\tau

with
B^{\varepsilon}_{J+1}\in\mathbf{S}^{-2-2\kappa_{0}}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
.
We write

B^{\varepsilon}_{J+1}=B_{J+1}+B^{1,\varepsilon}_{J+2}

with
B_{J+1}\in\mathbf{S}_{\delta}^{-2(J+1)-2\kappa_{0}}(\mathcal{D})
and
B^{1,\varepsilon}_{J+2}\in\mathbf{S}^{-2(J+2)-2\kappa_{0}}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(\mathcal{D})
. Then, the preceding equation writes

(C.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,s)^{*}\widehat{A}\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,s)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{J}\varepsilon^{j}\widehat{A}_{j}(t,s)

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{J+1}\int_{s}^{t}Q^{\varepsilon}_{B_{J+1}}(t,s,\tau)d\tau+\varepsilon^{J+2}\int_{s}^{t}\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau,s)^{*}\,\widehat{B_{J+2}^{1,\varepsilon}}(t,\tau)\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau,s)d\tau.

We focus on the term involving
Q^{\varepsilon}_{B_{J+1}}(t,s,\tau)
that we treat
as in the preceding step. We obtain by (
C.6
)

Q^{\varepsilon}_{B_{J+1}}(t,s,\tau)=Q^{\varepsilon}_{B_{J+1}}(t,s,s)+\varepsilon\int_{s}^{\tau}\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau^{\prime},s)^{*}\widehat{B}_{J+2}^{2,\varepsilon}(\tau,\tau^{\prime})\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau^{\prime},s)d\tau^{\prime}

with
B_{J+2}^{2,\varepsilon}\in\mathbf{S}^{-2(J+2)-2\kappa_{0}}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(\mathcal{D})
.
We set

A_{J+1}(s,t)=(t-s)Q^{\varepsilon}_{B_{J+1}}(t,s,s)

and

B^{\varepsilon}_{J+2}(t,\tau)=B^{1,\varepsilon}_{J+2}(t,\tau)+\int_{s}^{\tau}\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau^{\prime},\tau)^{*}\,B_{J+2}^{2,\varepsilon}(\tau^{\prime},\tau)\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau^{\prime},\tau)d\tau^{\prime}\in\mathbf{S}^{-2(J+1)}_{\varepsilon,\delta}(\mathcal{D}).

The equation (
C.7
) then becomes

\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,s)^{*}\widehat{A}\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,s)=\sum_{j=0}^{J+1}\varepsilon^{j}\widehat{A}_{j}(t,s)\\
+\varepsilon^{J+2}\int_{s}^{t}\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau,s)^{*}\,\widehat{B_{J+2}^{\varepsilon}}(t,\tau)\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(\tau,s)d\tau

and we concludes the proof by Proposition
4.4
.
∎

C.2.
Asymptotic behavior of the propagator

In this section, we analyze the propagator
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,t_{\rm in})
, and compare it with

\mathcal{U}_{KS}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})
, the propagator for

KS(t)=k(t)\mathbb{I}_{m}+\varepsilon K_{1}(t)
.

Lemma C.3
.

We assume (
C.1
) and (
C.2
). Then,
there exists a formal series

Y^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})=\sum_{j\geq 0}\varepsilon^{j}\,Y_{j}(t,t_{\rm in})\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\varepsilon,\delta}^{1}(\mathcal{D})

with
Y_{0}(t,t_{\rm in})=\mathbb{I}_{m}
and
Y_{j}(t,t_{\rm in})\in\boldsymbol{S}_{\delta}^{1-2j}(\mathcal{D})
for
j\geq 1
,
such that for all
t\in I_{\delta}
,

\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})=\,\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})\,\mathcal{U}_{KS}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in}).

Proof of Lemma
C.3
.

If such a
Y^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})
exists, it must satisfy

\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})=\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})\,\mathcal{U}_{KS}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})^{*}.

As a consequence, the operator
\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})
solves

\displaystyle i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})

\displaystyle=\,\widehat{K}(t)\,\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})\,\mathcal{U}_{KS}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})^{*}-\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})\,\mathcal{U}_{KS}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})^{*}\,\widehat{KS}(t)

\displaystyle=\widehat{K}(t)\,\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})\,-\,\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})\widehat{KS}(t)

with
\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon}(t_{\rm in},t_{\rm in})=\mathbb{I}_{m}
.
We write

(C.8)

i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})=\left[\widehat{k}(t)\,\mathbb{I}_{m}+\varepsilon\widehat{K_{1}}(t),\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})\right]+\sum_{j=2}^{N}\varepsilon^{j}\widehat{K_{j}}(t)\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in}),

and we deduce a set of recursive equations. Looking to the leading order term, we obtain

\partial_{t}Y_{0}(t,t_{\rm in})+\{k(t),Y_{0}(t,t_{\rm in})\}-\frac{1}{i}[K_{1}(t),Y_{0}(t,t_{\rm in})]=0,\;\;Y_{0}(t_{\rm in},t_{\rm in})=\mathbb{I}_{m}

and therefore

Y_{0}(t,t_{\rm in})=\left({\mathcal{R}}(t,t_{\rm in})^{*}\mathbb{I}_{m}{\mathcal{R}}(t,t_{\rm in})\right)\circ(\Phi_{k}^{t,t_{\rm in}})=\mathbb{I}_{m}.

Then, the terms of order
\varepsilon^{j+1}
give equations of the form

(C.9)

\partial_{t}Y_{j+1}(t,t_{\rm in})+\{k(t),Y_{j+1}(t,t_{\rm in})\}-\frac{1}{i}[K_{1}(t),Y_{j+1}(t,t_{\rm in})]=L_{j+1}(t,t_{\rm in}),\;\;Y_{j+1}(t_{\rm in},t_{\rm in})=0,

where the source term

L_{j+1}(t,t_{\rm in})
depends on
Y_{\ell}(t,t_{\rm in})
for
0\leq\ell\leq j
. For example, for the first terms we have

\displaystyle L_{1}(t,t_{\rm in})

\displaystyle=K_{2}(t)Y_{0}(t,t_{\rm in})=K_{2}(t)\in\boldsymbol{S}^{-1}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})\quad\text{and thus}\quad Y_{1}(t,t_{\rm in})\in\boldsymbol{S}^{-1}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D}),

\displaystyle L_{2}(t,t_{\rm in})

\displaystyle=K_{2}(t)Y_{1}(t,t_{\rm in})+K_{3}(t)\in\boldsymbol{S}^{-3}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})\quad\text{and thus}\quad Y_{2}(t,t_{\rm in})\in\boldsymbol{S}^{-3}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D}),

where we have used the multiplication property of the symbol classes, see
Remark

4.2
. More generally, we obtain

L_{j+1}(t,t_{\rm in})=\sum_{\ell=0}^{j}K_{2+\ell}(t)Y_{j-\ell}(t,t_{\rm in})\quad\text{and thus}\quad Y_{j+1}(t,t_{\rm in})\in\boldsymbol{S}^{-1-2j}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D}).

∎

Remark C.4
.

If we assume
\delta\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}
and write

Y^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})=\sum_{j=0}^{J}\varepsilon^{j}\,Y_{j}(t,t_{\rm in})+\varepsilon^{J+1}R_{J}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})

with
J\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then
Proposition

4.4
implies that for all
j=1,\ldots,J
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exist constants

C_{j,k}>0
and
C_{J,k}>0
such that

(C.10)

\displaystyle\|{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(Y_{j}(t,t_{\rm in}))\|_{\mathcal{L}(\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{j,k}\,\delta^{1-2j-k},

(C.11)

\displaystyle\|{\rm op}_{\varepsilon}(R_{J}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in}))\|_{\mathcal{L}(\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{J,k}\,\delta^{-1-2J-k}

Corollary C.5
.

Assume
\delta\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}
and take
J\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Denote
Y^{\varepsilon,J}(t,t_{\rm in})=\sum_{j=0}^{J}\varepsilon^{j}Y_{j}(t,t_{\rm in})
. Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then, there exists
C_{J,k}>0
such that

\left\|\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,t_{\rm in})-\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon,J}(t,t_{\rm in})\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{KS}(t,t_{\rm in})\right\|_{\mathcal{L}(\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})}\leq C_{k,J}|t-t_{\rm in}|\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta^{2}}\right)^{J+1}\delta^{-\kappa_{0}-k}

where
\kappa_{0}>0
is the universal constant of Lemma
4.3
.

Proof of Corollary
C.5
.

We use (
C.9
), and we obtain
\partial_{t}Y_{j+1}\in S^{1-2(j+1)}_{\delta}(\mathcal{D})
. Then, we estimate the remainder in (
C.8
). By Lemma
4.3
and Proposition
4.4
, we obtain in
\mathcal{L}(\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon,J}(t,t_{\rm in})-\left[\widehat{k}(t)\,\mathbb{I}_{m}+\varepsilon\widehat{K_{1}}(t),\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon,J}(t,t_{\rm in})\right]-\sum_{j=2}^{J}\varepsilon^{j}\widehat{K_{j}}(t)\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon,J}(t,t_{\rm in})

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta^{2}}\right)^{J+1}\delta^{-\kappa_{0}-k}\right),

and we deduce

i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,t_{\rm in})-\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon,J}(t,t_{\rm in})\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{KS}(t,t_{\rm in})\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\varepsilon\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\delta^{2}}\right)^{J+1}\delta^{-\kappa_{0}-k}\right),

which gives the result after integration from
t_{\rm in}
to
t
.
∎

C.3.
Propagation of wave packets

If
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\rm in}
is a wave packet and
K(t)
a matrix-valued Hamiltonian of the form (
C.1
) satisfying (
C.2
), then the action of the unitary propagator
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,t_{\rm in})
on
\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\rm in}
can be described precisely. Following Section 14.2 of
[
15
]
, Theorem 77, we have the following result.

Theorem C.6
.

Assume
\delta\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}
and

\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\rm in}={\rm WP}_{z_{0}}^{\varepsilon}(\vec{f}^{\varepsilon})
,

\vec{f}^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{0\leq j\leq N}\varepsilon^{j/2}\vec{f}_{j},\;\;\vec{f}_{j}\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m}).

There exists a family
(\vec{U}_{j}(t))_{j\geq 0}
defined on the interval
I_{\delta}
such that

(i)

For all
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
t\in I_{\delta}
,
\vec{U}_{j}(t)\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
,

(ii)

For all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
j\geq 2
, there exists a constant
C_{k,j}>0
such that

\sup_{t\in I_{\delta}}\,\sup_{|\alpha|+|\beta|\leq k}\|x^{\alpha}\partial_{x}^{\beta}\vec{U}_{j}(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C_{k,j}\,\delta^{1-j-k}.

(iii)

For all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, there exists
C_{k,N}
and
N_{k}
such that for all
t\in I_{\delta}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,t_{\rm in})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\rm in}-{\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(t,t_{\rm in},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{t}}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}{\mathcal{R}}(t,t_{\rm in})\,{\mathcal{M}}[F(t,t_{\rm in})]\vec{U}_{j}(t)\right)\right\|_{\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{k,N}\ \left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{N+1}\delta^{-k-\kappa_{0}}.

Here,
z_{t}=\Phi_{k}^{t,t_{\rm in}}(z_{0})
, while
F(t,t_{\rm in})=F(t,t_{\rm in},z_{0})
is the stability matrix (see (
1.15
)) and
{\mathcal{R}}(t,t_{\rm in})={\mathcal{R}}(t,t_{\rm in},z_{0})
satisfies the equation (
C.5
) for
z=z_{0}
.

Besides

(C.12)

\displaystyle\vec{U}_{0}(t)=\vec{f}_{0}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\vec{U}_{1}(t)=\vec{f}_{1}+{\bf b}_{1}(t,t_{\rm in})\vec{f}_{0}

where

\displaystyle{\bf b}_{1}(t,t_{\rm in})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i}\sum_{|\alpha|=3}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\int_{t_{\rm in}}^{t}\partial_{z}^{\alpha}k(s,\Phi_{k}^{s,t_{\rm in}}(z_{0})){\rm op}_{1}^{w}((F(s,t_{\rm in})z)^{\alpha})ds\,\mathbb{I}_{m}

(C.13)

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{\rm in}}^{t}{\mathcal{R}}(t_{\rm in},s)\partial_{z}K_{1}(s,\Phi_{k}^{s,t_{\rm in}}(z_{0})){\rm op}_{1}^{w}(F(s,t_{\rm in})z){\mathcal{R}}(s,t_{\rm in})\,ds.

Of course, the result holds for
K(t)=k(t)\mathbb{I}_{m}
, the scalar case, and for
K(t)=KS(t)=k(t)\mathbb{I}_{m}+\varepsilon K_{1}(t)
, which corresponds to the simplest perturbation of the scalar case by adding a subprincipal term.
In order to emphasize the characteristics of each of these cases, we detail the proof along these three cases: scalar, scalar with subprincipal perturbation, scalar with general perturbation. The first two cases have been considered in
[
15
]
, the strategy of which we follow. We note, that only in the third case the
\delta
-dependence of the estimates arises.

C.3.1.
The scalar case

In this section, we review the approximation for the standard scalar situation
K(t)=k(t)\mathbb{I}_{m}
with
z\mapsto k(t,z)
subquadratic for
t\in I_{\delta}
.

Proof of Theorem
C.6
in the scalar case.

We
look for an approximation of the propagated wave packet
\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\rm in}
by

(C.14)

\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{N}(t):={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(t,t_{\rm in},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{t}}\left(\vec{B}^{\varepsilon}_{N}(t)\right)

for some time-dependent Schwartz vector-valued and time dependent functions
z\mapsto\vec{B}^{\varepsilon}_{N}(t,z)
.
We have the two relations

\displaystyle i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{N}(t)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(t,t_{\rm in},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{t}}\left(-(\partial_{t}S(t,t_{\rm in},z_{0})-p_{t}\cdot\dot{q}_{t})\vec{B}_{N}^{\varepsilon}(t)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\dot{z}_{t}\cdot(J\widehat{z})\,\vec{B}_{N}^{\varepsilon}(t)+i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\vec{B}_{N}^{\varepsilon}(t)\right)

\displaystyle\widehat{k(t)}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{N}(t)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(t,t_{\rm in},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{t}}\left(\widehat{k(t,z_{t}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}z)}\vec{B}_{N}^{\varepsilon}(t).\right)

We look for
\vec{B}^{\varepsilon}_{N}(t)
of the form

(C.15)

\vec{B}^{\varepsilon}_{N}(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}\vec{B}_{j}(t).

We perform a Taylor expansion of
z\mapsto k(t,z_{t}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}z)

(C.16)

\displaystyle k(t,z_{t}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}z)

\displaystyle=k(t,z_{t})\,+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\nabla k(t,z_{t})\cdot z\,+\varepsilon\frac{1}{2}{\rm Hess}\,k(t,z_{t})z\cdot z\,

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{j=3}^{N+2}\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}L_{j}(t)[z]^{j}+\varepsilon^{\frac{N+3}{2}}R_{N}(t,z_{t},\sqrt{\varepsilon}z)[z]^{N+3}

for some coefficients
t\mapsto L_{j}(t)
uniformly bounded on
I_{\delta}
and a smooth function
z\mapsto R_{N}(t,z_{t},z)
satisfying uniform symbol estimates for
t\in I_{\delta}
.

We now construct the elements
\vec{B}_{j}(t)
,
0\leq j\leq N
, by solving recursive equations, that stem from
the classical flow and the action. We want to choose the
\vec{B}_{j}(t)
so that

i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{N}(t)=\widehat{k(t)}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{N}(t)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{\frac{N+3}{2}}).

Then,
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{K}(t,t_{\rm in})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\rm in}=\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{N}(t)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{\frac{N+1}{2}})
in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
with an error constant independent of
\delta
. We now construct the approximation
\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{N}(t)
in several steps.

(i)

The equation (
1.14
) for the action writes
\partial_{t}S(t,t_{\rm in},z_{0})=p_{t}\cdot\dot{q}_{t}-k(t,z_{t})
and allows to get rid of the terms of order
\varepsilon^{0}
.

(ii)

The equation for the flow
\dot{z}_{t}=J\nabla k(t,z_{t})
removes the terms of order
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
.

(iii)

For the term of order
\varepsilon
, we choose
\vec{B}_{0}(t)
so that

i\partial_{t}\vec{B}_{0}(t)=\frac{1}{2}{\rm Hess}\,k(t,z_{t})\widehat{z}\cdot\widehat{z}\,\vec{B}_{0}(t)

with
\vec{B}_{0}(t_{\rm in})=\vec{f}_{0}
, which is solved by

\vec{B}_{0}(t)={\mathcal{M}}[F(t,t_{\rm in})]\vec{f}_{0}={\mathcal{M}}[F(t,t_{\rm in})]\vec{U}_{0},

according to (
C.12
) since
\mathcal{R}(t,t_{\rm in})=\mathbb{I}_{m}
in the scalar case.

(iv)

Finally, we treat the terms in
\varepsilon^{1+\frac{j}{2}}
with
j\geq 1

by solving the equations

i\partial_{t}\vec{B}_{j}(t)=\frac{1}{2}{\rm Hess}\,k(t,z_{t})\widehat{z}\cdot\widehat{z}\,\vec{B}_{j}(t)+\vec{G}_{j}(t)

with
\vec{B}_{j}(t_{\rm in})=\vec{f}_{j}
and the source term
\vec{G}_{j}(t)
depends on
\vec{B}_{0}(t),\ldots,\vec{B}_{j-1}(t)
. Thus, by Duhamel formula:

\vec{B}_{j}(t)=\mathcal{M}[F(t,t_{\rm in})]\vec{f}_{j}+\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{\rm in}}^{t}\mathcal{M}[F(t,s)]\vec{G}_{j}(s)ds.

In the case
j=1
, we have

\vec{G}_{1}(t)=\widehat{p(z)}\vec{B}_{0}(t),\;\;p(z)=\sum_{|\alpha|=3}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{z}^{\alpha}k(t,z_{t})[z]^{\alpha}\mathbb{I}_{m}.

Moreover, by the (exact) Egorov theorem for quadratic Hamiltonians (see (
1.19
)), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}[F(t,s)]\,\widehat{p(z)}\,\mathcal{M}[F(s,t_{\rm in})]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}[F(t,t_{\rm in})]\,\widehat{p(F(s,t_{\rm in})z)}

This gives the expression of
\vec{U}_{1}(t)
in (
C.12
).
∎

C.3.2.
The scalar case with subprincipal perturbation

We now focus on the case
K(t)=KS(t)=k(t)\mathbb{I}_{m}+\varepsilon K_{1}(t)
, a subquadratic symbol with scalar principal symbol and matrix-valued perturbation.
We claim

(C.17)

\mathcal{U}_{KS}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\rm in}={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(t,t_{\rm in},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{t}}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}\vec{B}_{j}(t)\right)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{\frac{N+1}{2}})

with

\vec{B}_{j}(t)=\mathcal{R}(t,t_{\rm in})\mathcal{M}[F(t,t_{\rm in})]\vec{U}_{j}(t)
as in (
C.12
) for
j=0,\ldots,N
, and
\vec{B}_{j}(t)
is determined by a recursive equation in terms of
\vec{B}_{0}(t),\ldots,\vec{B}_{j-1}(t)
.

Proof of Theorem
C.6
with simple subprincipal term.

We argue as in the scalar case and look for an approximate value of the form (
C.14
).
We now have the two relations

\displaystyle i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{N}(t)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(t,t_{\rm in},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{t}}\left(-(\partial_{t}S(t,t_{\rm in},z_{0})-p_{t}\cdot\dot{q}_{t})\vec{B}_{j}(t)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\dot{z}_{t}\cdot(J\widehat{z})\,\vec{B}_{j}(t)+i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\vec{B}_{j}(t)\right)

\displaystyle\widehat{KS(t)}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{N}(t)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(t,t_{\rm in},z_{0})}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{t}}\left(\widehat{KS(t,z_{t}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}z)}\vec{B}_{j}(t)\right)

and perform a Taylor expansion of
z\mapsto KS(t,z_{t}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}z)

\displaystyle KS(t,z_{t}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}z)

\displaystyle=k(t,z_{t})\,\mathbb{I}_{m}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\,\nabla k(t,z_{t})\cdot z\,\mathbb{I}_{m}+\varepsilon\left(\frac{1}{2}{\rm Hess}\,k(t,z_{t})z\cdot z\,\mathbb{I}_{m}+K_{1}(t,z_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{j=3}^{N+2}\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}L_{j}(t)[z]^{j}+\varepsilon^{\frac{N+3}{2}}R_{N}(t,z_{t},\sqrt{\varepsilon}z)[z]^{N+3}.

Here again, the function
z\mapsto R_{N}(t,z_{t},z)
is smooth and uniformly bounded fo
t\in I_{\delta}
with bounded derivatives.
Similarly, the
L_{j}
-s are bounded on
I_{\delta}
.

We then verify that the properties of the classical flows and of the action allow to
choose
B^{\varepsilon}_{N}(t)
of the form (
C.15
) so that

i\varepsilon\partial_{t}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{N}(t)=\widehat{KS(t)}\varphi^{\varepsilon}_{N}(t)+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{\frac{N+3}{2}}).

The terms in
\varepsilon^{0}
and
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
are dealt with as in the scalar case.
However, the terms in
\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}
with
j\geq 2
yield modifications, due to the presence of the subprincipal term. We thus have to revisit the steps (iii) and (iv) of the proof in the scalar case.

(iii)

For the term of order
\varepsilon
, the equation of
\vec{B}_{0}(t)
now writes

i\partial_{t}\vec{B}_{0}(t)=\left(\frac{1}{2}{\rm Hess}\,k(t,z_{t})\widehat{z}\cdot\widehat{z}\,\mathbb{I}_{m}+K_{1}(t,z_{t})\right)\vec{B}_{0}(t)

with
\vec{B}_{0}(t_{\rm in})=\vec{f}_{0}
, which is solved by
\vec{B}_{0}(t)=\mathcal{R}(t,t_{\rm in})\mathcal{M}[F(t,t_{\rm in})]\vec{U}_{0}(t)
given in (
C.12
).

(iv)

The terms in
\varepsilon^{1+\frac{j}{2}}
with
j\geq 1

yield the equations

i\partial_{t}\vec{B}_{j}(t)=\left(\frac{1}{2}{\rm Hess}\,k(t,z_{t})\widehat{z}\cdot\widehat{z}\,\mathbb{I}_{m}+K_{1}(t,z_{t})\right)\vec{B}_{j}(t)+\vec{G}_{j}(t)

with
\vec{B}_{j}(t_{\rm in})=\vec{f}_{j}
and source terms
\vec{G}_{j}(t)
depending as before on
\vec{B}_{0}(t),\cdots\vec{B}_{j-1}(t)
. Duhamel formula now gives

\vec{B}_{j}(t)=\mathcal{R}(t,t_{\rm in})\mathcal{M}[F(t,t_{\rm in})]\left(\vec{f}_{j}+\frac{1}{i}\int_{t_{\rm in}}^{t}\mathcal{R}(t_{\rm in},s)\mathcal{M}[F(t_{\rm in},s)]\vec{G}_{j}(s)ds\right).

Indeed,
\mathcal{R}(t,t_{\rm in})=\mathcal{R}(t,t_{\rm in},z_{0})
is a matrix, independent of
z
, which commutes with the action of the scalar operator
\mathcal{M}[F(t,s)]=\mathcal{M}[F(t,s,z_{0})]
for all
s\in[t_{\rm in},t]
.

In the case
j=1
, we have

\vec{G}_{1}(t)=\widehat{p(z)}\vec{B}_{0}(t),\;\;p(z)=\sum_{|\alpha|=3}\frac{1}{\alpha!}\partial_{z}^{\alpha}k(t,z_{t})[z]^{\alpha}\mathbb{I}_{m}+\partial_{z}K_{1}(t,z_{t})\cdot z.

Moreover, by the exact Egorov theorem for quadratic Hamiltonians (see (
1.19
)), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(t_{\rm in},s)\mathcal{M}[F(t_{\rm in},s)]\widehat{p(z)}\mathcal{R}(s,t_{\rm in})\mathcal{M}[F(s,t_{\rm in})]=\mathcal{R}(t_{\rm in},s)\widehat{p(F(s,t_{\rm in})z)}\mathcal{R}(s,t_{\rm in}),

where again we have used the commutation of the
z
-independent matrix
\mathcal{R}(t,t_{\rm in})
with the scalar operator
\mathcal{M}[F(t,t_{\rm in})]
.
This gives the expression of
\vec{B}_{1}(t)
in (
C.12
) and concludes the proof of the claim (
C.17
).

∎

C.3.3.
The scalar case with general perturbation

We can now prove Theorem
C.6
in full generality and we have to take into account the effects of the terms
K_{j}(t)
with
j\geq 2
, in view of assumption (
C.2
).

Proof of Theorem
C.6
.

We first reduce to the evolution of wave packets by the subprincipal propagator
\mathcal{U}_{KS}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})
. Indeed, if
k,J\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

using Corollary
C.5
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\rm in}

\displaystyle=\,\widehat{Y}^{\varepsilon,J}(t,t_{\rm in})\,\mathcal{U}_{KS}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\rm in}+\mathcal{O}((\varepsilon/\delta^{2})^{J+1}\delta^{-\kappa_{0}-k}

in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
.
Combining this with the result of the preceding subsection, that is (
C.17
),
we have to balance an approximation of the order
\varepsilon^{J}
with one of the order
\varepsilon^{(N+1)/2}
. With a suitable choice of
J
, we have

\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\rm in}={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(t,t_{\rm in},z_{0})}\sum_{j=0}^{J}\sum_{n=0}^{N}\varepsilon^{j+\frac{n}{2}}\,\widehat{Y}_{j}(t,t_{\rm in})\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{t}}(\vec{B}_{n}(t))+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{N+1}\delta^{-k-\kappa_{0}}\right).

We now note that
Y_{0}(t,t_{\rm in})=\mathbb{I}_{m}
and, for
j\geq 1
,
n\geq 0
,

\widehat{Y}_{j}(t,t_{\rm in}){\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{t}}(\vec{B}_{n}(t))={\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{t}}\left({\rm op}_{1}\!\left(Y_{j}(t,t_{\rm in},z_{t}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}z)\right)\vec{B}_{n}(t)\right).

Sorting the double sum into powers of
\sqrt{\varepsilon}
, we thus arrive at

\mathcal{U}_{K}^{\varepsilon}(t,t_{\rm in})\psi^{\varepsilon}_{\rm in}={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(t,t_{\rm in},z_{0})}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z_{t}}\!\left(\vec{B}_{0}(t)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\vec{B}_{1}(t)+\sum_{n=2}^{N}\varepsilon^{\frac{n}{2}}\vec{C}_{n}(t)\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{N+1}\delta^{-k-\kappa_{0}}\right)

where each
\vec{C}_{n}(t)
,
n\geq 2
, is a correction of
\vec{B}_{\ell}(t)
by terms involving
Y_{j}(t,t_{\rm in})
with

2j\leq n
. Therefore the estimate (
C.10
) implies our claimed final result.
∎

C.4.
Combined actions of propagators on wave packets

We work here in the interval
I_{\delta}=(t^{\flat}-\delta,t^{\flat}+\delta)
where
t^{\flat}
plays the role of an initial time.
We focus on the action of
\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{2}}(t^{\flat},t)\,\mathcal{U}^{\varepsilon}_{H_{1}}(t,t^{\flat})
on a wave packet, where
H_{1}(t,z)
and
H_{2}(t,z)
are two symbols satisfying (
C.1
) and (
C.2
) on
I_{\delta}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
. We denote their scalar principal symbols by
h_{1}(t,z)
and
h_{2}(t,z)
, respectively.

Applying
Theorem
C.6
to the operators
\widehat{H}_{1}(t),\widehat{H}_{2}(t)
, we obtain for all
s\in[-\delta,\delta]
, in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
,

(C.18)

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{H_{2}}^{\varepsilon}(t^{\flat},t^{\flat}+s)\mathcal{U}_{H_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}){\rm WP}_{\zeta^{\flat}}^{\varepsilon}(\vec{\varphi})

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}S(s)}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}{\rm WP}_{\zeta(s)}^{\varepsilon}\bigl({\mathcal{M}}(s)\vec{\varphi}_{j}(s)\bigr)+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{J+1}\delta^{-k-\kappa_{0}}\right)

where we denoted the combined center, phase and metaplectic transform by

(C.19)

\displaystyle\zeta(s)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{h_{2}}^{t^{\flat},t^{\flat}+s}\big(\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}}(\zeta^{\flat})\big),

(C.20)

\displaystyle S(s)

\displaystyle=S_{1}(t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})+S_{2}(t^{\flat},t^{\flat}+s,\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}}(\zeta^{\flat})),

(C.21)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}(s)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{M}}[F_{2}(t^{\flat},t^{\flat}+s,\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}}(\zeta^{\flat}))]\,{\mathcal{M}}[F_{1}(t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})].

Here, we have used the commutation of the scalar operators given by the metaplectic transforms with the fixed matrices of parallel transport, which are included in the
\vec{\varphi}_{j}
.
Moreover, for all
s\in[\delta,\delta]
and for
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\vec{\varphi}_{j}(s)\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
and

(C.22)

\vec{\varphi}_{0}(s)=\mathcal{R}_{2}(t^{\flat},t^{\flat}+s,\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}}(\zeta^{\flat}))\mathcal{R}_{1}(t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})\vec{\varphi}.

Pushing forward the analysis as in
[
23
]
[Section 5.2]; we obtain the following statement, of which we will give all the elements of proofs.

Lemma C.7
.

Assume
\delta\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\vec{\varphi}\in\mathcal{S}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
and
(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})\in I_{\delta}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
. Then, there exists a family
(\vec{\varphi}_{j}(s))_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of vector-valued Schwartz functions parametrized by
s\in[-\delta,\delta]
and such that for all
s\in[-\delta,+\delta]
and
J\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we have in
\Sigma^{k}_{\varepsilon}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{H_{2}}^{\varepsilon}(t^{\flat},t^{\flat}+s)\mathcal{U}_{H_{1}}^{\varepsilon}(t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}){\rm WP}_{\zeta^{\flat}}^{\varepsilon}(\vec{\varphi})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{J}\,\varepsilon^{\frac{j}{2}}\,{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}\left({\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}\widetilde{\Lambda}(s)}{\rm e}^{i\widetilde{p}_{\varepsilon}(s)\cdot(y-\widetilde{q}_{\varepsilon}(s))}\vec{\varphi}_{j}(s,y-\widetilde{q}_{\varepsilon}(s))\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathcal{O}\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}{\delta}\right)^{N+1}\delta^{-k-\kappa_{0}}\right),

with, using notation (
1.36
) and (
1.37
), and setting
\widetilde{z}_{\varepsilon}(s)=(\tilde{q}_{\varepsilon}(s),\tilde{p}_{\varepsilon}(s))
,

\widetilde{z}_{\varepsilon}(0)=0,\quad\dot{\widetilde{z}}_{\varepsilon}(0)=(\alpha^{\flat},\beta^{\flat}),\;\;\widetilde{\Lambda}(0)=\dot{\widetilde{\Lambda}}(0)=0\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\frac{1}{2}\left(\ddot{\widetilde{\Lambda}}(0)-\dot{p}(0)\cdot\dot{q}(0)\right)=-\mu^{\flat}.

The proof of Lemma
C.7
relies on a careful analysis of the asymptotics of the functions
\zeta(s)
and
S(s)
, and of the operator
\mathcal{M}(s)
. We will use the next Lemma, the proof of which is postponed to the end of the section.

Lemma C.8
.

Let
\zeta
and
S
be defined as above, and write
\zeta(s)=\zeta^{\flat}+z(s)
with
z(s)=(q(s),p(s))
. Consider
\Lambda
given for
s\in[-\delta,\delta]
by

\displaystyle\Lambda(s)

\displaystyle=S_{1}(t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})+S_{2}(t^{\flat},t^{\flat}+s,\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}}(\zeta^{\flat}))-q(s)\cdot p^{\flat}.

We have

(C.23)

\displaystyle\zeta(0)

\displaystyle=\zeta^{\flat},\;\;\dot{\zeta}(0)=(\alpha^{\flat},\beta^{\flat}),\;\;S(0)=0,\;\;\dot{S}(0)=p^{\flat}\cdot\alpha^{\flat},

(C.24)

\displaystyle\Lambda(0)

\displaystyle=\dot{\Lambda}(0)=0,\;\;\frac{1}{2}\left(\ddot{\Lambda}(0)-\dot{p}(0)\cdot\dot{q}(0)\right)=-\mu^{\flat}.

Proof of Lemma
C.7
.

We start with (
C.18
). We then have by Lemma
C.8

z(0)=0
and
\dot{z}(0)=(\alpha^{\flat},\beta^{\flat})
.
We use the properties of the wave packet transform

\displaystyle{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z}={\rm e}^{-\frac{i}{2\varepsilon}p\cdot q}\,\widehat{T}^{\varepsilon}(z)\,\Lambda^{\varepsilon},

with

\displaystyle\widehat{T}^{\varepsilon}(z)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{\varepsilon}(p\cdot\hat{x}-q\cdot\hat{\xi})}\;\;\mbox{and}\;\;\Lambda^{\varepsilon}\varphi(x)=\varepsilon^{-\frac{d}{4}}\varphi(x/\sqrt{\varepsilon}),\;\;x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}.

We obtain

\displaystyle{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta(s)}

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{-\frac{i}{\varepsilon}p^{\flat}\cdot q(s)}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{-1}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{z(s)}

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{-\frac{i}{\varepsilon}p^{\flat}\cdot q(s)}{\rm e}^{-\frac{i}{2\varepsilon}p(s)\cdot q(s)}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}\Lambda_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\widehat{T}^{\varepsilon}(z(s))\Lambda_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle={\rm e}^{-\frac{i}{\varepsilon}p^{\flat}\cdot q(s)}{\rm e}^{-\frac{i}{2\varepsilon}p(s)\cdot q(s)}{\rm WP}^{\varepsilon}_{\zeta^{\flat}}\widehat{T}^{1}(z_{\varepsilon}(s)),

with
z_{\varepsilon}(s)=z(s)/\sqrt{\varepsilon}
. By the translation properties
of the metaplectic transform
[
15
, Section 3.3]
, we have

\widehat{T}^{1}(z_{\varepsilon}(s)){\mathcal{M}}(s)={\mathcal{M}}(s)\widehat{T}^{1}(\widetilde{z}_{\varepsilon}(s))

with the new center

\widetilde{z}_{\varepsilon}(s)=\widetilde{z}(s)/\sqrt{\varepsilon},\;\;\widetilde{z}(s)=F_{1}(t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})^{-1}F_{2}(t^{\flat},t^{\flat}+s,\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}}(\zeta^{\flat}))^{-1}z(s)

We observe that

\widetilde{z}(0)=z(0)=0,\quad\dot{\widetilde{z}}(0)=\dot{z}(0)=(\alpha^{\flat},\beta^{\flat}).

Since

\widehat{T}^{1}(\widetilde{z}_{\varepsilon}(s))\varphi(y)={\rm e}^{\frac{i}{2}\widetilde{q}_{\varepsilon}(s)\cdot\widetilde{p}_{\varepsilon}(s)}{\rm e}^{i\widetilde{p}_{\varepsilon}(s)\cdot(y-\widetilde{q}_{\varepsilon}(s))}\varphi(y-\widetilde{q}_{\varepsilon}(s)),

the phase
\widetilde{\Lambda}(s)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Lambda}(s)

\displaystyle=S(s)-p^{\flat}\cdot q(s)-\frac{1}{2}\,p(s)\cdot q(s)+\frac{1}{2}\,\widetilde{p}(s)\cdot\widetilde{q}(s),

yields the approximation of the Lemma. Indeed,
we have
\widetilde{\Lambda}(0)=\dot{\widetilde{\Lambda}}(0)=0
,

\ddot{\widetilde{\Lambda}}(0)=\ddot{\Lambda}(0)
and we conclude
by Lemma
C.8
.
∎

It remains to prove the asymptotics of Lemma
C.8
.

Proof of Lemma
C.8
.

We begin with the function
\zeta
and
we compute the Taylor expansion at the order 2 for
(q(s),p(s))=\zeta(s)-\zeta^{\flat}
at
s=0
.
Let be
h=h_{1},h_{2}
. We have :

(C.25)

\displaystyle\Phi_{h}^{t,t_{0}}(z)=z

\displaystyle+(t-t_{0})J\partial_{z}h(t_{0},z)+\frac{(t-t_{0})^{2}}{2}\left(J\partial^{2}_{t,z}h(t_{0},z)+J\partial^{2}_{z,z}h(t_{0},z)J\partial_{z}h(t_{0},z)\right)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(|t-t_{0}|^{3}).

Applying this formula, we obtain (omitting the argument
(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})
in the functions
h_{1}
,
h_{2}
and their derivatives)

\displaystyle\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}}(\zeta^{\flat})=\zeta^{\flat}+sJ\partial_{z}h_{1}+\frac{s^{2}}{2}\left(J\partial^{2}_{t,z}h_{1}+J\partial^{2}_{z,z}h_{1}J\partial_{z}h_{1}\right)+\mathcal{O}(|s|^{3}),

\displaystyle\zeta(t)=\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}}(\zeta^{\flat})-sJ\partial_{z}h_{2}(t^{\flat}+s,\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}}(\zeta^{\flat}))+\frac{s^{2}}{2}\left(J\partial^{2}_{t,z}h_{2}+J\partial^{2}_{z,z}h_{2}J\partial_{z}h_{2}\right)+\mathcal{O}(|s|^{3}).

We deduce

\displaystyle\zeta(t)

\displaystyle=\zeta^{\flat}+sJ\partial_{z}(h_{1}-h_{2})+\mathcal{O}(|s|^{3})

\displaystyle+\frac{s^{2}}{2}\left(J\partial^{2}_{t,z}(h_{1}-h_{2})+J\partial^{2}_{z,z}(h_{1}-h_{2})J\partial_{z}h_{1}+J\partial^{2}_{z,z}h_{2}J\partial_{z}(h_{2}-h_{1})\right),

and, for further use, the relation

(C.26)

\displaystyle-p^{\flat}\dot{q}(0)=

\displaystyle-p^{\flat}\cdot\partial_{q}(h_{1}-h_{2}),

(C.27)

\displaystyle-p^{\flat}\cdot\ddot{q}(0)=

\displaystyle-p^{\flat}\cdot(\partial^{2}_{t,p}(h_{1}-h_{2})+\partial^{2}_{z,p}(h_{1}-h_{2})J\partial_{z}h_{1}+\partial^{2}_{z,p}h_{2}J\partial_{z}(h_{2}-h_{1}))

We continue with the function
\Lambda
and we use Taylor expansion of the actions for general Hamiltonian
h
. In view of (
1.14
) and (
C.25
), we have (omitting the argument
(t_{0},z_{0})
in the terms of the form
\partial^{\alpha}h(t_{0},z_{0})
)

\displaystyle S(t,t_{0},z_{0})

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}(p_{0}-(s-t_{0})\partial_{q}h)\cdot(\partial_{p}h+(s-t_{0})(\partial^{2}_{t,p}h+\partial^{2}_{z,p}hJ\partial_{z}h))ds

\displaystyle\qquad-\int_{t_{0}}^{t}(h+(s-t_{0})\partial_{t}h)ds+\mathcal{O}((t-t_{0})^{3})

\displaystyle=(p_{0}\cdot\partial_{p}h-h)(t-t_{0})-\frac{(t-t_{0})^{2}}{2}(\partial_{t}h+\partial_{q}h\cdot\partial_{p}h-p_{0}\cdot(\partial_{t,p}^{2}h+\partial^{2}_{z,p}hJ\partial_{z}h))

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathcal{O}((t-t_{0})^{3}).

We first apply the formula with
h=h_{1}
,
t=t^{\flat}+s
,
t=t^{\flat}
and
z=\zeta^{\flat}
, which gives (when the arguments of the functions are omitted, they are fixed to
(t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})
)

\displaystyle S_{1}(t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})=

\displaystyle\,s(p\cdot\partial_{p}h_{1}-h_{1})

\displaystyle-\frac{s^{2}}{2}(\partial_{t}h_{1}+\partial_{q}h_{1}\cdot\partial_{p}h_{1}-p\cdot(\partial_{t,p}^{2}h_{1}+\partial^{2}_{z,p}h_{1}J\partial_{z}h_{1}))+\mathcal{O}(s^{3}).

We now use the same formula with
h=h_{2}
,
t=t^{\flat}
,
t_{0}=t^{\flat}+s
,
z_{0}=\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}}(\zeta^{\flat})
. We obtain

\displaystyle S_{2}(t^{\flat},t^{\flat}+s,\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}}(\zeta^{\flat}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-s

\displaystyle(p_{1}(t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})\cdot\partial_{p}h_{2}(t^{\flat}+s,\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}}(\zeta^{\flat}))-h_{2}(t^{\flat}+s,\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}}(\zeta^{\flat})))

\displaystyle-\frac{s^{2}}{2}(\partial_{t}h_{2}+\partial_{q}h_{2}\cdot\partial_{p}h_{2}-p\cdot(\partial_{t,p}^{2}h_{2}+\partial^{2}_{z,p}h_{2}J\partial_{z}h_{2}))+\mathcal{O}(s^{3})

The treatment of the term of order
s
has to be performed carefully for
S_{2}(t^{\flat},t^{\flat}+s,\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}}(\zeta^{\flat}))
.
We obtain

\displaystyle S_{2}(t^{\flat},t^{\flat}+s,\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}}(\zeta^{\flat}))=-s(p\cdot\partial_{p}h_{2}-h_{2})-s^{2}(-\partial_{t}h_{2}-\partial_{q}h_{2}\cdot\partial_{p}h_{1}+p\cdot(\partial^{2}_{t,p}h_{2}+\partial^{2}_{z,p}h_{2}J\partial_{z}h_{2}))

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{s^{2}}{2}(\partial_{t}h_{2}+\partial_{q}h_{2}\cdot\partial_{p}h_{2}-p\cdot(\partial_{t,p}^{2}h_{2}+\partial^{2}_{z,p}h_{2}J\partial_{z}h_{1}))+\mathcal{O}(s^{3})

\displaystyle\;\;=\,(p\cdot\partial_{p}h_{2}-h_{2})s+\frac{s^{2}}{2}(\partial_{t}h_{2}+\partial_{q}h_{2}\cdot\partial_{p}(2h_{1}-h_{2})-p\cdot(\partial_{t,p}^{2}h_{2}+\partial^{2}_{z,p}h_{2}J\partial_{z}(2h_{2}-h_{1})+\mathcal{O}(s^{3})

As a consequence,

\displaystyle S_{1}(t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat},\zeta^{\flat})+

\displaystyle S_{2}(t^{\flat},t^{\flat}+s,\Phi_{h_{1}}^{t^{\flat}+s,t^{\flat}}(\zeta^{\flat}))=s\,p\cdot\partial_{p}(h_{1}-h_{2})+\frac{s^{2}}{2}(\partial_{t}(h_{2}-h_{1})

\displaystyle-\partial_{q}h_{2}\cdot\partial_{p}(h_{2}-h_{1})+\partial_{p}h_{1}\cdot\partial_{q}(h_{2}-h_{1})

\displaystyle+p\cdot(\partial_{t,p}^{2}(h_{1}-h_{2})+\partial_{z,p}^{2}(h_{1}-h_{2})J\partial_{z}h_{1}+\partial_{z,p}^{2}h_{2}J\partial_{z}(h_{1}-h_{2})))+\mathcal{O}(s^{3}).

Combining with (
C.27
), we obtain

\displaystyle\Lambda(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{s^{2}}{2}(\partial_{t}(h_{2}-h_{1})-\partial_{q}h_{2}\cdot\partial_{p}(h_{2}-h_{1})+\partial_{p}h_{1}\cdot\partial_{q}(h_{2}-h_{1}))+\mathcal{O}(s^{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{s^{2}}{2}(\partial_{t}(h_{2}-h_{1})-\partial_{q}h_{2}\cdot\partial_{p}(h_{2}-h_{1})+\partial_{p}h_{1}\cdot\partial_{q}(h_{2}-h_{1}))+\mathcal{O}(s^{3}).

Finally, we observe

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\ddot{\Lambda}(0)-{\dot{p}(0)\cdot\dot{q}(0)})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{t}(h_{2}-h_{1})-\partial_{q}h_{2}\cdot\partial_{p}(h_{2}-h_{1})+\partial_{p}h_{1}\cdot\partial_{q}(h_{2}-h_{1})+\partial_{p}(h_{2}-h_{1})\cdot\partial_{q}(h_{2}-h_{1})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{t}(h_{2}-h_{1})-\partial_{q}h_{1}\cdot\partial_{p}(h_{2}-h_{1})+\partial_{p}h_{1}\cdot\partial_{q}(h_{2}-h_{1})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{t}(h_{2}-h_{1})+\left\{\frac{h_{1}+h_{2}}{2},h_{2}-h_{1}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=-(\partial_{t}f+\{v,f\}).

∎
